The President's Message To The Congress of the United States: 

All over the world, America is known as a “land of liberty.” For the settlers who first came to America by ship in the 17th century, this new land promised a New World, and a new chance to make possible the oldest of dreams - the dream of personal liberty. The early settlers and explorers found an abundance of land - virgin forests, untouched meadows, bountiful streams, and sweet-smelling air - that vastly exceeded anything the kings of the old world could have ever imagined. In his first inaugural address, Thomas Jefferson spoke of a country with land sufficient to "the thousand thousandth generation." There was so much land in America that there was no way to restrict it to a privileged few. Instead of locking it up for the exclusive use of royalty, the Founding Fathers made possible the widespread ownership of the lands west of the original colonies by anyone brave enough to take the risk, to grasp the main chance, and to hope for a better tomorrow.

The settlement of this great and generous land and the development of its resources created a diverse and expansive American republic of hope, opportunity, experimentation, mobility, and personal freedom. Americans created so much wealth, and shared it so widely, that eventually ordinary men and women could afford to travel West just for pleasure. Americans' fascination with the West, and advances in transportation, education, and science, contributed to a successful popular movement to restore and beautify cities with public parks, arboreta and gardens, and to preserve other places of natural beauty or curiosity. The Federal government began to set aside wondrous places "for the benefit and enjoyment of the people,” such as Yellowstone in 1872, when the Congress established it as the first national park. Today the National Park Service manages 341 units on 76 million acres preserved as parks, monuments, historic, cultural, and recreation sites. A philosophy of conservation and wise use was championed by 

Theodore Roosevelt, who signed legislation in 1905 that created the Forest Service and brought about the national forest system that today includes 191 million acres of national forests and grasslands, managed under multipleuse principles.

The first unit of the modern National Wildlife Refuge System dates back to President Roosevelt's designation in 1903 of Florida's three-acre Pelican Island as a refuge for colonial nesting birds. Popular interest in the conservation of native birds at that time led to the establishment of independent iii

Audubon societies in many States, and a national association was formed in 1905. By 1910, most States had an agency protecting wildlife and fisheries. 

The Migratory Bird Act of 1918 authorized international agreements and Federal management for migrating waterfowl and other birds. Today the 

Fish and Wildlife Service manages 443 refuges on nearly 90 million acres, and administers the Endangered Species Act of 1973, that further protects native animal and plant species whose survival is threatened or endangered. 

Private organizations dedicated to the restoration of viable populations of such species, to the conservancy of rare habitats, or to the protection of wetlands that are critical to waterfowl have continued to enlarge their contribution to the preservation of natural systems. In recent years, the idea of protection of natural habitat has also been extended to marine environments through the establishment of marine sanctuaries. 

Earlier in our Nation's history, the cause of liberty was well served by the government making a rapid and flexible divestiture of its land, out of which our people created a prosperous and generous nation of communities. The preservation of parks, wilderness, and wildlife has also aided liberty by keeping alive the 19th century sense of adventure and awe with which our forefathers greeted the American West. Many laws protecting environmental quality have promoted liberty by securing property against the destructive trespass of pollution. In our own time, the nearly universal appreciation of these preserved landscapes, restored waters, and cleaner air through outdoor recreation is a modern expression of our freedom and leisure to enjoy the wonderful life that generations past have built for us. 

This Administration has understood the necessary relationship between freedom and opportunity, between opportunity and growth, between growth and progress, including progress in restoring and maintaining the quality of the human environment.

The same spirit of creativity and innovation that has created 17 million jobs has also benefited the land itself, making America the beautiful more beautiful still.

The accompanying report of the Council on Environmental Quality, which I am pleased to transmit today, provides an accounting of the natural systems and resources that Americans have accorded special protection by government agencies and by private voluntary associations active in States and communities all over this country. No other nation in the world has done more to preserve and improve its natural environment. We are a people who take pride in America, and in the environmental quality of the communities we helped to build, and who will continue to build our future as we did our past, in freedom.

orrald Reagan 

THE WHITE HOUSE

Preface 

Environmental Quality 1986 is the first of a trilogy of reports being published by the Council on Environmental Quality during calendar year 1988, in fulfillment of the National Environmental Policy Act of 1969 (P.L. 91-190, 42 

U.S.C. 4341)("NEPA"). Together these reports will set forth the most current information on a broad range of the major natural, manmade or altered environmental classes of the nation, as required by section 201 of NEPA. 

This first report focuses on environmental classes or media that are accorded special management protection for preserving natural values (i.e., birdlife, wetlands, marine sanctuaries and the public recreation lands); the second report will describe intensively used and manmade environments, such as are found in urban and suburban areas; and the third report will deal with lands where resources have been altered by human development but that are not heavily populated. Each report treats a limited number of selected topics in considerable depth, giving Congress, researchers and students of environment and natural resource policy a detailed accounting of the conditions and trends for the subject resource, the public and private programs affecting it, and the issues to be resolved in the future. 

This report also includes a comprehensive set of statistical tables (Appendix C) that corresponds to a new edition of the Council on Environmental Quality's Environmental Trends sourcebook, last published in 1981. The printing of this book of charts and graphs is still pending, but the updated data have been fully reviewed and are being published here in tabular form. 

This set of tables was selected by an interagency group convened by the Council on Environmental Quality to develop representative indicators of environmental trends, based on reliable and consistent information sources. 

The Council expects to continue to update these tables as new data become available. The statistical appendix has been prepared for the Council under contract with the Virginia Institute of Marine Science. vii

Birdlife of the United States 1 

Some have attempted to justify wildlife in terms of meat, others in terms of personal pleasure, others in terms of cash, still others in the interest of science, education, agriculture, art, public health, and even military preparedness. But few have so far clearly realized and expressed the whole truth; namely that all these things are but factors in a broad social value, and that wildlife is a social asset. - Aldo Leopold Of the over 9000 species of birds of the world, an estimated 12-15 percent occur in the United States and its possessions. Birds are among our most valued environmental assets. Not only do they play important roles in natural ecosystems and brighten our lives by their universal aesthetic appeal, they provide recreation and sport to millions. It is estimated that there are currently 10 million bird watchers in the United States, plus 2.7 million persons who hunt birds for sport. In 1980 alone, migratory bird hunting plus other non-consumptive uses of wildlife contributed an estimated 3.5 billion dollars to the economy, and involved over 430 million use-days. 1

Birds are interesting scientifically, too, because each species represents an independent solution to a unique set of environmental conditions. Some species are widespread and thrive in a variety of situations. Others are specialized for life in harsh and unusual circumstances. The latter are of great concern because the habitats they prefer or require are often very limited and are easily altered or degraded. In some cases, these habitats and their special birds are already gone. Well-known cases include the Ivory-billed 

Woodpecker, of the virgin hardwood forests of the river bottomlands in the southern United States, which were lost to logging; the Dusky Seaside Sparrow, of the coastal river marshes of the Florida east coast, which were diked and drained for development, roads and mosquito contol; and Bachman's 

Warbler, of the formerly extensive cane brakes of the Southeast, which disappeared as the lands were cleared for agriculture.2,3,4

The preservation of the living resources of Earth - its biodiversity is an acknowledged goal of the United States as stated in the National Environmental Policy Act of 1969. 5,6

This chapter outlines the status and major trends in the birdlife of the United States. We have selected birds, not because they are more valuable than other forms of wildlife but because the information on them is more complete than for other groups of vertebrates, and because the problems associated with the preservation of bird diversity illustrate those that affect wildlife in general. 

Status and Trends

Sources of Information 

Because dozens of public and private agencies have long taken a prominent interest in birds, much information on their status and population trends has been accumulated. There is, unfortunately, no central repository for this information, whose quality is highly variable. Some data are qualitative, useful only as indices to local and regional patterns of changing distribution. Other data, based on carefully designed quantitative approaches and carried out nationwide, are suitable for rigorous mathematical trend analysis. The duration of the data base varies widely, from a few years to many decades. As a result of this unevenness, documenting general trends is extremely difficult and for many species or groups the data allow no more than educated guesses. Some of the more comprehensive sources of information follow. 

The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS), which for over 100 years has been the federal government's lead agency for managing and conserving wildlife, has amassed long-term data on many groups of birds, especially those of economic importance. Much of this information remains unanalyzed, for example in the files of individual national wildlife refuges. Although FWS effort has traditionally emphasized waterfowl, in recent years the service has begun to pay attention to nongame species. One impressive 2

achievement has been the Breeding Bird Survey, a cooperative venture with the Canadian Wildlife Service. This program collects data on all bird species, but its greatest contribution has been to accumulate data on landbirds, which previously had been all but ignored by management agencies. Initiated in 1965, the survey determines population trends for species that nest in North America north of Mexico and that migrate across international boundaries. Another innovation of the FWS bearing on population trends is a series of atlases providing baseline data on the status and distribution of several major groups of birds. 9,10,11,12

The National Audubon Society also coordinates many studies that provide important long term data. These include: 

Christmas Bird Census. This program, which commenced in 1900, represents "the most extensive, longest term, continuous, and most geographically comprehensive data set in American ornithology." Today over 1300 censuses are made annually. "No other branch of field zoology... has any sample comparable in size, scope, and regularity.. >>12 Winter Bird-populations Study. Started in 1947, it employs standardized procedures to determine the numbers of birds wintering in specific habitat types. 13 Breeding-bird Censuses. This program, started in 1938, measures breeding bird populations in uniform habitats typical of large regions." 14 

The "Blue List". Initiated in 1972 as an "early warning system" for species undergoing local or widespread range contractions or declines, it is based on data from hundreds of amateur field ornithologists throughout the United States. About 56 species were listed in the continental United States in 1986. 15 In addition many private organizations nationwide have accumulated long-term data either on specific geographic areas or on selected groups of birds. 16 Among others, these include a half-century of data on hawk migration in the eastern United States (Hawk Mountain Sanctuary Association, Pennsylvania) studies of birds on the Farallon Islands, California (Point Reyes

Bird Observatory, California), surveys of migrating shorebirds along the Atlantic coast (Manomet Bird Observatory, Massachusetts) and protracted studies of bird populations in reserves maintained by colleges and universities. The ornithological literature also contains many synopses of trends in individual species which in recent years have been dominated by studies of 

Endangered Species. 17,18,19,20

In the following sections, we have attempted to document and analyze population trends in those groups of birds for which adequate data exist. In considering this challenge, it is important to recognize that birds are highly mobile. Perhaps 70 percent of the species occurring in the continental 

United States spend part of their annual cycle in other countries. As a result, inferences regarding changes must be based on a broad - and sometimes hemispheric perspective, for major determinants of change often take place far from the areas in which they are detected. This fact has long been acknowledged by the United States, through many international treaties dealing with the protection and conservation of birds (Tables 1 and 2).

Establishes Conservation 

Reserve Program Seabirds 

Well over 32 million pairs of seabirds (petrels, pelicans, cormorants, gulls, terns, auks, and their allies) of 106 species breed in North America (including continental North America, the Caribbean Sea, and the Hawaiian Islands). Allowing for non-breeding birds and species that are uncensused and likely underestimated, the total population probably approximates 100 million birds. To these may be added tens of millions of seabirds of a few species that breed in the southern hemisphere but spend their non-breeding season north of the equator.

Although many reviews and atlases concerning the status and distribution of seabirds exist, populations of most species have never been determined adequately. 10,11,21,22,23,24,25,26,27 This is due partly to logistic constraints associated with the remote and inaccessible areas used by nesting seabirds, and to the nocturnal habits of some; also involved, however, is the fact that the life cycle of seabirds "being partly terrestrial and partly pelagic, falls in the jurisdictional cracks between the Department of the Interior (a terrestrial agency) and the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Agency (an oceanic agency). As a result, it has rarely been possible to understand the status of seabirds in more than a general qualitative way 

(Table 3).

STATUS 

For some species, nevertheless, there are clear-cut trends, as evidenced by shifts in breeding range and changes in the number of colonies or reproductive success. Scavenging species such as gulls and fulmars are flourishing, because of increases in food provided by man, which includes offal from commercial fishing boats at sea and garbage dumps on shore. The spectacular population increases in several species of gulls in North America - which for some has involved a doubling or tripling of numbers in the past several decades - has sometimes led to local or regional declines of species that nest colonially with gulls, such as Common Terns in the 

Great Lakes or Roseate Terns on the East Coast.17,29,30

Despite some persecution by man, the fish-eating Great and Doublecrested Cormorants are increasing rapidly, with much of the change being attributable to increased protection and decreased use of organochloride pesticides. Brown Pelicans have shown increases since the mid-1970s for the same reasons, and the eastern population, once endangered, is now secure. 

Terns, as a group, seem to be declining. In addition to suffering from competition with gulls, they are particularly prone to disturbance and loss of nesting habitat, which have played a major role in the endangerment of Least and Roseate terns. Egg collecting as well as killing adults for food on the wintering grounds in South America has also contributed to the decline in the latter species.

Historically, particularly in the continental United States, most seabird species were more abundant than they are today. Populations were reduced by hunting, habitat manipulation, or the introduction of animals. Some species may never recover their historic range (e.g., Northern Gannet); others were driven to extinction (e.g., Great Auk). In the past several decades, however, the only period for which there are anything like adequate population estimates, the North American seabirds are mostly holding their own or increasing. The greatest immediate concerns are for species nesting in temperate and tropical areas, where increased human disturbance, exploitation of birds, the presence of introduced animals, and the loss of nesting areas are severe. This will be especially significant to species with very limited breeding distributions, such as Townsend's Shearwater and Elegant Tern.

Seabird Mortality

Although the status of most seabird species is good, substantial numbers are being killed or injured in some areas as a result of entanglement in, or ingestion of, man-made debris, particularly plastics. Identification of the "plastic problem" in the early 1980s resulted in extensive research by federal (NOAA/NMFS), state, and private agencies to document the situation and its extent, and in major educational programs to reduce the sources of pollution. While answers to many questions are pending, enough is known to put the problems into perspective. 31 Entanglement: Drift gillnets are used commonly in commercial fisheries in the North Pacific. Constructed from plastic and of light weight, these may be an incredible 50 km long and hang to depths of 8-12 meters below the surface, occupying the main foraging zone for many marine birds and mammals. Currently, there are five major high seas gillnet fisheries in the North 

Pacific: Japanese salmon mothership, Japanese salmon landbased, Japanese squid, Republic of Korea squid, and Taiwanese squid.

In the salmon mothership fishery 5000-6000 net sets are made annually by 300-500 ships, resulting in an estimated incidental take of 96,000-251,000 seabirds of 22 species. The landbased fishery, of similar size, may take an additional 130,000-290,000 individuals. The most susceptible are species that forage by deep diving, such are murres, puffins, and auklets. Shallow divers, including shearwaters, are less affected, and surface-feeders are rarely captured. Short-tailed Shearwaters comprise about 60 percent of the kill and Tufted Puffins 20 percent; in both species, mostly young birds are taken.

Despite the seemingly high capture rates, it is not clear that the fisheries are affecting the stability of seabird populations. The mothership operations, alone, are not considered a major problem. The average take of Shorttailed Shearwaters, for example, about 69,000 birds, is only one-tenth of the number of chicks killed legally each year in the southern hemisphere for human consumption. The total population of this species exceeds 20 million individuals. Similarly, annual losses of puffins may also be negligible on a species-wide basis. 32,33,34

Although the squid fishery, comprising about 700 boats, is larger than the salmon fishery, its effects are likely smaller, because (1) the fishery operates far offshore, where seabird densities are low, and (2) the dominant species are surface-feeders, which are rarely entrapped.

The total number of birds lost each year, however, is an unsatisfactory measure of impact because it masks the effects of local impacts. The offshore salmon fishery concentrates its efforts south of the westernmost Aleutians, where in 1983 nearly 9 percent of the Tufted Puffins in that area may have been killed. 34 Local impacts have also been adduced from other fisheries, especially those carried out in near-shore waters adjacent to seabird nesting areas. In British Columbia, a local salmon fishery is es-

timated to have netted 6 percent of the breeding Marbled Murrelets.” And along the central California coast from 1983 to 1986, gill and trammel nets used in the halibut and white croaker fisheries are thought to have drowned an estimated 45,000 Common Murres and 5000 other seabirds (mainly 

Pigeon Guillemots, loons, cormorants, and grebes) (Table 4). Concurrently, breeding populations of murres dropped from 209,000 to 90,000.

While fishing was not solely responsible some decrease resulted from major oil spills (1984 and 1986) and a change in the availability of prey during the 1982-83 El Nino - the California Department of Fish and Game ordered an end to gill netting in areas shallower than 20 fathoms along 150 miles of coast, an action that effectively terminated the fishery. Expanding fishing operations in the Gulf of California, Mexico, are also of concern, because thousands of diving birds winter there. An estimated 5000 Arctic 

Loons were drowned in nets during the northward migration in 1987. 36 ■ Plastic Debris: In the early 1960s independent studies in the North 

Pacific, North Atlantic, and New Zealand revealed that many species of seabirds were eating large amounts of plastic debris.37,38 The condition has since been documented around the globe in over 50 species, with the percentage of affected birds in some species increasing annually (at least into the late 1970s; Figure 1). The plastic is mistaken for food; small light-brown bits that resemble planktonic invertebrates are taken with greatest frequency. Typically, rates of contamination are highest in species that take crustaceans, cephalopods, or small plankton from the surface. Laysan 

Albatrosses, Short-tailed Shearwaters, Parakeet Auklets and prions are highly susceptible, with 50-100 percent of the birds being contaminated. 

Gulls, which also scavenge at the surface, and diving birds that feed on freeswimming fish, are rarely affected. Interestingly, adult seabirds ingest less debris than younger birds, showing that they have learned to recognize and ignore this form of pollution. 37,38

The question of whether the ingested plastic has a detrimental effect on the physical condition or reproductive performance of seabirds is under in- 10

vestigation. Direct physical damage seems unlikely. Seabirds often eat hard objects, which they are easily able to eject, to promote the mechanical digestion of prey. Furthermore, the risk of intestinal obstruction is "so infrequent ..as to be an unimportant cause of mortality."39 Seabirds containing plastic found dead in New Zealand were in no worse shape than birds that had not been contaminated.39

The evidence on indirect effects is poorer. Many seabirds feed their young by regurgitation and albatross chicks that have been fed large amounts of plastic fare poorly. 40,41 This may not be significant, however, because adults are likely to provide debris only when natural food is scarce, that is, in periods when chick survival would already be poor. Furthermore, its seems likely that "ingested plastic seldom impairs digestive efficiency in seabirds."39 At present, it appears that rates and amounts of ingested plastic are too small to have a detrimental effect on most species. 

In February 1987, President Reagan requested Congress to ratify Annex

V of the International Convention for the Prevention of Pollution from 

Ships (MARPOL), which prohibits disposal into the sea of "all plastics, including but not limited to synthetic ropes, synthetic fishing gear, and plastic garbage bags." Early ratification is assured. Although long-term monitoring will be needed to determine the degree of compliance and the rate at which floating debris diminishes, and while detailed information is still needed on seabird distribution and mortality, this environmental issue was addressed in a timely manner and can be considered well into the stage of resolution. 11

Waterfowl

The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service has management responsibility for 59 species of ducks, geese, and swans protected by the Migratory Bird Treaty Act; the Service establishes annual hunting regulations for 41 of these species. For decades it, along with other governmental and private agencies, has conducted annual censuses on the breeding grounds, at important points along the migratory routes, and in wintering areas. These data, along with assessments of weather and habitat conditions, provide a sensitive index to the status of many common species, which are used to derive harvest regulations and to guide management and research studies. Accordingly, the status and trends of waterfowl in North America are exceptionally well documented.

In North America the major breeding grounds for waterfowl extend from the northern United States to arctic Canada and Alaska. The major wintering areas are in the southern United States and Middle America. Early realization that a country by country approach to managing this important but mobile resource would be doomed to failure resulted in the passage of the Migratory Bird Treaty between the U.S. and Canada in 1916 and in subsequent treaty obligations with Mexico, Japan and the Soviet Union (Table 1). 

Originally, waterfowl management consisted mainly of regulating the harvest and acquiring and managing habitat, but it soon became clear that these steps would be inadequate unless habitats were also protected. This led to the passage of the Migratory Bird Conservation Act of 1929 and the Migratory Bird Hunting Stamp Act of 1934, which respectively, provided the authority and funding for acquiring refuges. Today the Fish and Wildlife 

Service maintains a total of 439 refuges in the United States, the great majority of which have the protection of waterfowl as a prime goal. 42 The annual production of waterfowl is highly dependent upon weather conditions and, so, varies greatly from year to year and from region to region. Species that nest at high latitudes may be unable to breed in years of heavy snow cover, whereas those that nest in the prairies fare poorly in years of drought. Such normal environmental fluctuations are of little significance to long-lived and highly fecund birds, in which reverses suffered by the occasional loss of a breeding season can be easily made up. Thus, it is data on long term trends that are of greatest relevance to waterfowl biologists. For ducks those trends are not encouraging.

Ducks Data from 1955 through 1987 reveal an erratic but continual overall decline in duck populations, with the 1985 report recording the lowest breeding population since annual surveys started in 1955. The general pattern shown in Figure 2 obscures the fact that some species have remained fairly constant whereas others, including some of the most highly prized game species, have 12

Of special concern is the status of populations in the Prairie-Parkland region of the mid-continent, which is the principal production area for ducks. Although comprising only 10 percent of continental waterfowl breeding habitat, this area two thirds of which is in Canada produces more than 50 percent of the fall population. It is also a major agricultural area that has been undergoing dramatic alteration. By the early 1960s land use practices, including draining and filling of potholes and lakes, had begun to exert a significant influence on waterfowl numbers. More recently, farming has intensified on marginal lands bordering the remaining wetlands, reducing the cover available to nesting waterfowl and facilitating predation by foxes and other predators (Figure 3). Further, the quality of the remaining wetlands may be lessened by the use of agricultural chemicals on adjacent lands. In the Canadian sector of the prairies, nearly 70 percent of the original habitat had been altered by 1964 and "in parts of North Dakota, where greater than 85 percent of the landscape has been tilled, waterfowl 46 

13

Figure 3. Prairie pothole wetlands in North Dakota reduced by extensive agricultural use. 

Source: U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, Laurel, Md. 

are incapable of replenishing their numbers." These practices are largely responsible for the decline in early-nesting species such as Mallards and Pintails. Species such as Gadwalls that nest later in the year, when cover is more plentiful, and Green-winged Teal, whose main breeding areas are to the north of the prairies, have remained largely stable (Figure 4). 

Declining waterfowl numbers are not restricted to central North America. The Black Duck breeds exclusively in the northeastern United 

States and Canada. Traditionally it has comprised a large part of the hunting take in the Atlantic Flyway and up to 50 percent of the bag in New England. Since 1955, winter surveys on Chesapeake Bay (Figure 5) as well as data from the breeding grounds have indicated a population decline of 60 percent, for reasons whose relative importance is hard to assess. Overhunting and the loss of a high proportion of young females, as well as acidification of nesting areas and reduced viability of ducklings have been suggested, and may be involved. But man-induced habitat changes, introductions and evolutionary processes have probably played greater roles.46,47,48

The Black Duck is very closely related to - and hybridizes readily with - the Mallard; some consider it only a local population of the Mallard.49

Until the early 20th century, the eastern range of the marsh-nesting Blacks was generally isolated from the central range of the pond-nesting Mallards. 14

In contrast to ducks, goose populations are generally healthy over much of North America, mainly because their breeding grounds are mostly well north of areas of human impact. Indeed 25 of 27 local populations of six goose species nesting in Canada are either stable or increasing.2 In Eastern 

North America Canada Goose numbers have quadrupled over the past four decades, establishing resident populations where the species did not formerly nest and becoming a nuisance in some areas. 53,54 

For four geese (Cackling Canada, Pacific White-fronted, Black Brant, and Emperor) nesting in western Alaska, however, population trends have been discouraging. On the Yukon-Kuskokwim delta, "the greatest goosenesting concentration in the world"55 the total population has declined from slightly under one million in the mid-1960s to 313,000 in the mid 1980s, with 

Cackling Geese dropping by 94 percent and White-fronted by 78 percent.42,56 "Habitat loss in the lower 48 states and increased predation are contributing to these declines. But most observers agree the main factors. are overharvesting by sport and subsistence hunting" by native peoples. 

A comprehensive flyway-wide approach involving restrictions on hunting 16

seasons and bag limits, reductions in spring and summer take, and an educational program are being undertaken to remedy this problem, and the preliminary results are encouraging.

Management of the Aleutian Canada Goose, by contrast, stands as an impressive success story. Nearly exterminated by foxes introduced onto its nesting islands, the race rebounded from 800 in 1970 to over 4000 today. 

Removal of the foxes, captive breeding programs and reintroductions, and 55 closure of migrating and wintering areas to hunting were responsible. Swans

Populations of Tundra Swans are also healthy, and are either stable or increasing throughout the species' range. And the Trumpeter Swan, considered on the verge of extinction in the 1930's, now numbers approximately 10,000. Introduced Mute Swans, which are not hunted, have increased dramatically in the eastern United States in the past several decades and, like the Canada Goose, have become nuisances in some areas. 

To ensure the perpetuation of waterfowl populations, the governments of Canada and the United States have jointly undertaken a 15-year program The North America Waterfowl Management Plan whose major goals are to preserve high quality waterfowl habitat and to influence land use practices on extensive areas across the continent. Using waterfowl numbers from 

1970-1979 as a realistic baseline, the plan calls for the maintenance of a breeding population of 62 million ducks, which will produce an average fall flight of 100 million birds. Specific goals are set for the major species (Table

Other Waterbirds 

A general decline in the amount and extent of wetland habitats must invariably result in a parallel decline in waterbird species.58,59 For most species, data are poor or lacking, but information on two species - one lake dwelling and one marsh dwelling - seem representative. 

The Common Loon is a large and shy fish-eating bird that breeds on large lakes in the northern United States and Canada. Its breeding range has decreased in the past century, concordant with increasing human use of its nesting areas for commerce and recreation. Numbers have also been affected by shooting, fishtraps, and oiling on the coastal wintering grounds. Intense interest, catalyzed by the North American Loon Fund, has resulted in an active management program involving federal and state, but especially private groups and individuals. They survey lakes, collect data, provide artificial nesting platforms, and develop public awareness to encourage loon protection and minimize disturbance to nesting areas. The results have been encouraging, as the species has begun to regain some of its former range. In some areas it also shows signs of adapting to the proximity of humans." 

The Clapper Rails of the western United States have been greatly affected by the loss of marsh habitat, to the extent that all three western races are considered endangered. The Light-footed Clapper Rail, which inhabits coastal marshes of southern California, is confined to a few small marshes, where nesting habitat is mostly of low quality. The U.S. population dropped from 205 pairs in 1980 to 142 in 1985; several hundred more exist in Baja 

California, where the species is also highly localized.61 Another race, the 

California Clapper Rail inhabits the San Francisco Bay area, where marshlands have decreased by 95 percent since the turn of the century. The current population of 1000-1300 birds is affected by predation from introduced red foxes and rats. An inland race, the Yuma Clapper Rail, which lives along the marshes of the lower Colorado River, numbers about 1000 birds, with half in the U.S. and half in Mexico." 63 

Colonial Wading Birds 

Wading birds, including the herons, egrets, storks and ibises are a broadly distributed group of 16 species in the United States. Because they depend on wetlands for feeding, nesting, or both, they are most abundant along the Atlantic and Gulf coasts. Their status has been monitored in recent years by the FWS, which found a significant correlation between the area of estuarine wetlands and the state populations of many species.64,65,66 18

Wading birds were probably much commoner in the 1800s than they are today, but during the peak of the feather trade, in the 1880-1900 period, populations of many species, especially egrets, were nearly extirpated. 

Given federal protection in the early 1900s, they began recovering and by the 1970s ranges of all species had reached their former limits. Total numbers, however are still probably much less than in historic times.65,67 

Reasons for current population levels and past changes are numerous, but hinge upon changes in wetland quality and quantity. Dramatic changes have occurred in south Florida, where the creation of levees and canals, and the irrigation practices in and adjacent to the Everglades and Lake Okeechobee, have altered the feeding regimes of all wading birds. "With the combination of wetland feeding habitats already lost forever and the serious mismanagement . . . of the remaining wetlands, we are watching the total collapse of a [South] Florida wading bird population that once numbered in the hundreds of thousands." As a result of these changes the Wood Stork is now endangered and the White Ibis persists in much-reduced numbers. 

Similar but less dramatic wetland changes have occurred in many other regions; nationwide, perhaps 16 percent of the nation's coastal wetlands have been lost since 1922. California has had the greatest loss rate (about 

3 percent per year, 1954-1974) and its wading bird populations are a fraction of the pre-1900 totals. ,,68 68 Not all man-created change has been detrimental. In maintaining the Atlantic, Gulf, and Great Lakes coastal waterways, the Army Corps of Engineers has created numerous islands from dredge deposition material. 

These have provided nesting colony sites for many waterbirds, and have been especially attractive to wading birds in Texas, Florida, North Carolina, New Jersey, and New York, where barrier islands have been heavily developed or disturbed by recreation." Without these "refuges", wading bird populations would be much smaller than they are. 69 70 Irrigation practices in the West and broadscale use of pesticides in rice, soybean, sorghum, and alfalfa crops have resulted in local poisoning of wading birds and shorebirds. The population impacts of such practices are difficult to assess. For endemic species, or highly localized populations, such as White-faced Ibis in Utah or Nevada, they could be great.

Overall, most colonial wading bird populations have remained relatively stable in recent years, with the exception of Wood Storks and Cattle Egrets (Table 6). Except in Florida, the major wetland losses do not appear to have caused substantial declines; in fact, many species have expanded or shifted their ranges in the past two decades.

Possibly declining because of habitat loss. Insufficient data. 72 By the early 1900s the distribution of such species as the Bald Eagle had already been greatly influenced by human activities and development. 

Beginning after the turn of the century and continuing through the 1950s, some raptors (e.g., Sharp-shinned and Cooper's hawks) were shot by the tens of thousands at such sites as Hawk Mountain, Pennsylvania and Cape May, New Jersey. Later, in the early 1950s through the mid 1970s, populations of several species declined dramatically due to reproductive failure induced by consumption of prey contaminated by DDT and other persistent pesticides. 73,74

General use of DDT was banned in 1972, and populations of species affected by its use are now increasing as a result of improved natural reproduction, augmented in some regions by man's efforts. Also, in 1972, raptors were given full protection under the Migratory Bird Treaty Act, making it against federal law to shoot them.75 

Overall, the health of most North American raptors at the species level appears good, although numbers of some species remain below "historical" levels. Of more than 50 hawk, eagle, falcon, vulture, osprey, harrier, kite,

and owl species that breed (or have recently bred) in the United States, six 76,77 (wholly or in part) are listed as endangered. One of these, the California Condor, is critically endangered. 

It is extremely difficult to monitor raptor populations. Most species are widely distributed and nest at low densities. Other species are nocturnal or nest in remote regions or in dense forests. Long-term data are well represented for a few species from the Breeding Bird Surveys or the Christmas Bird Counts. Some species, such as the Bald Eagle, are subjects of individual surveys. 8,78,79

In migration large numbers of raptors may be observed along mountain ridges and the perimeters of large bodies of water. Counts at these sites represent the best opportunities to gather data for a number of species. For example, 16 species are regularly recorded in fall migration at Hawk Mountain Sanctuary in the Appalachian Mountains of southeast Pennsylvania and the Cedar Grove Ornithological Station on the western shore of Lake Michigan in Wisconsin; counts at these locations began in 1934 and 

1936, respectively. 15,80,81

Because hawkwatching is now a popular form of recreational birdwatching (the Hawk Migration Association of North America gets regular annual reports from about 75 North American sites), there is great potential for using a volunteer cadre of observers to gather data. At present, however, statistically-useful data are produced at only a handful of sites, including six sites for which the FWS has provided partial support for annual counts over the last decade.

There are many problems in interpreting the data from migrating birds. 

The origins and destinations of migrants at any given point of concentration may not be known or may represent more than one region. This makes it difficult to relate changes in numbers of migrants to changes in the status of particular populations. Year-to-year variations in weather influence count results, due to changes in the detectability of migrants or their timing and selection of routes (Figure 6). Nonetheless, data sets from migration counts reveal some clear trends. • By the early 1970s, nesting populations of Bald Eagles had declined to their lowest known levels throughout most of the United States, but by the late 1970s natural productivity augmented by state-sponsored "reintroduction" programs (e.g., in 

New York) was increasing and so were numbers of eagles. 

This pattern is reflected in fall counts of migrants recorded at 

Hawk Mountain Sanctuary (Figure 7). 

• A similar success story may be described for the Peregrine Falcon, whose population decreased rapidly follwing the widespread use of DDT beginning the the late 1940s. By the mid-1960s there were no known peregrines nesting east of the Mississippi River, and numbers in the West were declining. Natural productivity in 21

Cade of Cornell University to augment wild populations via captive breeding. The reintroduction/release program started in the eastern United States and has continued in the Rocky Mountains and Pacific Coast states. In 1987 the Peregrine Fund returned the 2000th peregrine to the wild, and some nesting pairs are now found using ledges on buildings in major urban centers from Baltimore to Los Angeles. The New Jersey population is now at an all time high. • The Merlin and the Cooper's Hawk appear to have reversed earlier declines. Numbers of Coopers's Hawks, however, are still low compared to apparent populations in the 1930s. 

While the general picture is encouraging, there are reasons for concern for some species and within some regions. The Northern Harrier, for example, nests in or near wetlands, which have been greatly reduced and the species is threatened or endangered in many states. Although this decline is 22

Game Birds 

Game birds are hunted. There are 28 resident (non-migratory) species of the order Galliformes (partridges, grouse, turkeys, and quail) found in the

United States, and responsibility for their management lies mainly with individual states. Data on long-term trends are rarely available. Most species seem to be stable, and successful management and reintroduction programs for the Wild Turkey and Ruffed Grouse have resulted in significant increases and restoration to parts of their historic ranges. There are indications that Sharp-tailed Grouse and Sage Grouse are declining in the 23

northern plains and sagebrush areas, and a Gulf coastal plains species, Atwater's Prairie Chicken, has long been ranked as endangered. Loss of habitat to agriculture and overhunting are regarded as the major causes. 15 Woodcock and Mourning Doves are migratory and their status is monitored by the FWS, often in collaboration with the Canadian Wildlife Service and many hunters. For Woodcock, a spring survey is used to index the number of birds on the breeding grounds and a fall survey to determine recruitment into the population and the number of birds harvested. 

Although data on Woodcock have been collected for over two decades, they are difficult to interpret. The population east of the Appalachians has shown a long term decrease both in breeding birds, averaging 2.4 percent per year, and in the number taken by hunters; yet, recruitment rates seem to have remained stable. The central population, by contrast, has undergone a slight increase, but both recruitment and hunting success have dropped. The drop in the eastern population is evidently the result of changing land use practices, including the abandonment of farms and the loss of field habitats to succession. The drop in the central population is not easily explained, and the low recruitment is worrisome." 89 The Mourning Dove is one of the most abundant and widespread species in the United States, with fall populations estimated at 350 to 600 million individuals. Breeding Bird Surveys from 1965-1978 indicated that the species had increased significantly, especially in the northern part of the range, and appeared to be expanding northward; these trends have continued, perhaps abetted by changing agricultural practices in which large amounts of waste corn are left in the fields. Yet, another FWS study using very similar techniques reported that from 1966-1987 the dove had undergone a consistent, long-term decline, especially in the far West. 

These studies illustrate the difficulty obtaining and interpreting data, even on common game species that are of major interest to the public. Shorebirds

Forty-nine species of shorebirds (plovers, sandpipers, snipe, and allies) breed in North America. These were once hunted so extensively that populations of many species were greatly depleted or virtually eliminated. 

With the closure of hunting for all but the American Woodcock and Common Snipe by 1927, the majority have made substantial recoveries, crowned by several recent observations of the Eskimo Curlew, a species long feared to be extinct.

The FWS paid little attention to shorebirds until the late 1960s to early 1980s, when funds for nongame research allowed a few studies on the breeding ecology of some key species and on the foraging patterns and habitat requirements of migrants at staging areas. 

Because shorebirds breed over vast areas, with many species being restricted to remote arctic or subarctic areas, direct efforts to gather data on 24

populations are often pointless, making mandatory the use of indices. From 

1972-1983, Manomet Bird Observatory, Massachusetts, attempted to gather comprehensive data on shorebirds at all of the major stopping areas on the Atlantic coast. This led to a cooperative venture with the FWS to analyze trends for those 12 species for which the Atlantic coast intercepts most or all of the regional population. Significant declines were found for the Shortbilled Dowitcher and Sanderling, and possibly the Whimbrel. No trends were evident among the other species studied (Black-bellied Plover, Semipalmated Plover, Greater Yellowlegs, Lesser Yellowlegs, Willet, Ruddy 

Turnstone, Red Knot, Semipalmated Sandpiper, and Least Sandpiper).

Because of the great variability in the data and other factors confounding trend analysis, these preliminary results need to be expanded and tested by more detailed studies." 92 93 While trend data for most of species are lacking, those that nest on sandy beaches, such as Piping and Snowy Plovers, are particularly vulnerable, and Piping Plovers are locally endangered or threatened. Birds that breed in uncultivated grasslands including Long-billed Curlew, Upland Sandpiper, and Mountain Plover have also had reductions in their breeding range and are rarer than in the recent past. 101 Dependence on coastal marshes and other wetlands for a large part of the year makes shorebirds among the most vulnerable of all birds to environmental change. Many migratory species utilize traditional stopover sites annually, where individuals may spend several weeks accumulating fat reserves essential for refueling extensive non-stop flights to breeding or wintering sites.¹ At least 50 such sites are currently recognized in North 

America (Figure 8) and their loss or degradation could have important ramifications. The highest priority for nongame research by the FWS is to renew shorebird studies to include evaluating the importance of these and other potential migratory stopover points in the United States, which will allow improvements in analyzing population trends. A major international effort to establish a shorebird reserve system throughout the western hemisphere is also in progress. Songbirds

Approximately half of the North American avifauna consists of small landbirds such as warblers, thrushes, and finches. Prior to the instigation of the Breeding Bird Survey (BBS) in 1965 there was little chance of obtaining data on their population trends, either individually or collectively, on more than a local basis. The BBS uses an extensive network of observers throughout the continental U.S. and Canada to conduct a roadside survey along predetermined routes once each year at the height of the breeding season. The resulting data can be used on up to a continental scale to detect (1) short-term population changes that can be correlated with specific weather incidents, (2) recovery periods following catastrophic declines, (3) normal year-to-year variation, and (4) long-term population 25

Figure 8. Important stopover sites for migrating shorebirds in North 

America. [Sites shown are those believed to support 20,000 or more shorebirds of all species combined during the peak of migration.] 2 

Source: Senner, S.E., and M.A. Howe. 1984. Conservation of neartic shorebirds. Pp. 379-421.

In J. Burger and B.L. Olla (eds.), Shorebirds: Breeding behavior and populations, Plenum 

Publ. Co., N.Y. trends. One can also (5) map the relative abundance of each species, either year by year to show changes in distribution or relative abundance, or the average over a period of years, (6) determine trends across major physiographic or geographic regions, (7) study correlations between bird populations and changes in land-use changes, (8) make recommendations for mitigating undesirable bird populations trends, and (9) plan studies to analyze increases or decreases. 26

Analysis of trends indicated by the BBS and other local studies is complicated by inevitable changes within the study areas resulting from plant succession. Also, patterns of increase or decrease vary from species to species and from site to site. Finally, comparative data from which to confirm suspected trends are few. Nevertheless, the BBS and studies in the eastern 

United States in the past decade have reported a widespread and in some cases severe decline in woodland songbirds. For example, from 1968 to 1984 the number of breeding birds in a New Hampshire forest dropped from 214 to 80 adults per 10 hectares and the total number of species showed a persistent decline (Figure 9), with most of the losses occuring after 1977. Other studies show no decline." 94,95,96,97,98,99,104

Although decreases have been attributed to several factors, fragmentation of the forests seems to have played a major role.94,102,103,104,105,106 

FWS biologists have demonstrated that the size of a woodlot is important in determining whether or not a species will be present (Table 7). The species most sensitive to size effects were, almost without exception, long-distance migrants that winter in the Neotropics. The Wood Thrush, for example, occurred mainly in wood lots of 20 hectares or greater, while the Scarlet Tanager preferred those of 100 hectares or more; the probability of en-

Forest species are not the only landbirds whose populations are changing. The BBS revealed declines in a broad spectrum of field birds, (including Upland Sandpiper, Field Sparrow, Henslow's Sparrow), which apparently stem from habitat loss as farms and pastures revert to woodlots.

And in many western states, especially California, populations of birds that prefer riparian vegetation have declined to near extinction because of the near elimination of that habitat (Bell's Vireo, Yellow Warbler, Yellow-billed 

Cuckoo).100 Even so, the majority have yet to be considered for listing under provisions of the Endangered Species Act.

It has been widely suggested that the decline of North American birds that winter in the Neotropics is associated with tropical deforestation. 95,98,102

Although several species of Neotropical migrants that had been stable through 1979 have been decreasing recently, others showed the opposite trend, (Table 10) and the changes did not appear to be associated with any particular wintering areas. 107 99 Furthermore, no decreases in Neotropical migrants were detected in the 

Great Smoky Mountains (NC, TN) between 1947-48 and 1982-83. And in

New Hampshire "species wintering near their breeding grounds were more affected by winter events than those wintering in the tropics." ,,97 The cause of changes in these landbird populations is unknown and probably involves many factors. How one perceives these changes is partly a matter of perspective. When viewed over the past several decades, the recent decline of field-nesting species, for example, seems lamentable. Yet, considerations of habitat availability suggest that their current abundance in the eastern United States may not be much different now than it was in colonial times. Only long term data on bird populations and habitat availability can reveal such trends and allow them to be interpreted accurately.

Island Birds 

Ninety-three percent of all species and subspecies of birds that have become extinct since 1600 were once found on islands.108 Their high vulnerability is illustrated by bird life on the Hawaiian Islands and on Guam, where rapid and immense changes are in progress. Hawaii

The Hawaiian Islands have the highest percentage of endemic flora and fauna of any area in the world. They have also have had the greatest number of historic extinctions of birds. The present fauna includes only a fraction of the species that once occurred. Prior to the arrival of the Polynesians in 500 

A.D. there were more than 80 species of endemic birds in Hawaii. The 

Polynesians burned forests and brought along rats, pigs, and dogs that preyed on the native birds, some of which were flightless. This reduced the number of species by nearly one half. The arrival of European man continued the high rate of extinctions: two species of rails, three thrushes, four honey eaters, and nine honeycreepers having been exterminated since 1778.

Today only 37 species of native land birds live on the islands, 24 of these, or 65 percent, are endangered and all but three live nowhere else in the world. ,109,110

Also endangered are two of the 22 seabird species that nest locally 

(Table 11). 

Studies by federal, state, and university researchers have provided an exceptional record of the current status of the birdlife; the picture is bleak. 

Pressures on these populations - deforestation by logging and for development, disturbance by livestock and feral ungulates, avian diseases of which many are transmitted by introduced mosquitos, predation by introduced mammals, and the introduction of exotic birds and plants — are all continuing. Indeed, six species contain fewer than fifty individuals and are in imminent danger of extinction (Table 12). 

The Kauai Oo may already be gone; it was the last surviving species of a family that used to be represented by five species, and was perhaps the dominant group of canopy dwelling birds on the islands. Only trivial numbers of the two thrushes persist. The diverse subfamily of honeycreepers has suffered heavily since man arrived. Of 47 known species, only 22 remain, and 12 are endangered. Indeed, that number may be only 20 as the Akialoa and the Molokai Creeper are "perhaps already extinct." The loss of these species is especially tragic since many consider the honeycreepers to surpass the Darwin's finches of the Galapagos Islands as showcases of evolution. 112 Hawaiian Crow numbers have also plummeted, from hundreds in the 1960s to fewer than five wild birds today. Nine captives form the core of a restoration project. This is the only endangered Hawaiian perching bird being bred for reintroduction.

There have been previous reintroductions, however, and some management success. The Hawaiian Duck has been released on several islands and 32

2 19 24 <10 extinct is doing well on Kauai, where there are no mongooses. The Hawaiian 

Goose, or Nene, the last of eight species of geese that once inhabited Hawaii, numbered fewer than 50 in 1945. The population on Maui and 

Kauai, supplemented by captive breeding, reached nearly 1000 in the 1970s. 

Unfortunately, wild birds are not replacing themselves, and their persistence depends on intensive management. The Laysan Duck population 35

climbed from as few as 20 in the 1920s to hundreds by the 1970s; its future seems secure if exotic plants and predators are kept off Laysan Island, its only nesting area. Two species of seabirds, the Dark-rumped Petrel and

Newell's Shearwater, have also been helped by management efforts, which include trapping and poisoning mongooses around nesting colonies. 111 A few endemic species seem to be adapting to changing environmental conditions. The Hawaiian Hawk feeds on introduced animals, and the 

Elepaio, Apapane, and Amakihi are reinvading the low elevations where foreign plants and diseases are common. But, these are exceptions. Guam On Guam, the birdlife has suffered a truly catastrophic and irreversible decline, owing to intense predation by the Brown Tree Snake (Boiga irregularis). The snake was probably introduced via military cargo in the late 

1940s or early 1950s. Because of its nocturnal habits and ability to feed both in terrestrial and arboreal situations, it was able to thrive and by the early 1980s had spread over the entire island. It now occurs in tremendous abundance. 113 

Before the snake appeared, the avifauna of Guam consisted of 18 species, 12 of which were landbirds. The forest dwelling species declined in a geographic pattern that parallels the snake's spread. The first species to go were small and presumably the easiest to catch. By January 1986, seven species or subspecies had become extinct (Table 13), and populations of four others, reduced to fewer than 100 individuals, were on the verge of extinction. 114 

To forestall extinction, 29 Micronesian Kingfishers and 21 Guam Rails were captured for propagation in 1983-84. Captive kingfishers now number 

47 and rails 107, and although reintroduction on Guam will not be feasible until the snakes are controlled, plans are being made to liberate rails on nearby Rota Island in 1988. A similar kingfisher already exists on Rota, so the Guamanian race cannot be released and must be maintained in captivity for the forseeable future.

Restoration and Management 

The risk to island birds from introduced animals is well known; Hawaii and 

Guam provide textbook cases. On Hawaii, mongooses, feral cats, and dogs need to be eliminated or confined as do free-roaming hoofed animals of several types. This has been recognized in two recent court decisions, which held that feral sheep and goats as well as mouflon sheep must be removed from the critical habitat of the endangered Palila on the slopes of Mauna Kea. 161 The possibility of additional introductions of exotic species remains an ever-present worry. On Guam the problem of snake control is being studied intensively by the FWS, but no solution is imminent. The snake is adept at 36

Derricksen, pers. comm. stowing away and continues to spread, having been seen on other islands including Wake, Diego Garcia, Kwajelein, Pohnpei and Hawaii. The number of recent reports of it and other snakes (including venomous species) transported in military cargo is sobering. 113 One reason for the success of this snake on Guam is the abundance of alternative prey in the form of lizards. Whether conditions would permit it to thrive elsewhere is not known, but this risk is too great to be accepted. The prospect of exotic snakes being established anywhere, but especially on islands where there are endemic populations of landbirds, some of which are already endangered, and major seabird colonies, illustrates the critical need for unsparing methods to: (1) eliminate exotic animals from all island ecoysystems and (2) prevent their further dispersal into uncomtaminated areas.

The survival of most of the endemic Hawaiian birds requires saving and linking up large contiguous areas of undisturbed habitat that includes the 37

ranges of threatened species. This is crucial in mountainous areas above the elevation limit of introduced tropical mosquitoes and diseases. Captive breeding for some species may be feasible. Even so, it will be for naught if habitat destruction continues.

With long term efforts, degraded ecoysystems in mountain forests and coastal wetlands can be restored and the current downward spiral reversed. 

Furthermore, by focussing efforts on restoration of native communities "many of today's endangered species can possibly be elevated to at least threatened status. In doing so we will also preserve large numbers of plants and invertebrates.»>112

In Hawaii, the United States has a unique opportunity to take direct action to preserve and restore its own tropical forests. 

Doing so would demonstrate our commitment to the world-wide preservation of tropical forests and genetic diversity. 

Endangered Species

The Endangered Species Act (ESA) was enacted in 1973 to protect species that were in danger of extinction throughout a significant portion of their range (termed "Endangered") and those likely to become so in the forseeable future ("Threatened"). Not only does this act prevent actions that might be harmful, but it allows the government to take positive action, such as acquiring habitat or engaging in captive-breeding programs, to insure the survival of these species.

The FWS, the principal agency for implementing the ESA, uses two basic approaches to investigate problems. First, biologists at field locations study an endangered species in its native habitat to learn more about factors contributing to its decline. Second, research on captive animals is used to understand their physiological and behavioral characteristics, information that may be essential in restoration programs. 

Captive breeding and reintroductions are often proposed as methods for ensuring the recovery of a species. Yet, techniques are perfected for only a handful of species, and through the late 1970s it was controversial if any endangered or threatened bird “had been restored to a self-sustaining wild population as the result of releasing captive birds." A strong case can now be made for the Peregrine Falcon, whose recovery has been largely supported by the Peregrine Fund, and a few other species show promise of suc- 118,119 cess. ■The Masked Bobwhite had become extirpated in the U.S. because of the loss of habitat. After the establishment in 1985 of the Buenos Aires National 

Wildlife Refuge, Arizona, 2000 chicks were released in 1986 and 1987 from stock reared at the Patuxent Wildlife Research Center, Maryland. Their survival has been good and it appears that reintroduced birds have begun to breed. 38

The Dusky Seaside Sparrow: a case history 

The Dusky Seaside Sparrow illustrates some of the difficulties involved in attempting to save a species. The last Dusky died in June 1987. Its passing was especially notable for three reasons: (1) it represented the first known extinction of a North American bird that had been protected under the Endangered Species Act; (2) its breeding habitat was on refuges managed by the FWS; and (3) persistent efforts made to save the species, both in the wild and in captivity, were fruitless. 

This sparrow inhabited marshlands within 10 miles of the coast along the St. Johns River of Brevard County, Florida, and at the north end of Merritt 

Island. Once locally abundant, its numbers plummeted by the early 1950s, evidently in response to a decade of spraying DDT for mosquito control. 

Even so, Merritt Island still held 600 of its original 2000 pairs as late as 1957. 

Beginning in 1956, however, marshes in the area were impounded to control mosquitos and to create habitat for wintering waterbirds. This inundated and eliminated the vegetation on which the Duskies relied, so that by 1966 the sparrow was threatened with extinction. After a survey in 1968 revealed only 800 singing males remaining along the St. Johns River and 34 at Merritt island, the Department of the Interior established the St. Johns 

National Wildlife Refuge by purchasing 4000 acres of prime habitat and devised a plan to restore Dusky habitat on Merritt Island. Nevertheless, by 

1976 the bird was gone from Merritt Island.3,115 It was still hoped that birds in the St. Johns marshes could be reintroduced to Merritt Island, but much of the St. John marshes remained in private hands and disappeared under houses and a major freeway, or was drained. Then in the winters of 1973-76, a series of wildfires burned much of the refuge, sparing little habitat for the few remaining birds. 116 The last female disappeared in 1976 and in 1980 only four males survived in the wild. Three were captured for breeding, in the hope that backcrossing them with females from another race of Seaside Sparrow might preserve many Dusky genes, and a successful cross in 1980 produced four hybird young. But the breeding program was then stalled because the FWS refused to issue permits, even though the study was (1) experimental, (2) a last ditch effort, and (3) not supported by federal funds. When permits were finally issued in 1983, the birds were transported to Walt Disney World's Discovery Island Zoological Park. By then the males were 6-10 years old and the line could not be sustained.117 39

The Bald Eagle has been the object of reintroduction efforts in several states. Young have been "translocated" from populations in Alaska and 

Canada to locations in the Northeastern U.S. Captive-reared young are also used in recovery efforts for bald eagles. In 1987, 11 pairs held at Patuxent produced 42 eggs; 16 chicks were hatched, 15 of which survived. One eagle hatched in captivity in 1979 and released in Georgia nested in 1987. ■ The Mississippi Sandhill Crane is extremely rare. Chicks reared at 

Patuxent in 1985 and 1986 now make up approximately 40 percent of the wild flock of 45 birds at the Mississippi Sandhill Crane National Wildlife 

Refuge, Mississippi. ■Success with breeding the Guam Rail, as noted above, will result in its early reintroduction on Rota Island, where a wild population can be established.

Puerto Rican Parrots declined from an estimated population of over 100,000 in the 1800s to a low of 12 in 1971, largely because of the loss of more than 80 percent of the forests (Figure 11), and the spread of the Pearly-eyed Thrasher, which competes for nesting cavities with the parrot. The 

FWS, U.S. Forest Service, and Puerto Rico Department of Natural Resour ces established a captive population in the Caribbean National Forest and began to develop propagation techniques. These efforts have not met expectations, although captives did produce fertile eggs for the first time in 1987. 

Currently there are 39 birds in captivity and an estimated 59 in the wild. 

Chicks from eggs produced in the wild are reared in captivity and released. 

Radiotelemetry is used to monitor their movements, survival, and integration into the wild population. 120,121,122 Recognizing that breeding success in the wild can be as high as 70 percent, so long as the birds can be protected against predators, the FWS and National Audubon Society initiated a nestwatcher program in 1987. Staffed entirely by volunteers, it provided 90 percent coverage of the four known nests, which fledged six young. ■In April 1987, the last free-flying California Condor was trapped and placed in a zoo, joining 27 other captives. This controversial action was taken after it was decided that risks to the wild population were so great that extinction was imminent. A consortium of agencies involving the San Diego 

Zoo, Los Angeles Zoo, FWS, and California Department of Fish and Game is supporting a program of captive propagation, which had earlier been developed and tested successfully on Andean Condors. It is hoped that it will result in the reintroduction of California Condors into the wild in 19901992. To date, one chick has been produced. The Condor Recovery Plan is the most innovative and expensive - of any yet proposed. It relies on teaching young birds new foraging traditions, so that they will not encounter the environmental hazards faced by their ancestors. 123,124 Even if the program is successful, the condors will require intense and continual

Report. management for decades, and whether a truly "wild" population will ever be realized is debatable.

A general conclusion is that captive propagation is a feasible method for saving a few types of endangered birds. It is, however, very expensive and its cost-effectiveness relative to habitat preservation, except in the most extreme cases, is questionable. 124,125

Two other species, one endangered and one proposed for listing, are of current interest, because they illustrate some problems of managing wildlife on public lands. These are the Red-cockaded Woodpecker and Spotted 

Owl, both of which prefer old-growth forests, which are highly valued for their lumber. 126

The Red-cockaded Woodpecker lives in small colonies in the mature, open, longleaf pine woodlands of the southeastern United States. This habitat, maintained in the past only by recurring fires, has become very scarce because of fire control and lumbering. The total population probably does not exceed 10,000 birds. Eighty-four percent of all known colonies are on 41

federal lands, the majority in national forests. Although the species has been considered endangered since 1970, it has continued to decrease. The seriousness of the decline is illustrated by data from three national forests in east Texas, where the number of active colonies has decreased by an average of 9-10 percent each year since 1983. In 1987 only 55 of 258 colonies were active, and only 47 percent of those produced young. If this trend continues the species will be locally extinct by the mid 1990s. 127,128,129 

The Spotted Owl is a reclusive species, which in the northern part of its range in the westernmost United States is confined to old-growth Douglas fir forests. Its strong association and probable dependence on this habitat is illustrated by the fact that 93 percent of the 1500 known sites in Oregon are in forests older than 100 years. Nearly all such habitat occurs in national forests.

Independent scientific committees reviewing recovery or management 127,130 plans for both species have judged them inadequate. Indeed, a panel of experts reporting to The American Ornithologists' Union and Cooper Ornithological Society found that the plan for the owl would have led to a decline of perhaps 50 percent. “Such a conscious decision for population reduction to a critical level in a non-pest species must surely be unique in the history of wildlife management.' ,,130 .,,165

As the habitat available to the woodpecker and owl shrinks, an increasing percentage of their populations will be confined to public lands, and responsibility for their perpetuation will lie mainly with the Forest Service. 

Foresters are mandated by the National Forest Management Act and the Multiple Use and Sustained Yield Act to manage national forests for "multiple uses;" a traditional goal has been to maximize timber yield; another is to maintain viable populations of native vertebrates. Yet, they must also follow the mandate of the Endangered Species Act, which not only "prohibits activities that significantly modify or degrade a habitat . . .", but also "prohibits a balancing of interest for multiple use considerations." 

Adherence to these conflicting laws poses a problem to forest managers and, so, to the forest inhabitants.1 132 

Pest Species

Just as there are species that cannot adapt to the proximity of man or to changes in the environment, there are others that thrive in "disturbed" situations, sometimes to the extent that they may be considered pests. Undesirability may take many forms: depradations on agricultural products (waterfowl, blackbirds, crows) or at fish hatcheries (herons, kingfishers, gulls); threats to human health, owing to the accumulation of fecal material at large roosts (pigeons, blackbirds, starlings); hazards to aircraft (gulls, blackbirds); or competition with and exclusion of other species. Impacts can be great; blackbirds and starlings cause annual losses to agriculture exceed- 42

ing $million. Population growth can also be rapid. Ring-billed Gulls in the Great Lakes increased at an average of 11 percent annually between 1976 and 1984; similar increases were realized by several other gull species. 29,133

As the conditions that allow species to attain nuisance status are mainly associated with human activities (e.g., expansion of agriculture, cutting of forests, availability of human refuse), further increases are inevitable. Because nearly all birds are protected, federal and state permits may be required before control actions can be initiated. (Information on controlling pest species may be obtained through the FWS and U.S. Department of Agriculture.)

Status and Trends: A Summary 

In summary, despite ongoing and sometimes severe environmental changes, the status of birdlife in North America seems generally good. This is in part due to long dedication in the United States to the establishment of national preserves and wildlife management agencies, and to a conservation ethic that acknowledges the need to preserve biodiversity. 

How can the health of the avifauna be measured objectively? One index might be the number of species requiring special protection under the Endangered Species Act. By this standard, the birdlife of the continental

United States is in good shape: only 33 species or subspecies are listed (Table 11). [Two more species, Black-capped Vireo and Roseate Tern, were added in late 1987.] This is less than 0.5 percent of the total number of species occuring regularly in the United States. The same standard applied to island birds provides quite a different impression: 45 taxa, a very high proportion, are rare or threatened with extinction. Listing is a slow process, however, and both sets of lists could be greatly expanded by forms whose inclusion is biologically justified but for which formal action is pending or postponed. Two species listed as endangered (California Condor, Dusky 

Seaside Sparrow) are already extinct in the wild, two more probably so (Ivory-billed Woodpecker, Bachman's Warbler), and one (Texas Henslow's

Sparrow) died out while action was being contemplated; their fate was mostly sealed before the ESA was enacted.

Assessing trends is more difficult. One potential index might be the number of specially protected birds whose status has improved from endangered to threatened or have been delisted because of programs developed under the ESA. This standard cannot now be applied fairly, because of the time needed to write and instigate a Recovery Plan after a species is determined to be in trouble. (Plans have not been approved for all listed forms.) This is especially true for large animals with low reproductive rates, the ones that are most likely to become endangered. But even after making such allowances the score is poor, and much of the improvement that has been realized came independent of the ESA policies. We can conclude that the ESA has 43

been very effective in calling attention to wildlife problems, but its value as a management tool is far from resolved.134 

Because a major goal of the FWS is to prevent any migratory bird species from becoming threatened, it must "identify. those species. . . that demonstrate unstable or decreasing population trends in any significant portion of their range, together with reasons for the trends."135 In 1987, 407 species were nominated for inclusion, or 53 percent of the 774 species of nongame birds covered by the Migratory Bird Treaty Act! As the standards for nomination are highly subjective, it is questionable how many species are truly declining. The FWS considered only 30 to be of potential management concern on a national basis (Table 14); 7 are marsh or wading birds, 5 are birds of prey, 6 are marine or shore birds, and 13 are perching birds.
