Future Directions A Centralized Data File 

Efforts to monitor trends in the birdlife of the United States are good or excellent for some groups (e.g., waterfowl) and have recently improved greatly for a few others (e.g., colonial waterbirds). Migratory nongame birds, on the other hand, get almost no attention unless they reach the Endangered Species List.136 Data for most species, if available, are qualitative and cannot be analyzed efficiently because they are widely scattered and lack a central repository. At present, no agency, public or private, has the capability of compiling a comprehensive annual report on the "State of the 

Union on Wildlife.”

The desirability of a central data base was noted in a report to the Council on Environmental Quality. It stated that "The U.S. Government is the principal source of information on natural resources and environmental quality", but that users considered government forecasts neither credible nor timely. It recommended collecting more and better environmental data as well as the "creation of an index of governmental natural resources and environmental quality information...

This was echoed by the Council on Environmental Quality, itself: 

In spite of significant efforts expended on the collection of environmental data by a variety of unrelated and uncoordinated state and Federal agencies, no adequate system exists for the centralized collection, storage, maintenance and quality control of such data. 

A logical first step in determining whether a centralized file for biological data is feasible would be to coordinate information on one group of birds for which there are reasonable baseline data. This could be done by the Fish and Wildlife Service or, for seabirds, by the National Marine Fisheries Service, but could also be undertaken by a scientific society, conservation organization, or university.

Determining and Analyzing Population Trends 

If a data bank can be established, detecting trends will be greatly facilitated. 

At present, long-term studies of animal populations are too few to illustrate natural variations or to separate natural cycles from man-induced changes. 45

If bird populations are to be monitored as part of an overall conservation plan, regular censuses will need to be (1) established, (2) designed to produce statistically valid data, (3) encompass as many species or groups of species as possible, with emphasis on nongame species, and (4) carried out for long periods. A baseline of 10 years is a minimum term and even this is likely to be inadequate (10-year cycles are well known in Ruffed 

Grouse). 138,139

The selection of techniques is a critical step. Because the results of broad-based studies such as the BBS do not always correspond with those 8,159 derived from detailed studies in particular regions or even with other broad-based studies, as noted for the Mourning Dove - one must determine which programs will provide the most useful and reliable results. 

Where should such baseline studies be accomplished? Because the purpose is to detect changes in large populations, large study areas are required. While there are various public lands on which research can be conducted (national parks, national wildlife refuges, national forests, state and local parks) these are often managed for purposes that alter the environment and thereby diminish a site's value as a reference point. What is required are blocks of continuous habitat that are unlikely to be disturbed, except by natural events such as fire or plant succession, for the forseeable future. 140 

Two major networks of land holdings in the United States include longterm baseline ecological surveys among their responsibilities and maintain representative terrestrial or aquatic habitats. These are the Long-Term 

Ecological Research (LTER) sites of the National Science Foundation and the National Environmental Research Parks (NERP) of the U.S. Depart ment of Energy (Table 15). The 15 LTER sites, which range from 1000 to 

44,000 ha in extent, represent major ecological areas, and exist almost wholly for research. The NERP sites average larger (range 125,000 to 230,500 ha) but are less diverse and are not maintained entirely in a natural state, although efforts are made to set aside natural habitats. Both are valuable, but at neither are full baseline data gathered routinely. Some major habitat types (deserts, saline lakes) are not yet represented in either program. It is also important to encourage the development of long-term studies on other large reserves owned or operated by the government (e.g., Department of 

Defense), corporations, major universities, or private conservation organizations. 97,142

Not all monitoring efforts require access to large and undisturbed areas. 

Staging areas for migratory shorebirds and waterfowl may be quite localized. Yet, they are of great significance because a large percentage of a population may occur there each year, permitting rapid detection of trends or breeding success. 141 To obtain baseline data requires considerable effort, some of which can be provided by amateur enthusiasts, who have already been instrumental in the success of the Breeding Bird Survey and the more than 40 state breeding 46

bird atlases currently in progress. These projects provide important data and will become standard references for studies of distributional chan- 143,144 ges. But even with extensive volunteer help, there is neither time, manpower, or resources sufficient to gather information on all species or topics of interest. Therefore, monitoring programs must be selective and designed to address specific topics, such as the importance of forest fragmentation in breeding vs. wintering areas on the population size of songbirds, or whether population trends are widespread or are perhaps limited to specific habitats.

Another series of questions involves matters of immediate concern. The 

Chernobyl reactor accident in the Soviet Union in April 1986 illustrated that radionuclides from industrial accidents can have widespread effects on wildlife, which sometimes accumulate higher levels of contaminants than domestic stock living in the same region.145,146,147 The NERP network provides opportunities to study these effects. Another issue is the degree to which acid precipitation may have affected bird populations. Relevant data from North America are so scanty that the subject was not discussed in the 

National Acid Precipitation Assessment Program Interim Assessment, even though deleterious effects on a variety of water-associated birds have been alleged in Europe. Recent Canadian research, however, suggests that waterfowl production was reduced in areas of high acid deposition, and that fish-eating birds including the Common Loon and Common Merganser were "immediately at risk... throughout much of northeastern Ontario." 148 Other types of monitoring do not require access to any specific habitats.

The National Contaminant Biomonitoring Program of the FWS monitors long-lived, stable, environmental pollutants, such as organochlorines, that accumulate in biological tissue. Every third year since the late 1960s Starlings have been collected by FWS personnel throughout the lower 48 states; 

Mallard and Black Duck wings are submitted by hunters. Recently, levels of organochlorides have declined both on a national (Starling) and a flyway (duck wing) basis (Figure 12) and are gratifyingly lower than those associated with adverse biological effects. 

The FWS is also starting to monitor short-term and persistent pollutants in estuarine habitats that hold major concentrations of fish and wildlife. The first year of field research will emphasize wading birds, using biological, biochemical and chemical indices to determine the health of estuarine species.

To improve monitoring efforts, the FWS and Idaho National Engineering Laboratory (INEL) are reviewing the current programs to (1) examine how available information could be expanded or made more efficient without losing its linkage to past pollutant trends; (2) develop a plan for integrating data collected in different areas; and (3) recommend ways to assess pollutant problems that were not appreciated when current plans were developed. The Service and INEL are also planning to assess contamination on national wildlife refuges by identifying problems as they develop and 48

evaluating mitigation strategies. The program will also gather baseline data at sites suspected of impacting fish and wildlife both on and off refuge lands. 

The goal of monitoring is not to detect change but to determine the causes of change and to predict future trends and consequences. The complexity of biological systems renders those tasks inherently difficult. For example, BBS reported significant changes between 1985 and 1986 in the populations of 51 percent of the 246 species studied, many of which were not in accordance with long term trends.8,159 Reconciling these data remains a major challenge. 49

At present, data for most species are too scanty to detect trends, much less to infer causation. Long-term monitoring alone will clarify trends, but will probably contribute little to our understanding, unless congruent data on the biology of individual species throughout their entire range can be incorporated into any analysis. This includes basic research on factors that regulate animal populations and how communities are organized.99,150 

Birds as Indicators of Environmental Quality 

The development by CEQ of a simple but quantifiable index of air quality, which is based on an assessment of five common pollutants, raises the question of whether birds might provide an analagous index to the status of wildlife. How might this be achieved? Some consider that diversity is a general guide to an area's biological health. This measure, however, would have to be employed cautiously because (1) bird diversity is a reflection of the underlying plant and topographic diversity and could be an index only to environmental complexity, (2) diversity changes with time, as plant succession occurs, and (3) some healthy communities (saline lakes, tundra, grasslands) are characterized by their lack of diversity. Others, recognizing that obtaining data on all species is impractical, have suggested studies of "indicator species." The concept is attractive, but knowledge about most 130,151,152 species is insufficient to predict their responses to specific events. 

In theory, one could study migratory species that return to the same breeding grounds each year, as these should exhibit “strictly local responses to local perturbations."151 But the annual migrations of such species can involve intercontinental movements of thousands of miles, so that determining the causes of change would be difficult or impossible. One might also monitor predators, on the premise that these reflect the health of the entire underlying community; but that premise is not generally valid. While reproductive failures in eagles, falcons, and pelicans in the 1970s resulted from DDT residues that passed through in the food chain and accumulated in greatest concentration in these top predators, levels of radioactivity at a reactor cooling reservoir were highest in the herbivorous coot, not in fisheating birds. 153,154

It is unlikely, then, that any single species could serve as a general indicator of present environmental perturbations, much less of unanticipated changes that will occur in the future. Each species represents a unique evolutionary "answer" to a complex and unique set of ecological and historical challenges (this is a major reason to conserve biological diversity). Accordingly, its responses to change will be unique and not necessarily in tandem with those of other species in the same area or even with other members of the same species elsewhere. 89,97 

For these reasons, environmental monitoring will have to incorporate broad-based and long-term data for as many components of an ecosystem as possible. Because dominant species are the most likely to have the greatest 50

influence, and also occur in greatest abundance, studies emphasizing a representative assemblage of 5-10 of the commonest species at a network of locations in major habitat types are the most likely to produce useful and interpretable data in the shortest period. 

Conservation 

Before the turn of the century hunting for food or sport or fashion posed the primary threat to bird populations. Today habitat modification is the most pervasive and serious problem." The rationale for preserving large areas of natural habitats is well known. Many species, especially predators, require large areas; other species have narrow habitat requirements and cannot adapt to new situations. As habitat is lost, populations are reduced and eventually isolated, genetic diversity is lowered, and the probability of extinction increases. And if a small population is lost, chances for its range being recolonized are reduced because source populations have become 106,130 remote.

A goal of the FWS is to prevent species from becoming threatened or endangered. To do this most effectively and economically in the long run requires preserving large areas of habitat, preferably with connecting corridors. This will facilitate dispersal and promote the survival of many rare species along with commoner species. 

The federal government holds title to 32 percent of the United States, 727 million acres. Ownership is unevenly divided geographically, ranging from 0.4 percent of Connecticut to 85.8 percent of Alaska. Lands that are most highly protected (national parks, nature preserves, critical habitats, wildlife sanctuaries) are mostly small, isolated from other such preserves, and total at most 3-5 percent of the surface area of the lower 48 states. By contrast, Costa Rica (admittedly a very special case) has designated 20 percent of its area in national parks, biological reserves, and forest reserves, largely as a result of American urging and with significant private funding and technical assistance. 132 Whether future conservation decisions can greatly increase the extent of preserved ecosystems in North America is debatable. One plan of potential importance is the Conservation Reserve Program. Authorized by the Food 

Security Act of 1985, it enables the Secretary of Agriculture to set aside up to 45 million acres of highly erodible croplands by 1990. The program will remove marginal land from production and protect soil and water resources. The degree to which it may enhance wildlife populations is being investigated by the National Ecology Center of the FWS at Fort Collins,

Colorado. It is hoped that some land will revert to a natural state; that is uncertain, however, because these parcels are not set aside in perpetuity — or even for sufficient time to allow successional processes to operate and may later be reconverted for agriculture. To date, 22.9 million acres have been enrolled, many in the prairie pothole region that is so important for 

51

waterfowl production. The parcels vary in average size from 30-40 acres in the East to 700-900 acres in the West. Nonetheless, even in the most conservation conscious states, with the strongest management, regulatory, and acquisition mechanisms, wetlands continue to decline. 

Another major effort to conserve wetlands derives from the Western 

Hemisphere Shorebird Reserve Network (WHSRN). Shorebirds are the most international of migrants, with 36 of the 49 North American species spending the majority of their annual cycle in Central and South America; some range to Tierra del Fuego.93,141 Because wetlands are dwindling throughout the hemisphere, international efforts will be needed if the shorebird resource is to be maintained. The importance of the wetlands, which are spaced along a chain of breeding, stopover, and wintering sites, is underscored by the fact that very large fractions of several species may be found at only a few sites. The Copper River Delta, Alaska, for example, is a staging area for nearly 100 percent of the Western Sandpipers and Dunlin on the Pacific coast.

The WHSRN, a voluntary collaboration of government and private organizations, gives recognition to important shorebird habitats and promotes their cooperative management and protection as part of an international reserve network. Launched in 1985 by the Academy of Natural Sciences, Philadelphia, the Canadian Wildlife Service, and Manomet Bird Obser vatory, the network unites wildlife agencies, private conservation groups, and other organizations in an innovative effort to conserve shorebirds and their fragile habitats.141 Two types of sites are recognized. Hemispheric 

Sites hold more than 250,000 birds over the course of the year, or greater than 30 percent of a species' flyway population. Regional sites harbor more than 20,000 shorebirds in a year, or in excess of 5 percent of a flyway population.

In 1985, the lower estuary of Delaware Bay became the first site of the network; it holds over one million shorebirds each May, including 80 percent of the Red Knots that migrate along the eastern seaboard. Established by collaborative efforts involving the states of New Jersey and Delaware, the Public Service Electric and Gas Company and conservation groups, this site serves as a model. By mid-1987, five national agencies as well as 32 states or provincial wildlife agencies from North and South America had made commitments to support the network (Figure 13). 57 Because the needs for wildlife conservation are too great to be met by natural resource agencies, governments, private organizations, landowners, and other citizens will have to play a supporting role. Because increased governmental purchases of land are unlikely, funding must come principally from private organizations that use the resources. Of these, the most successful has been Ducks Unlimited, which since 1937 has raised $400 million to preserve millions of acres of wetlands in the U.S., Canada, and Mexico; these benefit many types of wildlife in addition to waterfowl. A new D. U. project - MARSH – provides matching funds to help states restore wet- 

52

Figure 13.

Shorebird Reserve Network.

Source: Myers, J.P. National Audubon Society. lands. Land acquisition is also the major goal of The Nature Conservancy, which has helped preserve rare and disappearing natural habitats, often of a type or size that does not qualify for inclusion in a government programs. 53

Land acquisition, per se, is not enough. "Ecologists have demonstrated the futility of trying to preserve all elements and processes of biological diversity in small isolated ecosystems,' 132 and have recognized that the value of any parcel is exponentially related to its size. Thus, programs analogous to the Conservation Reserve Program are needed to encourage large landholders to set aside and maintain biological preserves of some minimum size on a long-term basis. Similarly, conservation groups should consider merging their land-purchase funds so that large natural areas can be set aside. 106

Preservation alone is no guarantee of survival. Barro Colorado Island (15.6 km²) in the Panama Canal was formed between 1910-1914 and made a sanctuary in 1923. By 1970, 45 (22 percent) of its original bird species had been lost, 32 because of forest growth and succession, 13 because of isolation from mainland populations. Even greater losses (35 percent in 5 years) were reported in a small (87 ha) forest preserve in Ecuador, illustrating "what happens when a 'park' or 'natural reserve' is separated from surrounding natural vegetation, even if not subjected to human interference."155 155 156 

How, then, shall we proceed? What options remain open? How much of our biota can we realistically expect to be able to preserve, not into the next century but into the next millennium? And how can a consensus on priorities be achieved? Answering these questions will require critical thinking on national to global scales, based on the kind of information a National 

Biotic Inventory or Geographic Information Systems approach could provide - if either existed, 157,158

Biologists now recognize that it is time to move quickly and go beyond the steady-state approach to managing single species mandated by the Endangered Species Act to a broader view, one in which habitats and ecosystems are protected. Even more critical is the need to acknowledge that natural processes are dynamic and that changes are inevitable.124,125,128,158

There are several reasons for this. First, the single-species approach has not been very effective; endangered species become extinct more quickly than they are delisted because of deliberate remedial actions. Second, steadystates are abstractions and do not exist on a time frame that is relevant to long-term preservation. Third, current and anticipated rates of environmental change from population growth and climate change alone will simply overwhelm a "save every species" response. Finally, the costs of singlespecies management are high compared with an ecosystem approach. The cost of managing 257 species of vertebrates in the 7200 ha Sacramento National Wildlife Refuge, about $1 million per year, is less than the annual expenditure for the recovery program for the California Condor. 158 The world population continues to grow, increasing demands for food and transforming many natural habitats into monoculture plantations, which are mostly valueless to wildlife. If the effects of forest fragmentation on birds that winter in the Neotropics are now debatable, that is unlikely to be the 54

case much longer, because forests there are being lost faster than in the temperate breeding areas (Figure 14). This means that plans to perpetuate temperate zone birdlife must deal with increasing human and environmental needs throughout the hemisphere.

The prospect of "climate change" is equally daunting. Some models suggest that within a century the mean global temperature could increase by 68°F. While the precise effects are unknown, major shifts in rainfall patterns nd plant distribution would surely follow. Animals can shift ranges rapidly, but the plants on which they depend cannot; it may take centuries or millennia for tree species to equilibrate with new conditions. A predictable consequence is that plant communities worldwide will become fragmented far south of their new optimal ranges, increasing the potential for disruption of animal populations and for massive extinctions. 

These eventualities require new ways of thinking about conservation: for one, the recognition that current wildlife reserves cannot forever function in their present roles. Until 1983 Bear River Refuge, Utah, was one of our best waterfowl producing areas. It was abandoned in 1984, after an 8-foot rise in 

Great Salt Lake flooded the fresh water marshes where thousands of duck once bred. 55

Opportunity for Increased Private Role in Conservation 

Changes in natural systems and in the human interaction with natural systems occur so rapidly that a piecemeal approach cannot keep up with them. 

Yet there is still much about natural systems that is unknown. And we do not know what we are going to want to do with the resources of the land a decade or a century hence. Such uncertainty suggests we will benefit by a flexible, evolutionary approach to ecosystem preservation that incorporates public and private efforts to retain habitats in an undisturbed state. By approaching preservation from the habitat perspective, we do not need to know all the relationships among species before we can protect them adequately. Instead, we can rest assured that about all that is humanly possible in terms of preservation has been accomplished. 

An important advantage of the habitat approach to maintenance of natural systems is that it leaves the way open to substantial non-governmental activity in both the nonprofit and for profit sectors, which can tap the great creative wealth of the private sector to deal with many of these problems. The cases of private organizations such as Ducks Unlimited and 

The Nature Conservancy discussed earlier are well tested examples; even more encouraging is the growing corporate commitment to preservation either through conscientious management of their own land holdings or through contributions to nonprofit conservation concerns. 

Government policies can facilitate private efforts at the preservation of habitat and the maintenance of natural systems in a number ways, some in direct. The case of the Conservation Reserve Program under the Food 

Security Act of 1985 is one promising approach. The Coastal Barrier

Resources Act of 1982, which ended federal subsidies of construction activity on 700 miles of undeveloped coastal barriers, and thus helps preserve associated aquatic wetland habitats, may prove to be a model for approaching other ecologically fragile lands. Tax policy at all levels of government is also an important determinant in the uses made of private lands. High real estate taxes, for example, encourage converting open spaces, wherever they are, to revenue producing uses. Pollution control efforts should also work to ease pressures on natural systems in important ways, and make any program of habitat preservation more effective.

Given that all desirable habitat protection cannot be accomplished by government alone, perhaps the most promising policy is to encourage decentralized, low cost, voluntary initiatives. Even very small tracts of land can be valuable in protection of critical habitats. Glades, stream banks, isolated woodlots in the midst of larger landholdings, even backyards, form micro-wildernesses for many plants, small mammals, and invertebrate species. Where private landowners can combine their individual habitat preservation efforts, the benefits may be larger than each operating independently. This concept is employed by Operation Stronghold, based in 

Chiloquin, Oregon, a voluntary coalition of private rural landowners committed to improving wildlife habitat and conservation practices on private 56

lands. Some five million acres have been subscribed in this private, wholly voluntary program.

Over four decades ago, Aldo Leopold wrote: "If the private owner were ecologically minded, he would be proud to be the custodian of a reasonable proportion of such areas, which add diversity and beauty to his farm and to his community." And he worried about a system of land conservation that "tends to relegate to government many functions eventually too large, too complex, or too widely dispersed to be performed by government." He concluded that: "An ethical obligation on the part of the private owner is the only visible remedy for these situations.">166 

Although, as a nation, we are still "striving" for the ideal of harmony with land among our people, there is reason to be optimistic as work by public and private agencies and individual landowners continues to ease pressures on natural systems and to make the program of habitat preservation a more effective one. 1.

Some small Marshes and 

Swamps there are, but more profitable than hurtful. 

Captain John Smith

The word "swamp" made its first documented appearance in the English language in John Smith's General History of Virginia, New England, and the Summer Isles (1624). In the more than three hundred and sixty years since 

Captain Smith wrote his prospectus, swamps and other wetlands have been filled to build our cities, felled for their timber, excavated for their peat, sand, phosphate, and gravel; ditched, diked, leveed, flooded for reservoirs, pickled by excess salt, dissected by navigational canals, strip mined, polluted by toxic wastes, fouled with sewage, sapped of their waters, and, primarily, converted to agricultural uses. Though figures vary, it has been estimated that of the 215 million acres of wetlands present in the conterminous United States at the time of settlement, only 99 million acres, or 46 percent, remain. In certain areas, the decline has been even more severe. Iowa has lost 99 percent of its original marshes; 91 percent of California's wetlands are gone; eight million of Michigan's original 11.2 million wetland acres have been lost. By the mid-1970s, the annual rate of loss was estimated to be 458,000 acres, mostly in freshwater wetlands. Although much of this alteration was necessary and inevitable in a developing nation, it is partly a reflection of our inherited perception of wetlands through language and literature. 65

Wetlands in Culture, Language, and Literature 

Human association with wetlands goes back to our earliest beginnings. The remains of wetland dependent animals - frogs and fish have been discovered with early human fossils in Africa's Olduvai Gorge.' A number of early North American cultures, such as the Early Woodland and Upper Great Lakes peoples, were dependent upon wetlands. Many of the wall paintings and bas-reliefs of the Egyptians, who were well aware of the source of much of their food and clothing, faithfully depict wetland themes: remarkably accurate carvings of marshes, ducks, geese, and waterbirds in a naturalistic style which contrasts strongly with the usual stylized illustration of the people of the Nile. From a wetland sedge, Cyperus papyrus, came the earliest paper; papyrus columns and motifs are a common thread along with that of another wetland plant, the lotus - running through several thousand years of Egyptian architecture and design. 

The Cultural Inheritance 

10 But the perception of wetlands that come to us through the Judeo-Christian heritage is not a positive one. Certain wetlands have often been used as a metaphor for trouble, doubt, evil, and temptation; something to be redeemed." Psalms 40:2 reads: "He drew me up from the desolate pit, out of the miry bog, and set my feet upon a rock, making my steps secure.' This is later echoed in John Bunyan's The Pilgrim's Progress (1678 and 1684), in which figures the bog called the slough of Despond, "an impeding obstacle, a despairing low point, a clogged encumbrance, on the road to the promised land."11

The Pilgrim's Progress was hugely popular in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, shaping and embodying the Western and American attitude toward wetlands: the commonly held belief that they were bad ground, wastelands to be perfected or reclaimed from themselves. This attitude survived well into the twentieth century, with devastating consequen- 12 ces.

Colonists and the Early Naturalists 13 That this was an attitude of the average colonist is unquestionably true. It can be seen in the innumerable accounts of the early settlers, who wasted no time in redeeming the wetlands, even though they were dependent upon wetland productivity for their water and much of their food, clothing, and forage. It was also true of the early naturalists and conservationists, who usually reserved their praise for more endearing landscapes. William

Bartram, in his Travels, waxed rhapsodic over the swamp and river country of Georgia and northern Florida, while at the same time suggesting how productive the area might be were it converted to agriculture. Audubon had very little good to say about the "endless, labyrinthine" swamps of Florida, which depressed him. John Muir, the founder of the Wilderness Society and 66

WETLANDS 

the poet of the Sierras, fell afoul of the same "thorny, watery" Florida swamps, where he came down with a near fatal case of malaria, long a much feared wetlands evil.14

Place Names and Their Connotations 

These attitudes survive in place names, well or little-known: Great Dismal 

Swamp, Lostlog Swamp, Hell Hook Marsh, Devil's Swale, Devil's Bog, Lost 

Spirit Bog, Blasted Hope Bog, Perdition Swamp, Devil's Kettle. John Smith named the islands of the Chesapeake's Tangier Sound - great expanses of tidal salt marsh the Isles of Limbo, for an upper circle in a Dantean Hell. 

The names of specific wetlands often carry negative meanings. Swamp or bog, used as verbs, can mean to destroy, defeat, burden, overwhelm, hinder, slow, or impede. Bogs in particular carry sinister connotations which writers, mystery writers in particular, have long exploited. The boggy moor of Arthur Conan Doyle's The Hound of the Baskervilles is a major player in the plot, which abounds in minatory descriptions of the Great Grimpen 

Mire ("A false step yonder means death to man or beast."). Dorothy

Sayers used wetlands to effect in a number of her novels, most notably in Clouds of Witness, in which another mysterious bog figures. The trend has persisted for decades in American horror and suspense films,10 which must appeal to half buried superstitions about wetlands that remain in the public psyche. 16 

An Evolving Awareness 

Although literary and cultural historians have long noted the primacy of the natural landscape in the shaping of American thought and literature," the contribution of wetlands to this process has received relatively little recognition. However, they do figure strongly in the lore and folklore surrounding such figures as Captain John Smith, George Washington, Daniel Boone,

Davey Crockett, Mike Fink and the riverboat men. They are strong in the folkways and traditions surrounding wetland-dependant lifestyles around the country, such as the Pacific salmon fisheries, the Chesapeake Bay watermen, and the Cajuns of the Mississippi Delta. It is as if such recognition conflicts with our long and tenaciously held undervaluation of wetlands. 18 Poets and writers have usually chosen features of the landscape other than wetlands as settings for their work. Bartlett's Familiar Quotations contains seventy-four citations for rivers, fifty-two for oceans, fifty-four for mountains, twenty-seven for deserts, nine for prairies, and seventeen for forests. In the same volume are four references to marshes, two for swamps, four for glades, one for fen, and two for bogs, including Emily Dickenson's perverse: "To tell one's name - the livelong June - to an admiring bog.

But there has been an appreciative strain. Wetlands appear prominently in the writings of Cooper, Thoreau, Whitman, Hemingway, and Faulkner particularly in "Old Man", which is based around the great Mississippi 19 

67

River flood of 1927, and "The Bear". In works such as Life On the Mississippi, Tom Sawyer, and Huckleberry Finn, Mark Twain brought to life the nostalgic presence of the southern bottomland swamp, much of which is now gone. The following is Huck Finn's idyllic description of the Mississippi in full flood:

Day times we paddled all over the island in the canoe. It was mighty cool and shady in the deep woods even if the sun was blazing outside. We went winding in and out amongst the trees; and sometimes the vines hung so thick we had to back away and go some other way. Well, on every old broken-down tree you could see rabbits, and snakes, and such things; and when the island had been overflowed a day or two, they got so tame, on account of being hungry, that you could paddle right up and put your hand on them if you wanted to; but not the snakes and turtles - they would slide off in the water. 20 At the time this was written (1884), there did not exist much sympathy with this romantic version of the flooded swamp drainages, dikes, and levees had been built for decades in an effort to control such flooding, which was hardly romantic to those who had to cope with it. The historical policy of federal, state, and local governments was to assist in the drainage of wetlands. Wetlands along the Potomac were destroyed systematically following malaria outbreaks in the 1800s.22 In the mid-1800s, Congress granted to state governments a total of 65 million acres of wetlands for reclamation.23 21 In the 1800s, virtually the only people directly concerned with wetland preservation were the hunters and trappers who made their living directly from them. But we have seen in the succeeding century a gradual evolution in the public perception of wetland values. Waterfowl hunters were among the first to press for wetlands preservation. Concern over declining waterfowl numbers due to extensive wetland destruction led to the inception of the National Wildlife Refuge System in 1903; and, in 1929, to the Migratory 

Bird Conservation Act. In 1934, the annual Migratory Bird Hunting and 

Conservation Stamp (the “Duck Stamp") came into use. The $300 million revenues from the stamp have helped to preserve over 3.6 million acres of wetland habitat.24

But the change in attitudes toward wetlands was far from complete. The early 1900s also saw the initiation of large-scale agricultural drainage programs. Congress authorized millions of dollars to assist in drainage projects. Until the 1970s, the Department of Agriculture provided technical assistance to help farmers drain wetlands. For example, 25 In 1975, [the Agricultural Stabilization and Conserva tion Service Agricultural Conservation Program] sup ported drainage affecting more than 64,000 acres in the 68

WETLANDS 

three states of Minnesota, South Dakota, and North

Dakota alone. The Conservation Operations Program 

[provided technical assistance] in the installation of over 

7,200 miles of drainage ditches, over 22,000 acres of drainage land grading, and over 26,000 miles of subsurface drains. 26

In the 1978 Delmarva River Basin Study, the Department of Agriculture said that over 20,000 miles of new channels and drainage ditches were needed to maximize production of wet soils in the area." 27 Quasi-governmental drainage districts, organized for the purpose of irrigating and draining agricultural land, existed in almost every state, and were usually given the power of eminent domain to assist in construction of drainage ditches.28 At one time, more than 8,400 of these districts existed across the nation. The 1959 Census of Agriculture showed that 102 million acres were contained in drainage districts, and drainage development occurred on 92 million of those acres.

Since the 1950s, an increasingly sophisticated perception of wetland values has led to the initiation of a National Wetlands Inventory Project, the passage of coastal and inland wetland protection laws by certain states, and the preservation and rehabilitation of a number of large wetland complexes, such as the Everglades and Big Cypress in Florida. Another area in Florida provides a fascinating case study of changing attitudes: in 1961, the Army 

Corps of Engineers began channelizing the Kissimmee River. The 98-mile long meandering river was confined into a 48-mile canal, destroying 70-80 percent of the riverbasin's wildlife-rich marshes. Before channelization was even completed in 1971, restoration was proposed. The state launched a pilot program in 1983 to experiment with restoration techniques as the first phase of a restoration strategy that, if authorized, may ultimately cost over $100 million.29

This trend can also be seen in the willingness of citizens to commit often considerable parts of their disposable income and free time to numerous special interests (the Izaak Walton League, Ducks Unlimited, The Nature Conservancy, and a variety of smaller associations) devoted to wetland preservation in the private sector. It can also be seen in the gradual phasing out of federal expenditures that encourage wetlands destruction. 

Wetland Definitions and Classification

The term "wetland" is a modern invention. Prior to its general acceptance there existed, and still exists, an often bewildering multiplicity of terms to describe watery places. Some words were inherited from Europe, others from the native Americans, still others evolved from regional usage. In general dictionaries, some definitions are often synonymous and interchangeable; others have special regional meanings: swamp, marsh, meadow, bog, 69

hanging bog, fen, savannah, slough, swale, playa, pocosin, spong, pothole, mangrove, cyprus dome, vernal pond, Carolina bay, tulare, cienega, bottomland, seep; the list is as long as the country is varied. 

The very nature of wetland ecosystems has made precise definitions difficult. While cattail marshes and coastal saltmarshes are generally recognized as wetlands, others are more subtle and difficult to pin down. Vernal ponds and prairie potholes are often only periodically wet, either seasonally or cyclically. At other times they can resemble dry meadows or prairie. It is now recognized that there is no single, definitive, ecologically sound definition of wetlands, due to "the diversity of wetlands and because the demarcation between dry and wet environments lies along a continuum.”30 Because of variability of flooding depth and frequency, and because wetlands will usually exhibit some of the characteristics of both terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems,31 any definition is likely, in part, to be somewhat arbitrary. 

One of the earliest systematic wetland definitions is contained in "Circular 

39", a 1956 publication produced by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, which has a strong emphasis on wetlands for waterfowl production: 32 

The term 'wetlands'. . . refers to lowlands covered with shallow water and sometimes temporary or intermittent waters. They are referred to by such names as marshes, swamps, reedswamps, bogs, wet meadows, potholes, sloughs, and river-overflow lands. Shallow lakes and ponds, usually with emergent vegetation as a conspicuous feature, are included in this definition, but the permanent waters of streams, reservoirs, and deep lakes are not included. Neither are water areas that are so temporary as to have little or no effect on the development of moist-soil vegetation. 33 A more comprehensive definition, better tuned to wetland identification, was developed by the Service in 1979:

Wetlands are lands transitional between terrestrial and aquatic systems where the water table is usually at or near the surface or the land is covered by shallow water. For purposes of this classification wetlands must have one or more of the following three attributes: 1) at least periodically, the land supports predominantly hydrophytes; 2) the substrate is predominantly undrained hydric soil; and 

3) the substrate is nonsoil and is saturated with water or covered by shallow water at some time during the growing season of each year. 34 This definition has been used by the Service for wetland classification and was used as the basis for its National Wetlands Inventory Project, begun in 1974. 70

WETLANDS 

Wetland Classification

Although various classification systems have been devised by ecologists, hydrologists, and soil scientists, the system now used by most regulatory agencies in the United States is that of the Fish and Wildlife Service's National Wetlands Inventory (Table 1). It is a formal and inclusive scheme composed of systems, subsystems, and classes which, for the purpose of describing most wetland habitats and acreage, can be listed as Estuarine and Palustrine systems. Estuarine habitats include tidal salt marshes, tidal freshwater marshes, and mangrove swamps. Palustrine systems include inland freshwater marshes, northern peatlands or bogs, southern deepwater swamps (the bottomland forests of Huckleberry Finn), and riparian wetlands which occur along the banks of rivers, ponds, and streams. 

Wetland Values 36 That wetlands have "value" suggests that they perform functions which are of benefit to society.30 (Table 2.) Some of the values and functions documented by studies include: primary productivity, importance to wildlife and fisheries, storm damage abatement and flood control, water quality, water supply and recharge, energy, timber, endangered species protection, maintenance of the biosphere, recreation, and aesthetics. 

Primary Productivity 

37 In terms of total mass of vegetative material termed biomass or primary production - wetlands are often more productive than ecosystems such as grasslands, most croplands, temperate forests, and tropical rain forests. 

This productivity plays a particularly important role as the base and starting point of many food chains upon which humans depend. Coastal salt marshes and fresh water marshes appear to the most efficient of all wetland types, followed by swamp forests, bogs, fens, and muskegs." Such productivity varies with geography, climate, hydrology, and a number of other local factors; the process and is complex and is currently resistant to broad generalizations.4 38 40 

Fisheries and Wildlife Values 

41 Wetlands are perhaps best known for their support of wildlife, particularly ducks, geese, and other game birds. The Prairie Pothole Region of the

Dakotas is the largest waterfowl producing region in the country, and accounts for the majority of ducks fledged in the United States.¹¹ The Pacific, Central, Mississippi, and Atlantic flyways are all heavily wetland-associated,42 while major wetland areas along the Middle Atlantic and Southeast coasts, and in Louisiana and in California, provide critical wintering grounds for ducks and geese. The annual fall and winter waterfowl hunt is, in many areas of the country, a major cultural and social ritual of long 71

Recreation, Aesthetic, and Other Values 44 standing. Over five million Americans annually purchase "Duck Stamps", and such hunting supports a large subsidiary industry. The harvest of pelts from such wetland furbearers as muskrat, nutria, mink, otter, and beaver is important to local economies in certain areas. Non-game birds such as grebes, herons, nine members of the rail family, Wilson's phalaropes and at least thirty-seven other species of shorebirds, black terns, marsh wrens, and a number of sparrow and blackbird species, are also highly dependent upon wetlands for their survival. The westward shift of breeding and migrant long-billed curlews, and their consequent rarity in the East, can be accounted for by wetland destruction in the Midwest during the last century and a half.45 

46 Other animals such as turtles, alligators, some snakes, and particularly amphibians, spend at least part of their life cycle in wetlands, and in turn are influences upon them. Virtually all of the approximately 190 species of amphibians in North America require wetlands, at least for breeding. Countless species of invertebrates - annelid worms, mollusks, crustaceans, and 47 insects play a major role in wetland food chains and nutrient cycles." 

Although estimates vary, 60 to 66 per cent of all the saltwater fish and shellfish harvested annually in the United States are dependent upon coastal wetlands for spawning, food, and nursery functions; while inland wetlands contribute to the maintenance of proper estuarine salinities downstream. (Table 3.) Detritus, the breakdown product of the abundant salt marsh vegetation by microbial and other actions, is important to such economically significant species as shrimp, blue crab, gizzard shad, bay anchovy, blue and channel catfish, spot, Atlantic croaker, and striped mullet. Detritus is at the beginning of many estuarine food chains, and can affect offshore populations. Shrimp catches in the Gulf of Mexico are directly related to the amount of wetland available, and declines in fishery yield in this area are directly related to wetland loss along the Gulf shore, particularly in Louisiana. Commercial and game fishes in the Great Lakes are dependent upon freshwater inland wetlands along the shore for spawning, feeding, and shelter; declines in these stocks have been traced to the 50 73

+54 74

WETLANDS 

Flood and Storm Damage Protection 

55 Rainfall now produces "more flood per inch" than it did fifty or even twenty years ago, and flood damages now total billions of dollars annually. Wetland drainage, river and stream channelization, levees and dikes, and vast areas of impermeable pavement now ensure that peak runoff rates during floods occur earlier and are markedly higher than they were in the past." Modelling studies suggest that in rare large floods, peak stream flow increases significantly when upstream wetlands are removed from a system. The highest flood crest ever recorded at St. Louis, during the great Mississippi River flood of 1973, was caused by rainfall only twenty per cent above normal. Yet it nearly brought down the Corps of Engineers' Old River Control Structure - the civil works project of the highest national priority for the Corps - at the confluence with the Atchafalaya in Louisiana. Since that time, there have been seven high water "events" on the Mississippi, including the third greatest flood of the twentieth century, in 1983. 57 The catastrophic flood of 1955 in Pennsylvania and New Jersey, which caused enormous damage and great loss of life, was marked with one small exception. While surrounding valleys flooded violently, washing away bridges, barns, and homes, Tannersville Valley, in the Poconos, suffered no significant harm. Near the head of the valley is Tannersville Bog, which local residents had successfully fought to save from development. It is thought that the bog acted as an enormous sponge, sopping up the rainwater and releasing it gradually and safely. 58 Wetlands, particularly those in basins, but in most other types as well, decrease the velocity of floodwaters and provide temporary storage for them, resulting in lower flood flows and crests." Wetland vegetation binds and stabilizes substrates limiting erosion - dissipates wave energy and water velocity, and can buffer the land from wave damage. In Massachusetts, the Corps of Engineers decided that wetland protection and acquisition "Natural Valley Storage" was the most cost-effective solution to flooding problems along the Charles River watershed, near Boston. 60 61 Coastal salt marshes can act as effective hurricane buffers, damping wave action and acting as a reservoir for flood waters. Marsh and islandprotected coasts suffer less damage during hurricanes because of this buffer action. New Orleans has long been a major beneficiary of Gulf Coast wetlands during historic storms, though Louisiana is rapidly losing its barrier islands and marshes in the Delta, due to a complex of factors. 

Water Quality

By processes that are mechanical, chemical, and biological, wetlands act to improve the quality of the water which passes through them. Wetlands affect water purity by serving as settling areas for sediment, which drops out of the water as its velocity slows through spreading. Nitrogen and phos- 75

phorus can reduce water quality by causing algal blooms and eutrophication. Many wetlands function as nitrogen and phosphorus traps, although the effect can be seasonal and may vary with such factors as hydrology and decay. Studies of Brillion Marsh, a freshwater cattail marsh in Wisconsin which has long received wastewater, have found that it removed coliform bacteria (86 percent), nitrates (51 percent), turbidity (44 percent), suspended solids (29 percent), and phosphorus (13 percent) from the waters draining into it. An earlier study of the Tinicum Marsh in Philadelphia concluded that: "marshlands play an important role in reducing nutrient load in water and increasing oxygen content."66 Although evidence is accumulating which indicates that wetlands are capable of performing certain tertiary treatment functions,' this varies with wetland type, soil, hydrology, and climate. Wetlands can become phosphorous- and sewagesaturated, effectively shutting down this function, and it has been suggested that they may only act as short-term sinks unless the biomass which holds these materials is harvested.68 

Heavy metals, pesticides, and toxic chemicals can be immobilized in sediments as they pass through wetland systems. Aquatic and semi-aquatic plants, such as water hyacinth and alligator weed, can absorb heavy metals which are then stored in leaves, roots, and stems; a double-edged sword, since the same substances may then be passed up the food chain." That estuarine systems are efficient filters and collectors of such materials is one of the major problems of the nation's largest estuary, the Chesapeake Bay. An increase in the Bay's nutrient and toxic compound levels over the last thirty years has coincided with declines in submerged aquatic vegetation, striped bass, shad and oysters. 70 

Water Supply and Recharge 

71 72 73 Some wetlands may be areas of groundwater recharge, exporting water to subterranean aquifers. This function may vary, as with other values, with type, location, size, geology, hydrology, and soils." It is possible that some wetlands do not contribute to recharge simply because their soils are impermeable the original cause for the standing water in the first place." Peatlands, though often waterlogged, do not appear to contribute significantly to subsurface water, though cypress domes in Florida do." Recharge, as a function of edge-to-volume ratios, may be more important in small wetlands such as prairie potholes (important sources of surface water), which could be of regional importance considering their sheer numbers." Wetlands may prove valuable as indicators of the presence of subsurface aquifers;" the wetlands and forests of New Jersey's pine barrens are underlain by the huge Cohansey Aquifer, and community wells throughout the country are often located near wetlands. 75 

76

WETLANDS 

Energy Resources 

76 Wetlands peat has been used as fuel for centuries in Europe, particularly in Ireland, Finland, and Russia, where there are now modern plants utilizing sophisticated technology to produce electricity and heat. Peat, the accumulated remains of sphagnum mosses and other organic debris, occurs in various wetland habitats grouped under the general classification of "peatland", which includes northern bogs, fens, and southern pocosins. Most northern peats have been accumulating since the end of the Wisconsin glaciation, which left a scoured and poorly drained landscape favorable to peat formation. Seven per cent of the world's peat occurs in the United 

States, chiefly in Alaska, Minnesota, and Michigan." (Table 4.) Spurred by the fuel crises of the 1970s, programs were initiated in North Carolina and Minnesota to test the feasibility of using peat as a fuel. Findings have varied. 

One study found that peat, dried and processed, compared favorably with coal in energy output, and with a much lower sulfur content. It was estimated that the use of less than ten per cent of Minnesota's peat could supply the state's energy needs for thirty-two years." A more recent study suggests that peat-for-fuel is not competitive with cheaper coal, oil, and gas. First Colony Farms in North Carolina, and the Georgia-Pacific Corporation in Florida have recently initiated peat-based energy projects.79 78 Table 4. Such use is of course a consumptive, non-renewable use of peatlands, and its environmental impact has not been studied adequately. Studies by biogeographers and palynologists of pollen imbedded and preserved in peats has given a view of past landscapes and climates.80 Peat mining destroys the habitat of a series of often uniquely adapted plants (such as the insectivorous sundews, pitcher plants, and the Venus flytrap) and animals, HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

77

some of which are rare or endangered. Large scale burning of peats may result in the release of heavy metals stored in them, lead to the eutrophication of surface waters, increase surface runoff, alter freshwater systems and groundwater, and contribute to air pollution. 

Timber Resources 

Wetland forests have been consistent producers of timber for well over two hundred years, with relatively little noticeable degradation. Since colonial times, stands of Atlantic white cedar on the eastern coastal plain have been repeatedly cut for shingles and posts; yet, where there has been no drainage or filling of the cedar swamps themselves, the trees have regenerated. 

Canoeists passing through the seeming wilderness of cedar swamps in the Pinelands National Preserve in New Jersey are usually unaware that the swamps have been logged many times in the past. 

There are an estimated eighty-two million acres of wetland forests in the continental United States, three quarters of which are east of the Rockies and two thirds of which are deciduous; eighteen million acres of bottomland spruce/fir forests exist in Alaska. Bottomland forest types include the elm/ash/cottonwood association of the north, and the familiar oak/ gum/cypress stands of the south, which may be the most productive of all wetland forests. Innovative management of these forests will help to assure adequate future timber supplies. Areas along the lower Mississippi which have been converted, though not drained, to cottonwood monoculture have proven to be extraordinarily efficient timber producers. Such forested wetlands can provide multiple use functions, supporting timber production, wildlife habitat, flood control, and recreation. However, the trend of clearing and conversion for crops will probably continue; the maintenance of timbered bottomlands has historically been less attractive to landowners than their conversion to agricultural uses, which often produces more annual in- 84 come.

Endangered Species Protection 

With our wetlands have gone some of their better known inhabitants. That avatar of the southern bottomlands, the ivory-billed woodpecker, was partial of large tracts of virgin timber and particularly ill-adapted to cope with the disturbance and destruction of its habitat. One of the last documented sightings of the ivory-bill was in the Singer Tract of Louisiana's Tensas River bottomlands, in the 1940s. The tract was subsequently logged. The ivory-bill is almost certainly extinct in the United States; a few birds may still linger in 

Cuba's Oriente Province. Also gone from similar haunts is the Carolina parakeet. In spite of intensive searching, Bachman's warbler has not been unquestionably verified since the early 1960s, though there are sight records dating from 1978 and 1979, in and near South Carolina's I'on Swamp. 85 78

WETLANDS 

86 The fate of other species will depend upon the survival of their habitats. 

Such well known endangered species as the red wolf and the Florida panther now virtually restricted to the sloughs and strands of the Fakahatchee and adjacent Big Cypress - have found a refuge in wetland habitats; as have the snail kite, bald eagle, and American crocodile. The whooping crane, with a 1986 population of 105 birds (up from a low of 15 in 1941), is almost totally dependent upon coastal marshes, riverine wetlands, and freshwater marshes for its nesting, migration, and wintering grounds. 87 Celebrity birds and mammals comprise only a small fraction of the nearly 900 species listed as endangered or threatened in the United States, the majority of which are obscure reptiles, amphibians, fish, invertebrates, and plants.88 Many spend all or part of their life cycle in wetlands. Individual state lists of threatened and endangered species often show a preponderance of wetland species, particularly of plants. 

As wetland habitats contract and are isolated from larger systems, they have come increasingly to serve as refuges for endangered and threatened species. In effect, many of these isolated wetlands function as island ecosystems, surrounded as they are by large areas of dissimilar habitat, and they are prone to many of the stresses and disequilibriums as island systems when disturbed. In part, this is a response to natural and long-term changes in climate, though the main culprit is habitat destruction and alteration. 

Maintenance of the Biosphere 

Although the functions are not clearly understood because they are so large, wetlands may play a role in global chemical cycles. Waterborne nitrates, often washed abundantly into wetlands from agricultural runoff, are denitrified, returning free nitrogen to the atmosphere. Sulfates, the atmospheric products of the combustion of fossil fuels, reach wetlands as acid rain and runoff, where they are immobilized by various chemical processes. There is also evidence to suggest that methane, produced by certain wetland soils, plays a role in the ozone layer which shields the earth from ultraviolet rays. 

Wetlands modify temperature and water content in the lower atmosphere and, depending upon their size, can influence local and regional weather patterns. Unlike payed or drained areas, wetlands display a kind of thermal inertia when temperatures fluctuate; they modulate extremes. 

Studies of drained agricultural land near Florida's Lake Okeechobee during the severe winters of 1976/1977 and 1977/1978 found that they were as much as 5 degrees Celsius colder a critical difference in citrus growing areas than surrounding undrained land.92 

Recreation, Aesthetic, and Other Values

The abundant values of wetlands to recreational hunting and fishing are quite evident, and there are other pastimes tied up with them. Non-consumptive activities such as hiking, boating, nature photography, birdwatch 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

79

ing, and botanizing are often enriched by swamps, bogs, potholes, and other wet habitats. A recent survey ranked nature-walking as third, and birdwatching twelfth in a list of the twenty most popular leisure activities.93 

Some of the most productive birding is to be had in swamps, fresh and saltwater marshes, and tidal flats, the preferred habitat for many species. 

Western riparian wetlands, such as those at Cave Creek Canyon and Patagonia, in Arizona, are intensely birded by thousands of enthusiasts in search of rare birds and strays from Mexico.

Some Problems in Valuation 

Wetland "values" refer to a complex of tangibles and intangibles, some of which are quantifiable in dollar value fisheries, waterfowl harvests, some flood and water quality functions - others not. Biosphere functions, nutrient cycling, aesthetics, wildlife habitat, endangered species protection, and recreation are qualities resistant to an expression in dollars and cents." 

Also, in this context, value generally refers to society as a whole and not to a particular owner, to whom a wetland is often more valuable when converted to other uses. 95 For the individual landowner, wetlands may remain a problem. From an economic perspective, landowners are generally unable to capture positive externalities. Hunters, fishermen, and downstream neighbors may benefit from the existence of a farmland swamp, but the farmer himself must combat mosquitoes and unwanted wildlife, cope with an obstacle to efficient plowing, and pay taxes on unproductive land. These nuisance factors may be of overriding significance in private decisions to destroy wetlands. The economic incentives behind his decisionmaking process may thus be biased in favor of wetlands destruction. As we shall later discuss, government programs that subsidize destruction have frequently contributed to the bias. 96 Intangible values may appeal to the poet and the general public, though planners will, in the future, seek quantifiable data in order to weigh alternatives and establish long range goals. To date, several numerical evaluation techniques-based either on dollar, energy, or relative efficiency - have been devised by wetland scientists, all with an admitted subjective ele- 97 ment.

102 Only 95,000 acres of California's coastal marshes remain, of which only 19 per cent are relatively undisturbed; their critical value may derive simply 100 from present rarity." Inland freshwater wetlands, important wintering grounds for millions of waterfowl, are under heavy pressure in the state's Central Valley. 81

Effects of Agriculture 

Since the nation is now primarily urban and suburban, wetland destruction is popularly linked with construction of condominiums and other housing, shopping malls, office and industrial developments. But only thirteen per cent of recent wetland losses are due to such expansion, while the remaining eighty-seven per cent comes from agricultural and silvicultural conversion, most markedly in the prairie pothole region, the southern bottomlands, and in the pocosins of the Carolinas.104 

Prairie Potholes 106 Millions of small wetlands once dotted the glaciated prairie regions of Minnesota, the Dakotas, and Iowa. Known collectively as Prairie Potholes, the area is perhaps the most productive waterfowl regions on the continent, 105 and additionally supports at least twenty-eight species of non-game birds. 

Agricultural ditching and draining, often with federal assistance, has led to the loss of more than half of the total wetland acreage in the Dakotas, with considerably higher declines in Iowa and Minnesota. Drainage has also damaged the region's flood control capacity in certain areas. 107 108 

Southern Bottomlands 109 These familiar river-associated wetlands once stretched from Virginia and the Carolinas west to Texas and north to Iowa and Indiana. Less than twenty-five per cent remain nationwide; perhaps less than twenty per cent in the 

Mississippi alluvial plain. Although traditionally productive for timber, such use is not competitive with the conversion to cropland, particularly of soybeans, which is responsible for most of the recent losses. The consequences of these declines include increased flooding, loss of prime wildlife habitat, and increased runoff of the pesticides and fertilizers used by agriculture.110 

North Carolina Pocosins

Pocosins - the term is Algonquin for "swamp on a hill" - occur along the 

Southeastern Coastal Plain, most abundantly in North Carolina. Boggy habitats with a mixture of shrubs and evergreens, plus interesting plants such as the carnivorous Venus flytrap, pocosins were long ignored while more suitable agricultural land existed elsewhere. But recently they have become a classic example of the conflict between mutually exclusive uses of a declining commodity. Booming soybean prices in the early 1970s led to a 111 sharp increase in the agricultural conversion of pocosins. Timber companies, foreseeing timber shortfalls, drained, bulldozed, and planted pocosins to tree monocultures.112 Pocosin peats were mined for energy projects.113 Undisturbed pocosin acreage declined by sixty-nine per cent during the 1960s and 1970s, with parallel changes: increases in water and 82

WETLANDS 

fertilizer runoff; declines in downstream water quality and in estuarine shrimp harvests.114 

Louisiana's Coastal Marshes

Perhaps the most daunting of all the regional problem areas are the coastal marshes of Louisiana, which comprise approximately thirty to forty percent 115 of all the coastal wetlands in the conterminous United States. An interplay of natural and man-made factors has resulted in a huge annual conversion of marshland to open water, amounting in some estimates (as high as thirty square miles per year) to eighty-five per cent of the total national coastal wetland loss rate. 116 Causative factors (Table 6) include coastal subsidence, sea level rise, and loss of natural sediment load. "Big Muddy's" enormous sediment load, which has been building the present Delta over the last thousand years, is now, because of levee construction, delivered to the deep waters of the continental shelf; it is no longer being distributed over the Delta.
