In addition, thirty per cent of the Mississippi's flow now passes down the Atchafalaya, which has been attempting to capture the Mississippi in this century. It is thought by some that were it not for the Corps of Engineers' control structures at the confluence of the two rivers, the Atchafalaya would have done just that by the mid-1970s.117 Although the Mississippi has changed its course many times in the last seven thousand years, in the process creating much of southern Louisiana and parts of Texas and Mississippi, development during the last three centuries has been built upon the assumption that the river would always stay right were it was. Capture by the 

Atchafalaya would move the river's mouth some eighty miles southwest of New Orleans, effectively leaving the city stranded on a small tidal river, with potentially disastrous local and national consequences. 118 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

83

120 Other factors contributing to marshland loss in the Delta include loss of barrier islands, dredging and filling, and saltwater intrusion. A multitude of canals now widen annually at the expense of the marshes. Although the 

Atchafalaya is in the process of building a new delta and new marshes, and the Atchafalaya Basin is now the third largest contiguous wetland area in the United States, these gains do not offset losses in other parts of the state." 

The largest fishery in the United States is directly linked to these coastal marshes, and future landing declines have been projected for it. Management suggestions include prohibiting new canal construction, plugging existing canals, controlling further wetland development, managing river flow to increase sedimentation, developing techniques to encourage land formation in dynamic areas, and stabilizing the remaining coastal barrier islands. Many of these alternatives are expensive and conflict with present flood control policies; there is often a distinct note of pessimism in many reports on the problem. 121

Additional Concerns 

Even when particular wetlands have been protected, either through public or private initiative, they often face additional pressures from the adjacent lands that drain into them. An emerging concern is the problem of contaminants in wetlands. Contaminant problems of all types on refuges were 122 recently surveyed by the Fish and Wildlife Service. While hazardous waste problems caused by industrial dumping have harmed wetlands with roughly the same frequency as other ecosystem types, another contaminant problem has been confined primarily to wetlands: pollutants from agricultural irrigation. The most serious problem arose in the Kesterson National 

Wildlife Refuge in California's San Joaquin Valley. The 1,200-acre Kester son reservoir served a dual purpose as a wildlife refuge and as an evaporation pond for a Bureau of Reclamation irrigation project. Agricultural runoff flowed into the refuge from surrounding farmland. In 1984, nesting failures and deformed young birds led to the discovery that irrigation water was leaching naturally-occurring selenium from the surrounding soil. When it concentrated in the refuge ponds, it reached toxic levels. The Department of the Interior has cooperated with the State of California in determining how to clean up the site.

Similar problems may eventually arise in other areas where irrigation water picks up toxic substances and then flows into wetland areas. Although pesticides and fertilizers may be carried in the irrigation runoff, the most bothersome chemicals are those that occur naturally at safe, low levels in soil, but are leached out by subsurface irrigation water, then concentrated in wetland vegetation. To identify at an early stage any developing problems, the Bureau of Reclamation and Fish and Wildlife Service have undertaken studies of several areas in the western states. 34

WETLANDS 

Another example is the Great Swamp National Wildlife Refuge in central New Jersey. After a long and expensive battle to prevent the area from becoming a regional jetport, the refuge was established in 1960. It currently encompasses 6,833 acres and is an important waterfowl and nongamebird nesting area; a green island in a heavily developed area, important for recreation and outdoor education. Approximately fifteen million people live within fifty miles of the refuge, and over three hundred thousand visitors use its facilities annually. The Great Swamp was originally surrounded by farms, large estates, and woodland; the intervening years have seen construction of homes, large office complexes, and shopping areas on its periphery. The results have been increased silt loads, higher and faster peak flows and floods, and greater pollution, which may have interfered with the functioning of the refuge.123 

Programs and Activities 

Private Wetland Conservation

The degree to which hunters, in the past and in the present, have contributed to wetland conservation, citizen awareness, and participation has often been unfairly underestimated in the press and by other conservation groups. Hunters, through Duck Stamp purchases, annually and directly contribute to wetland preservation. Hunting groups have been among the first to privately purchase and restore wetlands, pioneering the idea that private stewardship of the land is a vital part of wetland preservation. Ducks Unlimited In 1930, waterfowl hunters concerned with dustbowl conditions, drought, and drastically declining numbers of ducks and geese, formed More Game

Birds in America; in 1935, this group conducted one of the first international waterfowl censuses. Using new aerial survey techniques, the census revealed that the vast majority of waterfowl produced on the continent came from the Prairie Pothole regions in the Midwest and Canada. Out of these early activities grew Ducks Unlimited, founded in 1937, which has since then been active in private wetland preservation and restoration projects in the 

United States, Canada, and Mexico. Since 1938, Ducks Unlimited, now with three hundred and fifty thousand members in fourteen hundred-plus chapters, has raised over a hundred million dollars for wetland purchases, and its publicity and education programs have contributed considerably to the public's awareness of the value of wetlands. 124 Ducks Unlimited has worked in cooperation with the federal government on several projects. It donated over $1.4 million to the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund to purchase waterfowl habitat for the National Wildlife 

Refuge System." The Fish and Wildlife Service, the Bureau of Land 

125 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

85

Management, and the Forest Service recently signed an agreement with Ducks Unlimited to enhance waterfowl production on refuges, national forests, and BLM lands.126 Ducks Unlimited pledged 10 percent of its revenues for this purpose. 

The Izaak Walton League 

Even earlier in the century, in 1922, another group of sportsmen, anxious over declining water quality and other (often wetland-related) factors which were leading to the degradation of streams and rivers around the country, formed the Izaak Walton League of America. The new organization initiated the nation's first national water pollution survey, in the 1920s. With 

46,000 members by 1986, the League has become an effective voice in such issues as water quality, wetland and stream conservation, soil erosion, and acid rain.

Acting under the precept that private organizations can act more quickly than government agencies in preserving critical habitat, the League's Endowment has acquired over 6,000 acres in such areas as diverse as 

Minnesota's Boundary Waters, the Voyageurs National Park, and mangrove 

127 swamps in Florida's Biscayne Bay. The League's Chesapeake Bay

Program, established in 1982, has worked to involve business, industry, and private individuals in the effort to clean up the Bay. A recent Chesapeake 

Bay Conservation Awards program, co-sponsored with the DuPont Corporation, recognized the efforts of private citizens and organizations who have contributed time and effort to help solve our largest estuary's problems.

Another IWLA program, Wetlands Watch, works to encourage citizens and groups to adopt and protect wetlands in their local area. A Wetlands 

Adoption Kit was developed in cooperation with the annual Waterfowl Festival; it contains educational materials and tips on wetlands preservation. In addition, the League has been active in educating the public on the value of non-tidal wetlands, which compose almost ninety per cent of the wetlands in the Chesapeake region. 128 

In Oregon, League members recently participated in "Riparian Enhancement Teams," restoring riparian wetlands along streams. This fragile habitat is located on federal lands used by cattlemen, whose livestock often overgraze the edges of the streams, causing loss of vegetation and siltation. 

Fencing the streams is the obvious solution, but federal agencies lack the manpower, and often the funds, for such projects. League volunteers have recently been working on fence repair and construction, with enthusiastic response from federal land managers: such voluntary aid extends agencies' conservation budgets. The program may soon be expanded to other western states, with the aid of organizations such as the Boy Scouts, 4-H clubs, and the Future Farmers of America. 

86

WETLANDS 

While the Izaak Walton League advocates legislation and federal involvement, it has also shown the need for citizen and private sector involvement in such issues as wetland preservation. 129 

The Nature Conservancy 

Although the federal government is by far the largest landowner of the nation's nearly 2.3 billion acres of land and inland waters, over two thirds including the majority of wetlands are held by private corporations, state and local governments, individuals, and non-profit groups. The vast majority of land east of the Mississippi is in private hands. The federal entity most commonly associated with wetlands, the Fish and Wildlife Service, actually controls less than five per cent of all wetlands.130 For this reason, the fate of wetlands depends in great part upon the actions of private landowners. It is upon just such an assumption The Nature Conservancy (TNC) is motivated. 

The Nature Conservancy, whose white oak leaf logo is frequently seen on car windows, is arguably the most atypical, innovative, results-oriented private sector conservation group today. Although it generally eschews making policy statements on environmental issues, it has itself proven to be the answer in a great number of issues. Founded in 1950 although its 

132 roots go back to 1917 The Nature Conservancy has preserved over 2.8 million acres of critically endangered habitats.131 Through the help of volunteers and paid staff, it manages over 1,000 preserves nationwide, from small microhabitats of a few acres, to huge systems such as the Conservancy's Virginia Coast Preserve, which covers thirty-five thousand acres of barrier islands and marshes. Operating under the assumption that, due to global and national habitat destruction, the best examples of endangered species and habitats will be gone by the end of this century, it has gone into the business of preserving natural diversity on a vast scale. During the period 1984 through 1986, when the federal government spent 60 million dollars for the protection of endangered species, TNC spent 180 million dollars for the same purpose. 133 134 Working in cooperation with private landowners, large corporations, and local and national agencies, TNC has preserved such prime examples of declining wetland habitats as Virginia's Great Dismal Swamp, Nevada's Ash

Meadows, and critical sandhill crane nesting areas on the Platte River.¹ In 

1982, the Conservancy established the National Wetlands Conservation

Project, a five-year, $50 million project funded in part by the Richard King Mellon Foundation. The result of this initiative has been the identification of hundreds of threatened wetland systems targeted for NWCP preservation.

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

87

State Wetland Protection Programs 

135 136 Many states have enacted laws preserving their coastal areas, including coastal wetlands. The 1972 Coastal Zone Management Act gave impetus for adoption of coastal protection standards in the 35 eligible coastal states and territories." Fewer states have programs protecting inland wetlands.137 State statutes explicitly regulating inland wetlands have been adopted in Massachusetts, Connecticut, Rhode Island, Minnesota, Delaware, Michigan, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, Wisconsin,

Maine, and Florida 138 Several of these statutes authorize local governments to operate the wetland protection regulatory program with state regulation occurring only in the event of local inaction. The existing statutes using a direct regulatory approach tend to contain several common 

139 1. Statutory purposes, policies, and legislative findings of fact regarding wetland losses and the need for protection. 

2. A definition of wetlands. Regulated wetland types generally do not include all of the types identified by the Fish and Wildlife Service classification system described above (Table 1). 

3. Authorization for wetlands mapping. Although the FWS National

Wetlands Inventory (described below) is mapping wetlands throughout the country, several states are undertaking their own cooperative efforts, to augment the federal program. A detailed inventory of wetland types, sizes, and location is a valuable tool for planners and scientists. 

4. A requirement that landowners obtain permits for specified alterations of wetland areas such as draining or leveling the area. Those provisions usually include criteria for granting or denying permits, criteria for evaluating the public interest, and procedures for applying and issuing permits. 

Programs may be operated by a state natural resources agency or by local governments. 

5. Penalties for failing to obtain or comply with a permit. 

6. Exemptions for various land uses, often including agricultural drainage. 

7. Appeal procedures, either through an administrative process or judicial review.

This list of common provisions does not, however, fully describe the entire spectrum of wetlands protection approaches. Many states regulate wetlands as a component of other environmental regulatory statutes, including those applicable to floodplains or wild and scenic rivers. Other states have adopted voluntary, user-fee, or incentive-based approaches. Among the innovative approaches available to state and local governments are the following: 

1. Lower property taxes for land preserved as wetlands (or in other natural conditions). Tax incentives may include a special category of assessment for unimproved land, a tax credit for wetlands preservation, or tax credits for participating in cooperative management programs. 141

WETLANDS 

2. State hunting stamp programs, similar to the federal Migratory Bird 

Conservation Stamp program, through which hunters and other stamp purchasers finance wetland protection programs. 

3. Water allocation programs in areas where water availability is a major limiting factor for wetlands. The state may directly allocate water for wetlands, or might recognize instream flow rights for privately owned wetlands. 

4. Public involvement and education. Programs in several states provide opportunities for volunteers to enhance wetlands. 

5. Hunting laws that provide extra benefits to landowners who have enhanced private habitat." 

6. Technical assistance to landowners seeking to enhance the wildlife habitat value of their wetlands. 

7. Purchase of wetland preservation easements. Scenic or conservation easements can protect particularly valuable and sensitive wetland habitats. 

State programs for the protection of wetlands have several strengths in comparison to federal regulation. First, states generally control property taxes, which can heavily influence decisions to convert wetlands to more financially rewarding uses. Thus they can alter tax deductions and credits to favor wetlands preservation. Second, states control the allocation of water, and they are thus the proper jurisdiction to address water availability, a key aspect of wetlands preservation. Third, states are able to tailor wetland preservation programs to address local geography, wildlife, wetland types, and economic factors. The wetlands protection process is often tied to local zoning so that wetlands protection can be weighed along with other local environmental concerns.

The following sections describe selected state programs, including examples of traditional regulation, economic incentives, and voluntary action. 

Dredge and Fill Permit Programs 

The state best known for its unique wetland environments is Florida, where wetlands have been greatly altered by development. Less than fifty years ago, the state promoted massive drainage schemes. Title to 22 million acres of wetlands (almost 2/3 of the state) went to the state government under the Swamp Lands Act of 1850. Florida utilized much of this land to promote railroads, canal and drainage companies, and real estate development. 

South Florida has lost over a quarter of its wetlands since 1960.145 143 144 146 147 Florida's first statute governing dredge and fill activities in navigable waters was enacted in 1957. A second statute, the Florida Environmental 

Air and Water Pollution Control Act,' was enacted in 1969, and regulated water quality in navigable and non-navigable waters. A new statute, the 

Warren S. Henderson Wetlands Protection Act of 1984148 was enacted to explicitly protect wetlands.

The new law parallels the standard regulatory approach outlined above and, in many ways, is similar to the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers Section 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

89

150 

404 program.

The State and Corps cooperate in processing of dredge and fill permits. There is a joint application process, although regulatory reviews proceed independently. The new law attempts to clarify regulatory juris- 151 diction and expands it to approximately 65 percent more wetlands. A specific determination of public interest is part of the decision-making process. Factors to be considered include public health and safety, endangered or threatened species, conservation of fish and wildlife, erosion or shoaling, fishing or recreational values, marine productivity, and impact on the property of others. 152 Most of the regulatory program is administered by the Florida Department of Environmental Regulation. Agricultural activities are generally exempt from this program, but are regulated through the State's water management districts. The statute contains several unique provisions, including one governing mangroves, and one allowing discharge of partially treated wastewater into certain wetlands to demonstrate that wetlands can 153 purify wastewater without impairing biological functions. 154 

The Delaware Wetlands Act, enacted in 1973, is also a standard dredge and fill permit program. However, it contains a provision designed to help alleviate excessive impacts of regulation on landowners. The law specifically provides for court review of permit denials and other actions of the Department of Natural Resources and Environmental Control. If the court finds that the action takes the landowner's property without just compensation, it will invalidate the order. 150 The Department is given the opportunity to acquire or to condemn a fee simple or lesser interest in the property. If it does so, then the court decision does not take effect. If the 

Department fails to acquire the land within two years, the permit must be granted.

Federal-State Cooperation 

Since federal and state wetland programs are often similar or even overlapping, cooperation clearly makes sense. For applicants, coordinated permit processing can eliminate duplicative paperwork and speed processing time.

States may assume control of the Corps of Engineers 404 program, if ap proved by the U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA). The state program must be at least as strict as the federal program although it can have a greater scope than required by federal law. As with Corps of Engineers permitting, the federal EPA reviews and may object to any dredge and fill permit application. The only state that has fully assumed primacy for the 404 program is Michigan. 157 158 Federal grants and technical assistance programs assist state wetland programs. Described elsewhere in this chapter are the National Wetlands 

Inventory and Land and Water Conservation Fund programs. Under sec tion 306 of the Coastal Zone Management Act, the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration may provide grants for tidal wetland 90

WETLANDS 

programs. For example, Washington used such a grant to establish a wetland office that provides expertise for local governments and state agencies. 

It has assisted in wetlands inventory and mapping, assessing project impacts, wetland creation and rehabilitation, scientific research, and public education 159 160 Unfortunately, cooperation does not always come easily. In North 

Dakota wetlands issues have centered largely on federal/state relations in the context of the waterfowl production area program, administered by FWS. Under the program, FWS may purchase easements over privately owned wetlands with funding from the Migratory Bird Conservation

Fund. The program required state approval prior to acquisition of land. 

In 1961, the governor of North Dakota approved acquisition of over 1.2 million acres of wetlands easements for waterfowl production areas, but reserved the right to individually review fee acquisitions.161 In later years, the scope of gubernatorial approval became the subject of litigation, which was finally resolved by a U.S. Supreme Court decision in favor of the United States. 162 The easement contracts utilized by the Fish and Wildlife Service required that grantors would not drain, fill or level the land. If they did so, the remedy was a civil action, usually to enjoin future violations. Enactment of the National Wildlife Refuge System Administration Act¹63 in 1966 changed the legal atmosphere. The waterfowl production areas were incorporated into the National Wildlife Refuge System. Easement violations on privately owned land became crimes and were subject to criminal penalties. 104 Litigation between landowners and FWS became, and has remained, a frequent occurrence.

Other sources of friction between North Dakota and the federal government have been federal payment of in-lieu taxes, the Garrison Water Diversion project, and ambiguities in determining the exact acreages covered by federal easements." As a result, federal acquisition of acreage in the prairie pothole region has proceeded slowly. An effort to resolve the federal/state difficulties continues, to ensure protection of the important waterfowl breeding grounds of the prairie pothole region. 165 

State Habitat Stamps 

166 Nebraska has two major wetland areas: the Sandhills and the Rainwater 

Basin. Since 1950, 90 percent of the wetlands in the latter area have been destroyed. Over 2,000 landowners are participating in a cost-sharing program between the state government and private landowners to create new habitat and preserve existing habitat. The program is funded by the state "habitat stamp" program. Under this program, Nebraska hunters are required to purchase a stamp in addition to their hunting licenses, with funds used to acquire critical wildlife habitat. 167 Funds from the Habitat

Stamp program are also used for state land acquisition. 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

91

Water Allotment 

An important component of wetlands preservation is the availability of an adequate supply of water suited to an area's ecological needs. Texas recently faced the problem of protecting a flow of fresh water to its bays and estuaries. In 1985, the state legislature adopted the Omnibus Water Bill.168 

The Bill is designed to protect the flow of fresh water to bays and estuaries through three mechanisms: (1) conditioning permits for water appropriations near the coast; (2) undertaking scientific studies of estuarine systems; and (3) appropriating 5 percent of the water yields of new reservoirs near the coast, built with state financial assistance, to provide water for bays and estuaries. 169 

Tax Incentives

Forty-four states offer some form of preferential tax treatment for land in agricultural, open space, forest, or recreational uses. Although many of these statutes may be applied to wetland areas, wetland protection is 170 generally not an express statutory objective. Iowa, Connecticut, Michigan, and Washington offer tax abatement expressly for landowners who preserve wetlands.171

A non-regulatory, economic-based approach to wetlands protection has been adopted in Minnesota." The Minnesota program exempts qualifying wetlands from local property taxes and has established a tax credit procedure.173 To qualify for a property tax exemption, wetlands must generally be over 2-1/2 acres, be preserved in their natural condition, and not be used except for wildlife or water conservation purposes. Wetlands meeting this definition are automatically exempted from property tax by the county. 

Taxpayers may also participate in a voluntary tax credit program if they agree not to drain the wetland during the year the tax credit is received. 174 175 The Minnesota Water Bank Program provides compensation to landowners who have been denied permission to drain wetlands for agricultural purposes. Under the program, the State Department of Natural Resources offers qualifying landowners several options including outright purchase for management by the State Division of Fish and Wildlife as a wildlife management area (where the land will be open to public access and hunting); purchase of a permanent easement restricting drainage, cropping, grazing, and pasturing; or participating in a 10-year water bank agreement in which the landowner agrees not to drain or fill the wetland or to crop, pasture, graze, or burn the area. Other custom-designed compensation agreements can be negotiated. On the average, two projects per month have been completed under the Water Bank Program. 176 177 92

WETLANDS Public Education and Involvement 

Many states have programs to educate the public about wetland values, and public participation is frequently a component of other wetland programs. 

Connecticut and Vermont publish state newsletters highlighting wetland is- 178 sues. 179 Kentucky is involving the public in wetlands protection through its Water 

Watch program, patterned after North Carolina's Stream Watch program and similar to the Izaak Walton League's privately operated program described above. Water Watch enhances public awareness of wetland values, using the program's mascot, "Ollie Otter." An important activity is encouraging community groups (such as schools, environmental groups, garden clubs, sportmen's clubs, scouts, and industry) to “adopt” a wetland. The Kentucky Division of Water provides groups training and information about water quality indicators, macroinvertebrate identification, sampling procedures, and wetland processes. Groups are then asked to perform watershed studies, identify problems associated with their wetlands, and monitor water quality changes. In the words of a Division of Water representative, the Water Watch program will "promote individual responsibility for a common resource; enhance citizen understanding and support; and communicate the importance of environmental quality in attracting industry and tourism."180

Federal Wetland Protection Programs 

On the federal level, criticisms that the Clean Water Act's Section 404 permit program intruded upon private property rights and created processing delays, while government land acquisition programs suffered from budget scarcities, led wetlands advocates to seek alternative protection strategies. 

Congress in recent years has passed several pieces of legislation that, evaluated together, create a comprehensive wetlands protection program. 

To a large extent, these programs adopt a promising new approach to wetlands preservation, influencing private-sector decisionmaking by removing economic biases toward destruction. A particularly attractive feature of the expenditure-limitation approach is that it ensures wise use of federal resources by curtailing programs that subsidized environmentally destructive activity. These new developments accomplish the following: • Halt most uses of agricultural subsidies to destroy wetlands. • Eliminate several tax deductions or credits that encouraged wetlands degradation. • Initiate admission fees for wildlife refuges. • Substantially increase revenues devoted to federal and state wetland acquisition.

With these measures in place, enhanced wetlands protection should be achievable without an expansion of regulatory programs. 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

93

Economic Expenditure Limitation Approaches 

■ Swampbuster:181 As described above, the leading cause of wetlands destruction is agriculture. The 404 permit program does not apply to many agricultural conversions, since the Clean Water Act exempts normal agricultural and silvicultural operations from the requirements of a 404 permit. Agricultural wetlands loss was exacerbated by agricultural programs that encouraged farmers to put wetlands and other marginally productive acreage into production. 

The "Swampbuster" provision of the Food Security Act, approved on 

December 23, 1985, dramatically changed the incentive structure presented by agricultural programs. Swampbuster derives its unusual name from its political antecedent, a "sodbuster" provision that discouraged converting highly erodible land to cropland.182 While this provision does not prevent agricultural wetlands conversion, and does not directly regulate private activity, it does reverse the long-term trend of subsidizing wetlands conversion. 

Under the Swampbuster provision, any farmer who produces crops on newly converted wetlands is ineligible for participation in the major farmer assistance programs administered by the Agricultural Stabilization and 

Conservation Service, the Commodity Credit Corporation, the Farmers Home Administration, the Federal Crop Insurance Corporation, and the 

Soil Conservation Service. Farmers may not participate in the affected programs if they drain, dredge, fill, level or otherwise manipulate wetlands for the purpose of making crop production possible. The law allows subsidized crop production under some circumstances, for example where natural droughts allow seasonal wetlands to be cropped without permanently destroying their wetland values.

The program is beneficial not only because it saves wetlands, but also because it reduces crop surpluses. The denial of agricultural benefits associated with prohibitions on wetlands conversion went into effect for the 

1987 crop year. 183 ■ Coastal Barrier Resources Act: 184 This 1982 legislation was "the first comprehensive attempt to link federal fiscal policy with natural resource conservation.»>185 

Upon signing the measure into law, President Reagan praised it as "precisely the sort of imaginative environmental legislation this administration encourages." ,,186

During the 1960's and 70's, the federal treasury was frequently called upon to assist homeowners in rebuilding barrier island homes destroyed by storms and hurricanes. Although the human health risk and economic folly of this circular process were apparent, federal money was also being spent to encourage construction of new homes on the shifting sands of the barrier islands. These fragile and unique areas were being degraded through fiscally and environmentally unwise subsidies. The Act breaks the cycle by relying 94

WETLANDS 

on economic realities to deter most unsound new residential and commercial development.

The Coastal Barrier Resources Act prohibits expenditure of federal funds to develop new areas of the barrier islands extending along the Atlantic coast. It also provides protection to substantial areas of wetlands adjacent to coastal barrier islands. Specifically, the Act prohibits or restricts new federal assistance that encourages residential and commercial construction, including flood187 and disaster insurance, highway and airport construction, water and sewer systems, housing and business loans, and shoreline erosion projects. Section 10 of the Act requires the Secretary of the Interior to report on the System and include recommendations on additions to, or deletions from, the System and for modifications to the boundaries of existing units, as well as recommendations for the conservation of the fish, wildlife, and other natural resources of the System. Congressional action is necessary for any changes to occur in the existing System. 188 Department of the Interior Study: Further extensions and refinements of the expenditures limitation approach are being studied. The Emergency 

Wetlands Resources Act requires the Secretary of the Interior to prepare a report reviewing federal statutes, regulatory mechanisms, expenditures, tax provisions, and financial assistance, to determine which programs induce wetlands degradation and which ones protect and enhance wetlands.1 The study is also required to evaluate and compare all management alternatives, including federal, state, and local actions, and initiatives by private organizations and individuals.

Executive Order 11990: This 1977 Executive Order requires federal agencies to minimize destruction of wetlands in federal land management and disposal, federally financed construction, and federal programs affecting land and water use. It does not extend to permits or licenses for private parties' activities on non-federal property. E.O. 11990 is implemented through separate regulations or guidance documents for each agency, utilizing existing authorities. The primary mechanism for monitoring compliance is the budget process. OMB Circular A-11 requires federal agencies to submit an annual certificate of compliance with E.O. 11990. 189 

Changes in Tax Provisions Affecting Wetlands Destruction 

Several portions of the Internal Revenue Code directly or indirectly encouraged landowners to destroy wetlands. This has been particularly significant in the agricultural sector. Tax deductions and credits that have distorted the economic incentives for destruction of wetlands include: • deductions for land clearing costs ⚫ deductions for drainage expenses ⚫ credit for the installation of drainage tile

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

95

• deductions for depreciation on capital investments, including equipment, needed for draining and farming wetlands ⚫ deductions for interest payments on loans used to convert wetlands to cropland.

The 1986 Tax Reform Act changed many of the tax considerations in wetlands drainage. The Act repealed or amended several of these deductions as part of an overall movement toward "flattening" taxes. It also added a provision specifically addressing conversion of wetlands for agricultural uses. The new tax provision complements the swampbuster program by denying tax advantages to those who sell converted wetlands. Specifically, converted wetlands sales are denied capital gains treatment; profits are taxed at the higher rate applicable to ordinary income. 190 Another significant tax law provision facilitates preservation of wetlands by conservation groups. As described elsewhere in this chapter, several private organizations, including the Nature Conservancy and Ducks Unlimited, have preserved millions of acres of wetlands through acquisition in fee or by easement. Gifts of privately owned acreage account for a significant amount of their acquisitions. Likewise, federal and state governments sometimes receive donations of wetlands. For example, the 

Prudential Insurance Company recently transferred 120,000 acres of wetlands at Alligator River in North Carolina to the federal government for preservation. Federal policies, particularly tax policies, can influence the effectiveness of efforts to obtain contributions of wetlands. Internal 

Revenue Code section 170192 allows a charitable deduction for donation of conservation easements to qualified conservation organizations. The 

Treasury Department published final interpretative regulations in 1986." 

The statute and regulations make clear that qualifying donations of lessthan-fee interests can be deducted. 193 

Refuge Entrance Fees 

194 A significant new development is the imposition of entrance fees at many FWS wildlife refuges. In areas where fee collection is administratively practical, visitors will be asked to pay an entrance fee unless they hold Duck 

Stamps or National Park Service Golden Eagle passports." Revenues col lected from refuge entrance fees will be divided between refuge operating expenses and land acquisition. The formula allocates 30 percent for operation and maintenance of the collecting refuge unit and the remainder of the refuge system, and 70 percent to the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund for land acquisition. The revenue base for wetlands acquisition has also been expanded to include a tariff on imported firearms. 195 Beyond their obvious budgetary impacts, entrance fees are important for two reasons. First, entrance fees allocate the cost of maintaining lands to those who use them. This is consistent with the historical tradition of wet- 96

WETLANDS 

lands preservation, particularly including the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund, which reflects a user fee approach. 

Second, refuge fees should expand recreational opportunities on private land. While the right of access to private hunting lands may be something for which hunters will (and do) pay, the availability of "free" public hunting areas discourages use of private lands. If public lands are not available for free, then private landowners may be more able to charge fees for recreational use of their wetlands. Private hunting clubs and hunting leases can be more competitive. This will create an incentive for additional private preservation activities, since there will be more economic reward for preservation. The potential scope of the reward is substantial: FWS estimated that in 1980, 5.3 million people hunted migratory birds, spending over $600 million, 197

This factor, coupled with innovative state hunting law changes allowing private landowners to capture more of the benefits of wildlife production, should enhance incentives to manage private lands for wildlife. 196 In addition to refuge entrance fees, some refuges also charge user fees for certain specified recreation activities. According to the 1986 Federal 

Recreation Fee Report, 19 refuge units charged recreation user fees for such activities as use of a facility (e.g., a duck blind), ORV permits, trapping, etc. Total collections at that time were $137,000. For FY 1987, this figure has increased by more than $100,000. 

Land Acquisition Programs 

The Office of Technology Assessment estimates that 10 million acres of wetland in the lower 48 states are subject to federal ownership, easement, or lease, together with another 29 million acres owned by the federal government in Alaska.198 Government land acquisition has long formed the backbone of wetlands preservation efforts, particularly through the Migratory 

Bird Conservation Fund program. Better known as the "Duck Stamp" 

program, the fund recently celebrated its 50th successful year of operation. 

It is funded by the most direct beneficiaries of waterfowl protection efforts - hunters. Specifically, hunters are required to purchase stamps before hunting migratory waterfowl. Duck stamps thus fall into the broad category of user fees, since the beneficiaries, not taxpayers at large, fund the program. Hunters have recognized the benefits of the program, and tend to support it enthusiastically. Another component of the program has been creatively utilized to benefit wildlife programs: an annual art contest to select the stamp's design, and subsequent marketing of spin-off products, provides additional revenue. 199 

Through the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund, the United States has acquired 3.66 million acres of wetlands for inclusion in FWS wildlife refuges. (Table 7.) Acquisition plans are approved by the Migratory Bird 

Conservation Commission, and are accomplished on a willing seller basis.

Since 1934, Duck Stamp revenues have totaled $326.6 million through FY 87. 201 A specialized program funded through the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund is the Waterfowl Production Area Program. Other sources of funding for wetlands for inclusion in the National Wildlife Refuge System are refuge entrance fees203 and the Land and Water 204 Conservation Fund Act of 1964.1 The latter is funded by taxes on motorboat fuels, sale of excess government property, and Outer Continental Shelf oil revenues. Between 1965 and 1982, 61,585 acres were purchased in this manner. 

Other grant programs for state purchases are the Federal Aid to Wildlife Restoration Act (Pittman-Robertson Act), through which states acquired over 3.5 million acres of wildlife habitat, and the Federal Aid in Fish Restoration Act (Dingell-Johnson and Wallop-Breaux Acts). Although these programs do not emphasize wetlands acquisition, some wetlands may be purchased as habitat. In an effort both to preserve wetlands and to reduce the number of acres in agricultural production, the Department of Agriculture leases waterfowl habitat wetlands from farmers for ten-year periods. 

The main focus of the Water Bank program 205 has been on prairie pothole wetlands in the northern Great Plains.206 The program is authorized to spend $30 million per year. The Conservation Reserve program, established in the 1985 Food Security Act, 208 is expected to preserve significant amounts of wetlands along with other undeveloped lands. 207 

The Emergency Wetlands Resources Act adds several new sources of revenue for wetlands acquisition.

The Act encourages states to utilize

WETLANDS 

money from the Land and Water Conservation Fund for purchasing wetlands in addition to recreation lands. As noted above, it transfers revenues from a tax on imported firearms to the Migratory Bird Conservation Fund and it specifies that the bulk of refuge entrance fees will be used for acquisition. Finally, the bill forgives a loan from the Land and Water Conservation 

Fund to the Migratory Bird Conservation fund. Taken together, these funds amount to additional tens of millions of dollars for wetlands. 209 210 A significant question is whether wetlands acquisition programs have successfully targeted the most valuable wetlands. The point is addressed in the Emergency Wetlands Resources Act, which establishes a national wetlands priority planning mechanism." A draft priority plan was released in 

September 1987. The Secretary of the Interior, in consultation with the heads of the Environmental Protection Agency, the Department of Agricul ture, and the Department of Commerce and the governors of each state, will soon finalize the plan, identifying the wetland types or regions that should have highest priority in federal and state acquisition efforts. This plan is to consider (1) wildlife, (2) fisheries, (3) surface and groundwater quality and flood control, (4) outdoor recreation, (5) the threat of future losses to particular wetland types, and (6) the extent to which various wetland types have declined from historic levels. Wetlands must also be considered in the State 

Comprehensive Outdoor Recreation Plan, which guides allocation of Land and Water Conservation Fund money by the states.

Other Federal Initiatives 

In May 1986, the Secretary of the Interior and the Canadian Minister of the 

Environment signed the North American Waterfowl Management Plan. The 

Plan builds upon international cooperation initiated in 1916 with signing of the Convention for the Protection of Migratory Birds. Since that time, government and private sector efforts in the two countries have made significant strides: [Millions of acres of wetlands have been saved from destruction, a large chain of waterfowl refuges spanning the flyways has been established, treaties and laws protecting waterfowl have been enacted and rigidly enforced, thousands of research projects have been undertaken, disease outbreaks have been fought, and millions of dollars have been expended to preserve and manage waterfowl habitat across the continent." 223 The Plan recognizes challenges facing not just the respective national governments, but also individuals, private conservation and hunting organizations, and state, provincial, and territorial governments. Achieving waterfowl protection goals is a task too large and too costly to be borne by national governments alone.224 The Plan emphasizes cooperative planning, coordinated management, and public/private joint ventures to address waterfowl issues such as habitat conservation, harvest management, environmental pollution, diseases, scientific research, and long-term downward trends in some duck populations.

An important role that has been played by the federal government is compiling and disseminating information about wetlands. The key informational program is the National Wetlands Inventory, led by FWS, which is comprehensively mapping the nation's wetlands. Often compiled in cooperation with state and local governments, the resulting maps are useful in regulation, land acquisition, scientific trends analysis, and other protection programs. Thus far, FWS has completed an inventory of wetlands in 46 percent of the contiguous U.S. and 12 percent of Alaska. Mapping has been completed in eight states and is in progress in 39 more. The bulk of mapping is to be completed by 1998. Other federal statutes that encourage wetlands preservation include the Coastal Zone Management 

Act, the Endangered Species Act, the Fish and Wildlife Coordination Act, and the National Environmental Policy Act.

WETLANDS 

Major Issues to be Resolved

Legal Developments Affecting Wetlands Regulation 

Thirteen years after passage of the Clean Water Act's wetlands protection provision, Section 404, the United States Supreme Court heard the first case challenging its validity. In United States v. Riverside Bayview Homes, Inc." the Army Corps of Engineers sued a Michigan developer to halt filling of a wetland near Lake St. Clair.

The Court rejected the developer's claims that the 404 program suffered from constitutional difficulties. The Court considered two arguments: (1) that the Clean Water Act regulated only those waters traditionally navigable, and (2) that the 404 program unconstitutionally took private property without just compensation.

Congressional power over navigable waters has been defined in a multitude of cases stretching back to the 18th Century. Although Congress used the term "navigable waters" in the Clean Water Act, the Court ruled that Congress did not mean pollution control measures under Section 404 to be limited to navigable waters as traditionally defined. Instead, Congress relied in part on the commerce power. Accordingly, non-navigable wetlands with water flowing to or from navigable water may be regulated. However, the 

Court left one significant question unanswered: whether Congress empowered the Corps to regulate isolated wetlands that are not connected to open waters." 233 The second argument, based on the Fifth Amendment's prohibition against taking private property without just compensation, has frequently arisen in wetlands regulation. Taking was raised as a defense to wetlands regulation even prior to establishment of the Clean Water Act's 404 program. Courts have expressed differing views about the standards to be applied in determining whether particular wetlands regulations presented constitutional difficulties, although most cases have found wetlands regula- 235 tions not to be takings." 234 The takings questions received considerable attention from the Court of Appeals in the Riverside litigation, which held that the Corps regulations needed to be read narrowly to avoid unconstitutionally taking private property. The Supreme Court rejected this view. Rather than declaring the entire program unconstitutional, or confining it within narrow bounds, the Court required an examination of takings questions on a case-by-case basis. 

The government must compensate owners if regulation, as applied in a particular case, is so severe that it forbids a landowner from making productive use of his property. A taking requiring compensation occurs "when a permit is denied and the effect of the denial is to prevent ‘economically viable' use of the land in question.

The Court did not decide whether the Michigan developer's land had been so severely restricted that compensation was required. ,,236 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

101

# ## ## # # **== th Wh 

Another case, First English Evangelical Lutheran Church v. Los An geles, 237 reiterated the need to determine whether "economically viable" use of property is prevented by regulation. In a context closely related to wetlands regulation, the Court held that a floodplain regulation that prevented any construction was a taking for which compensation was required. A third recent Supreme Court case, Nollan v. California Coastal Commission, found a taking where the California Coastal Commission required a public access easement as a condition for obtaining a building permit. 238 The Riverside case, in conjunction with the First English and Nollan decisions, has implications for future wetlands protection strategies. If regulators require conservation easements or similar conveyances of property in conjunction with dredge and fill permits, the easement must satisfy the stringent nexus requirement established in Nollan. The easement requirement must serve the same government purpose as the permit process.

Although the overall 404 program survived a challenge to its constitutionality, government agencies cannot rely on stringent regulation to always be a cost-free method of protecting wetlands. Where denial of a wetlands permit deprives an owner of viable economic returns from his property, compensation must be paid. In extreme cases, fee acquisition may be necessary. In less extreme cases, compensation may not be constitutionally required, but may be wise legislative policy in order to assure that no single individual is forced to bear the burden of a societal decision to preserve wetlands.

Land Acquisition Programs and Budgetary Constraints 

Land acquisition programs have drawbacks. Most obviously, the high price of land acquisition limits the scale of the program, and may not be as costeffective as other protection mechanisms. 

As a recent report by a Senate Committee put it:

America is facing critical financial challenges that require us to make hard choices among many worthwhile programs throughout the Federal Government. With respect to wetlands, we face the need to balance this important program with other programs vital to the Nation's well-being. 239 Hidden costs beyond the purchase price also escalate the burden on taxpayers. Land management personnel must be paid Civil Service salaries and retirement checks. Federal land management planning processes are expensive and time-consuming, limiting the land manager's flexibility and responsiveness. Privately operated wetland preservation organizations such as duck clubs can produce the same environmental benefits at no cost to taxpayers, and may even pay taxes. 102

WETLANDS 

In a nation where a third of the land is owned by the government, additional federal land acquisition for environmental protection seems anomalous. The problem is that the federal government's holdings are not necessarily the most environmentally sensitive lands. Western arid lands were retained in federal ownership, while wetlands were transferred to the private sector as potentially arable. Creative proposals have recently surfaced to exchange existing federal landholdings for environmentally valuable lands. For example, the Department of the Interior proposed a land exchange that will trade 69 acres of land at a surplus Bureau of Indian Affairs property in Phoenix, Arizona, for 108,000 acres of wetlands in south Florida, and $39 million in cash. The exchange is likely to be ratified by Congres this year. The land acquired for protection at minimal expense to taxpayers would include habitat of the endangered Florida panther." 240 Another method for minimizing the cost to taxpayers of land acquisition is to acquire only the minimum interest in land necessary to preserve wetland values. More wetlands area may be protected for a smaller near-term cost through easements or leases. Conservation easements prevent development and other activities that destroy wetlands. Specialized provisions may be negotiated, like what and when crops may be grown, public access, and other management arrangements. Easements may be of limited duration, or permanently transfer an interest in the land. The exact interest to be acquired depends in part on the government's plans for the land. If frequent public use or intensive habitat manipulation is necessary, then fee acquisition may be preferable.

Problems with easement acquisition have also surfaced. Litigation seeking to void perpetual conservation easements has occurred in some instances. States and local governments have been frustrated because their eminent domain authority does not extend to federal lands, even where the federal interest is just an easement. Federal responsibility for hazardous wastes found on easement lands has not been clearly determined. For the most part, however, conservation easements provide landowners with economic incentives to preserve wetlands and thus counterbalance economic pressures to develop. 

Wetland Creation

Although new wetlands have been created incidentally by gravel and quarry operations, highway construction, dredge and fill operations, and other activities, the potential now exists for the deliberate creation and restoration of certain wetland types. To date, the large majority of new habitats are ecotone wetlands created by farm pond construction." 

241 The Corps of En gineers has recently been investigating the possibilities of coastal and riverine wetland creation in the disposition of dredged material produced in dredging operations. 242

陪​科 ​10 Promising techniques exist for the large scale creation of coastal marshes along the east and west coasts, and have been used successfully to stabilize shorelines in North Carolina and Georgia. In Florida, studies have also been conducted on the feasibility of mangrove swamp creation and on reclamation of wetlands affected by phosphate surface mining. 243 244 The value of mitigation requiring the creation or purchase of additional wetlands to replace those lost during development remains a controversial subject, and the process has met with mixed success around the country." 

Mitigation in some cases may simply require a developer to purchase and preserve another wetland for the one being destroyed or altered, resulting in a net wetland loss. In other cases, mitigation may require project modifications to minimize the direct loss, or more intensive management of remaining wetlands to increase their productivity and value.

The Fish and Wildlife Service has been experimenting with the concept of mitigation banking, so small contributions can be consolidated into larger projects. Mitigation banking involves the receipt of habitat units of credit, determined by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service's Habitat Evaluation 

Program for wetlands enhancement work performed in advance of a permit application, which can later be used to fulfill mitigation requirements imposed by regulatory agencies. This concept is being field tested by Tenneco, Inc. on 7,200 acres of the Tenneco LaTerre property near Houma, Louisiana under a 1984 Memorandum of Agreement among the private landowner, three federal and two state agencies." 245 246 Many states are now requiring developer-funded mitigation, and some, such as Oregon, insist that there be no net loss. 'It seems likely that mitigation will be used with increasing frequency in the future, particularly in heavily developed areas of the country. 

Looking to the Future 

An abundance of land resources are designated for protection: the scenic beauty of national parks, the undisturbed serenity of wilderness forests, the wildness of untamed rivers, the unique ecology of endangered species habitat, the aesthetic desirability of urban open space, the fragility of coastal barrier islands. Yet not every tract of undeveloped land can be considered sacred without stopping human progress. Not every hill deserves the same reverent protection as Mount Rainier or Mount McKinley; not every marsh merits the preservation afforded the Everglades. Lands must somehow be ranked and valued, and the value for competing uses must be compared. 

Ideally, the mix of environmental preservation and economic development will be balanced so that the final overall valuation is maximized. As a society, we would be best off if each piece of land were used for its most highly valued use, be that a national park or a steel mill. Factories would be constructed in the areas best suited for industrial development, while con- 104

WETLANDS 

servation uses would preserve the most ecologically and aesthetically valuable lands.

One potential route for accomplishing this is a massive planning effort. A comprehensive research and study effort, a vast number of variables fed into a computer, and a powerful land use code could produce a nationwide land management plan imposing some conception of resource optimization. This scenario carries current environmental protection policies to their logical culmination.

Economic theory, however, has revealed that central planning is theoretically and practically incapable of accomplishing the complex value weighing that it would be called upon to perform.247 No computer and no planning office could handle the necessary range of variables, not because the planners would be incompetent, but because they would need to assess each individual person's preferences to determine a nationwide set of preferences. 

An even more disturbing difficulty with comprehensive planning is that private property and economic freedoms must necessarily yield to centrally planned directives.

Another mechanism avoids the dilemma created by central planning. 

Markets are mechanisms for moving resources to their most highly valued uses, and the pricing system is the most practical way of comparing values. 

Of course, environmental pollution has often been referred to as a "market failure" because the market does not adequately account for externalities. 

Unless a market can internalize costs and benefits, environmental amenities will be undervalued. The accuracy of market forces in valuing natural resources is also affected by distortions introduced by government policies. 

The question is whether further advances in protecting scarce wetland resources should come through regulation, through government land acquisition, or through removing distortions that hamper operation of beneficial market forces. At the very least, in times of severe budgetary constraints, responsible policymaking requires a consideration of whether programs may be working at cross-purposes, and whether alternatives that save taxpayers money may reasonably be substituted for costly regulatory programs. Furthermore, even with vast losses of wetland acreage, wetlands currently cover five percent of the land area of the 48 contiguous states, an area roughly the size of California.248 Eliminating government subsidies that have, in the past, unnecessarily encouraged wetlands destruction will not save every wetland acre. But it will influence private decisionmaking enough to protect aggregate wetland acreage.

Policymakers have begun to realize that wetlands destruction is often caused or subsidized by government policies. In response, recent legislation has abolished some overtly destructive aspects of federal programs and has thus significantly altered the government's role in wetlands preservation decisions. As described elsewhere, the Swampbuster provision, changes in the Internal Revenue Code, and successful operation of the Coastal Barrier Resources System, limit federal subsidies for wetlands destruction. 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

105

H This Administration has consistently supported eliminating subsidies that unnecessarily encourage destruction of wetlands. In 1983, the Administra tion proposed the Protect Our Wetland and Duck Resources Act 

(POWDR), which would have prohibited certain federal expenditures in wetlands of five or more acres with significant environmental values. The 

POWDR bill was a first step toward limiting destructive expenditures, and formed the basis for subsequent legislation. 

A federal expenditure prohibition internalizes costs of wetlands destruction. Economic rationality and environmental protection may often be compatible, if they are allowed to operate without the distortions produced by federal expenditures. [U]nburdened by acquisition costs, regulatory constraints, and planning intervention, the expenditures limitation approach avoids many of the political and legal pitfalls of more traditional government efforts to conserve natural resources. 250 Recent movements toward the use of market forces and voluntary arrangements show that wetlands protection can be enhanced while adhering to the principles of a conservative Administration. Among the important principles that should guide our policies are the following: • Restriction should not lightly be placed on individual economic freedoms and private property rights. This principle is based on the central importance to American political values of the right to freely engage in productive activity, and also based on an assumption that private landowners must bear the long-term consequences of their land-use decisions and therefore are generally good stewards of their land. The bulk of wetlands (87 million out of 95 million acres, excluding Alaska) are on private property, so wetlands preservation is largely a problem of private land use. 251 • Private landowners should not be unduly burdened with a land use restriction that benefits the general public. Just compensation should be paid for land when regulation prevents reasonable development. • The costs of programs should generally be borne by those who enjoy the benefits. Hunters, fishermen, birdwatchers, recreationists, and others who derive significant benefits from wetlands should pay for wetlands preservation through user-type fees, private programs, and area entrance fees. • Private sector initiatives should play a major role in environmental protection, relying on the demonstrated ability of private organizations to protect land. The Nature Conservancy, Ducks 106

WETLANDS 

We are becoming increasingly aware of the value of wetlands - as habitat for waterfowl, fish and other wildlife, for recreation, as a part of the cycle for water purification and groundwater recharge, and for aesthetic purposes. Wise decisions about wetlands preservation, management, and conversion to other uses will need careful stewardship by the federal government of the wetlands it owns, innovative policies by local and state governments to influence the multitude of private decisions affecting wetlands, and reliable scientific information. Voluntary private efforts can play an important role in educating the public, informing landowners about how to manage wetlands to improve their habitat value, and implementing wetland management programs. Using the power of economic incentives, voluntary initiative, and wise stewardship, we can achieve the proper balance between preservation and development. 2.

Marine Sanctuaries
