When we go down to the low-tide line, we enter a world that is as old as the earth itself - the primeval meeting place of the elements of earth and water, a place of compromise and conflict and eternal change. 

Rachel Carson 

The Edge of the Sea 

From the early days of the Republic, the United States has had a strong national interest in its coastal waters for commerce, navigation, and defense. 

Dependent on the ocean and ships for defense against its enemies and for importation of necessary goods, this young government had to appropriate some of its scarce economic resources to chart the coastal waters so that ships could be assured of safe passage. 

However, federal claims of national authority over ocean and coastal waters would not be a major issue for a hundred years. It was commonly understood by federal and state governments that the submerged lands and resources of the three-mile territorial sea were "owned" by the coastal states, and that most uses within the three-mile limit could be regulated by the states. During most of the nineteenth century the federal government had all it could handle defending itself from foreign threats and expanding to new territories to the west.

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

117

LUMPOUND WIT 

+! uniläIR

As coastal states became more populated, public works projects proliferated in established harbors and heavily travelled watercourses, often impeding interstate commerce. In 1899, the Congress passed the Rivers and 

Harbors Act (30 Stat 1151-1153) giving the Army Corps of Engineers the authority to approve plans for the construction of bridges, dams, dikes, and causeways, and prohibiting any obstruction to the navigable capacity of any waters of the United States unless approved by Congress. 

This early but important piece of legislation paved the way for enhancing federal management and regulation of ocean/coastal waters in this century, and provided a means for addressing specific problems or opportunities. 

Until the 1920s, there were few major disagreements over whether states could regulate coastal waters. But, as improved marine technology provided economic profits from extraction of offshore oil and gas, the significance of potential revenues from control of submerged lands loomed very large. 

Regulation of oil and gas deposits became a concern of the federal government and the focus of many disputes involving state versus federal rights. In three landmark decisions (U.S. v. California, 1947, 332 U.S. 19; U.S. v. Texas, 1950, 339 U.S. 707; U.S. v. Louisiana, 1950, 339 U.S. 699), the U.S. Supreme

Court held that the federal government, rather than the states, has paramount rights in the submerged lands and natural resources of the territorial seas of the United States.

However, offshore oil and gas extraction was not the only concern of the federal government in the first half of this century. By the mid-1930s, the 

United States was experiencing growing pressures on its offshore fishing stocks, especially from the Japanese who conducted intensive salmon fishing operations off Alaska. In 1937, President Roosevelt stated that every nation has the right to protect its own food supply in waters adjacent to its coast. 

He considered issuing a Presidential proclamation closing the sea area along the Alaskan coast to all fishing to create "a kind of marine refuge where one is essential to end depletion (of the resource)." 

President Roosevelt indicated directly to Under Secretary of State Sum ner Welles that what he had in mind was an Executive proclamation by the President, declaring that on account of the peculiar scientific conditions which exist with regard to the habits of salmon and which consequently affect the salmon fisheries industry, the waters of the Pacific Ocean between the three-mile limit and that point of the ocean bed where the water reaches a depth of 100 fathoms, must be considered as territorial waters indispensable to the proper safeguarding of this important portion of the food supply of the American people.

He stated that he had under consideration the proclamation of these waters as territorial waters of the ... 118

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

United States, and as a national game preserve within which no fishing, whether American or of any other nationality, could be undertaken, except under the prior issuance of a license permitting such fishing, to be issued by the government of the United States.¹ 

No action was taken on this idea until almost forty years later with the passage of the Fishery Management Conservation Act of 1976. However, 

President Roosevelt's correspondence clearly indicates the beginning of a new era in which the federal government would increasingly assert its claim to jurisdiction over the ocean and near-coastal waters of the United States. 

When Harry Truman became President on April 12, 1945, immediately following Roosevelt's death, two marine-related proclamations were awaiting his signature: the Department of the Interior had prepared a draft proclamation on management of submerged lands, and the Department of State had written a draft proclamation on marine fisheries management. 

Both documents had been approved by President Roosevelt on March 31, 1945. After several months of editing, Presidential Proclamations on the 

Continental Shelf (No. 2667) and Fisheries (No. 2668) were issued on September 28, 1945. The Truman Proclamation of 1945 claimed for the first time United States jurisdiction and control over the natural resources of the subsoil and seabed of the continental shelf beyond the three-mile limit of the territorial sea off the coasts of the United States. The Proclamation initiated a new rule of international law in this regard. However, it would take another eight years to obtain legislative support for these Executive Orders and to resolve the issue of state and federal jurisdictional interests within the territorial seas.

Following the three landmark Supreme Court cases in 1947 and 1950, the Congressional debate intensified over the issue of state control of the territorial sea. The first major piece of legislation to accomplish greater state control was vetoed by President Truman in 1952, and the debate spilled over into the Presidential campaign of both parties. The Democratic candidate lost the election, and shortly after taking office, President Eisenhower signed the Submerged Lands Act of 1953 (67 Stat. 29). By this Act, the 

United States confirmed to and vested in the coastal states the seabed and the resources of the territorial sea within three geographical miles of their respective coastlines (Florida and Texas were granted jurisdiction out to three marine leagues, based on colonial boundaries). However, the federal government retains authority over commerce, navigation, international agreements, and national defense. These paramount authorities are extensive, and to a certain degree, constitute a pervasive control over the waters of the territorial sea, at times conflicting with state authorities for managing marine resources or submerged lands. 

In the fall of 1953, President Eisenhower signed the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act which establishes federal jurisdiction, control and authority 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

119

- vna zgjat Au !!!! !!! over the subsoil and the seabed of the outer continental shelf. This act not only upheld and implemented the Truman Proclamation which extended U.S. jurisdiction beyond three miles, it also made clear that beyond three miles federal authority takes precedence over submerged lands. The right to navigation, and in general, to activities other than submerged lands activities, are not affected by the Act. The Act is of considerable importance, since it establishes the basic authority for the Department of the Interior to lease outer continental shelf (OCS) lands. With the passage of this Act, a new era in U.S. ocean law and regulation of resources had begun. 

A National Ocean Policy

Over the next two decades a comprehensive national ocean policy gradually emerged. The early emphasis was on marine research and development. 

One of the major studies of the early sixties was undertaken by the Interagency Committee on Oceanography (ICO), which prepared a long-range national oceanographic plan for the years 1963-1972, "Oceanography, The Ten Years Ahead." According to this plan, the basic interests of the U.S. should be "to exploit the ocean and its resources in the public interest, in enhancement of our society and culture, international posture, and our economic growth." 

Five specific objectives were identified: • strengthen basic sciences • improve national defense • manage resources in the world ocean • manage resources in domestic waters and 2 protect life and property, insuring the safety of operations at sea.“ 

By the 1960s, development of oil and gas reserves in the outer continental shelf had become a top national priority in the United States to offset our dependence on foreign sources. Projected mineral shortages also precipitated competition among nations to develop sea-floor minerals, primarily manganese nodules. An increased interest in finding a source of inexpensive protein resulted in an unparalleled exploitation of the world's fisheries, thus creating for the first time the possibility that some fisheries could become completely depleted. 

By 1966, the Congress was sufficiently interested in the multiplicity of these issues to enact a number of water-related laws, including the Clean 

Water Restoration Act (P.L. 89-753) and Water Resources Planning Act 

(P.L. 89-90). Congress also declared a 12-mile contiguous fishery zone (P.L. 

89-958) and established the Sea Grant Program (P.L. 89-688).

Of great significance for coastal ocean policy was the Marine Resources and Engineering Development Act of 1966 (MREDA). This first legislative ly articulated statement of national ocean policy was much broader in scope 20

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

than the legislation of the 1950s. The goal of the United States was declared to be "to develop, encourage, and maintain a coordinated, comprehensive, and long-range national program in marine science for the benefit of all mankind...." In addition to earlier goals of developing and utilizing marine resources, the Act also established some new social objectives, including: • The expansion of human knowledge of the marine environment. 

The preservation of the role of the United States as a leader in marine science. • The advancement of education and training in marine science. 

The Act (P.L. 89-454) established the Commission on Marine Science, Engineering, and Resources to review the known and contemplated needs for natural resources from the marine environment and to undertake a study of national ocean programs and interest. In 1967, President Johnson appointed Julius Stratton as Chairman. The so-called Stratton Commission 

Report entitled “Our Nation and the Sea," was released in January 1969.

The report was wide ranging and included a number of specific recommendations for new legislation, for international agreements and for new institutional arrangements. Of particular significance was the breadth of its vision of just how important the oceans and their resource would become. The 

Report's introduction declared that "the advancement of the Nation's capability to use more effectively its marine environment deserves recognition as a major mission of government." 

The Stratton Commission Report has served as a principal guide for Congressional and Presidential ocean-related actions. Among its recommendations was the establishment of an independent oceans agency or department represented in the President's Cabinet. In July 1970, President 

Nixon submitted his Reorganization Plan No 4, creating a new civil agency, the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA). Instead of being independent, however, NOAA became the largest bureau in the 

Department of Commerce. The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA), also established in late 1970 by Presidential Executive Order, was established as an independent agency.

Awareness and concern for the ocean environment had been growing in the public's consciousness, especially after highly publicized cases of environmental pollution resulting from drilling operations or from maritime accidents. Of particular concern were the sinking of the "Torrey Canyon" off the coast of England in the late 1960's, and the Santa Barbara oil spill in 1969. However, concern over the ocean environment extended beyond oil pollution to the reported destruction of marine fisheries as the result of dumping waste products, including chemicals and nerve gas containers, in the ocean.

In 1972, the Congress had a large number of bills under consideration that addressed a variety of ocean related issues. By the end of the year Congress had enacted a number of statutes which affected the ocean and its 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

121

resources, including the Coastal Zone Management Act, Marine Mammal 

Protection Act, Ports and Waterways Safety Act, Federal Water Pollution Control Act and the Marine Protection, Research and Sanctuaries Act. 

This body of legislation has served as the fundamental core of environmental protection and use management measures for U.S. coastal and marine environments. In the ensuing years, the trend of protection and wise management has continued with the passage of other coastal and marine acts and amendments, most notably the Deepwater Ports Act (1974), Fishery Conservation and Management Act (1976), Amendments to the Coastal Zone Management Act of 1972 (1977), Clean Air Act (1977), Clean Water Act (1977), National Ocean Pollution Research Development and Monitoring Planning Act of 1978, and Amendments to the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act of 1953 (1978). 

National Marine Sanctuaries Program 

The National Marine Sanctuary Program, administered by the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA) was established to designate and manage nationally significant marine areas by regulating human activities in those areas to protect their integrity. Through research and education activities, the program also seeks to increase scientific understanding of marine ecosystems and to inform the public about the wise use of marine resources.

Protection of ocean resources can take a variety of forms and reflect a range of restrictions on human use. The program emphasizes the sustainability of the oceans as an ecosystem, as well as human use of the oceans. 

Sustained human use is possible only if the overall health and resilience of the oceans is maintained. Marine sanctuaries provide for "multiple use"; however, human use must be balanced with measures to maintain the health and integrity of the ecosystem.

Areas designated as marine sanctuaries range in size from less than one square mile to over 1,252 square nautical miles in area. Some are located near land and receive many visitors, while others, farther offshore, are primarily of interest to the scientific community and fishermen. 

Each sanctuary is unique, and accordingly each is managed and regulated by NOAA with regard to its location and the specific nature of, and threats to, its resources. However, in all sanctuaries, decisions need to be made regarding the level and type of management and enforcement required to preserve the integrity of the resource; the type of research that is needed; and the appropriate segments of the public which need information so they can understand, appreciate and help to protect the sanctuary. 

NOAA's Marine and Estuarine Management Division (MEMD) is responsible for administering and managing all national marine sanctuaries. 

MEMD conducts an active research program within designated marine sanctuaries focused on management issues which relate to the protection of 122

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

significant resources. Each sanctuary has a research framework for its specific management concerns. Generally, sanctuary research priorities follow a pattern which includes studying: ⚫ general characteristics and distributions of resources, habitats, and uses; ⚫ the changes over time in resources, habitats and uses (abundance and distribution) and the influences of natural variability and human-caused disturbances on these long-term trends (monitoring); and ⚫ possible cause-and-effect relationships accounting for changes observed in resource distribution, abundance and diversity. 

As with research, sanctuary education programs are carried out within the general context of management of the sanctuary. The focus of all interpretive activities is on increasing public understanding and appreciation of the sanctuary which is essential to the achievement of resource protection goals. The area of management and research is therefore covered in some detail. Opportunities for educating the public are less available than on a land-based resource. However, interpretation may occur in three distinct types of programs:

On-site programs aimed at visitors and recreational users traveling to the sanctuary; • Land-based programs, usually at a visitor center, aimed at an interested public visiting the area but not able to travel to the sanctuary; and • Land-based programs aimed at "extension audiences” such as school children and others who live in the coastal area near the sanctuary.

The highest priority for all national marine sanctuaries is long-term protection of the resources. Sanctuary management plans establish the framework for achieving this goal by tailoring management programs to individual site needs. On-site staff are guided by the policies and procedures established by MEMD. They also are responsible for liaison with other federal and state agencies involved in the sanctuary's management. 

Seven national marine sanctuaries have been designated and three more are under active consideration. The seven sanctuaries in order of designation are:

Marine Ecosystems Protected by Marine 

Sanctuaries

The locations of the seven national marine sanctuaries are representative of a number of marine environments. These include nearshore, open water, and benthic ecosystems in temperate and tropical areas. Four sanctuaries are located in open water: the Gulf of the Farallones, Channel Islands, Gray's Reef, and the Monitor. The Channel Islands and Gulf of the Farallones sanctuaries include islands, and the latter extends to the mainland. 

Both sanctuaries thus encompass nearshore ecosystems in the temperate zone. Three other sanctuaries are located in nearshore ecosystems in the tropical zone, with outstanding coral reefs and seagrass beds: Key Largo, Looe Key, and Fagatele Bay.

A unique resource being preserved and studied through the National Marine Sanctuary Program is the U.S.S. Monitor. The Monitor, the famous 

Civil War ironclad that fought the C.S.S. Virginia (formerly U.S.S. Merrimac) in the first battle between ironclad warships on March 9, 1862, sank during a storm 16 miles off the coast of Cape Hatteras, North Carolina, in 230 feet of water. 124

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

Temperate Nearshore Ecosystems

Nearshore ecosystems occur where the tide ebbs and flows and marine organisms must cope with wide variations in conditions ranging from being left high and dry to being pounded by waves which smash against the rocks with great force, threatening to sweep away anything that is not firmly anchored. 

In the temperate regions of the United States, the two most extensive intertidal ecosystems (between the high tide and low tide line) are the sandy intertidal and the rocky intertidal systems. 

The sandy intertidal habitat is the one most familiar to people who like to visit the coast. Visitors sometimes see sand crabs and other small creatures scurrying under their feet but are unaware of virtually thousands of invertebrate animals hidden in the sand. Animals in this kind of environment have to protect themselves from the heat and drying of the sun through physical adaptations and by burrowing into the sand. This also protects them from the long bills of shorebirds.

The rocky intertidal habitat provides a home for myriad small creatures such as mussels, barnacles, crabs, and marine algae like sea lettuce and feather boa kelp. As the waves rise and fall, the tolerance of the organisms is tested, and those who can tolerate exposure to air the longest are in the uppermost zones. Kelp beds, which provide shelter to millions of individual animals along the West Coast, are similar to forests. Each kelp plant is anchored to hard, rocky substrate by a tangled holdfast. The plants grow where the water spreads their fronds to use light energy from the sun. The moving water constantly renews nutrients, brings in fresh supplies of oxygen, and flushes away waste products.

Offshore Open Water and Benthic Ecosystems 

The most widely distributed ecosystem in the world is that of the open ocean. These open waters, called pelagic zones, differ greatly from coastal waters. The most obvious difference is that plants and animals have no substrate or bottom to which they may attach themselves. A more subtle difference is that these offshore waters do not receive nutrients from land run-off and rivers. As a result, open ocean waters are generally less productive than the nutrient-rich waters near the shore. 

In the open sea, the food chain starts with phytoplankton, plants that live in the euphotic zone and rely mainly on ocean currents for movement. 

Phytoplankton use the energy of the sun to produce carbohydrates, which are used as their main source of energy. Some of the phytoplankton are eaten by zooplankton which in turn are eaten by nekton (fish, whales and other mammals), the free-swimming creatures of the sea.

Where the physical and topographical conditions are right, as in the case of upwelling, productivity may increase tremendously. Upwelling occurs predominately along the west coasts of the continents, where nearshore surface water is pushed offshore by prevailing winds or the rotation of the 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

125

DULAR HUR AUDAUN 

earth. The displaced surface water is replaced by upwelling deep water which is cold and rich in nutrients. The combination of sunlight, favorable temperature, and nourishment results in exceptionally larger growths of phytoplankton, resulting in one of the ocean's most productive ecosystems. 

The benthic environments, referring to the ocean bottom, cover an oceanographic range from the exposed region of the littoral system (out to a depth of 200 meters) to the bottom of the deep-sea system. The benthic profile provides a special habitat for a unique ecosystem. In the Atlantic, off the southeastern coast of the United States, the broad, sandy, almost barren continental shelf is irregularly broken by banks of rocky out-croppings covered with plants and animals. 

Off the coasts of Georgia and North and South Carolina, hard ground banks occur on the inner, middle, and outer continental shelf at depths of 50-80 feet, 100-130 feet, and 200-330 feet, respectively. Some of these banks may rise only a few feet above the bottom and be covered by shifting sand. 

Others may have a vertical height of 20 feet or more, and include overhanging ledges, caves, burrows, and troughs littered with sandy rocks. These rocky outcroppings, which are often called "hard bottoms," provide firm surfaces to which plants and animals can attach themselves. Different communities develop depending on whether the hard bottom is exposed to the warm waters of the Gulf Stream or to colder inshore waters and the runoff of freshwater from the land.

Another distinct benthic ecosystem is the seamount, a submarine hill that rises more than 1000 meters from the sea floor. Seamounts that reach the surface are called islands. Seamounts can be formed after an island subsides, but most are of volcanic origin. Seamounts that have flat tops are called guyots, possibly formed by wave erosion. When seamounts reach high enough into zones where light penetrates, they support rich ecosystems where fish, marine mammals and seabirds congregate. Abyssal hills are volcanic extrusions that rise less than 1000 meters from the seafloor. 

As opposed to "above-the-seafloor" ecosystems, submarine canyons cut the continental shelf and slope. Submarine canyons are thought to have formed at times of lowered sea level. They are characterized by a V-shaped cross section and winding rock walls.

Tropical Nearshore Ecosystems 

Coral reefs are considered the equivalent of tropical rain forests in their complexity, diversity, and productivity, and are very sensitive to environmental changes. Reef-building plants and animals need bright light from the sun, clear water and water temperatures above 70° Fahrenheit (21°C).

Coral reefs off the coast of the Florida Keys are among the northern-most coral reefs in the western hemisphere.

Coral reefs are built up through the accumulation of the remains of coral skeletons, algae and other organisms which secrete limestone. Zooanthel- 126

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

lae, microscopic plants that live within the tissue of hard corals, provide part of the energy needed by corals and synthesize protein that enhances the build-up of skeletal material. However, a significant portion of the carbon fixed by algal symbionts is passed on to the coral. The food caught by the coral supplies both the coral and the algae with nitrogen and phosphorus, which is then cycled back and forth between the two. 

Coral colonies often begin with a single larva that floats as part of the zooplankton until it finds a substrate to which it can attach itself. After settling, the larva becomes an adult polyp. Although many coral colonies look like plants, corals belong to the same group of animals as anemones, sea fans, and jellyfish. Many visitors are surprised to learn that coral reefs are alive and populated by millions of tiny coral polyps. The species of coral in any one area depends on a number of factors, among them location relative to the shore, water depth, and the intensity of wave action and light. 

Coral reef ecosystems, although very efficient, rely to some extent on adjacent sea grasses and mangrove forests as sources of primary production. 

Seagrasses play a dominant role in nearshore ecosystems and provide the basis for a food web built on detritus. The principal grasses in tropical regions are turtle grass and manatee grass. Seagrass beds also provide protection from predators for fish which spend much of their time on coral reefs.

Both coral reefs and tropical sea grass beds depend on the production of food by nearby mangrove forests. Leaf litter from mangroves provide detritus, which serves as a major source of primary production. Mangrove roots provide protection for some juvenile organisms which spend their adult lives on coral reefs. Mangrove forests fringe about 75 percent of the world's tropical coastline. Mangroves grow best where there is little wave energy and where the annual temperatures are above 66 degrees Fahrenheit. The diversity of animal species in mangrove communities is great, in part because mangroves are ecotones, transitional areas between marine and terrestrial ecosystems. 

Conditions and Trends 

Gulf of the Farallones National Marine Sanctuary

The boundaries of the Gulf of the Farallones National Marine Sanctuary encircle 948 square nautical miles of distinct ocean and nearshore habitats off the California coastline, north and west of San Francisco. The sanctuary's boundary extends to the coast, and its mainland boundary stretches over two coastal counties, following the mean high tide line from Bodega Head (Sonoma County) south to Rocky Point (Marin County), except along the Point Reyes National Seashore where it follows the park's one-quarter-mile offshore boundary. From the mean high tide lines, the sanctuary boundary extends to six nautical miles offshore between Bodega Head and the Point 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

127

DAM MIN amy ww HIN 

Reyes Headlands. At the headlands, the sanctuary's boundary balloons out to include waters within twelve nautical miles of the Farallon Islands, 25 miles off the mainland, and then arcs back to shore at Rocky Point. In addition to its vast ocean areas, the sanctuary incorporates the nearshore environments of Bodega Bay, Estero Americano, Estero de San Antonio, Tomales Bay, Drakes Bay, Duxbury Reef, Bolinas Lagoon, and Bolinas Bay. 

Beneath the ocean's surface, the continental shelf is wider here than any other area on the West Coast, reaching a width of 26 nautical miles. In the expanse between the Farallon Islands and the mainland, known as the Gulf of the Farallones, the shelf is generally flat and slopes gently to the west and the north from the mainland. The particular topography of the shelf provides an exceptionally large and relatively shallow foraging area and habitat for coastal and oceanic seabirds, marine mammals, and fish species.

Biological productivity in the sanctuary is largely influenced by several physical processes, notably two major ocean currents flowing at different levels and in opposite directions. Outflow from San Francisco Bay estuarine systems also influences regional water circulation patterns. Seasonal upwelling of cold, nutrient-rich waters into the photic zone provides for the production of phytoplankton - the basis of the oceanic food chain. Resources Sanctuary habitat zones are diverse within this productive system: ■ Coastal wetlands include lagoons, estuaries, salt marshes, and tidal flats where salt water mixes with fresh water drained from the land. The variety of subhabitats in Bolinas Lagoon supports an abundance of diverse life, which adapts to seasonally-changing salinities. Cordgrass, pickleweed, communities of clams, tube-dwelling worms, ghost shrimp, herring, juvenile salmon, bat rays, small crustaceans, shorebirds and waterfowl thrive here. ■Bays and coastal waters (Tomales, Bodega, Bolinas, and Drake's Bays) occur near the shore where coastal and ocean systems overlap. Calm nearshore bays, buffered by prominent headlands, provide wildlife with shelter from ocean storms. Inland watersheds infuse these areas with food-rich sediments, sparking marine organism growth throughout the water column. 

Worms, clams, and snails colonize bottom sediments; skates, starry flounder, and halibut hover over sandy shoals. Common murres, pelicans, and a variety of terns forage at the water's surface, while harbor seals pass between feeding and resting spots. ■ Islands provide isolated, rocky refuge for marine wildlife. At the sanctuary's center lie the North, Middle, and Southeast Farallon Islands, collectively designated as Farallon Islands National Wildlife Refuge. Undis turbed except for the visits of researchers, the islands annually provide resting and breeding sites for over 7,000 seals and sea lions, and over 300,000 128

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

seabirds. Dense colonies of cormorants, auklets, common murres, storm petrels, and western gulls nest on rocky slopes and in underground burrows. 

On pocket beaches and marine terraces, elephant seals, harbor seals, Stellar sea lions, and California sea lions crowd during fall and winter to establish breeding territories.

Intertidal zones, like Duxbury Reef, are found in coastal areas between high tide's wave spray and low tide's exposed rocks and reefs. A myriad array of tiny organisms are adapted to the pounding waves, constantly changing temperatures and salinity levels, and dehydrating air and sun created by the tide's twice daily ebb and flow. Mussels and barnacles fasten onto rocks to withstand waveshock; urchins and clams burrow for protection. Anemones and limpets cover themselves with pebbles or cling hard onto rocks to withstand hot temperatures and dry spells. Juvenile forms of many finfish abound, including sandsole and copper and canary rockfish.

Competition for space is fierce; yet life flourishes here, assured by plentiful food drifting in the water. ■ Pelagic zones comprise the vast majority of the sanctuary and form an immense, three-dimensional, fluid environment devoid of rock or shelter. 

Here, where sunlight penetrates no more than ten meters below the water's surface, tiny plant and animal plankton drift with prevailing currents. 

Cetaceans and pinnipeds navigate through huge regions, relying on their acute senses of sound, rather than sight, for both location of prey and accurate migratory paths. Some fish species, like salmon, can actually smell their way home to coastal rivers. Upwelled nutrients in wide shallows over the continental shelf trigger local chain reactions in the food web: phytoplankton attract animal plankton, which attract anchovies, herring, and rockfish, which attract foraging seabirds and pinnipeds (sea lions) which may attract killer whales or sharks. ■ The benthic zone, or ocean floor, is cold, dark and under constant great pressure from the weight of the water above it. Within this habitat, the season never changes, so benthic organisms can reproduce year-round. In the absence of light, no plants live here. In spite of scarce food, bacteria in the marine ooze support a surprising variety of worms, brittle stars, sea cucumbers and other bottom dwellers able to survive in these conditions. 

The most spectacular component of the sanctuary's marine life is its nesting seabirds. The Farallon Islands comprise the largest seabird rookeries in the contiguous United States. Over 300,000 birds nest here annually. Residing at the Farallones is virtually the entire world population of ashy storm petrels; additionally, single colonies of western gulls, pelagic and Brandt's cormorants, black oystercatchers, pigeon guillemots, and Cassin's auklets are among the largest found anywhere. A wide variety of both marine and coastal species may also be found along the sanctuary's shore nesting loca tions: Bodega Rock, Tomales Point, Bird Rock, Point Reyes Headlands, HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

129

请​解 ​Tho HIM

ww WIT HARL 

Bear Valley Rocks, and Double Point. Double-crested cormorants, Leach's storm petrels, rhinoceros auklets, and tufted puffins are frequently seen. 

Significant populations of waterfowl and shorebirds regularly use the sanctuary's major estuarine and sandy beach habitats, such as curlews, willets, godwits, sandpipers, dunlins, herons, and egrets. Lagoons and protected bay areas attract large numbers of ducks, grebes, kingfishers, ospreys, and scoters.

Over twenty species of marine mammals have been recorded in sanctuary waters. The site provides the most important habitat in northern California for pinnipeds. With the exception of the northern fur seal, the pinniped species found here are year-round residents. These are Stellar sea lions, California sea lions, harbor seals, and northern elephant seals.

Providing both a vital link in the ecosystem and a major aesthetic value to the sanctuary are five species of great cetaceans: the blue, humpback, sperm, sei, and California gray whales. Gray whales are commonly observed during their annual migrations along the coast from late November until June or July. Other smaller cetacean species using sanctuary waters for food or migratory passage include the Pacific pilot whale, killer whale, false killer whale, Baird's beaked whale, Curvier's beaked whale, common dolphin, Risso's dolphin, Pacific white-sided dolphin, northern right whale dolphin, harbor porpoise, and Dall's porpoise.

The wide continental shelf and shoreline configuration of the sanctuary are especially important to chinook and coho salmon, northern anchovy, rockfish, and flatfish populations. High productivity of these fisheries is enhanced by the diversity of sanctuary habitats. The site's many bays and estuaries provide particularly important feeding, spawning, and nursery areas for Pacific herring, smelt, starry flounder, surfperch, halibut, and rex sole.

The sanctuary's marine flora provide vital food, protection, and breeding areas for thousands of coastal fish and marine birds. There are significant communities of kelp beds, salt marshes, and eelgrass beds, all contributing to the vitality of the system's fauna. Kelp beds are concentrated along the mainland coast between Point Reyes Headlands and Bolinas Lagoon; most beds are dominated by short kelp algae, which is constantly grazed upon by sea urchins.

In addition to the abundant flora, fauna and varied habitats, there are significant historical and cultural resources ranging from inundated early human sites to shipwreck sites documenting the early exploration of the California coast by Spanish sailors during the 16th century and the subsequent development of California from the gold rush days to modern times. 

One of the most significant sites yet to be discovered, but believed to be within the sanctuary, is the wreck of the San Augustus, an early Spanish "Manila Galleon," that, if located, would be the oldest shipwreck in western 

North America. 130

Management The on-site administration and management staff presently consists of a 

NOAA sanctuary manager, deputy manager, sanctuary warden, and a secretary. Through cooperative and inter-agency agreements, activities are coordinated with the California Department of Fish and Game and the 

Western Regional Office of the National Park Service (involving the Golden Gate National Recreation Area). 

The National Park Service provides administrative and logistical support to the sanctuary, as well as coordination of activities between the sanctuary and the Golden Gate National Recreation Area. Sanctuary staff, with the cooperation of environmental and educational groups, provide interpretive services, such as education programs and presentations to the public about the sanctuary.

The California Department of Fish and Game is responsible for the enforcement of regulations protecting living marine resources in state waters, including such waters within the sanctuary. By cooperative agreement with 

NOAA's Marine and Estuarine Management Division, the Department of 

Fish and Game also enforces sanctuary regulations and supplements its enforcement of living marine resource regulations within the sanctuary. These responsibilities are carried out by the sanctuary warden. Regulations

Sanctuary regulations prohibit certain activities in whole or in part. These include: ⚫ hydrocarbon exploration and development • discharge of substances • alteration or construction on the seabed ⚫ commercial transport vessel operations within two miles of specified biologically sensitive areas disturbing marine mammals by flying motorized aircraft at less than 1000 feet over the waters within one nautical mile of biologically sensitive areas ⚫ removing or damaging any historic or cultural resources. 

Channel Islands National Marine Sanctuary 

Encompassing 1,252.5 square nautical miles of nearshore and offshore waters, the boundaries of the sanctuary include waters within six nautical miles surrounding the four northern Channel Islands and Santa Barbara Island. This sanctuary, the largest in the national system, presents extraordinary opportunities and challenges to those involved in its management. 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

131

RANDAVI 

INDAR! TH MAD NB 

THE MU 

The sanctuary is situated in the Southern California Bight, where a wide section of the continental shelf is dissected by submerged canyons, basins, and ridges. The northern Channel Islands (San Miguel, Santa Rosa, Santa

Cruz, and Anacapa) and Santa Barbara Island are actually the above-water peaks of these extensive offshore ridges. Relatively shallow shelves surround the islands, extending three to six nautical miles from the coastlines. Beyond the shelves, the bathymetry either drops sharply (from 1600 to 2500 feet), or slopes gradually (from 600 to 1200 feet).

Submarine areas of the northern Santa Barbara Channel are characterized by active seismic faults, shallow oil and gas reservoirs, and natural oil and gas seeps. The accentuated bottom relief and varied substrate of the sanctuary contribute to the richness of its marine life. Underwater geology consists of undifferentiated sedimentary rock and quaternary volcanic rock, with unconsolidated gravel, sand, shell rubble, silt, and clay sediments. The changes in depths from shallow shelves to deep trenches, combined with gradations from deepwater silt and clay deposits to shallow sand and rock, permit a wide variety of intertidal and subtidal habitats. Resources A huge spectrum of marine life, from microscopic plankton to enormous blue whales and basking sharks, find food, shelter, breeding area, and migratory passage within this extensive ecosystem, located from eight to forty nautical miles off the southern California coast. The remarkable biological diversity is largely attributable to the site's location at the transition point between two biogeographic coastal provinces. Here, where northern currents converge with southern, causing seasonal upwelling, extensive mixing occurs of cold temperate, tropical, and endemic (or regionally limited) species.

While the sanctuary supports exceptional concentrations of seabirds, fish, shellfish, kelp, and intertidal organisms, by far the most dramatic element of Channel Islands' marine life is its extraordinary number and variety of marine mammals. Over thirty species have been recorded, the most conspicuous and numerous of which are six species of pinnipeds. Island shelves and shoreline habitat provide critically important feeding and haulout areas for California sea lions, Stellar sea lions, northern fur seals, northern elephant seals, harbor seals, and Guadalupe fur seals. With the exception of the Guadalupe fur seal, all are year-round residents of the site. Pinniped concentrations vary across the sanctuary, the greatest numbers occurring around the western edges of San Miguel Island. San Miguel is the only location in the United States (and perhaps the world) providing rookeries for all five species of pinnipeds. Santa Barbara Island supports breeding populations of California sea lions, northern elephant seals, and harbor seals; and harbor seal rookeries also exist on Santa Rosa, Santa Cruz, and Anacapa Is- 132

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

lands. The sanctuary's pinnipeds constitute a major link in the Southern 

California's Bight's food chain and its ecological balance.

Cetaceans are the most transient of sanctuary inhabitants. Twenty-seven species have been observed; of these, eighteen are seen regularly. Because they are so frequently sighted, common dolphins, Pacific white-sided dolphins, bottle-nose dolphins, short-finned pilot whales, killer whales, and minke whales are considered "residents", although little is yet known about their life histories, behavior, or areas of concentration. Non-resident northern right whale dolphins, Dall's porpoises, and Risso's dolphins can also be seen frequently in sanctuary waters. 

Migratory pathways for several great cetaceans intersect the sanctuary, including California gray whales, humpback whales, blue whales, fin whales, and sei whales. Less commonly observed is the rare Pacific right whale. All are currently listed as “endangered" species. Because world populations of these animals are so small, the frequency and diversity of sightings are particularly notable, and of special interest to both researchers and sanctuary visitors.

Although marine birds number fewer than those found in and around the Farallon Islands to the north, species diversity at Channel Islands identifies the sanctuary's importance as one of the richest resource areas for marine birds in the United States. Over sixty species use these areas for nesting, feeding, wintering, and/or migratory staging. Eleven of sixteen resident species of marine birds nest on the sanctuary's islands and rocks. Interestingly, most of these populations formerly nested on California's mainland, but because of developmental pressures on their habitats, the birds have shifted to offshore areas.

San Miguel Island, and the smaller surrounding islets of Prince Islet, Castle Rock, and Richardson Rock, support the most abundant and diverse avifauna in the sanctuary: nine species have established nesting colonies in these areas. Particularly notable as well are Anacapa Island, which provides the only permanent rookery in California for the endangered brown pelican; and Santa Barbara Island, which supports both the largest United States colony of Xantus' murrelets and the only United States colony of black storm petrels.

In all, over 20,000 pairs of marine birds nest on islands within the sanctuary. Prominent among the species found here are western gulls, Xantus' murrelets, Brandt's cormorants, brown pelicans, pigeon guillemots,

Cassin's auklets, ashy storm petrels, pelagic cormorants, and double-crested cormorants. Present in fewer numbers are Leach's storm petrels and black storm petrels. The birds are dependent on the sanctuary's rich fish resources, and are thus also important predators in the Southern California Bight food chain. Although the biology and ecology are well documented for a few species, information is still lacking on colony sizes, reproductive success, foraging areas, and habits of most resident marine bird species. 133

Descriptions are also incomplete on the sanctuary's subtidal environments and species assemblages. Predominant nearshore communities are structured around kelp forest rock bottoms and shallow sand bottoms. The better-studied kelp forests exhibit luxuriant growths of giant kelp (Macrocystis pyrifera), throughout the sanctuary's shallow areas (between 26 feet and 112 feet). The densest, most extensive canopies are found along protected island shores (e.g., southwest San Miguel, southern and western Santa Rosa and Santa Cruz, northeastern Santa Barbara, and all around Anacapa).

Numerous other brown algae are associated with the giant kelp and provide shelter for over 800 plant and animal species, including at least 125 species of fish. Thriving in this community are sponges, kelp crabs, spiny lobsters, abalone, octopus and squid, sea stars, and sea urchins. Common fish species include garibaldi, opaleye, kelp bass, California sheepshead, sea perch, painted greenling, and several species of rockfish. Rockfishes and surfperches are especially notable for their commercial and recreational importance. Shallow sandy bottom communities are also widespread in the sanctuary, although they generally support less algal growth and fewer fish and invertebrates species. Commonly encountered in these areas are sea pansies, polychaetes, sand dollars, rays, sanddabs, and turbot.

In deep subtidal areas, benthic fauna are diverse in silt-dominated canyons, with thin, living mats of encrusting sponges, bryozoans, and tunicates covering canyon walls. Deep-water invertebrates include copepods, euphausiids, (pelagic shrimp), squid, and the pelagic red crab. Offshore fish are abundant; over thirty species are common in the sanctuary. Small schooling species, such as northern anchovy, Pacific saury, sardine, mackerel, and squid are especially important to the overall food chain. 

The sanctuary's intertidal habitats are relatively well documented, and include rock shelves, boulder beaches, tidepools, rubble piles, and sand flats. 

Rocky intertidal habitats, in particular, display a high diversity of plant and animal life, characterized by abalone, mussels, barnacles, limpets, attached marine algae, sea lettuce, starfish, sea urchins, and tidepool fishes. Remote areas of Santa Cruz, Santa Rosa, and San Miguel support high concentrations of black abalone, a species once prevalent in areas now more accessible and crowded. Sandy beach areas provide less diverse and crowded habitats for burrowing clams, amphipods, isopods, crabs, and associated shorebirds.

In addition to its myriad natural resources, the sanctuary harbors numerous shipwrecks of historic significance. Among these is the wreck of the 1853 sidewheeler Winfield Scott, located in Frenchy's Cove, and listed on the National Register of Historic Places. 

Uses

The Channel Islands National Marine sanctuary supports scientific, recreational, and commercial uses, many of which depend on the unique environ- 134

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

ment and abundant resources of the area. The multiple use character of the sanctuary contributes to its significance and is an important factor in its management.

The marine environment of the sanctuary provides an ideal setting for a variety of recreational activities. The predominant uses are boating, windsurfing, sport fishing, diving, and nature viewing. Several activities such as tide-pooling and island hiking also represent uses dependent on the aesthetic resources of the sanctuary.

The abundant marine resources of the sanctuary support several large year-round and seasonal fisheries. The more important harvests of the sanctuary, based on dollar value are: sea urchin; jack and Pacific mackeral;

California halibut; red, black and pink abalone; rockfish; northern anchovy; spiny lobster; rock crab; swordfish; thresher; and angel shark. Kelp harvesting is another commercial activity undertaken in the sanctuary. The giant kelp beds of the Channel Islands have been harvested for about 30 years. 

The sanctuary is adjacent to several major shipping routes serving one of the most urbanized areas in the world. Two major traffic lanes run through the Santa Barbara Channel and partly through the sanctuary. These lanes are adjacent to one another in opposite directions, approaching within two miles of Anacapa Island at the eastern end of the Channel and within about 20 nautical miles of San Miguel Island at the western end, in accordance with the traffic separation scheme established by the U.S. Coast Guard. 

Petroleum products, both crude and refined, constitute the most common product shipped through the area. The channel waters around the sanctuary are also widely used by supply ships servicing offshore oil and gas leases in this region of the outer continental shelf. 

Oil and gas development began in Santa Barbara County in the late 1800s. Since then, hydrocarbon development has occurred in the vicinity of the sanctuary in the nearshore waters off Santa Barbara and Ventura 

Counties but not within the sanctuary.

Both the Navy and Air Force conduct a variety of military exercises in the sanctuary. These include missile launches, inert bomb drop exercises, mining exercises, and some submarine activities. All are strictly controlled and require extensive zones which are safe for the public. 

Management Management activities include resource protection, research, and interpretative programs. Compatible visitor use is particularly important to effective sanctuary management. The large size of the sanctuary and the high level of both commercial and recreational use of sanctuary resources have required a relatively greater investment of on-site staff and management effort. The on-site staff consists of a NOAA sanctuary manager, interpretive specialist, research/education coordinator, enforcement coordinator, two enforcement rangers, and a secretary.

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

135

Administration and management at the sanctuary is accomplished by NOAA staff and through several cooperative agreements. The Channel Islands National Marine Sanctuary overlaps several existing jurisdictions. 

Coordination and cooperation among the responsible agencies have been an important aspect of sanctuary management since designation. 

The National Park Service administers the Channel Islands National 

Park which includes the islands and, for administrative purposes, the area within a one nautical mile of the islands. Management of the park focuses on the protection of terrestrial resources which are dependent on the marine environment. In like manner, the islands' shores provide critical habitat for many marine resources. Therefore, a close working relationship between the sanctuary and park is critical for ensuring complementary management of both environments. At present, an interpretive specialist, enforcement coordination, and enforcement rangers are provided for the sanctuary through an inter-agency agreement between the sanctuary and the park. Enforcement rangers are assigned to the sanctuary for three-quarters of their time.

As in the Gulf of the Farallones National Marine Sanctuary, the California Department of Fish and Game is responsible for the management of living marine resources in California, including the state waters portion of the sanctuary. Consequently, the California Department of Fish and Game is involved in the resource protection needs of the sanctuary, and also participates in the sanctuary's research program. 

The special ecological qualities of the sanctuary attract scientific researchers from many institutions such as the University of California, the 

Santa Barbara Museum of Natural History, Hubbs Sea World, Scripps In stitute of Oceanography, and from many federal agencies. 

Research organizations and non-profit groups also provide educational programs for the public. Many organize educational tours to the northern 

Channel Islands. Naturalists accompany school groups on these excursions and provide them with information about sanctuary resources. Sanctuary staff work closely with other agencies in the implementation of sanctuary research and interpretative activities. Regulations

Sanctuary regulations prohibit certain activities in whole or part. These include: • hydrocarbon activities ⚫ discharge of substances • alteration of or construction on the seabed ⚫ commercial vessel operations (i.e. vessels engaged in the trade of carrying cargo) within one nautical mile of an island • disturbing marine mammals or birds ⚫ removing or damaging historical or cultural resources. 136

MARINE SANCTUARIES Gray's Reef National Marine Sanctuary 

Gray's Reef National Marine Sanctuary consists of 16.88 square nautical miles of high seas waters 50 to 70 feet deep, lying approximately seventeen and-a-half nautical miles east of Sapelo Island, Georgia. Gray's Reef is one of the largest and most popular "live bottom" reefs on the South Atlantic Continental Shelf, and is named in recognition of zoologist Dr. Milton B. 

Gray of the University of Georgia Marine Institute at Sapelo Island, whose offshore collections during the 1960's contributed much to understanding the benthic communities of coastal and continental shelf areas off of 

Georgia's coast.

Resources Among the species attaching themselves to the hard bottom on the inner side of the shelf, where Gray's Reef National Marine Sanctuary is located, are hard corals such as star coral, cup coral and branching eye coral. These species occur near the northern limit of their geographical range. 

Soft corals at Gray's Reef include sea fans and sea whips, whose thousands of individual polyps may take the form of delicate feathers or whips. The live bottom (or hard bottom) reef system began to form as an intermittent layer of limestone rock about 13 million years ago, during the Pliocene Geologic Age. Rock "outcrops", or ledges, formed in a north to southwest direction. During the Pleistocene or "Ice" Age which followed, sea levels fluctuated widely over eons of time. The rock ledges were alternately exposed to air or submerged below sea level, resulting in periodic erosion and the formation of undercuts and caves due to storms and wave action. Outcroppings were typically separated by wide expanses of sand.

When sea level finally stabilized approximately 5,000 years ago, the live bottom community began to develop permanently and grow. Since then, the substrate has undergone little change, except for the sandy areas, which are subject to weathering, shifting, and slumping. These processes have created a complex environment of caves, burrows, troughs, and overhangs, which are often referred to as marine oases. Uses

Situated in a transition zone between the warm Gulf Stream and more temperate coastal waters, these geological formations provide a nurturing, sheltered habitat for an unusually large number of marine species, many of which would not otherwise be found here. The variety and abundance of life at Gray's Reef are why fishermen and others refer to this area as a “live bottom". Seaweeds, sponges, hard and soft corals, and feathery tube worms attach to and cover the rocky surfaces, and form the basis of a food chain. which includes bottom-dwelling snappers, groupers, black sea bass, porgy and sheepshead. Larger predator fish are also attracted to the area, such as 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY

MMD www barracudas and greater amberjacks. The seasonal migrations through 

Gray's reef of bluefish, jack, cobia, mackerel and bonito make the sanctuary a popular recreational and commercial fishing area. 

Many divers and underwater photographers are attracted to Gray's Reef by colorful tropical fish (damselfish, blennies, gobies, wrasses and angelfish), which browse close to reef surfaces amid clusters of sea urchins, starfish, sea whips, and sea anemones. Small schooling resident fish (triggerfish, tomtates, cubbyus, and filefish) are found hovering above. 

Crustaceans, like shrimp, spiny lobsters, and crabs stay sheltered in caves by day, and roam the reef to feed at night. Other invertebrates flourishing here include mussels, conchs, scallops, and sea snails, such as the deer cowrie and common slipper snail. Octopus may be found skirting across the sandy bottom surrounding the hard bottom or living beneath the overhangs. 

Three species of threatened or endangered sea turtles are occasionally seen at Gray's Reef, notably the loggerhead. These large, slow-moving, ancient reptiles are believed to lodge themselves under rocky ledges and remain in a hibernation-like state from November to April. Of additional interest to scientists is the migratory passage of endangered right whales through the sanctuary.

Management The sanctuary is administered by a project manager in MEMD. Through a cooperative agreement with the University of Georgia, interpretive programs and services are provided to the public from the sanctuary headquarters on Skidaway Island, in Savannah. Regulations

Sanctuary regulations prohibit certain activities in whole or in part. These include: • alteration of the seabed use of wire fish traps, bottom trawls and explosives ⚫ taking or damaging bottom formations and other natural or cultural resources discharge of substances.

Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary 

Southeast of Miami, where the Florida Keys begin to curve along the mainland toward the Gulf of Mexico, lies the Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary, encompassing 103 square nautical miles of living barrier reefs. 

Extending from Florida's three-mile jurisdictional limit seaward to the 300foot isobath, the sanctuary is approximately twenty miles long and varies in width from three to six miles. 38

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

The sanctuary was designated to provide protection and management for the environmentally sensitive South Florida Coral Reef Tract, which is annually visited by close to a million SCUBA divers, snorkelers, sport and commercial fishermen, boaters, water skiers, swimmers, and researchers. 

The Florida Keys are comprised of a crescent-shaped chain of small limestone islands, curving around the south end of the mainland and bending west of the Florida peninsula. Not surprisingly, the ocean floor in this area is underlain with Key Largo limestone, with post-Pleistocene Age limestone overlaying; a top layer is formed of calcium carbonate sediments. 

The sanctuary occurs in an area of sub-tropical climate. Prevailing winds, seasonally averaging between 5 and 15 mph, are easterly to southeasterly; however, variations are not uncommon. Summer months are subject to storms and hurricanes.

Water circulation in the sanctuary results from the combined effects of tide, wind, and the Florida Current. In outer reef areas, where the current's influence is strongest, water temperatures range from 68°F to 89'F. Salinity levels are relatively constant throughout the year. Resources Bottom topography of the sanctuary is characterized by a series of low shelf banks, or ridges, alternating with shallow channels which parallel the Keys and the outer edge of the shelf. The best-developed coral reefs are found on the outer edge of the shelf banks. Moving from shore to offshore, five distinct reef tract zones occur within the sanctuary, reflecting local and regional geomorphological differences: the Hawk Channel, mid-shelf reef platform, sand channel, offshore reef, and deep reef.

The Hawk Channel averages three miles in width and up to fifteen feet in depth. Extending from Biscayne National Park southward well into the lower Florida Keys, Hawk Channel is characterized by soft, fine bottom sediments with sparse seagrass patches. These areas are flanked by scattered hardgrounds and patch reefs. Water clarity is highly variable because of long-lasting suspension qualities of fine-grained bottom sediments. ■ The mid-shelf, or intermediate, reef platform presents a mosaic of habitats, including patch reefs, hardgrounds, barrier-type reef formations, sand bottoms, and seagrass beds. Due to the influx of offshore water from the Florida Straits and coarser sediments resistant to resuspension during turbulence, water clarity is much improved over inshore areas of the sanctuary. The size and complexity of coral formations are generally less than that found at offshore reef sites. Notable exceptions are the Key Largo 

Dry Rocks and the Grecian Rocks reef areas, which display large hard coral development and densely-packed elkhorn coral, respectively. 

HARVARD LAW LIBRARY 

139

? 12:

EL 27 67 2 ■The sand channel is clearly defined in the sanctuary's northern regions (and in Biscayne National Park). Long breaks in the "fore reef" area results in a lack of channel definition in the middle and southern portions of the sanctuary.

The offshore reefs include popular diving spots, such as Molasses, French, Carysfort, and The Elbow reefs. Molasses Reef is a barrier-type coral reef with a lagoon community on its shoreward side. This complex, productive reef displays a well-developed spur-and-groove system on its outer face, and an extensive back reef rubble zone. French Reef also displays a spur-and-groove system on its outer face, although active coral growth extends further seaward than at Molasses Reef. Spurs, often interlaced with caves and passages, are dominated by massive star corals. The back reef exhibits extensive rubble. Coral growth patterns are similar at The 

Elbow; and an old shipwreck rests upon its reef. Carysfort Reef demonstrates zonation and structure which are typical of Caribbean barrier reefs; its well-developed back reef and elkhorn coral reef crest may be exposed at low tide. A sloping fore reef gives way to a narrow gently sloping soft coral community which ends abruptly in extensive staghorn coral thickets, occurring uniquely at approximately fourteen meters water depth. Further offshore, a barren zone grades into a deep reef slope, ending in a sand terrace.

The deep reef occupies fifty square nautical miles on the slope of the continental shelf in water depths greater than 100 feet. At approximately 100 feet, coral and other epibenthic organisms grow atop outcroppings and isolated, relict carbonate spurs. Extending to 130 feet are deep reef formations surrounded by algal-covered sediments. Seaward to 180 feet, the sanctuary floor displays algal-covered cobble, rose and finger corals, occasional large sponges and tile-fish burrows. Beyond this depth, a fine sand plain extends to 300 feet, and is largely barren.

Within each of the categories described above, one or more of five distinct biological communities is supported: seagrass and sand; patch reef; hardbottom; outer coral reef; and deep water reef. ■Seagrass communities growing on sand substrate begin at the intertidal zone along the shore and extend almost continuously seaward to the back reef areas of the outer reefs. Among six species of marine grass, turtle grass (Thalassia testudinum) and manatee grass (Syringodium filiforme) dominate. Attached to blades of grass, and to rocks and rubble, an array of algae and encrusting invertebrates are found, including sponges, hydrozoans, flatworms, and tunicates. Other bottom-dwelling invertebrates are also abundant and varied: sea stars, sea cucumbers, urchins, snails, slugs, clams, pen shells, octopods, and squids. These seagrass meadows provide important nursery areas for juvenile fishes and invertebrates, notably shrimp and lobster. Both fish and invertebrate species exhibit nocturnal and diurnal 140

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

behavior patterns related to their feeding, resting, and hiding activities. 

During daytime, damselfish, wrasses, parrotfish, and goatfish browse in and out of the area; night foragers include snappers, groupers, squirrelfish, and grunts.

Patch reef communities exist among loose aggregations of massive head corals. Dominant species include star, brain, and finger corals. Characteristically, these reefs are surrounded by coarse sand and rubble, which is grazed by parrotfish and sea urchins. The activities of most mobile invertebrates, such as crabs, lobsters, snails, octopus, and sea stars, are nocturnal; by day, these species are hidden in caves and under crevices. Nocturnal predator fish - black groupers, blue tangs, bluestriped grunts, and French angelfish tend toward colors of drab gray and brown. During the day, brilliant yellow, blue and green fishes dominate the reef: sergeant majors, bluehead wrasses, queen and rainbow parrotfish, and juvenile white grunts.

Hardbottoms are bedrock areas covered by encrusting and small head corals, octocorals, and associated communities. Unlike coral reef areas, hardbottoms occur on a substrate not formed by the recent reproduction and growth of associated fauna, i.e., corals. Diversity of habitats and thus species is markedly lower here than in reef communities. Here, small knobs of coral are common, including starlet, elliptical star, golf ball star, lobed star, finger, and knobby brain corals. Associated with these species are green and brown algae, and several sponges, soft corals, and invertebrates. 

Outer coral reef communities are the most diverse and luxuriant in the sanctuary. Active vertical and lateral coral growth is enhanced by the mix of open ocean circulation and relatively high energy current conditions. Reef height averages ten to fifteen feet in the approximately fourteen miles of bank (or outer) reefs occurring within the sanctuary. Massive branching elkhorn corals are chief builders of bank reefs, and optimally occur to a depth of twenty-five feet. In less turbulent areas, staghorn and clubbed finger corals are prevalent. Massive heads, such as star and brain corals, often become top heavy over time by the extensive undercutting of boring sponges, worms, and mollusks. Less abundant hard corals grow among the bank corals, and include lettuce, fingers, fungus, elliptical star, flower, brain, solitary disk, and fire corals. Associated fish species are also abundant and diverse in outer reef communities. The sanctuary's prohibition on spearfishing has generally caused fish here to be less shy of divers; and daytime visitors may see many species, including amberjacks, damselfish, gray snappers, lizardfish, scorpionfish, blennies, moray eels, porkfish, squirrelfish, drums, cardinalfish, perch, tilefish, goatfish, flounders and rays. ■ Deep water reef communities extend to depths of 130 feet, beyond which the bottom is covered with sand and algal rubble. Approximately fifty percent of the sanctuary occurs in waters between 100 and 300 feet deep. To 

HARVARD I AW LIBRARY 

141

run USA compensate for the reduced light conditions at these greater depths, dominant coral species such as star, lettuce, and branching corals assume a flattened, plate-like structure allowing maximum interception of solar radiation. Sunshine fish and tobaccofish dominate in this darkened community. 

Of particular historical interest in the sanctuary are numerous shipwrecks, the best known of which is the World War II freighter Benwood, sunk near the southern boundary in 1942. Older sunken wrecks include the 

British frigate HMS Winchester; the steamer Towanda; the schooners Tiorva and Windlass; and the barge Charles W. Baird. Unique to this sanctuary is a nine-foot bronze statue known as the Christ of the Deep, and located at Key Largo Dry Rocks in about twenty-five feet of water. Marked by an orange and white surface buoy, the donated statue was placed by the Florida Park Service in 1961 in the then-named John Pennekamp Coral Reef State Park. Management The sanctuary is administered by an on-site staff consisting of a NOAA manager, marine biologist, interpreter, three rangers and a secretary. The interpreter and rangers are provided by the Florida Department of Natural 

Resources through a cooperative agreement. The Key Largo National 

Marine Sanctuary has approximately 750,000 visitors a year. Regulations

Sanctuary regulations prohibit certain activities in whole or in part. These 

⚫ removing or damaging coral or other natural or cultural resources ⚫ anchoring in a manner that damages coral; mooring bouys must be used where provided ⚫ dredging, filling, excavating ⚫ discharges ⚫ wire fish traps, trawls, and explosives ⚫ spearguns and weapons.

Looe Key National Marine Sanctuary 

Thirty miles northeast of Key West, Florida and 129 miles southwest of 

Miami, the Looe Key National Marine Sanctuary encompasses a spec tacular portion of the "spur and groove" coral formations found intermittently along the Florida Reef Tract. The entire tract extends in a southwest direction, paralleling the Keys for 200 nautical miles and ending at the Dry Tortugas. While the most extensive reef areas are found in the tract's northern portion, areas of considerable size occasionally occur along the remainder of this chain of living reefs. 142

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

The 5.3-square-nautical-mile sanctuary is named for the HMS Looe, a 

British frigate which foundered on the reefs in 1744. Its boundaries follow the outline of a parallelogram, bordered on the south by the Straits of Florida and on the north by Hawk Channel. Within the sanctuary, a diverse assemblage of fishes and invertebrates inhabit the overhangs, crevices, sandflats, and other microhabitats created by the corals. Resources Coral communities are particularly interesting and vulnerable at this general location. They are able to tolerate only minor fluctuations in the water's physical and chemical parameters. The most severely limiting natural factor affecting coral reef development is the seasonal drops in water temperatures. Coral reefs in the Florida Keys generally grow (and recover from damage) at half the rate demonstrated by Caribbean reefs. Bedrock for this area is of dual origin, limestone and oolite. 

The Looe Key National Marine Sanctuary is divided roughly into five ecological/topographical zones, which collectively provide habitat utilized by all major taxa of reef-dwelling organisms. 

The patch reef (or back reef) is a flat, relatively shallow area stretching from Hawk Channel south to the reef flat. Water depths average eight meters in this zone dominated by a mixture of turtle grass, manatee grass, and algae. Scattered throughout are numerous, low-profile patch reefs averaging less than two meters in height. Because this zone is sheltered from violent wave action by the reef flat to its south, a lagoon-type reef environment is generally apparent, although considerable suspension of water column sediment load occurs during periods of rough weather. Dominant patch reef fauna are octocorals, notably sea feathers and sea whips. Sponges are also well-represented. Important stony corals include fire coral and staghorn coral, which occur with greater frequency here than anywhere else in the Looe Key area. Other commonly encountered patch reef inhabitants include anemones, mat-forming zoanthids, serpulid and sabellid worms, and several crustaceans, notably arrow crabs. Spiny lobsters are particularly abundant in the patch reef, utilizing the area for both habitat and feeding. 

The reef flat adjoins the patch reef on the south, occurring roughly in the shape of an isosceles triangle, with its base facing south toward the Straits of Florida and its apex pointing landward to the north. The reef flat is marked by a transition from seagrasses to an extensive sand flat, and an elevation of the bottom to within two meters of the sea surface. Moving southward, the reef flat becomes shallower, and terminates abruptly at an area of rock and rubble which forms the northernmost rim of the fore reef. Water depths in this zone generally average one-half meter; there is no profile save the seagrass ridges. The benthos consists primarily of calcareous sand, rubble, coarse sediment, and extensive seagrass beds. In addition to turtle and 

HARVARD I AW LIBRARY 

143

nun UMNO = 3 ww ww 

BU 

ww manatee grasses, the reef flat exhibits an increased abundance and variety of green, red, and brown algal species.

Sessile benthic fauna are of minor significance in the reef flat; the occurrence of sponges and stony corals is also very limited. Of note, however, is the sporadic occurrence of octocorals in patches near the zone's seaward terminus, including the smallest of all Looe Key octocorals, Pterogorgia citrina, which is found only within this zone. 

Commonly-seen reef flat invertebrates include queen conchs, pen shells, holothurians, and reef squids. Abundant populations of brittle stars, crabs, small gastropods, pelecypod mollusks, and echinoderms thrive in this environment.

The fore reef is undoubtedly the most spectacular portion of the sanctuary; exhibiting a well-developed, high profile spur and groove coral system. The fore reef borders the reef flat in very shallow water and slopes to a sand bottom at a depth of approximately nine to eleven meters. At its center, the width of this zone spans 350 meters; its overall east-to-west length covers about 1500 meters. The most shallow coral spurs occur just below the surface of the ocean. This narrow zone (less than twenty meters), known as the "reef crest", forms the leeward end of the spur and groove system. Within this zone are massive growths of fire coral (Millepora complanta), and sections of walls and channel bottoms are lined with the urchin Diadema antillarum. Of interest, elkhorn coral (Acorpora palmata), usually characteristic of reef crest areas, is not found here. Moving seaward, fire corals still dominate; however, elkhorn coral clusters are irregularly interspersed. Although A. palmata was apparently the primary construction element of the spurs, they are not presently dominant. 

Likely the most important section of the Looe Key reef occurs beyond, where spur profiles reach seven meters in height. Here, active building occurs by the dominant "mountainous" star coral (Montastraea annularis). 

Star corals are also found on spur tops, accompanied by species of brain corals.

The last section of spurs is a relatively flat extension of the preceding high-profile portion of this extraordinary system. Abundant invertebrates found within this extended area include bivalve molluscs, gastropods, and brittle stars. Encrusting species of red algae comprise the dominant vegetation, although algal flora is distinctly barren here, the likely result of sea urchin grazing pressure.

The fore reef exhibits the greatest number and variety of sanctuary fishes; virtually every species within the system is found here, with the exception of strictly deep-water species. Particularly abundant are tomate grunts (Haemulon aurolineatum) and yellowtail snappers (Ocyurus chrysurus).

The deep reef, which occurs as a sandflat at about nine to eleven meters in depth and gradually slopes downward, begins at the seaward edge of the spur and groove system. The western half of this sandflat is intersected by a 144

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

deep reef, occurring just beyond the end of the spur and groove system as a finger-like extension of scattered coral outcrops.

To the south, the deep reef gradually changes from flat to a slope of increasing steepness. In its deepest portions, the slope forms a small drop-off, which ends at about 30 to 35 meters on a sandflat. Similar to the patch reef, octocorals dominate the deep reef area, particularly plexaurids. Within the 

Looe Key system, two deep-water species of octocorals are found only here: 

Ellisella barbadensis and Iciligorgia schrammi. Relatively common are sponges, polychaetes, and species of red, brown, and green algae. 

Several species are only found in the deep reef zone, including purple fish (Chromis scotti), sunshine fish (Chromis insolatus), spotfin hogfish (Bodianus pulchellus), and scamp (Mycteroperca phenax). Abundantly present are butterflyfishes, hamlets, groupers, blue chromis, and creole wrasses.

Uses The lack of phytoplankton at the seaward edge of the deep reef creates a particularly clear blue-water environment, which is partially located within the sanctuary's parameters. Several species of commercial and recreational importance are fished here including: amberjack, grouper, hammerhead shark, king, Spanish and cero mackerel, dolphinfish, ballyhoo, and pompano. 

Approximately one kilometer of sand bottom separates the end of the deep reef from the sanctuary's final zone, the deep ridge. The ridge lies parallel to the margin of the continental shelf, and exhibits very little profile. 

Only a few meters wide, the deep ridge includes an outcrop of living coral reef at about forty-five meters in depth. This reef is formed primarily of plate-like colonies of Montastraea cavernosa. Deep-water octocorals are also present. The biological importance of this final sanctuary zone to the total Looe Key ecosystem is not yet completely known or understood. Management The sanctuary is administered by an on-site staff consisting of a manager, interpreter, two rangers and a secretary who are provided by the Florida Department of Natural Resources through a cooperative agreement. The 

Looe Key National Marine Sanctuary has received approximately 60,000 visitors a year.

Regulations Sanctuary regulations prohibit in whole or in part the same activities as at 

Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary.
