Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary 

Fagatele Bay National Marine Sanctuary lies 2,300 miles southwest of 

Hawaii, on the southwest shore of Tutuila Island, American Samoa. At 163 acres, or one quarter square nautical mile, Fagatele Bay is the smallest biological site within the National Marine Sanctuary system. This sanctuary is also the system's newest. The bay is characterized as a pristine marine environment typical of terraced coral reef ecosystems associated with high volcanic islands of the warm Pacific Ocean. Significant marine and shoreline habitats support a diverse array of marine mammals, birds, fishes, algae, and benthic organisms. Around the bay, avifauna are extremely abundant, dominating all other bay species. The coastal forest near the southwest end of Fagatele Bay is the primary roosting area for thousands of "flying foxes", or fruit bats (the only mammalian species endemic to American Samoa).

Tutuila is the largest and most populated of the seven islands comprising American Samoa, a United States Territory. All of the islands are volcanic in origin. American Samoa forms the eastern portion of the Samoan archipelago, and is located approximately 1,000 miles south of the Equator. 

Tutuila is the top of a composite volcano rising about three miles from the ocean floor. Consisting primarily of basaltic rock, the Island's bulk is formed of rough and ropey lava flows. Rapid submergence of the Island during the most recent sea level rise (Pleistocene Era) resulted in limited and discontinuous areas of fringing reefs around Tutuila. The volcanic composition of the Island indicates deep-depth contours near the shorelines. Tutuila's coasts are irregular, rocky, and composed of steep cliffs, except in areas near the mouths of drowned alluvial valleys. 

Fagatele Bay was formed in recent geologic times when the ocean breached the seaward side of Fagatele Crater and flooded the cone. Rocks around the bay are volcanic and slope soils are silty clay loams. Beaches are primarily composed of calcareous sand, with some volcanic sand. Sand deposits extend underwater offshore for twenty to thirty feet, then merge with the reef platform, which is primarily composed of consolidated limestone and encrusting coralline algae. 

Reef platforms near the beaches are about two hundred feet wide, and occur at water depths of around two feet. Bottom relief on the platform averages one foot. In varying widths, these platforms fringe the interior bay shore. The reef front drops almost vertically to five to ten feet, and then gradually slopes seaward to depths between fifteen and twenty feet. The slope extends up to 300 feet offshore, and also contains separated pinnacles rising to within four or five feet of the bay's surface. The bay bottom, covered with rubble, occurs at about 120 feet, approximately 1100 feet west of the bay's pocket beach. 146

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

Resources Corals are the most conspicuous members of the bay's benthos. The coral reef system of Fagatele Bay is typical of shallow, clear, tropical waters; reef construction occurs by corals and coralline algae. The community is diverse, with dominant coral species including Pocillopora verrucosa, Favia sp., Galaxea sp., Goniastrea sp., Acropora humilis, Porites lutea, and Palythoa sp.

Prominent algae include Cheilosporum sp., Bryopsis sp., and Halimeda sp. 

Notable Bay invertebrates are anemones, lobsters, limpets, clams, octopi, sea cucumbers, and sea urchins.

Not surprisingly, fish species are diverse and abundant throughout the bay. Over one hundred species are identified in the bay's reef front area; sixty additional species are found in the reef flat area. Consistently numerous are damselfishes, surgeonfishes, wrasses, butterflyfishes, parrotfishes, and anemonefishes. In addition, southeastern portions of the bay are frequented by hawksbill, leatherback, and green sea turtles. 

Occasional Pacific bottlenose dolphins and spinner dolphins are seen in the bay. Less frequently, sperm whales or humpback whales may be observed in waters surrounding the Island. Management

As the most recently-designated national marine sanctuary, full staffing for on-site administration and management of Fagatele Bay has not yet been completed. Sanctuary activities are currently coordinated between National 

Program staff in Washington, D.C. and an on-site coordinator at American 

Samoa's Development Planning office. The planned sanctuary staff will include a manager, assistant manager, enforcement officer, and possibly an education coordinator.

Fagatele Bay is located entirely within Territorial waters; therefore, successful sanctuary operation requires close cooperation between NOAA and Territorial agencies. It is particularly important that sanctuary programs are sensitive to and compatible with Samoan culture. To strengthen ties and enhance understanding of the sanctuary program, the sanctuary manager will train local staff and volunteers to recognize situations potentially threatening to the natural resources and environmental quality of the sanctuary. Enlisting the assistance of volunteers will provide an "early warning system” to aid in effective handling of problems and violations.

In addition, planned educational programs will focus on the development of a coordinated curriculum among American Samoa, Hawaii, Guam, the South Pacific Commission, and other Western Pacific agencies. Overall objectives of the programs include raising public awareness of the value of marine resources, the importance of coral reef ecology, and the role of healthy reefs in enhancing fishery resources. The development of a visitor interpretive center will further these objectives.

Monitor National Marine Sanctuary 

Underwater archeology, a relatively new discipline, is bringing increased historical awareness to the public. Historic shipwrecks present a discernible "fingerprint" of the society that produced the ship and its contents, and can reveal, if approached scientifically, much useful information about the people who constructed it and operated it. Other nations have acquired considerable experience in the recovery and curation of historic shipwrecks, notably the well-preserved 1628 warship Wasa, recovered intact from Stockholm Harbor in 1961, and the 1545 Tudor warship Mary Rose, recovered from 40 feet of water near Portsmouth, England. The United States has learned many useful lessons from studying these projects as we develop our own policies for the management of historical and cultural resources. Resources The sunken remains of the Civil War ironclad warship U.S.S. Monitor are located approximately sixteen miles south-southeast of the Cape Hatteras 

Light, North Carolina, in 220 feet of water. Sanctuary boundaries incorporate a vertical water column, one mile in diameter, extending from surface to sea bottom.

The U.S.S. Monitor was a queen among ships. It was the first ship built entirely of iron, the first ship with a revolving gun turret, the first ship with marine toilets below the waterline, the first ship with an anchor that could be raised or lowered below deck, the first with forced-air ventilation, and the first ship to be designated a national marine sanctuary and also a national historic landmark. 

The Monitor wreckage represents a unique legacy from America's past: 

Its ironclad design configuration - commonly referred to as a "cheeseboxon-a-raft" was the forerunner of a new era in naval architecture and warfare. When it was launched January 30, 1862, the ironclad proved 148

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

impregnable to shot and shell, as promised by the ship's designer, Swedish American John Ericsson. It withstood the most celebrated battle in 

American naval history that of the two ironclads, U.S.S. Monitor and 

C.S.S.Virginia (U.S.S. Merrimac) - fought near Hampton Roads, Virginia, on March 9, 1862. The battle, which ended in a draw, nevertheless revolutionized navies through world-wide recognition of the preeminence of ironclad over traditional warships. While being towed in preparation for operations against Ft. Fisher, North Carolina, the Monitor was unable to weather heavy gale-driven seas and sank off Cape Hatteras, on December 31, 1862, taking 16 officers and crew members to their deaths. 

Discovered in 1973, the ship's present condition is directly related to damage which occurred at the time of sinking, deterioration resulting from more than a century of immersion in sea water, and suspected depth charging during World War II by coastal defense forces. The Monitor's hull lies inverted and is supported off the sea floor by the displaced turret. 

Although there is extensive damage to the lower hull forward of the midships bulkhead, much of the remaining ship is excellent condition, considering the length of time it has been subjected to almost constantly changing current directions and velocities. These changes are influenced by eddies created by the Gulf Stream, to the east of the site. Current directions have been observed to change 360° within a 24-hour period; velocities vary from 

0.02 to over 1.5 knots at the bottom; and surface currents are considerably stronger. The ocean floor in the vicinity of the wreckage is composed of sand, shell hash, and clay. Bathymetric profiles indicate the bottom surface slopes gently to the southeast. Visibility in the area varies from 10 feet to over 100 feet, depending upon turbidity, the presence of microorganisms, and the intensity and direction of the currents. 

The state of North Carolina nominated the Monitor as a national marine sanctuary in September 1974. The comprehensive management afforded by sanctuary status provides for study of the ship's design and construction, and analysis of Monitor artifacts to permit insight into the technological development of an industrial society. One such study resulted in the recovery of the ship's unique four-fluked anchor in 1983.

Management Goals for the Monitor's long-term management include: the protection and preservation of the ship and its records, documents, and archaeological collections; the systematic scientific recovery and dissemination of historical and cultural information from the site; the preservation and development of the physical remains of the Monitor to enhance the ship's significance and interpretive potential; and the enhancement of public awareness and understanding of the Monitor as an historical and cultural resource through educational services and materials. 149

Since designation in 1975, twelve scientific expeditions have been conducted in the sanctuary to collect oceanographic, environmental, engineering and archeological data. NOAA has sponsored five of these expeditions. 

The initial research concentrated on studying the physical condition of the wreck and its interaction with the marine environment. The most extensive expedition was in 1979 when 49 mixed-gas lockout dives were conducted from a submersible, setting the record for the deepest archeological excavation in the United States. Over 100 artifacts, including the Monitor's lantern and anchor, have been recovered from the site. 

On March 9, 1987, in conjunction with the 125th anniversary of the battle between the U.S.S. Monitor and C.S.S. Virginia (Merrimac), the Mariners' 

Museum in Newport News, Virginia, was designated as the principal museum for the Monitor Collection of Artifacts and Papers. Future planning calls for a permanent interpretive exhibit, archives and research library to be established at the museum. The museum also plans to build traveling exhibits. Regulations

Sanctuary regulations prohibit certain activities in whole or in part. These include: anchoring, stopping, or drifting within the sanctuary salvage or recovery operations • diving ⚫ dredging or wrecking devices ⚫ underwater detonation • seabed drilling or coring • cable laying ⚫ trawling.

Programs and Activities Legislative History of the National Marine Sanctuary 

Program Official references to the notion of marine sanctuaries are noted as far back as 1966, but the legislative history of the National Marine Sanctuary Program dates back to 1968 when several bills were introduced in the 90th Congress to establish marine sanctuaries off the coasts of California, Massachusetts, and New Hampshire. These bills were largely the result of concern over the degradation of popular marine recreation areas, such as garbage and oil washing up on popular beaches. The scientific community also expressed concern over the potential effects of dumping waste 150

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

300 UE lities of t tens

JULIA 

XCITE anten 불 ​· · Tine tok ga hex materials into coastal waters. For example, chemical DDT washing into the ocean off the south Texas coast caused the density of speckled trout to drop from 30 per acre in 1964 to 0.2 per acre in 1969. It was estimated that onefifth of the nation's shell fish were being lost to health-endangering pollution. A coral reef system off Florida's coast was showing signs of dying, and some scientists were calling a 100-square-mile area off the coast of New Jersey a "dead sea”.

In the wake of such reports, some 30 bills were introduced in Congress to establish sanctuaries where all harmful activities would be prohibited. 

However, because many of these bills were directed at introducing moratoria on mineral exploration, specifically oil and gas, they encountered strong opposition. As a result, they were not reported out of the House 

Committee on Merchant Marine and Fisheries.

Throughout the hearings on various sanctuary bills, whether related to oil and gas exploitation moratoria or to ocean dumping, several themes became apparent. Many users were concerned over possible restrictions on legitimate uses of coastal waters or resources under these waters, should a sanctuaries bill be passed. The Department of Defense was concerned about having to curtail defense related activities, while the Department of the Interior was concerned with interference with its Outer Continental 

Shelf (OCS)leasing mandates.

On the other side, many fears were expressed that some of our resources were in danger of being destroyed, and that steps should be taken to ensure against permanent or potential damage, and to preserve our resources. 

Marine sanctuaries were proposed as conservation areas, research laboratories, outdoor museums for education and sources of aesthetic enjoyment. Others proposed that marine sanctuaries be set aside to protect unique, rare or representative features of oceans, coastal and other waters and total ecosystems. Still another concept that emerged from these hearings was that of sanctuaries as multiple-use areas in which management regulations would primarily serve to protect values related to conservation, recreation, ecology or aesthetics.

Against this background, the President's Council on Environmental 

Quality (CEQ) published a report, "Ocean Dumping - A National Policy," 

in October 1970. The CEQ report summarized the immediacy of the problems created by disposal of wastes at sea and the need for clear national policy and legislation to regulate the pollutants being added to the oceans by the United States. It also called for appropriate international action. 

Early in the 92nd Congress, President Nixon submitted proposed legislation to implement the recommendations of CEQ's ocean dumping report.

The bill was introduced by Senator J. Caleb Boggs for himself and 37 

Senators as S. 1238, and was referred jointly to the Committees on Commerce and Public Works. Other bills were also introduced to regulate or ban the disposal of waste materials in the ocean. These included S. 192, in- 

WADVARD I AW LIRRARV 

151

troduced by Senator Nelson, S. 1082, introduced by Senator Case, and S. 

1286, introduced by Senator Boggs. Hearings were held in March and April 1971. On August 4, 1971, the 

Subcommittee on Oceans and Atmosphere ordered H.R. 9727, the companion bill to S. 1238, to be reported to the full Senate Committee on Commerce. Three months later, the Commerce Committee reported H.R. 9727, with amendments in the nature of a substitute text with an amended title, to the Senate on November 12, 1971. It would be another year before the legislation would be approved by both Houses of Congress. 

The bill acted upon by the Senate on November 21, 1971, contained only two titles: Title I "Ocean Dumping" and Title II "Comprehensive 

Research on Ocean Dumping". Significantly, when a marine sanctuary provision (Title III of H.R. 9727) was proposed to the Senate, it was soundly rejected after vigorous debate. Two principal reasons were given for the rejection, one based on a jurisdictional conflict within the Senate Committee structure, and the other on U.S. jurisdiction in the oceans. The debate within the Commerce Committee centered on the possibility that marine sanctuaries might constitute a "creeping jurisdiction" beyond the legal U.S. territorial sea limit of three miles, should some of the sanctuaries be located in high seas waters. Senator Hollings, who chaired the Commerce Committee, observed: "The issue is not the usefulness or desirability of marine sanctuaries... the issues are how to establish them, to whom they will apply, and with what ramifications on other interests of the United States. . . . In raising the question of marine sanctuaries in international waters, we are caught in one of the most complex international legal arenas known today. 

And when all of the interests (including international regulation on ocean dumping) are weighed in the balance, I feel we must reject the proposal.' 

However, Senator Hollings continued, the Committee considered that marine sanctuaries were "appropriate where it is desirable to set aside areas of the seabed and the waters above for scientific study, to preserve unique or rare characteristics of the ocean, coastal, and other waters and their total ecosystems. . . . We envision such sanctuaries as natural areas set aside primarily to provide scientists to make baseline ecological measurements... and it was with these thoughts in mind, that the Committee on Commerce reported out favorably S. 582, the National Coastal and Estuarine Zone

Management Act of 1971, which contains a provision for estuarine sanctuaries.. Sanctuaries under that act, however, could not extend seaward farther than the limits of the territorial waters of the United States." 

The principal objection in the Senate debates over marine sanctuaries had to do with the appearance that Title III would extend the jurisdiction of the United States over the water column - as opposed to merely the shelf itself overlying the outer continental shelf, in contravention of recognized international law which regards such waters as high seas. 152

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

The other major objection to the marine sanctuaries provision in the Senate was based on a potential conflict over U.S. interests in the outer continental shelf. These interests, from the Senate Interior Committee's perspective, were oil and gas exploration. One Senator posed a possible scenario in which one Cabinet member was selecting areas of OCS for leasing while another Cabinet member was selecting the same areas which would not be leased, and said that such a situation "would only promote mischief, conflict and ineffective government within the executive branch.' 

Two Senators suggested an amendment placing a two-year moratorium on offshore drilling along the Atlantic coast to permit the "establishment of marine sanctuaries where there are unique research, conservation, recreational, ecological, or aesthetic values." The proposed moratorium also would allow time for independent evaluations of the risks of off-shore drilling before the proposed sale of oil leases in early 1973, they said. 

However, the contrary view prevailed. Letters of opposition to the establishment of marine sanctuaries were read into the record from the Departments of State, Defense, Interior and Commerce, and the Office of Management and Budget. Title III relating to marine sanctuaries was soundly rejected. After many additional proposed amendments, the Senate bill entitled "Marine Protection and Research Act of 1971,” was passed in 

November 1971 by a vote of 73-0.

Meanwhile, in the House, H.R. 9727 was reported by the Committee on 

Merchant Marine and Fisheries on July 17, 1971. The Committee's bill clarified and strengthened the enforcement provisions of Title I and added two new titles. Title II provided authority for short-range research by the 

Secretary of Commerce on the environmental effects of ocean dumping.

Title III authorized the Secretary of Commerce to establish marine sanctuaries in cooperation with affected states, and, where necessary, with the governments of other countries. The title of the bill was the "Marine 

Protection, Research, and Sanctuaries Act of 1971."

Approximately 30 other bills on ocean dumping were considered by the 

Committee on Merchant Marine and Fisheries during the 92nd Congress, most of them containing marine sanctuary provisions. In the preceding Congress, eight bills were before the Committee explicitly calling for the establishment of marine sanctuaries; legislation for this purpose had also been introduced in two or three Congresses before that. 

The principal issue in the House debates over marine sanctuaries was the adversarial role that sanctuaries might play in inhibiting off-shore exploration for oil and gas. The major fight against the marine sanctuary provision was led by Chairman of the Interior and Insular Affairs Committee. He contended that the marine sanctuary provision was "directed at the mineral leasing program authorized by the Outer Continental Shelf Lands Act over which the Interior and Insular Affairs Committee has oversight responsibility." He further claimed that his committee was not advised of this legislation and that the Department of the Interior was not offered an 

WADVARD LAW LIRRARV 

153

An unam U! Lat 3 -丹​m湯

kna DA www opportunity to testify on Title III. He therefore moved to strike Title III, stating that the title was a Committee amendment and not part of the Administration's bill, and furthermore that the Department of the Interior was "uniquely well-qualified to identify those natural values deemed worthy of consideration in the establishment of marine sanctuaries." His amendment to strike Title III was supported by many Members, but it was also vigorously opposed.

The opposition to this motion sometimes took the direction of a direct attack on offshore oil and gas exploration. A New York Congressman cited the potential devastation that oil spills off our coasts create, and while recognizing the national interest in developing new energy sources, he said, "we may erroneously risk sacrificing long-term values for short-term gains."

Along with another Representative from New York, he offered an amendment which would preclude the Secretary of the Interior from issuing any new leases for the mining or extraction of any oil from any area designated or under study for possible designation as a marine sanctuary. The amendment was defeated.

Other Members who argued against the original amendment said that marine sanctuaries as proposed in Title III were not intended to prevent legitimate uses of the sea. "They are intended," said one Representative, "to protect unique areas of the ocean bordering our country.... A sanctuary is not meant to be a marine wilderness where man will not enter. Its designation will ensure very simply a balance between uses." 

When the debate ended, the view that marine sanctuaries provided a necessary balance in the competing uses of the ocean prevailed. The motion to strike Title III was defeated. On the issue of extending U.S. jurisdiction over the water column out to the continental shelf, one Congressman summed up the House view by stating that, "The question of extra-territoriality is a red herring. This has been handled by an amendment to the bill, and we have limited this (title) to American citizens and have extended the jurisdiction of Title III only to the limits that we may properly and justifiably do under international law." The Chairman of Subcommittee on 

Oceanography offered a further clarification, stating "the designation of a marine sanctuary does not change the United States position in its international relationships. Therefore, the marine sanctuaries cannot infringe upon the normal rights of innocent passage in territorial waters, the rights of navigation through international straits, or the freedom of navigation outside of territorial limits, nor of the rights involving freedom of fishing outside the contiguous fisheries zone. In addition to innocent passage, the right of aircraft flying over is not affected by this bill." The debate being concluded, the question was called. There were 304 yeas, and 3 nays, and 126 not voting. The House passed H.R. 9727 on September 9, 1971.

On October 9, 1972, nearly a year after the House had passed its bill, a Conference Report was issued stating the views of the House and Senate on H.R. 9727. A compromise had been reached on which ocean waters would 154

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

be included in the ocean dumping bill. The Senate had wanted the bill to cover only those waters outside the territorial limits of the United States. 

The House bill extended the coverage to oceans, estuarine and other coastal waters affected by the tides, and the Great Lakes, insofar as the Act regulates dumping of materials. The compromise was the "base line" - the line from which the 3 and 12 mile limits are computed as provided by the Convention on the Territorial Sea and the Contiguous Zone, signed by the 

United States in Geneva on April 29, 1958. 

Otherwise, the Conference adhered, with minor language changes, to the House position. They retained the Title III marine sanctuary provisions, tightening the language to make it clear that the implementation of that title could not in any way conflict with international law or principles. In remarks endorsing the Conference report to the House, one Representative who attended the Conference cited the report as providing an "example for all future environmental legislation . . . because it attempts to guard against over-reaction to pollution problems by establishing a sensible balance between the need to protect and preserve our environment and the need to maintain and promote industrial and economic development.' ,,7 He was partially correct. The Marine Protection, Research and 

Sanctuaries Act of 1972, approved by both the House and Senate on October 13, 1972, and signed into law by President Nixon on October 23, 1972 (P.L. 92-532), did not solve all of the jurisdictional problems that were debated. However, it did set the stage for a rational, consistent approach to evaluating the national interest in the oceans at any given point in time, recognizing that balancing is always a dynamic, never a static process. 

Implementation of the National Marine Sanctuary Program Title III of Marine Protection Research and Sanctuary Act of 1972 

(MPRSA) represented a significant change in the development of federal ocean policies from earlier single-issue environmental legislation, which had been enacted to prevent degradation from such actions as dredging, dumping and drilling; Title III took a balanced approach toward uses of the oceans' resources, treating them as having inherent natural values worthy of being protected or restored. 

MPRSA authorized the Secretary of Commerce, after consultation with the Secretaries of State, Defense, the Interior, and Transportation, the Administrator of EPA and the heads of other interested federal agencies, and with the approval of the President, to designate those areas of ocean waters as far seaward as the outer continental shelf, of other coastal waters where the tide ebbs and flows, or of the Great Lakes and their connecting waters, which he determines necessary for the purpose of preserving or restoring such areas for their conservation, recreational, ecological or aesthetic values.

LADVARD LAW IIQDADV 

155

PURUUMIT (289 100M⠀⠀ In cases where a marine sanctuary may include waters lying within the territorial waters of any State, the Secretary must consult with and give due consideration to the views of the responsible officials of the state involved. 

The Governor of any state whose waters are included in a sanctuary may certify that the designation, or a specified portion thereof, is unacceptable to the state. In such a case, the area certified as unacceptable may not be included in a designated sanctuary until such time as the Governor withdraws the certification.

Prior to designating a marine sanctuary outside the territorial jurisdiction of the United States, the Secretary of State is required to enter into negotiations with other governments for the purpose of arriving at necessary agreements to protect the sanctuary and to promote the purposes for which it is designated.

As a further requirement to consult with affected parties, the Secretary must hold public meetings in the coastal areas which would be most directly affected by such designation, for the purpose of giving proper consideration to the views of any interested party. 

Necessary and reasonable regulations are to be issued to control any activities within the designated sanctuary, but no regulation can be applied to a person who is not a United States citizen if the sanctuary waters lie outside the territorial jurisdiction of the United States.

The law contained stiff penalties for violation of any regulation, up to $50,000 for each violation, with each day of a continuing violation constituting a separate violation. A vessel used in the violation of a regulation may be considered liable "in rem." District Courts of the United States were given jurisdiction over all marine sanctuary cases. 

On March 13, 1974, the Secretary of Commerce delegated the authority for the National Marine Sanctuary Program to the Administrator of the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA). Anticipating the need for establishing sound policies in setting up a very complex program, NOAA awarded a contract to the Virginia Institute of Marine Science (VIMS) to study the history of the Act, including various concepts of marine sanctuaries and how they might be developed, and to obtain views from an array of interested and potentially affected individuals and organizations. 

VIMS conducted a six-month study, then invited approximately 100 people to a National Workshop on Sanctuaries in November 1973 to review and discuss the information assembled by VIMS. Presentations were made by federal and state agencies and private organizations with resource protection programs, as well as the oil and fishing industries. The potential legal, economic, scientific, political and land-use impacts of the program were also discussed. The proceedings of the of the VIMS workshop, published in 

February 1974, were used to develop guidelines setting forth the philosophy and procedures for conducting the marine sanctuary program." 156

MARINE SANCTUARIES Guidelines and Regulations 

NOAA's first marine sanctuary guidelines were issued for comment on March 19, 1974. Those who commented were principally concerned about how NOAA would address the multiple use issue in the final regulations. 

Opinions for and against multiple use were expressed. The final guidelines came down firmly on the side of permitting multiple use to the extent uses were compatible with the primary purpose(s) for which the sanctuary was established. Classifications were created to identify specific purposes for which sanctuaries could be designated, as follows: • Habitat Areas - for the preservation, protection and management of essential or specialized habitats representative of important marine systems; • Species Areas for conservation of genetic resources; • Research Areas for scientific research and educationin support of management purposes, and to establish ecological baselines against which to compare and predict the effect of man's activities, and to better understand natural processes; to preserve esthetic and 

Recreational and Esthetic Areas recreational values; and 

• Unique Areas - to protect unique or nearly one of a kind geologic, oceanographic, or living resource features. 

The final guidelines closely followed the language in the Act regarding the need to consult with other federal agencies, state officials and foreign governments, depending on where the sanctuary was located, and handling of violations of regulations. In the more nebulous area of how sites would be nominated and designated, the guidelines stated that NOAA would stimulate and establish a coherent system of sanctuaries in various categories, leaving the door open to nominations from federal, state or local officials, or any other interested persons.

It was unclear just how NOAA would select from the original nominations active candidates for further consideration leading to designation. The methodology prescribed in the guidelines for soliciting and choosing candidate sites later proved to be unwieldy, and was changed to a systematic process with a scientific basis in a later iteration of the regulations. 

The National Marine Sanctuary Program (NMSP) was officially launched, without much fanfare but with high expectations, when NOAA published final regulations (39 FR 23254) on June 27, 1974. Within a very short time, NOAA received its first nomination from the Governor of North Carolina, James E. Holshouser, Jr., who nominated the seabed where the famous Civil War ironclad, U.S.S. Monitor, sank in a storm in 1862, 16 miles off Cape Hatteras, North Carolina. Over the next year, other nominations were received for coral reefs in the Florida Keys; manatees in Florida's

Crystal River; killer whales in Washington's Puget Sound; and three coastal areas in California (Santa Cruz, Monterey, and San Luis Obispo).

Monitor Designation 

The Monitor site was designated as the nation's first marine sanctuary in record time, just five months. Nominated on September 4, 1974, and officially designated on January 30, 1975, the Monitor National Marine Sanctuary is in a class by itself because it preserves a cultural rather than biological resource. The designation recognized the ship's historic significance and the contribution of its design - still not totally understood by contemporary vessel designers made to marine architecture, which ended forever the era of wooden fighting ships. On the historic occasion of the designation ceremony for the Monitor, Secretary of Commerce Frederick Dent presented a picture of the potential value and role that marine sanctuaries would serve in arriving at a balanced use of the sea's resources: 

There is no heritage upon which marine sanctuaries can rest, no record on which to measure the contribution. But the potential is tremendous viewed in terms of the interrelationship between the marine sanctuary program and those other conservation activities conducted by NOAA... . In this montage, we have what amounts to a substantial body of law spelling out a major national environmental obligation; a commitment to the proposition that as demands for the world's resources increase and intensify, the obligation and the opportunity to provide for balanced, well-managed, environmentally sound use of those resources go hand in hand.

Final regulations for the Monitor National Marine Sanctuary were issued on May 19, 1975. Activities which would adversely affect the sanctuary were strictly prohibited. However, the regulations authorize the Administrator of 

NOAA to permit prohibited activities under certain circumstances (e.g., research on the Monitor) on the condition that “any information and/or artifacts obtained in the research shall be made available to the public." [15 

CFR Section 924.6]. The first of a long series of research efforts was initiated by the Monitor Research and Recovery Foundation, which received the first 

NOAA marine sanctuary permit on June 29, 1976.

Key Largo Designation 

Within less than a year, a second marine sanctuary was designated to protect a 100-square-mile portion of the Nation's largest coral reef system. 

On December 18, 1975, NOAA designated the Key Largo National Marine 

Sanctuary in the Florida Keys.

Fortunately, the reef tract had received some measure of protection by 

Presidential Proclamation in 1960. Under a provision of the Outer Con- 

158

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

tinental Shelf Lands Act (1953), President Eisenhower withdrew the Key 

Largo Coral Reef from leasing and established the Key Largo Coral Reef 

Preserve on March 17, 1960. The proclamation establishing the preserve requested the Secretary of the Interior"... to prescribe rules and regulations governing the protection and conservation of the coral and other mineral resources in this area and to cooperate with the State of Florida and its conservation agencies in the preservation of the reef." [Proclamation No. 3339, 25 Fed Reg 2352 (1960)]. The State then added its own level of protection by designating the reef as the nation's first underwater state park in 1961. 

The designation of Key Largo Coral Reef Marine Sanctuary was an important step for the marine sanctuary program by providing a clear example of how marine sanctuaries could bring federal protection to an important marine ecosystem while still allowing traditional uses such as recreation and fishing to occur. In interim regulations drafted in 1975, NOAA emphasized that removal or destruction of most natural resources was prohibited as was dumping, dredging, filling, polluting, excavating and building. Swimmers and divers could still swim through the coral, but not touch it or break it off; boaters were still allowed to cruise about the reefs but not drop anchor on them. Fishermen were allowed to catch spiny lobster and stone crab, but they had to respect catch limits established to maintain species populations. 

Designation of Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary enabled the federal government to monitor the uses of the reef, and use its enforcement authority where necessary to protect the reef from careless abuse. Equally important was the federal ability to provide money for scientific research on the reef, which has increased understanding of reef ecology in other areas. 

Early Funding History 

The marine sanctuary program was somewhat hampered in the early years because it had no appropriated funds. When Title III was passed, the Congress authorized $10 million for each of the fiscal years 1973-1975. A 1975 amendment extended funding authorization for fiscal year 1976 and the transition quarter at $7.75 million. However, despite the level of authorized support, no funds were appropriated, nor were funds requested for the marine sanctuary program by the administration from 1972 to 1979. The program operated with funds reprogrammed from other NOAA activities, such as the National Estuarine Sanctuary Program (CZMA, Section 315). 

In the 1975 Senate hearing, the NOAA Associate Administrator called the National Marine Sanctuaries Program "a powerful tool for conservation and protection of the Nation's most valuable marine areas." When asked why NOAA had not requested funds to implement Title III in fiscal years 1973-75, he stated that costs could be kept to a minimum by using existing operational capabilities within NOAA and other agencies, such as using the 

Coast Guard for monitoring and enforcement. The Coast Guard had provided operational support to Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary for many years at no cost to NOAA.

159

The Environmental Message 

President Jimmy Carter made this identification of potential marine sanctuaries a topic of major importance in his May 23, 1977, Environmental 

Message to Congress, which stated:

Existing legislation allows the Secretary of Commerce to protect certain estuarine and ocean resources from the ill effects of development by designating marine sanctuaries. Yet only two sanctuaries have been established since 1972, when the program began. 

I am, therefore, instructing the Secretary of Commerce to identify possible sanctuaries in areas where development appears imminent, and to begin collecting the data necessary to designate them as such under the law. 

Because the Alaskan outer continental shelf is particularly sensitive and controversial, I am directing [the Secretary of the Interior] to give special emphasis to it. I have also asked him to work closely with the Secretary of Commerce as she identifies potential sanctuaries in areas where leasing appears imminent.

The President's mandate surprised the environmental community as well as the oil industry on two counts: that marine sanctuaries were being promoted by the administration, and that the preferred location of marine sanctuaries would be primarily where development "appears imminent." 

It was now incumbent upon NOAA to begin to identify possible marine sanctuary sites, acquire the necessary data to support designation, and establish coordination procedures with other agencies, particularly the Department of the Interior regarding offshore oil and gas development. 

NOAA immediately began to develop and publicize draft site selection criteria, and requested federal, state and local agencies, as well as the general public to recommend appropriate sites for NOAA to consider. 

The marine sanctuary program was suddenly on a fast track. NOAA said it would publicize criteria on August 1, 1977, develop a list of prospective candidate sites by September 30, have nominations ready by December 31, and designate marine sanctuaries by May 1, 1978." 

Staff from various ocean offices in NOAA were detailed to the Office of Coastal Zone Management (OCZM) to handle the additional workload. 

However, NOAA also depended heavily on other federal agencies to develop criteria and participate in the screening and final selection process. 

NOAA specifically requested help from the Environmental Protection

Agency and Department of the Interior (Bureau of Land Management, Bureau of Outdoor Recreation, Fish and Wildlife Service, National Park 

Service, and U.S. Geological Survey). 50

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

By February 1978, over 170 nominations were received from public and private sources. After taking a preliminary look at the recommended sites and eliminating duplicate nominations, NOAA reduced the number to about 100 areas which would receive further consideration. 

Although the timetable for designating sanctuaries by May 1978 was unrealistic, NOAA was still optimistic about complying with President Carter's mandate. In a May 1978 letter to the White House, NOAA stated that its "present objective is to designate five marine sanctuaries in the first quarter of 1979, which will be preceded by interagency consultation and informed White House clearance in the fall of 1978. An additional five marine sanctuaries will be designated by early 1980 and in each subsequent year, up to a total of 25 to 30 sanctuaries.” ,,10

NOAA established an Office of Ocean Management which would be "responsible for bringing NOAA's scientific enterprise and information base to bear on ocean resource use decisions being made throughout the government. Our objective is to assure comprehensive evaluation of the full range of ocean values affected as various agencies make mission-related decisions.... We hope to find ways to assure coordinated national decisions for optimum use of marine areas without further complicating federal decision making and regulations."

NOAA determined that the next marine sanctuary would be the Flower 

Garden Coral Reef in the Gulf of Mexico. Others would soon follow in areas offshore of California, Massachusetts and Alaska. These plans were contained in a "Plan to Implement the President's Mandate," which stated: "Priority must be given to those areas where development is most imminent. 

Two such areas have been identified: Lower Cook Inlet, Alaska, and Georges Bank, Massachusetts, which are scheduled for oil and gas lease sales in October and November 1977. There may be other areas under equal threat of development and they should be identified and given equal priority in the screening process.' ,,12 

Congressional Oversight of the National Marine Sanctuary 

Program NOAA's plan to provide comprehensive management for ocean areas between the edge of the Outer Continental Shelf and the ocean high tide line was received with apprehension by the federal agencies which regulated oil and gas on the OCS, and by some Members of the Congress. At the oversight hearings on the marine sanctuary program in 1978, both the House and Senate committees proposed amendments to tighten the designation procedures. The Senate Committee added a provision for legislative veto of marine sanctuary proposals, allowing 120 legislative days for the veto of sites that encompassed over 1,000 square miles. Congress sent a very clear signal that NOAA had probably overreacted to President Carter's mandate, and in any event was going far beyond the legislative interest of Title III.

161

B An WISH funovi í Complicating NOAA's position was the strong Congressional objection to the specific proposal for designating East and West Flower Garden

Banks Marine Sanctuary in the Gulf of Mexico. The Draft Environmental 

Impact Statement (DEIS) for Flower Gardens was published on April 13, 1979, and public hearings were held in May. The Final Environmental Impact Statement (FEIS) was scheduled for release in April 1980, but the project became too controversial for NOAA to proceed. A Congressman from Louisiana wrote to the Secretary of Commerce, criticizing NOAA for proposing a five-year oil and gas moratorium in Flower Garden Banks without consulting the Department of Energy or interested Members of Congress. He also took issue with NOAA's general approach to the designation process saying that where existing regulatory authority may be implemented to protect the values identified in Title III, a sanctuary should not be established. On August 6, 1979, the same Congressman requested that the 

Comptroller General of the United States conduct an immediate investigation of the marine sanctuary program and all of its policies to see if it served any useful purpose or was merely duplicative of other existing programs which could accomplish the same results with less complicated procedures. 

However, before the General Accounting Office (GAO) could begin its investigation, House Bill, H.R. 5018 was introduced on July 31, 1979, to repeal Title III of the Marine Protection, Research, and Sanctuaries Act of 

1972. In explaining his reasons for wanting to terminate the marine sanctuaries program, the sponsor cited poor performance, duplication of effort with unnecessary additional cost and government red tape, ignoring scientific expertise, and direct conflict with other ocean programs such as oil and gas, and fisheries. Specifically mentioned as examples of poor planning were the proposed designations of Flower Garden Banks, and Georges Bank off the coast of Maine. The bill died in Committee, but the controversy resulted in unfortunate publicity for the marine sanctuary program. NOAA decided it would be best to discontinue pursuing designation of Flower Garden Banks or Georges Bank as marine sanctuaries, and these efforts were dropped.

The report from the Office of the Comptroller General on the marine sanctuaries program was published on March 4, 1981. It was generally supportive of continuing the program, and concluded: "We have pointed out that some overlapping and duplication exists between Title III and other federal laws and regulations. Nevertheless, on balance, and considering the program's legislative history, it is apparent that Title III provides certain benefits not provided in other laws." The report also cited a Congressional 

Research Service opinion that Title III "permits a holistic approach to management of defined marine areas that is not readily attainable through resort to statutes focusing on specific environmental impacts. ,,13

Even as the marine sanctuary program was subjected to critical review during this period, however the Congress also appropriated funds for Title III for the first time in FY 1979 in the amount of $500,000; an additional 162

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

$1.75 million was appropriated in FY 1980. These appropriations supported additional staff to develop new program regulations and work on designation of sanctuaries. 

Procedural Refinements

As NOAA began to evaluate recommended sites, it became apparent that the scope and objectives of the marine sanctuary program needed to be clarified and that extensive coordination and consultation with a wide range of agencies and interest groups was required. The evaluation process necessitated a refinement of procedures which slowed the proposed timetable for sanctuary designation, but also created the need for new program regulations. NOAA proposed the new regulations on February 5, 1979, following consultation with both environmental and industry representatives. The draft version of the regulations elicited extensive public comment. Following full evaluation of these comments, final regulations were published effective 

July 31, 1979.

An oversight hearing was held on February 21, 1980, before the House Committee on Merchant Marine and Fisheries and its Subcommittees. The newly-appointed NOAA Assistant Administrator for Coastal Zone Management characterized the new marine sanctuary regulations as follows: 

The regulations formalize some of NOAA's policies and procedures developed in the administration of the Act and attempt to give the public a better understanding of the objectives of the program. For instance, under the proposed regulations, NOAA must make an initial evaluation of sites recommended for possible marine sanctuary status within three months of the receipt of the recommendation. All interested parties are thereby informed about the status of recommended areas as expeditiously as possible.

The regulations specify criteria that an area must meet to be placed on the initial list of recommended sanctuary sites and later to be selected from that list for further evaluation as an active candidate for designation. The initial criteria articulate the program's emphasis upon the protection of ecological and natural resources. NOAA believes we must preserve those special marine areas which are characterized by outstanding ecosystems and other biological values, and the marine sanctuaries program remains the only legislation which promotes comprehensive, geographically focused regulation of an area to protect such distinctive and valuable resources against the full spectrum of potential threats to their well-being. 

LADWADD IAW LIDDADV 

163

na trike Con WHI The criteria for the selection of a site for consideration as an active candidate for designation recognize the importance of determining, prior to designation, whether existing regulations can protect the area's resources in the absence of designation. An evaluation must be made of the type and estimated economic value of other uses of the resources of the area which may be foregone as result of designation. The sanctuary regulations also require consultation and coordination with the Regional Fishery

Management Council having jurisdiction over waters recommended for sanctuary status, and coordination with affected States.

Finally, the regulations incorporated several procedural amendments which had passed the House in 1978 and which were adopted by the House Merchant Marine and 

Fisheries Committee in the last session, wherever such changes are possible within the present statute. In particular, the designation document by which any sanctuary is created must state the geographic boundaries of the sanctuary, the features of the area that require protection, and the types of activities that may be subject to regulation. 

Likewise, the Governor's certification of unacceptability of the inclusion of State waters within a sanctuary is expanded so the Governor may certify that certain terms of the designation as well as its geographic scope are unacceptable in State waters. 

To manage the process of evaluating more than 100 recommended sites, the regulations provided for the establishment of two lists: List of Recom mended Areas (LRA) and List of Active Candidates (LAC). Since any per son could recommend an area for designation, an initial screening took place, and within three months a recommended site either made the LRA or was rejected. The LRA was published periodically in the Federal Register; however, listing on the LRA did not imply that designation would ever occur. A second review of sites on the LRA was carried out using more stringent criteria, which included an economic analysis of values foregone as a result of marine sanctuary designation. Sites which made it past the second review became active candidates. These sites were then subject to a complicated process involving: public workshops; public hearings; consultation with all relevant federal and state agencies and other interested parties; and publication for public review of a DEIS, draft designation document and regulations implementing the designation, and an FEIS. Upon further consultation with all appropriate federal agencies and Regional Fishery Management Councils, the Secretary of Commerce transmitted the designation document to the President for approval. 164

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

These procedural reforms facilitated the designation of four marine sanctuaries in 1980 and early 1981, the first since December 1985: 

• Channel Islands National Marine Sanctuary: Designated in 

September 1980, the sanctuary supports the largest and most varied assemblages of marine mammals in the world. • Point Reyes-Farallon Islands National Marine Sanctuary (later renamed Gulf of the Farallones National Marine Sanctuary): 

Designated in January 1981, the sanctuary supports the largest seabird rookery in the contiguous United States. 

• Looe Key National Marine Sanctuary: Designated in January 1981, the sanctuary includes an outstanding example of "spur and groove" coral formations and supports a diverse community of invertebrates and fishes. 

• Gray's Reef National Marine Sanctuary: Designated in January 

1981, the sanctuary supports one of the largest nearshore hard bottom reefs in the South Atlantic.

Program Development and Plan 

In the course of designating four marine sanctuaries within five months, it had also become apparent that NOAA's process of handling the continuum from nomination to designation was inefficient and unwieldy. The List of 

Recommended Areas which had been compiled from recommendations of individuals, government agencies and organizations, were often, supported by only limited information, were sometimes construed as attempts to block oil or other development, or were otherwise weak. Additionally, the LRA list itself caused confusion regarding the status of the areas listed, raising the possibility that most of the sites on the LRA would be designated eventually, regardless of how implausible the recommendation might be. There also was concern over how the resources would be managed, since a sanctuary management plan was not prepared until after designation. 

To clear up these additional problems, NOAA published a National 

Marine Sanctuary Program Development Plan (PDP) in January 1982. The 

PDP clarified the goals for the program and established a completely new process for nominating and designating marine sanctuaries. The List of 

Recommended Areas (LRA) was abolished. In its place would be a Site 

Evaluation List (SEL) to be developed on sound scientific and regional principles.

NOAA awarded a contract to develop a list of candidates that would meet a stringent set of scientific criteria that were acceptable to both industry and environmentalists. The heart of the new process was the establishment mentmentmentmentof eight regional teams of marine scientists who would be responsible for suggesting and evaluating sites in their respective areas. Each team was composed of three or four scientists with par- 

LIANWAND I AM LIDDADV 

165

Pay En

WHI Ph ticular expertise and experience in that region. Some of the teams included scientific representatives from the petroleum industry. 

Evaluation of sites for the SEL involved two essential steps: an assessment of the natural resource values and human use values of the area; and an analysis of practical management concerns such as potential human impacts, relationships with other programs, sanctuary size, accessibility, surveillance and enforcement problems, and economic considerations. 

The regional teams nominated from 4 to 19 sites in their respective regions. Brief site descriptions were prepared for each location. These three-or-four page descriptions (with maps and references) were mailed to interested public officials, environmental organizations, and public and private interest groups throughout the region. National environmental organizations and industry representatives received packages from all eight regions. Recipients had 45 days for direct comment on these sites, and up to 

75 days to nominate additional sites.

When the public comment period was over, the regional teams reconvened to study the comments and recommendations. Finally, their choices were narrowed by NOAA to three to five sites per region, representing the best and most unique representatives of the nation's marine ecosystems. In 

August 1983, NOAA published the final Site Evaluation List in the Federal Register. The SEL is made up of 29 sites. Listing a site on the SEL does not mean that the site is a proposed sanctuary, or that it will necessarily be considered as an active candidate for sanctuary designation. However, with limited exceptions, NOAA will only consider those sites on the SEL as active candidates for marine sanctuary designation. Thus, the SEL serves as a pool from which potential marine sanctuaries will be drawn. Sites remaining on the SEL after a five-year period are reevaluated. This procedure effectively removed the criticism, often leveled at the program in the early days, that sites were proposed only to halt offshore oil and gas activities. 

One site was already going through the designation process prior to publication of the SEL. In March 1982, Fagatele Bay in American Samoa was recommended for designation by the Governor of Samoa as a means of protecting the bay's pristine character, and also expanding scientific research of marine ecosystems associated with high islands in the Pacific, especially coral reefs that had been infested by the crown-of-thorns starfish. 

The designation process took four years. Fagatele Bay was designated as the seventh national marine sanctuary on April 29, 1986.

One month after the Program Development Plan was published, NOAA proposed revised regulations for continued implementation of the National Marine Sanctuary Program. The revised regulations codified all of the procedural changes and concepts contained in the PDP and more importantly, laid out detailed site identification and selection criteria. Notably, in connection with the criteria, the revised regulations introduced under the program's mission and goals a new term, “natural resources and human use values," to represent the statutory terms "conservation, recreational, 166

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

ecological, or esthetic values.” After an extended period for comments and review of comments, the Final Regulations for the National Marine Sanctuary Program were published in the Federal Register on May 31, 1983. 

Reauthorization Act - 1984 

Meanwhile, the Congress was preparing for hearings on the reauthorization of Title III of the Marine Protection, Research, and Sanctuaries Act of 1972. 

As in the past, the Congress had some reservations about the authority given to NOAA under Title III to comprehensively manage (i.e., regulate) whole portions of the ocean, notwithstanding the improvements in the National Marine Sanctuary Program following publication of the PDP and Final Regulations.

Senate Bill S. 1102, Marine Sanctuary Amendments of 1983, was eventually passed by the House, where it received strong support even from some of its former detractors because new provisions had been added giving the Congress a greater consultative role. For example, one section contained 14 inter-related standards and criteria, each requiring a Secretarial finding or determination following extensive interagency review. In making a finding, the Secretary was required to consult with: the House Committee on Merchant Marine and Fisheries; the Senate Committee on Commerce,

Science and Transportation; federal and state agencies; and Regional 

Fishery Management Councils affected by proposed sanctuary designations. 

Further, the Secretary was required to prepare, as part of an environmental impact statement, a resource assessment report documenting the present and potential uses of the area, including fishing, minerals, and energy development. Where fishery regulations were necessary, the Secretary had to provide the appropriate Regional Fishery Management Council with the opportunity to prepare the draft regulations. If the Council declined, or failed within a reasonable time to prepare appropriate regulations, the Secretary was required to prepare the draft regulations. 

Finally, S. 1102 established a 45-day Congressional review-and-reporting period prior to final site designation, and established Congressional and gubernatorial disapproval processes. The Congress could disapprove the designation of a sanctuary by a joint resolution, which then required the approval and signature of the President.

Senate bill S. 1102 contained some other notable changes. The rationale underlying site selections was clarified, with emphasis now clearly on areas of national significance. Also for the first time, historical values were recognized as appropriate for protection and conservation. In addition, research and educational programs were considered as necessary and reasonable to of Title III. 

carry out the purposes

Toward the end of the ninety-eighth Congress, the Marine Sanctuary Amendments of 1984 were approved. With the President's approval of 

Public Law 98-498 on October 19, 1984, the National Marine Sanctuary 

Program was reauthorized through Fiscal Year 1988. 167

Marine Sanctuaries Today 

Following the designation of sites in the late 1970s and early 1980s, the emphasis in the National Marine Sanctuary Program shifted to site management, including research, education, and stewardship for public benefit. 

Given the size of some of the sanctuaries and their distance from the mainland, the management task is not an easy one. On-site management programs generally entail: • a management-oriented research program • interpretive programs designed to establish the site's "identity on the water," increase public awareness of sanctuary resources, and build understanding and support for marine protected area management ⚫ on-site protection activities through preventive surveillance, enforcement of regulations, and emergency response in the event of resource-threatening incidents.

In most of the sanctuaries, an on-site manager handles day-to-day activities and problems. The sanctuary manager is an important contact point for the local community and tourists, and plays a key role in promoting public understanding of the value of the site. The sanctuary staff is small, but 

NOAA has been successful in using cooperative agreements through which on-site personnel from federal and state agencies are assigned under contract to certain aspects of sanctuary management, such as surveillance and enforcement. This is a cost-effective approach.

Emergency Response Contingency Plans 

A number of management problems have emerged in the mid-1980's, particularly problems related to resource-threatening events requiring emergency response and extensive follow-up. Key Largo and Looe Key have experienced a rash of accidental boat groundings, ranging from small craft and sailboats to large ocean-going vessels. The larger boats have caused severe damage to certain species of coral that may take hundreds of years to rebuild. The worst incident occurred at Key Largo Sanctuary on August 4, 1984, when the 400-foot Cypriot-registered freighter M/V Wellwood ran aground on Molasses Reef.

Molasses Reef lies inside the southern boundary of the sanctuary and is the most popular reef in the area for divers. The Wellwood is the largest vessel ever to run aground in the sanctuary, and its size resulted in significant environmental damage. The freighter was removed from the reef on August 

16, with the aid of four tugs after all her fuel and 10 percent of her cargo had been off-loaded. Large areas of slow-growing, high-profile coral formations were obliterated where the vessel's hull cut into the bedrock. NOAA immediately began a coordinated research effort to assess the biological damage to the reef and associated fauna. On August 10, the U.S. Depart- 168

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

ment of Justice filed a complaint in U.S. District Court seeking $20 million in natural resource damages caused by the grounding of the M/V Wellwood, and another $3 million for clean-up, restoration, and salvage costs incurred by the United States, including penalties. 

On the West Coast, the most serious threat has been from a major oil spill. On October 31, 1984 the 632-foot tanker T/V Puerto Rican experienced two explosions off San Francisco Bay within sight of the Golden Gate

Bridge. A raging fire hampered salvage efforts, and the tanker drifted into the Gulf of the Farallones Sanctuary, where the stern broke off and sank. A cargo of volatile petroleum products and the tanker's fuel were released. into sanctuary waters, contaminating the waters and causing the death of thousands of birds and other fauna. NOAA initiated several research projects to assess the impacts of oil spills on sanctuary resources. The Justice Department again filed a complaint in U.S. District Court seeking over $1 million in natural resource damages plus $100,000 for violating sanctuary discharge regulations.

When national marine sanctuaries were first established, these kinds of events were not anticipated. However, contingency plans now exist to assure that these areas of national significance are restored when a disaster strikes. 

Cultural Values 

More recently, the National Marine Sanctuary Program has focused its attention on the conservation of submerged cultural resources. The 1984 

Amendments emphasized this aspect of the program by specifying objectives for the preservation of cultural, historical, archeological and paleontological values. The National Marine Sanctuary Program is the only federal mechanism for protection of nationally significant cultural resources beyond the territorial sea. The program's only cultural resource, the Monitor, has always been the exception among the rest of the sanctuaries because its major value is finite, fragile and non-renewable, rather than biophysical and mostly renewable. Cultural resources pose different management problems, especially in finding ways for public understanding of appropriate ways to enjoy the resource and still ensure its long-term survival. 

NOAA will be preparing a new set of National Marine Sanctuary Program regulations to implement the provisions of the Marine Sanctuary Amendments of 1984 (P.L. 98-498). Revisions are necessary to reflect and codify procedural and some policy changes contained in the amendments. 

The new regulations will include a section on "Site Selection for Sites with Historical Qualities of National Significance," to incorporate cultural values. A supplemental SEL will be proposed for historical sites and published in the Federal Register. NOAA will be developing guidelines for submitting applications for archeological research permits. A policy statement on management of historical and cultural resources in all marine sanctuaries was issued in December 1986.
