International Assistance 

The Marine and Estuarine Management Division (MEMD) has a long history of involvement in international activities related to marine protected areas. Beginning with the 1982 World Congress on National Parks and 

Protected Areas held in Bali, MEMD has been active in promoting the management of marine protected areas worldwide. The following is a summary of its major activities: • In 1982, the Division was represented on the resolution committee of the World Congress on National Parks and Protected Areas in 

Bali. The Bali Declaration was instrumental in directing the international community's awareness of marine protected areas. • In 1984, the Division provided funding to the International Union for Conservation of Nature and Natural Resources to publish 

Marine and Coastal Protected Areas: A Guide For Planners and 

Managers. This book is recognized as the source book on planning and managing marine protected areas. • From 1985 to 1986, and as a result of the Bali meeting, the Division collaborated with the U.S. Department of State "Man and the 

Biosphere Program" (MAB) and the National Park Service to plan and conduct an international seminar on marine protected areas. 

The seminar was held in June 1986. Twenty-nine participants from 

22 countries participated in the two week seminar. The program focused on current problems facing the development and effective management of marine protected areas. 

Proceedings from the seminar are currently being published. The proceedings will include an action plan which resulted from some of the issues raised during the 1986 seminar. As a follow-up to the recommendations outlined in the action plan, the division has organized an international network of marine protected area professionals. The network seeks to provide an international forum for managers, scientists, educators, policy makers and non-government organizations with an interest in marine protected areas. The network is small and serves as a vehicle for promoting marine protected areas worldwide. The MEMD collaborates with MAB to produce the newsletter, "The Marine Connection". The newsletter continues to serve members of the network and is the only international newsletter strictly focused on marine protected areas. The Division developed a database of experts in marine protected areas and plans to publish a directory of individuals in the network. • In 1987, as part of network activities, the Division participated in the organization of the 4th World Wilderness Congress', "Ocean 

Wilderness Seminar" in September. In addition, as part of the pre-congress activities, the Division developed and conducted a two day course on management-oriented interpretive techniques 170

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

for marine protected areas. The "Ocean Wilderness Seminar,” a four-day session of the Convention of Delegates to the World Wilderness Congress provided this office with an opportunity to collaborate on the development of a program which focused on the role of marine protected areas in the management of ocean resources for sustainable use. The seminar resulted in the drafting and adoption by the congress of a resolution which articulates the current international philosophy on the role of marine protected areas in large ocean ecosystem management. • Currently, with assistance from staff at several of the national marine sanctuaries and national estuarine research reserves, MEMD provides on-site training on the operation and management of marine protected areas. The sanctuary biologist at 

Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary has provided expert advice to several countries on establishing a mooring buoy system. This system has become widely used in coral reef marine protected areas in reducing anchor damage to the resources. Providing technical assistance and information exchange is a valuable service to the international community of marine protected areas. 

Looking to the Future

Three sites are actively being considered for designation, one in the Pacific, one in the Atlantic, and one in the Gulf of Mexico. Designation of these sanctuaries, one each in FY 1988, FY 1989 and FY 1990, will expand the national marine sanctuaries to ten sites. On-site operations will begin in the fiscal year following designation. 

Cordell Bank 

Twenty miles west of Point Reyes, California, Cordell Bank the northernmost seamount on California's continental shelf peaks at 115 feet below the surface of the ocean. A 101 square-nautical-mile area centered on 

Cordell Bank is currently proposed for designation as the eighth national marine sanctuary. 

Since 1978, the non-profit organization "Cordell Bank Expeditions," has conducted a series of annual high resolution depth surveys and SCUBA dives which have helped identify a unique environment that sustains an ecosystem of over 450 species. The wide variety of bottom organisms living on and around the bank's surfaces include arthropods, worms, protozoa, brittle stars, crabs, urchins, red algae, anemones, sponges, hydrocorals, sea cucumbers, sea stars, decorator crabs, hydroids, tunicates, holothurians, and gastropods. Higher surfaces of the bank are densely packed with layers of these organisms, in some places a foot thick. 

LLAQUADD LAUE LIDDADV 

171

MDH) EX z បង / ใน ศ ศ ST ​www. 枝​科 

​DOMIN 

Although many of the offshore and nearshore species at this site are also found elsewhere, the bank's particular combination of species, and the depth variations at which they occur, is found nowhere else. Especially surprising is the presence in these deep waters of several intertidal species. 

Previously undescribed or unknown species of algae, snails, scallops, and sponges have also been found at Cordell Bank. Researchers believe this special community occurring in stratified habitats is made possible only by the specific Cordell Bank environment of rocky ridges and pinnacles, strong currents, and exceptionally clear water. 

Thriving in this environment is the rare Allopora californica, a purple hydrocoral. This species survives only in very clear, clean water, where it provides habitat for several entirely dependent species found nowhere else: the pink snail (Pedicularia californica); the small barnacle (Armatobalanus neferns); and the polychaete worm (Polydora alloporis). 

The richness of nutrients and organisms at Cordell Bank attracts an unusual number of fin fishes, most abundantly rockfish, of which there are fourteen species. Other important species include lingcod, jack mackerel, and king salmon. Not surprisingly, numerous marine mammals and sea bird species find the waters of Cordell Bank an excellent feeding area. Nine cetacean and five pinniped species have been identified at Cordell, notably Dall's porpoises and California sea lions. Over forty species of sea birds forage in Bank waters, including common murres, Cassin's auklets, pigeon guillemots, western gulls, cormorants, and shearwaters.

Proposed sanctuary regulations would prohibit discharges of oil and other potentially harmful materials, and removal of or damage to benthic organisms (except as specifically permitted for research or educational purposes). Hydrocarbon exploration and development activities could be subject to regulation if, in the future, the Cordell Bank area is no longer excluded from the Department of the Interior's outer continental shelf lease sale program.

NOAA plans to complete the designation process in FY 1988. Because of the proximity of the existing Gulf of the Farallones Sanctuary to the proposed Cordell Bank Sanctuary, NOAA will manage and operate these sanctuaries jointly. 

Flower Garden Banks 

The East and West Flower Garden Banks represent the northernmost living shallow-water tropical coral reef community in the Gulf of Mexico. Located 

115 miles southeast of Galveston, Texas, on the outer edge of the Continental Shelf, the two Flower Garden Banks are situated approximately 10 miles apart. 

They are isolated from other reef systems by over 300 nautical miles (550 km) and exist under hydrographic conditions generally considered marginal for tropical reef formation. The composition, diversity, and vertical distribu- 172

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

tion of benthic communities on the banks are strongly influenced by this physical environment. Epibenthic populations are distributed among several interrelated biotic zones, including a Diploria-Montastrea-Porites zone, a Madracis mirabilis zone, and an algal-sponge zone. 

The complex and biologically productive reef communities that cap these banks offer a combination of aesthetic appeal, and recreational and research opportunities matched in few other ocean areas. These reef communities are in delicate ecological balance because of the fragile nature of coral and the fact that the banks lie on the extreme northern edge of the zone in which extensive reef development can occur. In addition to their coral reefs, the banks contain the only known oceanic brine seep in continental-shelf waters of the Atlantic Ocean. 

Because of these features, the Flower Garden Banks are particularly valuable as resources for scientific research. NOAA announced Flower 

Garden Banks as an active candidate for national marine sanctuary designation on August 2, 1984. Draft proposed regulations for the candidate site have been reviewed and endorsed by the Gulf of Mexico Fishery Manage ment Council. A Draft Environmental Impact Statement/Management Plan and proposed implementing regulations will be issued in the summer of 1988. The major activities proposed for regulation include removing or damaging coral and other resources, depositing or discharging any material or substance, and anchoring by large vessels. NOAA plans to complete the designation process in FY 1989. 

Norfolk Canyon

This site, located approximately 60 nautical miles off the coast of Virginia, is the southernmost submarine canyon in a series of prominent, deep-water features on the eastern continental margin of the United States. Like their counterparts on land, submarine canyons are some of the most rugged features on the face of the Earth. Deep V-shaped valleys punctuate the canyons and swift currents flow around steep, rocky, and often unstable canyon walls. In spite of its ruggedness and depth, Norfolk Canyon supports an abundance of marine life. Huge Alcyanarian tree corals and "pueblo villages" (areas along the canyon wall where large invertebrates and some finfish dig extensive depressions, caves and burrows for their lodging) are especially prominent.

Major human activities pursued on the eastern continental margin include commercial and recreational fisheries, mineral resources development, ocean dumping, and military operations. Although the Norfolk

Canyon area may not provide uniquely important fishing grounds compared with other canyon or intercanyon areas on the outer shelf or upper slope, it is visited regularly by vessels from a variety of fisheries. Squid, red crabs, lobsters, and scallops are actively fished in this area, and it is trawled for various demersal finfish as well. Other species sought commercially in this 173

ни Nam area include butterfish, offshore mackerel, and swordfish. Tilefish, landed both north and south of Virginia and caught primarily in canyon areas, are not fished in Norfolk Canyon.

Because of its distance from shore, Norfolk Canyon is not as heavily used by recreational fishermen as are nearshore waters. However, various offshore pelagic species are sought in this area, among which the white marlin (Teptrapturus albidus) is the most important.

NOAA is using information gathered during a June 1986 scoping meeting at the Virginia Institute of Marine Science (Gloucester Point, VA) in the preparation of a draft environmental impact statement and draft management plan document for this candidate site. Designation is scheduled for FY 1990.

Revision of the Site Evaluation List (SEL) 

Earlier this report discussed the development of the SEL and the 1984 

Amendments to Title III of the MPRSA which clarified the authority of the 

National Marine Sanctuary Program to protect areas of the marine environment with "historical, cultural, archaeological, or paleontological significance. Although some sites on the existing natural resource based SEL, such as Thunder Bay in Lake Huron and Isle Royal-Apostle Islands in Lake

Superior, contain significant shipwrecks, these sites were not selected for that reason.

Revised regulations, based on the 1984 amendments, establish a process to identify and designate historic sites. The process begins with NOAA's appointment of a panel of experts comprising a Marine Cultural Resource Evaluation Team. The purpose of the team is to recommend to NOAA a list of marine sites with historical or cultural values of special national significance.

Following preparation of this list, NOAA will publish a Federal Register notice describing the sites proposed for placement on the SEL. The notice would be available for public comment for at least 90 days. The proposed historic and cultural SEL would then be revised, made final and used to select additional historic sites for designation. 

A process also exists to update the natural resources SEL. The revised regulations call for a reevaluation of "any site remaining on the SEL for five years." Using the Federal Register-public comment process, sites "no longer highly-qualified in accordance with the program's mission and goals" can be removed from the SEL and new sites can be added. This review and reevaluation process will take place in late 1988 through early 1989. 

Issues to be Resolved

While the number of designated sites is likely to increase from seven to ten by FY 1990, the necessity of reducing overall federal spending will not allow federal funding for support of the sanctuary program to increase at the same 174

MARINE SANCTUARIES 

rate. This situation calls for good, creative management at the federal level, including an identification of those aspects of sanctuary operations which are appropriately performed by federal staff, and which activities, such as education and interpretation, can be performed by private foundations, non-profit groups and volunteers. Another area for improvement is program operations. There is a need to standardize on-site operations and procedures. By so doing, the public can be better served and improvements in efficiency can lead to at least modest cost savings. 

Another issue is who should bear the cost of emergency clean-up and mitigation resulting from ship groundings and other accidents. The grounding of the M/V Wellwood on the reefs of Key Largo National Marine Sanctuary caused extensive coral damage. The cost of repair of this damage and an assessment of the recovery of the reef, a long-term process, has been borne solely by the program, using operational funds. Members of Congress in bipartisan bills and public interest groups have been discussing alternative funding proposals including the establishment of an emergency response and damage assessment fund to handle these unexpected and nonrecurring costs.

These issues are likely to be part of the discussion surrounding the reauthorization of Title III. For the time being, however, as the more than one million annual visitors to our national marine sanctuaries can attest, the program is, and will continue, preserving and managing these valuable marine resources for the nation.

The overriding advantage of the National Marine Sanctuary Program is that it is one single program offering administrative and managerial capability for preserving nationally significant marine areas. Without the program, management of a coral reef for example, would be divided among the Minerals Management Service, National Marine Fisheries Service, and respective Regional Fishery Management Councils. The National Marine 

Sanctuary Program combines responsibility and accountability, coupled with research and public awareness programs, that are focused on the wise stewardship of unique marine areas.

Take Pride in America 4 K.P.A 

All Americans should take pride in their outstanding public lands and historic sites that belong to everyone. ... We must all work together for a renewed awareness that these lands are our lands. 

President Ronald Reagan

In a message to the Congress at the time of the 1986 State of the Union, President Reagan inaugurated "Take Pride in America," a public awareness campaign to restore respect for the nation's public lands and to promote voluntary stewardship of our public resources. During the first year of the campaign, many activities both public and private were brought together under the rubric of Take Pride in America (TPIA). Public service announcements produced by the Advertising Council helped to publicize the campaign nationally. Federal land managing agencies incorporated Take 

Pride in America into their normal community and public affairs work. A 

Take Pride in America National Awards Program was instituted to recognize outstanding examples of volunteer land stewardship. States and private sector organizations became increasingly involved in the campaign. 

This chapter examines the events which inspired the campaign, reviews the campaign activities during its first year, and assesses its future prospects for improved management of the nation's public lands. The Council on Environmental Quality has selected Take Pride in America as the subject for 177

th VER 溫​回​情 ​HOM www பஜார் Bru இதல் ww www www special focus in this Environmental Quality Report because of its unique potential for involving so many individual Americans in the conservation and wise use of the nation's valuable natural resources. 

The Public Lands Historical Background 

When the nation was young, vast areas of land came into the public domain as the United States acquired new lands along our expanding frontiers. In 

1812 the General Land Office (GLO) was formed to oversee the disposition of this land.2 The GLO transferred much of the land to the states for public purposes such as higher education; to the transcontinental railroads; and to homesteaders anxious to take advantage of the new nation's abundant resources and the chance to share in the land that Thomas Jefferson had earlier called sufficient to "the thousand thousandth generation." The great wealth created by the settlement of the West and the industrial revolution in 19th Century America made possible a large, well-educated, middle class society that enjoyed increasing leisure and widely-shared affluence. 

Americans' fascination with the West, first through art and poetry, and later through personal experience afforded by railroad travel for personal pleasure, led to a popular movement to preserve parks and places of natural beauty or curiosity. 

By 1872, Congress had created Yellowstone National Park, an important milestone in federal retention of the public lands. Shortly thereafter the foundations were laid for a forest reserve system. In 1905, during the Ad ministration of President Theodore Roosevelt, Congress transferred 85.6 

million acres to a bureau in the Department of Agriculture that would become the U.S. Forest Service. The idea of federal responsibility for land management was further advanced in 1916 when President Wilson signed the bill which formally established the National Park Service in the Department of the Interior.

In the ensuing years additional prime forest lands were designated as "national forests," and areas of scenic or historic significance were added to the National Park System. The GLO continued to dispose of the remaining lands until passage of the Taylor Grazing Act of 1934. This effectively ended the era of major disposal of public domain lands and established the Grazing Service in the Department of the Interior. Rexford Guy Tugwell, one of 

FDR's "Brains Trust," wrote that with the Taylor Grazing Act had ended "private settlement which has strongly influenced the shaping of American character, and the establishment of individual enterprise as the dominating feature of American economic life." Two decades later, in 1946, President 

Truman merged the Grazing Service and the GLO to form the Bureau of Land Management (BLM). Another major federal land managing agency is the Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS), which since 1939 has also been a bureau in the Department of the Interior. Until then the FWS was two separate bureaus: the Bureau of Fisheries, which was in the Department of 178

Commerce, and the Bureau of the Biological Survey, which was originally in the Department of Agriculture. 

Today the U.S.D.A. Forest Service, the Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS), the BLM and the National Park Service manage most of the 732 million acres of federally owned land (Table 1). Other federal landholders include other bureaus within the Department of the Interior (DOI), as well as the 

Department of Defense (DOD), Department of Energy (DOE) and the 

Tennessee Valley Authority (TVA). In addition, states and local governments own 154 million acres of land. This means that nearly 40 percent of the nation's land is publicly owned.

Table 1. Public land acreage, by agency. 

AGENCY 

BLM FS FWS NPS DOD

The Problem Abuses of the Public Lands 

On February 27, 1985, during testimony before the United States Senate Committee on Energy and Natural Resources on the Department of the Interior's proposed budget for fiscal year 1986, Senator Bingaman of New 

Mexico asked Secretary Donald Paul Hodel a question about the theft of one of the largest petrified logs from the Bisti Wilderness Area, and standing fossilized trees from Fossil Forest: 

Senator Bingaman: We've had a problem in New

Mexico recently with the theft of several large petrified logs from one of our wilderness areas because of lack of adequate monitoring of this area. . . . Is [your budget] going to provide adequate resources to effectively carry out your mandate in this area [?] 179

開 ​Hayowaty www. Ebm in вильно Midl EN hots 

Secretary Hodel: Knowing that hundreds of millions of acres are under BLM management... I am not particularly optimistic that we can protect all of the time all of the resources that are under our management in the fashion that would always prevent the situation you described from taking place.... It's a broader problem and I don't know that there are enough people or money in the Federal Treasury always to protect all the resources. 10 This answer ultimately did not satisfy the Secretary himself.11 Working with others in the administration, he investigated the larger question of abuse of the public lands. The objective was to work out a practical, effective approach to what seemed to be a growing problem." Abuses of the public lands have received an increasing amount of attention in recent years. 

Some abuses are malicious and blatantly destructive: defacing a statue in a public park, stealing artifacts from archeological sites or poaching wildlife. 

Others stem more from thoughtlessness: littering, failure to extinguish campfires and cigarettes, or walking off designated trails. Either way, the costs of such abuses are significant. Where the damage is irreparable, part of our historic or natural heritage is lost forever. 

Lack of awareness appeared to be one underlying reason for these neglectful and willful abuses of the public lands. The Take Pride in America campaign was therefore specifically designed to mitigate abuses by increasing awareness and public involvement. 

President Reagan explained his reaction to Secretary Hodel's proposal:

One of the hallmarks of our administration has been the concerted effort we've made to mobilize the American people, to get them involved in helping each other and in community building projects instead of just waiting for the government. We call it our Private Sector Initiatives 

Program. When Secretary Hodel came to me with the idea of a "Take Pride in America" campaign, I thought it was a 13 terrific idea.

Thus, Take Pride in America is intended not only to increase voluntarism on publicly owned lands, but to inspire widely shared land stewardship for communities all across America.

Vandalism and Looting of Archeological Sites 

This category of land resource abuse probably has received the most national publicity, and was a major impetus for the nationwide campaign. 

However, systematic, objective documentation of the problem is surprisingly limited. The National Park Service's Archeological Assistance Program now conducts a nationwide survey on the extent of the problem.

In FY 1985, the 180

only year for which figures are available, federal agencies reported 432 incidents of vandalism to sites on federal land. Table 2 lists the number of cases of vandalism reported by the different land management agencies. 

Table 2. Archeological vandalism of federal lands, FY 85.¹ 

14 

AGENCY 

FS 

Army 

DOI/BIA BLM FWS NPS 

Other 

NUMBER OF INCIDENTS 

233 39 24 100 4 31 2 432 These figures represent only those incidents which federal agencies reported, and are presumed to grossly underrepresent the problem. Many land management practitioners expressed the belief that any official tallies of the number of sites vandalized would be very low. This is because of difficulty of monitoring these sites, and because of the huge numbers of archeological sites spread all over federal lands, only a fraction of which have even been cataloged. For example, the BLM has been conducting its cultural resource management program for 13 years, and has systematically surveyed less than five percent of its lands. About 150,000 sites have been recorded on such surveyed lands, which represents roughly two percent of all federal land. “No large areas on the public lands have been found to be completely devoid of archeological remains... [therefore] estimated numbers of cultural resources on the public lands run literally into the millions."16

Although it is difficult to document the problem of archeological abuses on public lands with scientifically obtained field data, ubiquitous anecdotal evidence highlights the seriousness of the problem. Testimony submitted by the BLM to the House Appropriations Committee explains: 

In areas where looting and vandalism are prevalent, estimates on the scope of the problem may be based on partial information. For example, . . . in southeastern Utah, an area which includes a projected 100,000 prehistoric sites, it has been observed that about 90 percent of the known sites have been disturbed, looted or vandalized. In neighboring southwestern Colorado, it is estimated that more than 60 percent of the recorded Anasazi sites have been damaged. 

Elsewhere, estimates of damages to the more obvious and attractive sites range from 50 to 90 percent. Undamaged sites, i.e., sites that do not show evidence of deterioration except from natural causes are extremely rare. 17 181

10 1000 www 18 Such problems are not confined to the Southwest. Artifacts have been taken from shipwrecks in Biscayne National Park in Florida, from 

Petersburg National Battlefield in Virginia, from Malheur National Wildlife 

Refuge in Oregon, and from scores of other sites nationwide. 

A report written by the Oregon State Office of the BLM notes the danger in focusing only on the Southwest: "While all eyes are on the Southwest, vandalism continues in other regions and violators remain fearless of retribution.">19 

In spite of passage in 1979 of the Archeological Resources Protection Act (ARPA), the problem shows no signs of diminishing in the Southwest or elsewhere:

No noticeable decline has occurred in the extent of the commercial market in Indian artifacts. Nor has there been a decline in depredations by a hard-core group of determined hobbyists, individuals who have spent a lifetime collecting artifacts from public lands. . . . ARPA's passage and subsequent enforcement efforts appear not to have reduced these activities so much as they have pushed artifact collectors and traffickers to much higher levels of sophistication. ... 20 The high prices for artifacts in international art markets continue to be an incentive to both professional traffickers and hobbyists. Collectors will pay as much as $150,000 for an Anasazi basket, or $1,200 for Civil War artifacts, or from $70,000 to $100,000 for a mask from the Pacific Northwest. 21 Most federal officials agree a multi-pronged approach to archeological vandalism is necessary. Although Take Pride in America is mainly a public awareness campaign, government agencies have reinforced their existing efforts under the Take Pride banner. For example, Oregon BLM's OPERA- 

TION S.A.V.E. (Save Archeological Values for Everyone) adopted a three part program: 

1. Public education initiatives including public service announcements to highlight the issues in states and regions. 

2. Employee awareness and enforcement training is being conducted. 22 

3. Increased enforcement and prosecution under ARPA. 

In June 1986, Secretary Hodel wrote to Attorney General Edwin Meese, requesting support from the Department of Justice in conjunction with 

Take Pride in America: 

This effort is intended to increase citizen awareness of their responsibility to help care for our Nation's lands and resources. We hope our message will also encourage the 182

public to report violations of cultural resource laws on public lands.

I would like your assistance in spreading awareness of the importance of crimes against our heritage throughout the criminal justice community. It is important that we prosecute these crimes and impose appropriate penalties which will serve as examples to deter potential offenders." 23 Citizen reporting of incidents to law enforcement officials may be an important way in which the public awareness operates to reduce archeological vandalism if there is some assurance that legal action will result. 

Pierre Indian Learning Center - Explorer Scout Troop 279 Educational Institution – Top National Award Winner (1986) 

In 1982 Explorer Troop 279 entered into a formal agreement with Badlands National Park of South Dakota. Each year since then the scouts have conducted the park's annual bighorn sheep surveys, cleaned litter off trails, maintained wilderness campsites, and conducted other projects in the vicinity of the park. The most notable of these was the restoration of an Indian rock monument. The Sioux Indian Mosaic was built to honor a warrior who ran to warn his village of an impending attack. As with many cultural resource sites, vandals and erosion had greatly damaged the sacred site. 

Post 279 President Rayette Black Elk and his fellow scouts restored and cataloged the site, and cleared the area to facilitate viewing. The cooperative agreement draws on and fosters the Native Americans' sense of pride in their natural and cultural heritage. This project is certainly deserving of one of the top awards for educational institutions, since it encourages a land stewardship ethic among America's young Native Americans, whose past and future are interwoven in the land.“ 24 Exploitation of Fossils The problem of fossil removal from public lands is similar to the looting of human artifacts. It is illegal to collect dinosaur bones from public lands, or those of all other vertebrates, and collection of petrified wood and invertebrate fossils is restricted to those for personal use. The demand for jewelry made with dinosaur bones, and petrified wood for use in landscaping is increasing. This appears to be causing commercial collectors to intensify 183

Hodel to initiate the whole campaign. 26 

Forest Fires

Forest fire is a problem which has been very effectively addressed over the years through the Smokey Bear campaign. An Ad Council campaign from 27 1942-1986,2′ Smokey must be given much of the credit for the decrease in acres destroyed, from 15.5 million acres in 1950 to 3.0 million in 1985. 

However, 3 million acres is still a lot of timber, wildlife habitat and recreational area destroyed. In the five year period from 1979-1983, an average of 

4.1 million acres burned annually, and an average of 81 percent of those fires were caused by humans, mostly through carelessness.28 Although entirely separate from Smokey's efforts, Take Pride has learned much from "his" success in influencing the public's behavior.

Wildlife Poaching The misuse of wildlife resources is frequently mentioned in Take Pride in America literature. As with many other problems, threats to wildlife can run the gamut from the carelessness of a relatively few outdoorsmen, to highly organized criminal activities. Exceeding the take limit is undoubtedly a common violation. Other more serious abuses include: • 50 people were charged with killing eagles and illegally trafficking of feathers. • An Illinois man was convicted of killing two peregrine falcons, which are endangered species.29 • 48 people were arrested in connection with illegal hunts in and near Yellowstone. One guide was paid $20,000 for an illegally taken bighorn sheep.30

The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service Law Enforcement Division, in FY 

1986 alone, pursued over 5,200 violations of wildlife laws on national wildlife refuges, and agents reported an additional 14,000 cases which took place outside of the refuges.

According to the Chief of the Law Enforcement Division of FWS, reported wildlife crime is increasing:

It is no secret that a marked rise in the known incidence of commercial wildlife crime throughout the world has been recorded in the last several years. This is undoubtedly a result of increased enforcement as well as economic pressures and the profitability in large scale, commercially oriented operations involving fish and wildlife. The prices 184

people are willing to pay for wildlife are astounding. For example: jaguar skin - $3,000, polar bear skin - $5,000, eagle feather war bonnet - $5,000, and gyrfalcon - up to $100,000.32 33 These high prices provide great incentive for poachers to take eagles, falcons, bears, reptiles and other species illegally. Commercial markets are not restricted to collectors of exotic wildlife trophies. In North Carolina a ring of poachers sold deer to low income families for $30 to $40 each, and in Georgia, agents uncovered a thriving black market where poachers were selling the meat from alligators, deer, doves and endangered sea turtles.” In all these situations, perpetrators have compelling incentives to violate game laws. Conservation awareness developed through Take Pride in America is unlikely to stop poaching; but it may get others to report poaching incidents.

Many states do have report-a-poacher numbers to facilitate citizen reporting. 

Litter and Vandalism

These two problems are undoubtedly more widespread than any other single problem faced by land managers. For several reasons these are also the two problems most likely to be mitigated by a public awareness effort such as Take Pride. These abuses are not influenced by any compelling monetary incentive. Littering is usually attributed to laziness. Acts of vandalism are nearly as varied as they are common: shooting at signs, carving initials in backcountry cabins, breaking fixtures in public rest rooms, painting graffiti on a wall. Both problems undoubtedly also involve a deeper malaise than the lack of a stewardship ethic, such as the failure to seriously consider the consequences of one's actions.

Several studies document the causes of the litter problem. Keep America 

Beautiful conducted field testing which showed that public land is especially prone to litter problems. For one, people feel no responsibility for public property. Secondly, they feel someone else will pick up after them.34 If a maintenance worker is paid to pick up trash, some reason, then why not throw down a candy wrapper. It is a surprisingly common attitude.

The Texas Department of Highways and Public Transportation con tracted a study to find out who was responsible for the state's huge litter problem. Here is what they learned: • only 53 percent of the litter along Texas highways is caused deliberately; the remaining 47 percent blows out of vehicles • most of the deliberate littering is done by young people 10-19 yrs. 

37 percent / 20-29 yrs. 31 percent / 30-44 yrs. 12 percent men produce 71 percent of the litter, vs. 29 percent for women 185

MILI U 

LIMDOMANDEL DATA DIMDAVY 

⚫ 27 percent of the litter is contributed by drivers of pickup trucks, even though only 11 percent of the vehicles surveyed were pickups. 

In Texas nearly one quarter of all registered vehicles are pickups, so this subgroup undoubtedly makes a significant contribution to the problem. 35 "Don't Mess With Texas"

State Government – Top National Award Winner (1986) 

Texas had a serious and growing litter problem. The State Department of 

Highways and Public Transportation was spending millions of dollars a year to pick up trash and the cost was increasing, 17-20 percent each year. By 

1985 the figure had risen to $24 million dollars a year. The state decided to address the problem at the source, rather than sinking more and more money into removal efforts. They launched a $4 million dollar campaign which included an aggressive media campaign, with a "tough guy" approach similar to the Take Pride spots. In addition the state nurtured citizen involvement in the effort by signing "Adopt-a-Highway" agreements with community and youth groups who take responsibility for maintaining two miles of right-of-way. 36 37 "Don't Mess with Texas" has been an astounding success! First, it increased public awareness of the litter problem, by 28 percent in the first year. Second, the campaign reduced litter along the state's highways; by 29 percent in the first year, and this year litter is down 50 percent." The Texas 

Department of Highways and Public Transportation deserves Take Pride's 

Top Award. The campaign directly promotes the main goal of Take Pride, "increased awareness of the importance of wise use of public lands."38 Fur- thermore, other states will hear about "Don't Mess With Texas" through the Take Pride in America awards, and will be able to adapt the Texas approach to their own problems.

Similar groups of people and similar mind sets are likely involved in vandalism also. However, littering and vandalism are generally difficult to document. For instance, the Texas study was not designed to estimate cumulative litter tonnage for the state. Ironically, government agencies have not systematically collected data on the problems, even though litter removal and vandalism repair costs are incorporated into their normal maintenance budgets. Land managers and users of the public lands have long recognized litter as a serious environmental problem, and for reasons mentioned earlier, public lands receive the brunt of discarded trash. Each year budget of- 186

fices merely assume that a certain number of rest room facilities will be destroyed, or that many tons of litter will have to be picked up. Nevertheless, some data do hint at the seriousness of these two problems. 

Government agencies have to devote substantial resources to clean up after "litter-bugs." The National Park Service has conservatively estimated the amount it spends on litter cleanup: roughly $15 million annually, which is over 3 percent of its park operations budget." Volunteer clean up efforts on other public lands indicate how much trash accumulates. Three projects undertaken in conjunction with Keep America Beautiful Public Lands Day 

1986 collected remarkable piles of debris: 1) Texas Lakeshore Cleanup seven lakes, 25 tons, 2) Lake Mead National Recreation Area, Nevada 1500 volunteers amassed 215 tons, 3) San Diego Coastline 800 people gathered 8 tons.

Urban areas suffer disproportionately as the Keep America Beautiful, Glad Bag-A-Thon statistics in Table 3 suggest.

Take Pride in America The Campaign 

47 

When Secretary Hodel first proposed Take Pride in America, he stipulated the campaign would be consistent with two of the most prominent themes of the Reagan Presidency, the first being voluntarism. Take Pride in America is not being forced on anyone. Participation is voluntary, from the highest levels on down." The Department of Transportation signed on voluntarily. 

The scouts that cleaned up the C&O canal did so because they wanted to. 

The second major theme governing Take Pride in America is consistency with fiscal restraint. The campaign is being implemented with minimal expenditure of taxpayers' money. Usually government agencies work Take 

Pride into their normal program structure and budget. Rather than conducting the Take Pride in America campaign as a separate, distinct program, Secretary Hodel encouraged participation by each Interior agency as an extension of its regular mission to promote wise use of the federal lands. The Secretary has communicated these two conditions, in meetings with the Take Pride Task Force, throughout the campaign. 48 

Campaign Goals

The Take Pride in America campaign's three interrelated goals are to: ■ Increase awareness about the importance and wise use of America's natural and cultural resources. The vast majority of Americans are simply not aware of the lands being managed for them. Most Americans know about Mount Rushmore or the Grand Canyon, but a large portion of the land managed by the federal government goes unnoticed." The most accessible public lands are held by state and local governments. These include county and city playgrounds and parks, state forests and beaches, historic buildings and monuments. Even those who drive by these areas every day, or visit them on weekends, or after work, may not appreciate how important they are to all of us. Often such familiar community facilities are taken for granted. The most fundamental goal of Take Pride is to encourage all 

Americans to be aware of and to appreciate their public lands, whether close to home or far away.

Encourage an attitude of stewardship and responsibility for these resources. Beyond a genuine lack of awareness, few Americans seem to take personal responsibility for the publicly owned lands which they use. A strong land stewardship ethic is expected to encourage individuals to adopt more thoughtful personal behavior, thus avoiding such blights as littering and minor vandalism. The campaign also is intended to get Americans to go 188

beyond personal land stewardship to take responsibility for public lands. 

This may involve picking up beverage containers or wrappers discarded by others; or reporting the actions of others as in the case of wildlife poaching and archeological looting. In the same way that neighborhood crime watch programs encourage people to take responsibility for monitoring each other's property, Take Pride encourages people to take responsibility for the property of all Americans. 50 Promote participation by individuals, organizations, and communities in caring for our natural and cultural resources. One way Take Pride promotes participation is through the awards program identifying and recognizing outstanding stewardship activities in an effort to motivate others to get involved. President Reagan has described such efforts to get 

Americans involved in volunteer activities as "one of the hallmarks of our administration."51

In keeping with this spirit, the third major goal of Take Pride in America is to further promote volunteer participation in the activities of land management agencies. This is to be accomplished by a twofold strategy: 

1. recognize successful initiatives already underway, and 

2. encourage new volunteer activities.52

There are significant benefits derived from volunteer programs on public lands. Volunteers do valuable work; repairing trails, picking up litter, or building new facilities. People who have been volunteers will take pride in the lands that they have worked on. They would not consider littering or vandalizing, and they will watch how these lands are managed and how others treat the land." All the land management agencies should reap this type of benefit from the volunteer programs associated with Take Pride. 53 

Ad Council Campaign

Most Americans who hear about Take Pride will hear about it first through the Ad Council's public service announcements. At the request of the 

Secretary of the Interior, the Ad Council agreed in September 1985 to sponsor the public service campaign on behalf of Take Pride in America. The "Who We Are" description in the Ad Council's 1985-1986 Annual Report indicates how well its goals correspond to those of Take Pride in America:

Since 1942 the common thread among all our campaigns has been the promotion of individual volunteer actions to solve America's problems. And we in turn depend on literally thousands of volunteers from business, the media, and advertizing agencies to get this job done. 

The W.B. Doner Company of Baltimore volunteered its services to develop a strategy to promote the "Protect Your Public Lands Campaign," 189

MUKUURIT TUN 1 INIM⠀⠀ Wha BATH 71 Hom

WPH UREDOMIN 

MUTAN 

SDAH and determined that a tough guy approach would be the best way to effect a change in the way Americans treat public lands. President Reagan and 

Secretary Hodel personally enlisted the support of celebrity spokesmen 

Clint Eastwood, Louis Gossett, Jr., and Charles Bronson.

The Ad Council produced the public service announcements (PSAs) and distributed them to radio and TV stations all over the country in late spring of 1987. In a short taped message which introduces the "spots," Secretary 

Hodel appealed to the station's public service managers to run the ads, emphasizing his hope that the announcements would increase volunteer participation on public lands. In addition, the Ad Council sent out print ads to daily and weekly newspapers and magazines, including trade and consumer press.

In each announcement, the three celebrity spokesmen give slightly different messages around the same theme: 

Bronson: Are you gonna let a bunch of half-wits turn our public lands into a public disgrace? 

Gossett: Some real bad guys are wrecking the public land. Any good guys want to save it?

Eastwood: It seems some misguided individuals out there are abusing our public lands. 

These statements are intended to achieve the first objective of the advertisements; to "break through the apathy that many Americans have toward ,,56 ,,57 the proper use of recreation lands and resources,' »56 and to place a social stigma on the abuse or misuse of public lands. The second main objective is to "convince [Americans] that they should take an active role in their conservation." At the end of each of the spots, the announcer encourages people to write for more information: "Bad guys abuse public land, good guys save it. If you want to help, write Take Pride in America. Post Office 

Box 1339, Jessup, Maryland 20794."58 This is the address of the public response contractor. Prior to May 1987, people who saw the address on posters and booklets wrote an average of 26 inquiries per month (December 1986-May 1987). Since the release of the PSA's the number of inquiries increased dramatically; to 1,131 in June, and 966 in July. This is considered an excellent start, especially since people usually have to see the adds several times before they take time to write in." 59 People who write in are sent: a letter signed by Gossett, Bronson, and 

Eastwood; information on the campaign; a list of the contacts within the person's home state (includes state and federal land management agencies and private organizations involved in Take Pride); and a booklet entitled 

Take Pride in America: Examples of Stewardship from Across the Nation. It is up to the individual to sift through this information, determine what if any activities they would like to participate in, then contact the appropriate 190

agency or group. On request, a copy of an 11-minute video by Louis Gossett, Jr. is available, which is an excellent introduction to the campaign. 

The Advertising Council has sponsored several campaigns similar to 

Take Pride in America: Smokey the Bear and Woodsy Owl mentioned before, and Keep America Beautiful. However, the “hard line” approach used in the "Protect Your Public Lands Campaign” is unique, and although the Ad Council is optimistic about the prospects for a greater awareness of public lands, it is difficult to quantify the extent to which the PSAS have resulted in increased involvement.60

Each of the participating states distribute award applications and receive nominations. A number of these states hold first round awards competitions. Stephen Walker

Top National Award Winner – Individual Category (1986) 

Umpqua River Cleanup

The Blue Ribbon Panel selected Stephen Walker as a top national award winner for organizing the annual cleanup of the North Umpqua River in Roseberg, Oregon. He conceived the project in 1984 to clean up this outstanding trout fishing river, a significant economic and scenic resource for the community. Walker helps organize a combined community effort to clean up a thirty mile stretch of river that runs through BLM, Forest Service, county and private lands. Each year he helps a different local sportsmen's group sponsor and organize the event. In 1986, 300 volunteers collected 20 tons of liter, about 20 percent of which came from federal lands. Stephen 

Walker is a BLM employee, but he has promoted the cleanup on his own time. His efforts benefit the public lands and resources of the area, and have helped raise public awareness of litter problems in remote areas of public and private land. This corresponds exactly with the objectives of Take Pride in America.

A Blue Ribbon Panel consisting of dignitaries from the public and private sector selects the award winners. The stated criteria by which nominations are judged are: • Relevance to Take Pride in America goals; • Effect on the public lands; 192

• Effect on public awareness; and • Degree of involvement/commitment." 

1986 Awards 

The Take Pride Task Force received over 520 nominations in 1986, the first year of the awards. A screening panel selected 127 of these as finalists which were submitted to the Blue Ribbon Panel. The panel selected 38 top award winners (Table 5a). Secretary Hodel, who chaired the panel had this to say about the winners:

Each of these individuals and organizations symbolizes the spirit of America. They have responded in dedicated and innovative ways to President Reagan's 1986 State of the Union call for all Americans to take pride in our outstanding public lands and historic sites. Their efforts serve as models for projects and programs we hope will be emulated in communities across the country." 67 The award winners came from 24 states. Their projects were as diverse as the individuals responsible for them. Table 6 analyzes them according to some of the different goals of Take Pride: 19 of the top projects were conducted on or dealt with public land, 15 projects dealt with litter, 9 were primarily educational, and 2 were fund-raising efforts. [On July 26, 1988, the 1987 National Awards Ceremony was hosted by President Reagan on the South Lawn of the White House, where 94 top winners (Table 5b) and hundreds of finalists and semifinalists were honored for their efforts.]

Federal Administration of Take Pride in America 

Nine federal agencies participate in Take Pride in America through the 

Take Pride Federal Interagency Coordinating Committee. A coordinator has been appointed to the committee from each agency (Table 7). This committee meets frequently so that agencies may inform one another of their activities, thus fostering cooperation.69 

Major events and activities are managed by the Department of the Interior's Task Force on Take Pride in America, with six full time task force members.71 The task force works with participating states and with its private sector partners.

The Task Force operates the National Awards Program and disseminates information through each of the states. The state contact in turn coordinates the Take Pride efforts of federal, state, and local agencies, and private sector groups. 72 193

Department of the Interior

The Department of the Interior, along with the Department of Agriculture, is taking the lead in the Take Pride in America initiative for two main reasons. First, it was Interior Secretary Donald P. Hodel who originally proposed the idea for Take Pride in America. Second, the Interior Department manages about 70 percent of the land held by the federal government, 197

Table 7. Federal partners (agency coordinators)." 

1. ACTION (Wendell Moore) 2. Department of Agriculture (Ron Hunt) 

3.

Department of the Army-Corps of Engineers (Andy Davison) 

4. Department of Commerce (Karen Cardran) 5. Department of Education (Leroy Walser) 6. Department of the Interior (Peggy Venable) 7. Department of Transportation (Allaire Williams) 8. Environmental Protection Agency (Jill Collins) 9. Tennessee Valley Authority (Lynn Hodges) 

70 73 

506.6 million of 732.0 million acres. The benefits of the "Bad guys abuse public land, good guys save it»74 message are potentially great for the department. Take Pride is an umbrella campaign which encompasses much of the public and community affairs work of the department." As outlined earlier, the three Department of the Interior bureaus which manage the most land are the Bureau of Land Management, the U.S. Fish and Wildlife 

Service, and the National Park Service (Table 1). 

■ Bureau of Land Management: The BLM is the largest landholding entity in the United States, managing about 15 percent of all the land in this country, or 47 percent of all federal holdings. This land is managed for multiple uses, including recreation, and is scattered throughout the 12 western most states; about half is in Alaska. BLM's public affairs publication describes the nature of the lands they manage: 

These lands in the lower 48 are those which no one wanted; they were bypassed in favor of more promising areas during the course of settlement. The lands are the mountaintops, the steep canyons, vacant valleys, and sandy deserts. They have been described as 'long on desert and short on water'; they have also been called 'stretches of $76 picturesque poverty.'

The nature of BLM lands probably accounts for a general lack of public awareness of them. BLM lands are largely interspersed in a "checkerboard" pattern with private land. It is not practical to mark all the public land with signs. Furthermore, the lands have generally not been grouped into recognizable, named units, as are lands of the other land management agencies 

(e.g., Teton National Forest, Grays' Lake National Wildlife Refuge). People often do not know they are on public land. One of the major reasons for undertaking the Take Pride campaign was to correct a perceived lack of awareness of public lands. The lands of the Bureau of Land Management are the lands of which people are least likely to be aware, as the low visitation rate figures in Table 8 show. The BLM long ago recognized the need to increase public awareness and decrease abuse of their lands. The Johnny 

Horizon public education campaign of the early 1970's was a notable early 198

0.7 0.3 16.9 14.1 8.6 77 effort.” More recently, since 1982 the BLM has sponsored a volunteers program. One of the main purposes of this program is to increase public in- 78 volvement on the BLM lands. When Take Pride was started in 1986, the 

Bureau readily incorporated the campaign into its normal mission. "Rather than conducting the Take Pride in America campaign as a separate, distinct program, the Bureau of Land Management is seeking to integrate the theme into all its existing multiple-use program areas.' ,,79

The low public awareness of BLM lands is probably related to the kinds of problems experienced on these lands. Policing the lands is also a tremendous problem, with over 47 million acres of BLM land in Nevada alone.80 

BLM lands may suffer disproportionately as compared with other federal lands from looting and stealing of cultural resources. The arid climate of the 

American Southwest has helped preserve many archeological sites, many of these on BLM lands. In 1979 the Congress passed the Archeological 

Resources Protection Act (ARPA) to help land management agencies stem destruction of archeological sights. However, these abuses have continued, and have received attention from the media (e.g., Bassett, 1986; Goodwin, 1986) and from the Congress. All of this means the BLM is in a position to benefit greatly from the Take Pride campaign.

Since the initiation of the campaign, BLM has been actively promoting the Take Pride among the Bureau's employees, and have encouraged employees to spread the word to the public whenever possible. The BLM launched a media campaign of its own to spread the Take Pride message to help improve stewardship of the public lands in the West. The aims of this campaign are to stop littering, vandalism, and the disturbance of cultural resources on BLM lands.

In addition, Take Pride has also been a big help for BLM's growing volunteers program. Take Pride has given volunteers a theme, a banner to work under, tieing their efforts into a bigger picture. The volunteer program has been growing at a rate of about 60 percent each year for the past 2-3 years and the TPIA awards are an extra incentive which may increase volunteer participation even more. 81 199

■ National Park Service: The National Park Service is undoubtedly the most visible of the land management agencies. Included in the National 

Park System are the natural "crown jewels" of our nation: Yellowstone, the

Great Smoky Mountains, the Grand Canyon and Glacier National Parks. 

82 83 The Park Service manages a total of 341 units, only 48 of which are called "national parks." In 1985 the national parks received a greater portion of all visits to the park system than any other category. Thanks to the natural beauty of these areas the National Park Service has had a high profile since its inception in 1916.85 The other units include our national monuments, national historic sites, national recreation areas and national seashores, to name a few of the designations. These areas also tend to be well known and many, especially those in urban areas, have high numbers of visitors. In 1985, Independence National Historic Park in Philadelphia had 4.9 million visitors, Cape Cod National Seashore had 4.4 million visitors, and an astounding 18.4 million people visited Golden Gate National Recreation Area. 84 The introduction to America's National Parks and Their Keepers by geographer Ronald A. Foresta includes a discussion of the public image of the parks, and introduces some of the problems which the parks face today:

The image of the Park Service is that of the ranger on horseback part naturalist, part policeman, part resource manager, and even part educator. The image of the Park System is of remote places and past times. They are tied up with American memory and mythology; they are collages of Bierstadt paintings, heroic pioneers, log cabins, and Indian tepees. However... they are very much subjected to the problems and dilemmas of American life. 

We are given occasional hints of this. A television program focuses on pollution and crime in Yellowstone National Park. A newspaper article discusses vandalism in a national park close to Cleveland or Atlanta or New York." 85 Why do we have such problems in the parks? Many stem from high levels of visitation, rather than lack of awareness as is the case with BLM lands. 

The trend in visitation to the parks has been a steady increase from 200 million visitors in 1971, to 346 million in 1985. Also the Park Service presence in urban areas has been increasing since the creation of Gateway (New York- 

New Jersey) and Golden Gate (San Francisco Bay) National Recreation 

Areas in 1972. Therefore, the Park Service must respond to more people, and to problems more associated with urban life. Within the National Capital Region of the National Park Service the Park Police handle everything from homicide to minor vandalism. 86 87 200

The Park Service also faces some of the same problems experienced by other agencies. A recent article in "Ranger: The National Journal of the Association of National Park Rangers" addresses the problem of archeological looting on park lands:

Although the preponderance of cases occurring on Federal lands over the past few years have fallen under the jurisdiction of the BLM and the Forest Service, it seems more than likely that they will be on the increase in National Park Service lands in the future. Our sites are often as remote as those on other agencies lands, and may be patrolled just as infrequently. Because of the variety of resources protected in our parks, there are greater offerings for artifact poachers." 88 Take Pride in America has an obvious role for the Park Service, and a significant effort has been made to fuse Take Pride with existing programs. 

The Volunteers in the Parks (VIP) program has been facilitating citizen involvement in the parks since it was created in 1970. In 1985 Congress increased the funding of the program to $1 million." The number of participants has grown steadily from 8,000 in 1981 to over 26,000 in 1984. 89 90 Park naturalists frequently incorporate the public awareness campaign theme into a park's interpretive program. They tell children on nature walks about Take Pride and show the Take Pride slide show to visitors attending nightly amphitheater presentations. The campaign augments the Park 

Service's educational programs. 

The Park Service is also taking advantage of that aspect of the Take Pride message that encourages people to report abuses. The Park Service's Law 

Enforcement Division set up the Park Watch program, including special 

Park Watch numbers to report crimes by telephone.

A major National Park Service cooperative effort in 1986 was the clean up of Padre Island National Seashore in Texas, where seaborne garbage washes up on the beach. Five federal agencies, including the National Park 

Service, the Fish and Wildlife Service, National Oceanic and Atmospheric 

Administration, the Coast Guard, and the Minerals Management Service, are cooperating with shippers, oil rig operators, fishermen and recreational boaters to reduce the generation of such trash at the source. Seaborne litter from the Gulf of Mexico degrades the national seashores, several national wildlife refuges, Texas public lands, and threatens endangered sea turtles and many other wildlife species." The ecumenical spirit of Take Pride, which encourages the Park Service to cooperate with other federal agencies, states and the public is one of the keys to the success of the campaign. 201

cat # 

Dick Stanton

Federal Government Category - Top National Award Winner (1986) 

Dick Stanton, Superintendent of the C&O Canal National Historic Park, was one of two Park Service employees selected as a top national award winner. His efforts demonstrate the role a government employee can play in inspiring and organizing volunteer efforts on public lands. In November 1985 hurricane rains swelled the Potomac River. Floodwater devastated the canal causing an estimated $9 million in damage. Superintendent Stanton was already struggling with limited budget and staff. He devised a plan in keeping with the recently announced Take Pride in America initiative to involve scouts and other groups in a massive "Cleanup Camporee." In the summer of 1986, over 7,000 volunteers had the opportunity to help clean up one of our treasured national historic sites. With "Take Pride in America" as their credo, they worked an aggregate total of over 30 thousand hours removing debris. Over the course of the summer many of the youths worked on the 

C&O for a week or more, during which time they developed great pride in their work and in the public resource they were restoring. Furthermore, the stewardship ethic spawned in 1986 will be strengthened in coming years. "One Miler" agreements have been signed which give scout troops responsibility for the continued maintenance of one mile segments of the canal, all 

154 miles from Cumberland, Maryland to Washington, D.C. None of this would have happened without Take Pride in America and Dick Stanton. In the words of the Regional Director of the Park Service, "without [his] leadership and team spirit, we are sure that not only the park, but the 

American public would have suffered much heavier losses."91

Fish and Wildlife Service: The Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS) manages the third largest amount of land in the U.S., after the BLM and the Forest 

Service. There are 443 units in the National Wildlife Refuge System with a total of 89.9 million acres; all but 12 million acres are concentrated in a few refuges in Alaska." The refuges are well delineated by signs, and the purpose of the refuges is straightforward: "to conserve, protect and enhance wildlife and their habitats for the continuing benefit of the people.' 

Wildlife refuges are less likely to be located in or near urban areas than some Park Service lands, although there are important and successful exceptions, such as the San Francisco Bay NWR and the Edwin B. Forsythe

NWR at Brigantine on the New Jersey seashore. The Fish and Wildlife Service has acquired refuges in areas where wildlife needs them. Visitor pressure on the refuge system is much less than on other federal lands, averaging 28.1 million visitor hours, far less than the 1264.6 million experienced by the 

Park Service in 1985 (Table 8). 

"94 202

The Fish and Wildlife Service faces problems which are different in nature and in scope from those faced by other land managers. The FWS 

Division of Law Enforcement acted on over 5,200 cases of wildlife law violations on the refuges in FY 1986. In addition the Service's law enforcement officers turned over an estimated 500-750 cases to local police (this includes mugging, narcotics and vandalism cases)." As with all law enforcement however, there could never be enough agents to catch all of the violators. 

Changing the habits and attitudes of those who would abuse wildlife and habitat is one of the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service's main objectives under the Take Pride in America effort. Of course the other main objective of 

Take Pride is increasing volunteer participation. The U.S. Fish and Wildlife 

Service is fortunate enough to have a large constituency of active outdoor sports enthusiasts who want to help the Service with their mission, "conservation and sound management of our nation's fish and wildlife resources for the benefit of present and future generations." Sportsmen and women have a vested interest in helping to conserve and manage land and wildlife for hunting and fishing, and for hiking and wildlife observation. For example, waterfowl hunters help to buy vital wetlands through the purchase of duck stamps for hunting. Since 1934 over 290 million dollars in “Duck

Stamp" fees have bought 3.7 million acres of wetlands." [Revenues have totaled $326.6 million through September 30, 1987.] Other groups such as fishermen and birdwatchers have long been involved helping the service. 

Beginning right after World War I fishermen started helping with fish stocking. Since 1965, thousands of amateur birders have contributed data to the 

Service's Breeding Bird Survey. 

97 This spirit continues today and Take Pride in America provides a banner under which the FWS has united all its existing volunteer activities. Take 

Pride has also fostered new voluntary efforts. The campaign has simultaneously benefited from the volunteer spirit which existed among the sporting public, and has provided the impetus for more volunteer projects. The 

Fish and Wildlife Service's Pacific Coast region has reported 265 Take 

Pride in America efforts, of which 53 percent are new activities.

98 The 

Refuge Manager at Bombay Hook National Wildlife Refuge in Delaware, Paul Daly, stated, “Since the Take Pride in America campaign began last year, I've seen a tremendous resurgence of citizen involvement in caring for public lands."99

Although over the last five years there has been an overall increase in volunteer numbers and hours of work, the numbers for 1986 do not necessarily document a significant increase attributable to Take Pride as 1986 was the first year of the campaign. On the other hand, in 1986 Take 

Pride in fact may have inspired many of the 10,192 people that contributed 

478,934 hours of work valued at $3,828,881. 100 Perhaps the FWS's most notable volunteer program is run at Kilauea Point National Wildlife Refuge in Hawaii. This small refuge contains the nesting sites of thousands of seabirds. One part time and two full time employees had more work than they could handle managing the refuge and 203
