ww dealing with the half a million visitors annually. For the last four years these three have been coordinating the efforts of 125 volunteers. Ordinary citizens run the visitors center and serve as interpreters, conduct research and monitor nesting success. Take Pride in America rewarded the efforts of this team of volunteers and employees by selecting them as finalists in the 1986 Awards competition. 101 This project is an example of the volunteer efforts which take place on the refuges which are crucial to the success of the 

Refuge System. 102 The Service is also responsible for safeguarding and improving wildlife and wildlife habitat throughout the country, not just in the refuges. Volunteers are helping the Fish and Wildlife Service projects which help fauna directly and indirectly. One project on the Little Red River in Arkansas was a cooperative effort between Greers Ferry National Fish Hatchery, the

Army Corps of Engineers, and the local affiliate of Keep America Beautiful. Over 120 volunteers participated in this prime demonstration of cooperation on the local level between three national partners in the Take Pride in America campaign. In the process they made the river healthier for the fish, more suitable for recreation, and certainly more beautiful to look at. One last example demonstrates what federal agencies can do when they cooperate with well organized constituent organizations with special interest in the agency. The Bass Anglers Sportsman Society, (B.A.S.S.) has joined with Fish and Wildlife to sponsor another litter removal campaign aimed at beautifying the nation's lakes. During National Fishing Week, June 

1-7, 1987, these entities cosponsored the Clean Water-Take Pride in 

America National Lake Clean-Up Campaign. At the conclusion of fishing competition, the contestants weighed in a bag of trash along with their fish, 103 

Fish and Wildlife has a great potential volunteer force in those sportsmen that use wildlife resources, and Take Pride should help gain new recruits among this group of people. 

Other Interior Department Bureaus: Other bureaus are participating primarily by passing on the Take Pride in America message. They have incorporated the Take Pride logo into their letterheads, shown the slide program to visitors at their facilities, and encouraged employees to become involved in local volunteer projects. Other activities include: • developing a Take Pride in America video (Bureau of Reclamation) printing the Take Pride in America logo on USGS maps ⚫ nominating Indian groups for the national awards competition (BIA) ⚫ including the Take Pride message on brochures (Office of Surface 

Mining and Reclamation).104

Take Pride in America is a banner that all bureaus can use to promote their mission. By mentioning Take Pride and displaying the logo they can reinforce the work of others. 204

Department of Agriculture 

The other lead agency for the Take Pride in America campaign is the USDA. The USDA Forest Service manages over 190 million acres of public land, and much of what the department does through its other agencies concerns conservation on private lands, especially America's farmland. 

Secretary Lyng committed the Agriculture Department to be a major partner in the Take Pride in America campaign in July, 1986. At that time he said: "From the very beginning, the U.S. Department of Agriculture has sought to strengthen the conservation ethic in every community in America. 

Our participation in the Take Pride in America is a natural role for us.' 

The Department of Agriculture has consistently applied the Take Pride message to help in its efforts with private landowners.106 Take Pride in 

America is intended to tap into and heighten patriotic feelings in America, by getting Americans to take pride in their lands. That this pride should encompass private lands is acknowledged by the creation of a new awards. category in 1987 for private land stewardship activities. ,,105 The USDA operates through a department-wide TPIA Campaign Staff, detailed for this purpose, which initiates and coordinates USDA Take Pride In America activities. It is also responsible for two significant promotional activities for the national campaign. It enlisted the talents of the 

Songwriter's Guild of America to write a theme song, which the Oak Ridge Boys recorded. As ambassadors for Take Pride in America they are including the song in their 1987 Summer Tour. Secondly, USDA produced a moving, ten minute narrated Take Pride in America slide show. So far the 

Task Force has distributed over 5,000 of these to government offices all over the nation. The show promotes the conservation of America's "resources," so is appropriate for all agencies and organizations involved with the campaign. 107 The USDA is in an excellent position to promote good stewardship of private and public lands. In the words of Secretary Lyng, "We have, through our programs, a tremendous outreach capability.»108 Unlike many federal agencies, the USDA agencies have offices in virtually every county in the na tion. The Extension Service, the Soil Conservation Service, and the Farmers 

Home Administration have a presence in towns and cities all across the country. Agriculture Department employees interact daily with the local residents. The SCS soil scientist or the county extension agent are likely to know the farmers personally.110 Farmers in turn are likely to respond when the local soil conservationist tells them about Take Pride. 109 In order to facilitate the flow of information from Washington to the county offices, the department has set up a system of internal communication and management. In addition to this chain of communication within each agency, within each state there is communication between all the 

USDA agencies through the Food and Agriculture Councils (FACs).

FACS consist of each of the top officials for each USDA agency in the state, and are chaired by one of the council members. FACs have been respon- 111 112 205

! ! ! # 

TH M 14 sible for the establishment of TPIA teams in each state and county. Each team includes representatives of the Governor and other elected officials, all UDSA offices, all other federal partners, all private sector organizations, and any other group interested in Take Pride. UDSA has offered its offices and contacts to encourage the outreach. FACs are an ideal clearinghouse for communications regarding Take Pride on several levels: from the USDA 

Task Force on Take Pride, among the USDA agencies in the State, and between the USDA agencies and other organizations participating in that state's Take Pride effort. The Department of Agriculture communications network is well established, and is an effective tool for getting the Take 

Pride message to the American people. 113 

Friends of the Colorado Trail/The Colorado Mountain Club

Constituent Organization - Top National Award Winner (1986) 

Under the direction of Gudy Gaskill, the Friends of the Colorado Trail have built a 410 mile non-motorized recreational trail from Denver to Durango, mostly on Forest Service lands. The Deputy Regional Forester had this to say about the program:

Both the Forest Service and the State of Colorado are committed to the effort, but cannot hope to accomplish the task without the efforts of the Friends of the Colorado Trail. The volunteer program established to build and maintain the trail provides direct public involvement and creates awareness and appreciation of natural resource conservation. Over 1900 volunteers, including Governor Richard Lamm, labored to complete the trail (begun in 1973) by 1987. In addition, foundations and corporations, including the Adolph Coors Foundation and Nabisco, have provided financial support. The Colorado Trail is the product of the combined efforts of individual volunteers, citizen groups, governments and the private sector. As such it is an excellent example of the type of project Take 

Pride in America hopes to foster. 114 ■Forest Service: For the Forest Service, the message is the same as it is for the other land management agencies, "Bad guys abuse public lands, good guys save it."115 Vandals destroy fixtures and picnic tables and deface buildings in Forest Service campgrounds causing millions of dollars worth of damage each year. National forests are also looted for their archeological 206

treasures. There were 233 incidents of archeological vandalism reported by the Forest Service in FY 1985, more than any other agency (Table 2). Like 

BLM, with millions of widely distributed acres to patrol, the Forest Service faces the impossible task of monitoring public use of the national forests. 

Over four decades, the Ad Council's Smokey Bear campaign has successfully mitigated what had been the most serious threat to our national forests, forest fires. Woodsy Owl has served to remind young people not to "Give a 

Hoot Don't Pollute." Take Pride will complement these programs." Finally, Take Pride in America has been integrated with the highly successful Volunteers in the Forest Service program, established over fifteen years ago. In 1986, over 51,000 volunteers participated in this program. This number continues to grow each year, but it is difficult to determine whether Take Pride is directly responsible for any of the recent increase. 116 117 118 119 

Soil Conservation Service: The mission of the Soil Conservation Service 

(SCS) is to conserve the nation's soil resources. By working with America's farmers to promote wise land use and prevent soil erosion and degradation, the SCS also helps stem flooding and reserve water quality. Take Pride's objective to "help us appreciate the importance of all our public resources enables SCS field personnel to use the campaign to help promote their new volunteers program. The Take Pride message gets people to think about stewardship of their own lands, and SCS uses this to help educate landowners, so many field offices have been adopting the campaign's message. 120 121 

Other USDA Agencies: While 20 USDA agencies have provided the

Take Pride in America message to and through their normal constituencies, several have pursued special projects and leadership roles on both public and private lands: Agricultural Research Service field units are upgrading local environments with volunteers; Farmers Home Administration's outreach efforts encourage FFA chapters to participate; Extension Service agencies serve as local contacts in all counties and also work with 4-H chapter volunteers; and the Office of the Inspector General is encouraging employee involvement in local community projects. 

Army Corps of Engineers

The Corps is a major provider of recreation opportunities at 466 water resource projects located throughout the United States. In fact, the Corps' 

11.5 million acres of land and inland waters support 146 million visitor days 122 (1985) of recreation use, the second highest among all federal agencies. 

The TPIA campaign complements the Corps' growing volunteers program and annual Federal Lands Cleanup Day activities on these projects. Citizen volunteers and Corps employees organized cleanups, erosion control projects, recycling programs and many other activities to make the Take Pride campaign a success. Corps nominees to the national awards program 207

Bbm www went on to win seven 1987 awards, including three in the "federal agency" category (more than any other federal agency.) TPIA has had beneficial impacts on Corps resources and it rewards employees, volunteers and the nation for promoting stewardship on the public lands. 

Other Federal Partners

The other federal partners are generally playing a supportive role in the Take Pride campaign. Like the Army Corps of Engineers, the Tennessee 

Valley Authority manages significant lands for recreation, about 700,000 acres in its service region. TVA, Commerce, EPA, Education and Transportation all send representatives to the Federal Interagency Coordinating Committee and have helped with major functions such as the National 

Awards ceremony. Secretary of Education William Bennett asked the Chief

State School Officers to promote clean, safe school grounds under Take Pride, and asked teachers to get students involved in Take Pride volunteer activities. DOT is using Take Pride to help stem littering and the destruction of road signs along rights-of-way, and to discourage graffiti on public transportation. These are just two examples of the types of activities federal agencies conduct in conjunction with Take Pride. 123 State Involvement Some of the 44 states (Table 9) participating in Take Pride conduct formal state awards programs; others simply forward nominations on to 

Washington. This is one indication of the range of involvement in Take 

Pride on the state level. As with any such voluntary program, ambitious implementation of the program was limited to only a portion of the states at the beginning. Interior's Task Force compiled a state activity report in September 1986, and indicated seven of thirty-six states responding had made very good efforts to get their programs started. An additional twelve had 124 made good progress, while eighteen had a slow start. A year later, many states are sponsoring active, innovative Take Pride efforts. The following sections discuss how five states are conducting their Take Pride efforts. 

These states serve as examples of the variety of different approaches. 

ARIZONA is a state that has been plagued by abuses of public lands for many years. Over 44 percent of the sixth largest state is federally owned. 

Recently, the damage and defacing of public property has brought together federal, state, and local agencies to address this problem. Together they set up an 800-VANDALS number, and participated in the Keep America Beautiful Public Lands Day activities. They had already established many of the programs which Take Pride in America is intended to foster. Therefore, when President Reagan launched Take Pride, Arizona was ready. The governor appointed Alicia Bristow, Executive Director of the Commission on the Arizona Environment, to coordinate the state's Take Pride efforts. 126 208

She established a Take Pride steering committee consisting of state and federal land managers. One of their main priorities has been to quantify the amount of damage (in dollars) caused by vandalism on public lands. They found the amount of damage increasing in a number of areas: the Department of Transportation reported vandalism cost the Department $175,000 in 1981-82, and increased to $191,000 in 1984; in 1983 Phoenix Parks and 

Recreation reported $47,000 worth of damage to facilities in 1984 this increased to $100,000.127 They are updating these figures, but the information has already convinced the commission that the problems in Arizona are serious. The greater awareness which should come from the Take Pride in 

America campaign is needed to address these problems. Three Arizona nominations were selected as top 1986 award winners: Coronado National 

Forest for the RESPECT IT program; Page Elementary School for their cleanup of Glen Canyon; and the Arizona State Historic Preservation Office for their public education efforts.128 More outstanding projects will undoubtedly arise in Arizona thanks to the Commission and government agencies, which are working together to promote Arizona's Take Pride efforts. 129 

SOUTH CAROLINA provides an excellent example of what Take Pride can do for a state with relatively little federal land (6 percent). Carol Mc- 

Laughlin of the Governors Office on Energy, Agriculture and Natural Resources has led a very active campaign. South Carolina does have a serious litter problem which Take Pride will hopefully address. The governor had already established a Task Force on Litter which is promoting recycling, and trying to mitigate litter's negative impact on tourism. As is the W1 IN 209

case with federal agencies and other states, Take Pride is an umbrella campaign for South Carolina, and anti-litter programs fit well under that umbrella. Among these is the "Waterwatch Program," which encourages people to adopt a stream or pond, and then learn about resident plants and animals, learn the laws which pertain, and tell authorities about serious problems and law abuse. The governors office works closely with the USDA agencies in the state, especially the Soil Conservation Service, through the state FAC (Food and Agriculture Council). 

Ms. McLaughlin is very enthusiastic about Take Pride, "People are very receptive to participating. If the time is right, we might as well tap into this enthusiasm."130 Her main objectives are to provide information on the type of projects which are needed and can be done, and to publicize awards. She received 30 applications for awards in 1986, 10 of which were sent on to the national competition. In the end, the efforts of all paid off when The Charleston Clean City Commission won a top National Award. 131 

■ PENNSYLVANIA got its campaign off to a great start. In 1986 the State conducted its own extensive awards program, selecting the best nominations for national awards." Three of these became top winners: The Philadelphia Electric Company, for its educational workshops in the utility's Muddy Run Recreation Park; the Washington County Commissioners who initiated a plan to rejuvenate 3,000 acres of land, and the Keep Pennsylvania Beautiful program. 

The state is continuing with an aggressive campaign in its second year. Over 14,000 flyers were mailed out to individuals, local officials, citizen and youth groups, and media and businesses. The state contact for Pennsylvania is Vanyla Tierney in the Department of Environmental Resources. Her main goal this year is to set up an interagency coordinating committee which she hopes will spawn new initiatives in the state. 132 133 ■ DELAWARE, like South Carolina, has very little federal land (3 percent). 

State and locally owned lands are well policed, so there does not seem to be a serious problem with abuse of public land. Although there are volunteer projects which are conducted on public lands, a number of which were selected as national finalists, much of the effort in Delaware has addressed problems on private lands.134

In Delaware, the USDA's FAC, chaired by Wallace Caulk of the Farmers Home Administration (FHA), has taken a very active role in Take Pride. FAC works closely with the state coordinator promoting good stewardship practices on private agricultural lands. The four main USDA agencies involved are the FHA, the SCS, the Extension Service, and the Agricultural Stabilization and Conservation Service. Their "Farm of Distinction" campaign has been a cooperative effort with the state's Department of Agriculture. After receiving at least three nominations, and the consent of the owner, Take Pride in Delaware picked the 100 neatest farms in the state. They placed signs displaying the Take Pride in America logo in front of the honored farms. The purpose of the effort is to improve the ap- 210

pearance of agricultural areas to help foster tourism, and to get farmers to take pride in the appearance of their lands. 135 

COLORADO joined the campaign more recently. Nonetheless, five

Colorado projects were selected as National Awards Program finalists, three of which were selected as top award winners. Most of the nominations were submitted by the federal land management agencies which been promoting Take Pride in the state. Now that the governor has appointed a person in the State Department of Natural Resources as coordinator, the state agencies will be getting together with the federal agencies. Many 

Coloradans earn their living from and recreate on federal lands. Two of the top awards went to established volunteer trail crews, while the third went to a boy scout troop for their participation in the "Adopt-a-Forest" program. 

As with almost all the 1986 winners, Take Pride was not the original impetus for either project. However, the award certainly provided the groups with national recognition, which should help them attract more volunteers in future years. 136

In April 1986, Secretary Hodel invited representatives of private organizations to a luncheon to tell them about Take Pride, and to ask for their support. Most of those in attendance signed on enthusiastically, thus making 

Take pride in America a "private/public partnership."137 Table 10 lists the organizations that have formally joined the campaign.

Local Private Sector Involvement 

146 The private sector conducts nearly all of the volunteer projects which form the foundations of the campaign. Only those projects which originate with the three government categories (federal, state and local agencies) are not conducted by the private sector. Of the 141 finalists selected in 1986, 106 were private individuals, corporations, and organizations. MacDonald's of Hawaii, Houston West Chamber of Commerce, Richmond County Anti Litter Council, and Boy Scout Troop 92 of Greenville, Tennessee are all private sector entities which conducted volunteer efforts chosen as finalists by Take Pride. On the local level, the private sector is obviously the crucial element of the Take Pride initiative, and the growth of this local involvement is necessary to insure the future success of the campaign. 214

Pride in Jackson Hole

Public/Private Partnership – Top National Award Winner (1986) 

Pride in Jackson Hole is special since it is one the first projects which was inspired by Secretary Hodel's campaign. It is a community-wide effort, involving youth groups, senior citizens, private businesses, local government, and state and federal agencies. The intent of the campaign is very broad; to make Jackson Hole, Wyoming a better place to live and visit. In 1986 over 25 different projects had been completed; groups collected litter in Grand 

Teton National Park, a student council landscaped their school, the local chapter of Trout Unlimited improved habitat along three miles of Flat Creek, Teton Village conducted an extensive cleanup of their village, and a high school biology class worked on a soil stabilization project in the National Elk Refuge. The range of projects conducted as part of Pride in Jackson 

Hole are indicative of two important aspects of Take Pride in America: 

1. A land stewardship ethic should apply on all public lands; your street; the schoolyard; our grandest National Parks. Stewardship ethics developed in one area will apply to other lands. Pride in Jackson Hole involves all of these lands. 

2. Americans depend on their public resources for a variety of uses. In 

Jackson Hole the community's economy depends on the scenic beauty of the Tetons and other public lands. Through Pride in Jackson Hole, citizens are realizing that public lands depend on the active involvement of the com- 147 munity. 

Take Pride in America

Agenda for the Future 

One way to insure that Take Pride in America will continue for many years to come is to transfer some of the responsibility for the campaign to the private sector. Under the current administration, the federal government has transferred several programs to the private sector; for example, the seat belt and anti-drunk driving campaigns of the Department of Transportation, and the private funding of the Statue of Liberty restoration. 148 

The Take Pride campaign is particularly suited to further development and growth in the private sector. Although the public service advertising campaign and the national awards program focus exclusively on motivating citizen pride in and responsibility for the public lands, the theme has been kept purposefully broad to encourage private sector organizations to expand the concept and develop ideas and programs that benefit their missions and goals. Theme flexibility exists to expand, for example, to pride in 

American products, American labor, and America's competitiveness in the 215

world marketplace, to name but a few. The structure of the campaign is also suited to expanded private sector involvement. There has been no effort to formalize a campaign structure via federal charter, i.e., legislation or executive order. As a result, federal involvement can easily adapt to a supportive rather than a directive role.

Even if some portion of the Take Pride campaign is "privatized,” the federal government should continue some involvement to utilize the extensive internal communications network, especially in the Departments of the Interior, Agriculture and Education. There are a number of ways to retain federal involvement. One way is to include relevant Cabinet officers and agency chiefs on advisory councils or boards. Another is to encourage memoranda of understanding between various agencies and a private sector organization devoted to carrying on the goals of the campaign. Eventually, if the government desires, it can divest itself of the campaign. 

Privitization is one of the primary management goals for Take Pride in America in the coming months and the long term success and survival of the campaign may depend on it.

An Early Assessment 

There are many advantages to having a broad scope campaign with such an epic title. However, there are some important changes that organizers should consider to insure the effectiveness of the campaign. This assessment of the campaign focuses on some of the issues that need to be addressed if the campaign is to realize its fullest potential. 

Title 

The first suggestion centers around the campaign title: "Take Pride in 

America." The campaign is unabashedly designed to tap into patriotic feelings to help protect our public lands and natural resources. To insure Take 

Pride in America publicity actually benefits the public lands, the Take Pride logo could be accompanied by some further explanatory subtitle, e.g., "Protect Your Public Lands," or "Help Save America's Natural and Cultural Resources,” or “This Land Is Your Land" describes what the campaign is all about. Each Take Pride poster, letterhead, bumper sticker and postmark might include such a subtitle. People will then know they are taking pride in their public lands. In fact, the Department of Agriculture uses descriptors such as "America's Resources Need Your Help" and "Volunteer, Conserve Our Natural Resources," to reflect its legislated authorities. If the campaign is successful in its actual impact on the 

American landscape, it will in turn heighten Americans' sense of pride in their country, "from the redwood forest, to the Gulf Stream waters.” Good stewardship will help us to love our land and be proud to be Americans. 216

During the presentation of the 1986 National Awards, Vice President Bush spoke of this incomparable asset: 

I've visited all 50 states, and 74 foreign countries.

There's not one of those countries that has the comprehensive natural beauty of the United States of America. I know how much we have to be proud of." 149 It should be noted, however, that the appeal of this campaign is its "umbrella" nature, and its themes could well be expanded to whole new horizons, e.g., take pride in American labor, American products, American arts, etc., and suitable subtitles could clarify these efforts as well. Communication 

Take Pride in America's campaign organizers hope to get the Take Pride message out to as many Americans as possible. They are using as many information channels as possible. The Take Pride message may travel from the Task Force to the American people via four main routes: the Ad Council campaign, federal agencies, state coordinators, and private organizations. This multiple approach strategy means that the Take Pride in 

America can be very complex. The number of agencies and organizations involved is daunting, and Take Pride may have a slightly different role and meaning for each person or group of people involved. As many people working on the campaign point out, herein lies the greatest strength of the campaign. It is also Take Pride's greatest management problem. 

It is a strength for two reasons. First, it encourages many agencies to be involved, and enables them to mold the campaign into their diverse missions. Second, the big picture approach enables cultivation of a broad based land stewardship ethic which applies to all public land, and natural and cultural resources. Other awareness efforts involving public lands have focused on one class of abuse (e.g., Smokey Bear, Woodsy Owl). This campaign addresses problems at a more fundamental level. People will be educated about all public lands and abuses which take place, and are prompted to modify their behavior based on deeply held patriotic feelings. Environmental education may be unable to effect quick changes, but it ought to make a positive contribution to the commonly-shared values of our culture. 

However, at least some people have been communicating the program have done so selectively. Although most campaign workers have similar conceptions of what Take Pride is about, one young Park Service naturalist said that Take Pride was an anti-litter campaign started by Park Service Director Mott.

A BLM archeologist wrote that Take Pride is a campaign to "increase citizen awareness of our responsibilities to care for and protect our nation's cultural heritage.»151 Each focused on a particular effort being conducted under Take Pride; that is, litter control and cultural resource protec- 150 217

tion, but any visitor could be confused about Take Pride if they got these messages in succession.

Although each agency should use Take Pride to help with its particular mission, confusion can be avoided if the agencies involved more consciously conform their use of the campaign to the broader theme. New approaches to internal communication should be reviewed by the Federal Interagency 

Coordinating Committee. The Take Pride in America Task Force has started a newsletter beginning in September 1987,152 which should be a major vehicle for improved communication. 

Several state coordinators indicated that they had been lost at the start of the campaign, and were still unsure what they should be doing, or what federal agencies and other states were doing. In addition to the newsletter, regional meetings on Take Pride in America could facilitate cooperation on regional problems. These would involve state coordinators, federal, state and local land managers, and private organizations. For example, southwestern states could share information on solutions to looting of Indian cultural sites, and southeastern states could develop a combined effort to combat litter in conjunction with Take Pride. However, travel and related costs can often be substantial; the accomplishments derived from any such meetings must more than justify their cost, and may best be arranged in conjunction with other authorized duties. 

Is a More Intense Campaign Needed?

The first part of this chapter documented the abuses which led to the campaign. Litter and vandalism in particular cost land management agencies millions annually. If managers recognize that they have serious abuse problems, then they should respond with serious, focused responses. 153 As it stands now, Take Pride in America is by design a low budget, low paperwork campaign. The success of the campaign depends chiefly on the personal enthusiasm of those assigned to work on the campaign. The campaign is designed to be incorporated into and help with normal public affairs and land management work. Fortunately, there is a great deal of enthusiasm for Take Pride and the job seems to be getting done. It is impossible to say how long this will continue to be the case. 

The "Don't Mess with Texas" campaign (see Top Award Winner insert) may provide a useful lesson to Take Pride in America. In 1985, the Texas

Department of Highways spent $24 million a year picking up trash on their highways. They expected to spend an additional $4 million in 1986. Instead they budgeted $4 million and started "Don't Mess With Texas." Litter decreased by 24 percent in the first year. By the second year litter is down 

50 percent. Texas realized they had a serious problem. They made a significant investment and achieved remarkable results. They did not have to increase spending on litter cleanup; the campaign was already worthwhile. 154 218

Texas invested half of its budget on an education and media campaign. 

Take pride in America is heavily dependent on media public service directors to give Take Pride ads exposure. The Take Pride Task Force realizes that PSAs to address other critical issues such as drug abuse prevention and adult literacy are all competing for play time. However, the PSA's need significant play time if they are to be effective. 155 There simply needs to be more external communication to reinforce the message with the public. 

Familiarity with the message increases the likelihood that these values will pervade the popular culture. Undoubtedly it was Smokey's oft repeated: “Only YOU can prevent forest fires," that has had such a beneficial impact on human induced fire losses. If land managers can better document serious abuse problems, that all Americans are paying for, increasing prime time exposure for the Take Pride spots may save funds overall. Followup appeals to stations and publishers on behalf of the campaign may also be effective. 

Positive Feedback

People have been participating in Take Pride because they believe the campaign will work. Eventually, government employees and private sector partners will want to know that Take Pride is effective. Yet the effect of promoting a broad scope educational approach is difficult to measure, especially in the short term. “Don't Mess with Texas" and the Keep America

Beautiful System are easier to measure since they are aimed at one problem, roadside litter, for which there are well tested sampling techniques. 156 The Keep America Beautiful System provides positive reinforcement by publishing the results of studies which assess the effectiveness of its cam paigns. For example, a study by the American Public Works Association found: 

In four of ten "Keep America Beautiful System" communities studied, the cost/benefit ratio exceeded 1:10 - 

Cincinnati (1:18), Indianapolis (1:27), Macon-Bibb 

County, GA (1:53), and Memphis (1:111). In each case 

KAB was able to impact directly on sanitation operations. (The ratios indicate the cost of the program in tax dollars vs. the benefits received by the community as a result.) 

Furthermore, the report indicates: 

Reductions in the Photometric Index indicate that each site made significant gains in achieving their primary goal: litter reduction. Typical reductions are in the 60-70 percent range. 157 In order to document the effectiveness of Take Pride, agencies will have to provide for some sort of feedback, consistent with the restraint of avoid- 219

ing additional paperwork or budgetary burdens. The Take Pride in America

Task Force should consider designing a highly targeted, pilot scale system to monitor how the campaign is affecting the way people treat public lands. 

Elements of such an assessment might include: • tracking the amount spent on litter pickup and vandalism repair in a sample of each kind of land management unit; sampling a subset of the above group to determine the relationship between cost, and changes in the rates of litter and vandalism, using KAB's measuring techniques for roadside litter as a model; using questionnaires to assess how people perceive public lands, and the role Take Pride played in any measured changes. 

This pilot program could be expanded to assess changes in other abuse categories if the results warrant continuation of the campaign. 

Finally, few agencies report extensively on Take Pride volunteer activities, and may not be keeping good records of how many volunteer projects Take Pride has inspired. Take Pride organizers and coordinators of agencies' volunteer programs should immediately start to track the effectiveness of at least this aspect of Take Pride. State, regional and national coordinators could then know the results of their efforts, and be inspired to continue, or evaluate how to better publicize the campaign. 

Private Lands 

Starting in 1987, Take Pride in America has added a new category to its national awards program for private lands, to begin to recognize the substantial efforts that take place voluntarily on the 60 percent of the nation's lands which are held privately. It is estimated that some 300 million acres (an area over three times the size of California) are open for public recreation activities free or for a fee, and another 500 million acres are used for recreation by friends and neighbors with special permission of the landowner. 

Greater emphasis in the future should be placed on the efforts to Take Pride in America's communities, where most recreation demand and use arises. 158

Conclusion It is probably still too early to judge the campaign fairly. By August 1987, the campaign had been in full swing for little more than a year, and the public service announcements had been in circulation only a few months. However, the momentum of the campaign has accelerated into 1988. Ideally, several years from now, public lands will be noticeably different. It will be valuable to public policy analysis to be able to document the impacts of this effort on 220

the public lands, and attribute some portion of the change to Take Pride in America. However, this may be difficult to determine, unless a few systematic tracking measures are designed now. While this report presents data documenting serious abuses of public lands, hardly any of it is suitable as baseline data to judge the effectiveness of the campaign. 

What we can say is that Take Pride in America is off to a successful start: • nine federal agencies are full scale partners in the campaign, and are finding their niches in the campaign ⚫ the Departments of the Interior and Agriculture and the Army Corps of Engineers have incorporated Take Pride into much of their outreach work ⚫ 44 states have joined the campaign • numerous national and local private sector organizations are promoting the campaign • in the first two months after the Ad Council released its ads promoting Take Pride, nearly 2,000 people had responded by mail asking for information ⚫ there were over 520 nominations for the 1986 National Awards 

Program, and over 540 for the 1987 awards • 38 winning projects and many finalists got national recognition and VIP treatment in the first (1986) annual Take Pride in America National Awards Ceremony; 94 top national winners were selected for 1987, in addition to hundreds of finalists and semifinalists, all of whom were invited to the White House. 

After contacting hundreds of people working on the campaign, what strikes us most - what speaks most highly of the campaign's accomplishments and its future prospects — is the enthusiasm surrounding Take Pride in America. This enthusiasm is evident from the description of the award winning volunteer projects, it is evident from the work of federal and state employees and it is evident in the words of President Reagan at the Rose Garden ceremony to honor the winners of the 1986 Take Pride in America awards.159 

Today we've selected 38 recipients for our "Take Pride in America" awards. They've been selected from over 500 nominees. Let me suggest, however, that all those who contribute their time and effort are winners. And clearly, our country is the greatest winner of all. 221

Appendix A 

Activities of the Council on

Environmental Quality 

The Council on Environmental Quality (CEQ) was established by the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) which was signed into law on January 1, 1970. The Council consists of three Members, appointed by the 

President and confirmed by the Senate. The Council, through the Chairman, provides advice to the President on environmental matters, prepares the President's annual Environmental Quality Report, and assists federal agencies in the implementation of NEPA.

A. Alan Hill has served as Chairman of CEQ since 1981. Member Wil liam L. Mills, Ph.D., was appointed in 1983, and Member Jacqueline E. 

Schafer in 1984.

Office of the Chairman Chairman Hill participates in meetings of the President's Domestic Policy 

Council when environmental matters are being discussed. He also represents the President at formal national and international meetings concerned with environmental issues.

As the presidential advisor on the environment, Chairman Hill is actively involved in preparing and commenting on drafts of environmental messages, executive orders, and decision memoranda. To enable him to provide practical and informed advice to the President, the Chairman meets frequently with representatives of science, industry, agriculture, labor, conservation organizations, state and local governments, and other groups. 

The Chairman also directs the work of the Council and the Office of Environmental Quality, which provides support staff for the Council. 

In 1986, Chairman Hill assisted in the coordination of federal agency activities concerning the World Commission on Environment and Development (WCED). This independent body is an outgrowth of a United Nations 

General Assembly resolution aimed at preparing an "Environmental Perspective to the Year 2000 and Beyond." The WCED is examining an array of topics including air, water, and ocean pollution; hazardous and nuclear waste; deforestation and soil erosion; human shelter; land tenure; and industrial and environmental controls. Because the scope of the WCED 227

report is so wide with no issue central to one federal agency, the Department of State asked CEQ to coordinate the United States' efforts with the Commission.

Under the guidance of the Chairman, the Council continued its oversight of the work of the International Whaling Commission in 1986. CEQ participated in an interagency committee to oversee implementation of the moratorium on commercial whaling which went into effect in 1986. 

In addition, the CEO Chairman serves as a joint chairman of the Interagency Toxic Substances Data Committee. This committee was established to improve coordination in the generation, sharing, and management of information about chemical substance life cycles and effects upon the environment. The agency is also a statutory member of the Interagency Testing 

Committee (ITC). The ITC, established under the Toxic Substances Control Act, is charged with recommending to the EPA Administrator those chemical substances which should be given priority consideration for promulgating health and environmental testing rules. CEQ representatives attend the meetings of these two committees on a regular basis. 

Council Members

The two Council Members are responsible for specific projects and studies, in addition to representing CEQ on interagency committees and task forces. 

Dr. Mills has primary responsibility for the Council's Management Fund. 

The Management Fund is used for interagency environmental studies and projects. CEQ initiates or co-sponsors conferences, studies, surveys, analyses, publications, and research pertaining to current environmental issues. This work, which is performed under contract, is financed directly from the Management Fund to which funds are contributed by various agencies with environmental responsibilities.

Dr. Mills is responsible for assessing project proposals from other federal agencies, and for reviewing contracts initiated through the Fund, quality control, schedule monitoring, budget control, and payment certification. He also serves as the Council's liaison between the Office of Administration in the Executive Office of the President and potential contractors. 

Dr. Mills is also the Council's representative on the sub-Cabinet Working 

Group on Biotechnology, an interagency body which provides guidance on the adequacy of federal laws and regulations to protect public health and the environment from possible hazards associated with the products and processes of biotechnology. 

In 1986, Dr. Mills was in charge of the preparation of the 16th Environmental Quality Report for the Year 1985. In this capacity, he was responsible for developing and approving all material to be included in the report, both from in-house sources and from other federal agencies. The 1985 report focused on environmental protection policy and land use practices in 228

APPENDIX A: ACTIVITIES OF THE COUNCIL 

the United States. In addition, the report presented the first comprehensive review by the federal government since 1981 of international environmental issues which affect the United States. 

Council Member Schafer is responsible for overseeing expenditures of the Office of the Director of Research of the National Acid Precipitation Assessment Program (NAPAP), the research arm of the Interagency Task Force on Acid Precipitation (ITFAP). CEQ was designated by the White 

House to serve as Executive Secretary to the ITFAP; Member Schafer serves in this capacity. The ITFAP sponsors studies, projects, and conferences involving both the national and international community concerned with the problem of acid precipitation. The goal of NAPAP is to develop and improve on an objective and comprehensive scientific and technical information base for use by decisionmakers.

Council Member Schafer also serves as a member of the interagency 

Working Group on Energy, Natural Resources and Environment. In this role, she assists in the review of policy issues and in the preparation of briefing papers for the White House Domestic Policy Council. 

Office of Environmental Quality The Environmental Quality Improvement Act of 1970 established the Office of Environmental Quality (OEQ) to provide staff support for the Council.

The Chairman of CEQ serves as the Director of OEQ. CEQ also relies upon professional staff temporarily assigned from other federal agencies. In 

1986, the staff of the Office included the following individuals:

NEPA Oversight 

Throughout the year, the Council continued to fulfill its responsibilities with regard to the implementation and administration of the federal environmental analysis process. CEQ's activities in this area during 1986 are discussed in Appendix B of this report.

Environmental Policy Development Studies and 

Projects In 1986, CEQ continued its preparation of the second Environmental Trends sourcebook. This effort, begun in 1984, is being conducted and funded by approximately 15 federal agencies with environmental responsibilities; the Council has the central coordinating role in developing the report. Through the use of maps and charts, the report is designed to portray quantitatively the current conditions and trends in the quality of the environment.

The first Environmental Trends sourcebook was published in 1981. The revision of the report at five-year intervals was deemed to be a meaningful time period in which to assess discernible trends in the environment. The second report is scheduled for completion and publication in 1988. Publications During 1986, the Council published:

Environmental Quality, 1985, The 16th Annual Report of the Council on En vironmental Quality (released July 1987); 

Final regulation amending 40 C.F.R. § 1502.22, "Incomplete or Unavailable

Information," to remove the "worst case analysis" requirement (51 Fed. 

Reg. 15,625 (1986)). 230

Appendix B The National Environmental Policy Act Highlights of 1986 

During 1986, CEQ pursued a number of activities to fulfill its responsibilities for implementing the procedural provisions of the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA). Among those activities was an amendment of the section of NEPA implementing regulations addressing incomplete and unavailable information in the context of environmental impact statements. Other activities included review of agencies' NEPA procedures, advice to agencies concerning particular projects, and participation in international activities related to environmental impact assessment.

Also discussed below are some of the significant appellate and district court decisions dealing with NEPA issues during 1985. For example, in 

Steamboaters v. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, the Ninth Circuit 

Court of Appeals applied the CEQ NEPA regulations to relicensing activities of an independent regulatory agency, which had claimed that it was not bound by those regulations. In Van Abbema v. Fornell, the Eighth Circuit Court of Appeals emphasized a federal agency's responsibility to exercise independent judgement regarding the purpose of and need for a project, even when that project is proposed by a permit applicant. 

In another Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals case, Merrell v. Thomas, the court found that the Environmental Protection Agency was not required to follow NEPA when registering pesticides under the Federal Insecticide, Fungicide, and Rodenticide Act. In a district court ruling in Alaska Arctic 

Pipeline Co. v. U.S., the court permitted the Department of the Interior to assess fees for the full costs of an environmental impact statement against the plaintiffs, even though the plaintiffs permission for appropriate permits was ultimately denied.

The annual CEQ survey of NEPA litigation for the year 1985 shows the lowest number of NEPA lawsuits ever recorded. The discussion is supplemented with tables summarizing the major causes of action, types of plaintiffs, and defendant agencies, including a cumulative fifteen year perspective of NEPA litigation. 

231

CEQ and the NEPA Regulations: 1986 Activities 

CEQ promulgates and interprets regulations implementing the procedural provisions of the National Environmental Policy Act which are binding on all federal agencies. 40 C.F.R. 1500-1508 (1987). During 1986, CEQ con tinued its administration of these regulations. This responsibility includes: informal consultations with federal agencies regarding appropriate implementation of NEPA procedures; approval of federal agency NEPA procedures; informal consultation with state and local governments and private citizens regarding NEPA procedures; commenting on proposed legislation and testimony which is related to NEPA; designating lead agencies for the purpose of preparing environmental impact statements (EIS); making determinations on requests for emergency exemptions and alternative procedures for supplemental EIS(s); handling formal referrals to CEQ of major federal actions; participating in international activities related to environmental impact assessment; and disseminating information about the NEPA 

process to interested parties. During 1986, CEQ: 

Amended 40 C.F.R. 1502.22. This regulation addresses incomplete or unavailable information in an environmental impact statement. The final amendment requires all federal agencies to disclose the fact of incomplete or unavailable information when evaluating reasonably foreseeable significant adverse impacts on the human environment in an EIS, and to obtain that information if the overall costs of doing so are not exorbitant. If the agency is unable to obtain the information because overall costs are exorbitant or because the means to obtain it are not known, the agency must (1) affirmatively disclose the fact that such information is unavailable; (2) explain the relevance of the unavailable information; (3) summarize the existing credible scientific evidence which is relevant to the agency's evaluation of significant adverse impacts on the human environment; and (4) evaluate the impacts based upon theoretical approaches or research methods generally accepted in the scientific community. The amendment also specifies that impacts which have a low probability of occurrence but catastrophic consequences if they do occur, should be evaluated if the analysis is supported by credible scientific evidence and is not based on pure conjecture, and is within the rule of reason. The requirement to prepare a "worst case analysis" was rescinded. 51 Federal Register 15625, April 25, 1986. The regulation was effective on May 27, 1986.

Approved NEPA procedures for the Federal Communications Commis sion, the Environmental Protection Agency's construction grants program, the Agriculture Research Service, and final amendments to NEPA procedures for the Federal Highway Administration and the Urban Mass Transit. 

CEQ also reached agreement with the Federal Highway Administration to hold regional conferences with other agencies to discuss implementation of the revised procedures a year after their promulgation. 232

Designated the National Capital Planning Commission as lead agency for the proposed PortAmerica project in Prince George's County, Maryland; ■Granted emergency exemptions to: U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service, for destruction of steelhead trout infected with whirling disease at the Coleman

Soviet Environmental Law at Pace University School of Law. 

Significant Judicial Decisions NEPA Litigation 

Supreme Court Decisions 

River Road Alliance Inc. v. Corps of Engineers of United States Army, 106 S. 

Ct. 1283 (U.S. 1986)

The Supreme Court denied certiorari in this case, which was appealed from the U.S. Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit. The issue presented for review was the standard to be applied by the courts in ruling on an agency's decision not to prepare an environmental impact statement. The District

Court for the Southern District of Illinois had applied the "arbitrary and 233

capricious" standard in its ruling, finding little, if any, difference between the "reasonableness" standard and the "arbitrary and capricious" standard of review.

Justice White, in dissenting from the Supreme Court's denial of certiorari, noted the divergent standards of review in the various circuits. He pointed out that courts using the arbitrary and capricious standard emphasize the agency's discretion and expertise in its decision, while courts using the reasonableness standard emphasize the non-discretionary language of NEPA.¹ Justice White stated that he would grant certiorari because this regularly recurring conflict raises a significant question on the interpretation of a federal statute. 

Appellate Decisions 

The Steamboaters v. F.E.R.C., 759 F. 2d 1382 (9th Cir. 1985)

In Steamboaters v. Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the Ninth Circuit 

Court of Appeals applied the standard for determining whether to prepare an EIS to an independent regulatory agency which, at the time, had not adopted the CEQ Regulations. The Federal Energy Regulatory Commis sion (FERC) had granted a hydropower project an exemption from federal licensing procedures without first preparing an environmental assessment or an environmental impact statement. The court found that if the "plaintiff has alleged facts, which, if true, show that the proposed project may significantly degrade some human environmental factor"2, then an EIS must be prepared.

The court criticized FERC's decision not to prepare an EIS as "fatally flawed" on three main points. First, FERC failed to prepare an environmental assessment before making its decision not to prepare an EIS. The 

CEQ regulations require all federal agencies to prepare an EA to determine whether or not to prepare an EIS, unless the proposed activity is one that clearly requires an EIS or one that falls within a statutory exemption or a regulatory exclusion. The court pointed out that only Congress, the 

Judiciary, and the President are exempted from this requirement. The only regulatory exemption would be if the proposed project were one which normally would be categorically excluded from preparation of an EIS. The court quickly determined that the project in this case did not fall within such a categorical exclusion. Therefore, an EA was required.

Second, FERC failed to adequately explain its decision not to prepare an EIS. In its order granting the exemption, FERC found that the issuance of the exemption was not a major federal action significantly affecting the quality of the human environment; therefore the preparation of an EIS would not be required. The court indicated that FERC did not discuss the evidence presented by the agencies or how mitigation measures would prevent environmental damage. 234

An 'agency cannot... avoid its statutory responsibilities under NEPA merely by asserting that an activity it wishes to pursue will have an insignificant effect on the environment.' The agency must supply a convincing statement of reasons why potential effects are insignificant. 5 The court noted that regulations of the Council on Environmental Quality for implementing the procedural provisions of NEPA are mandatory and binding on all federal agencies. FERC's opinion in this case gave the court no indication that FERC had complied with these regulations.

FERC attempted to justify its decision not to prepare an EIS by arguing its reliance on the recommendations of the Fish and Wildlife Service and of the state fish and wildlife agency. The court reiterated the rule that no agency could rely on another agency's examination of environmental effects under NEPA. Each must independently assess the consequences of a project. FERC's exemption order failed to include such an independent assessment.

FERC further attempts to justify its decision on the basis of its adoption of measures to mitigate the impact of the hydropower project, thereby obviating the need for an EIS. Although the adoption of such measures may justify a decision not to prepare an EIS, in this case, it could not. FERC failed to specifically explain how the conditions would mitigate the impact of the project. Without this type of analysis, a decision not to prepare an EIS could not be justified.

Third, FERC also failed to consider proposed modifications to the existing dam at the site of the proposed hydropower project in determining whether or not to prepare an EIS. FERC claimed such consideration was unnecessary because of a stipulation that the modifications were separate and independent from the hydropower project. The court found this reasoning unsatisfactory; if the completion of one project will inevitably involve the irreversible and irretrievable commitment of resources to the second, both projects must be considered jointly. For two phases of a project, if it would be irrational to undertake the first phase of a project without undertaking the subsequent phase, both must be considered in the determination process. The court ordered that FERC must apply these tests to the issue of whether the proposed dam modifications should be considered in the EIS determination.

Van Abeema v. Fornell, 807 F.2d 633 (8th Cir. 1986) 

In this case, the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit focused on the Corps of Engineers' evaluation of alternatives prior to decision on a permit application for a facility to transload coal on the Mississippi River. The 235

Corps prepared an environmental assessment and finding of no significant impact, and included a number of mitigation measures in the permit. 

In examining the adequacy of the analysis prepared pursuant to NEPA, the Court of Appeals focused on the study of alternatives as required by Section 102(E) of NEPA; that is, the agency's responsibility to "Study, develop, and describe appropriate alternatives to recommended courses of action in any proposal which involves unresolved conflicts concerning alternative uses of available resources;" 42 U.S.C. 4332(2)(E). The Court emphasized that this evaluation of alternatives was to be based upon the general goal of an action: ... . it is not an evaluation of the alternative means by which a particular applicant can reach his goals. In the current proposal the general goal is to deliver coal from mine to utility.... In some discussion of alternatives to the proposal, the Corps has suggested that an alternative may not be feasible at least partly because the applicant does not own the necessary land or perhaps cannot gain access. 

The fact that this applicant does not now own an alternative site is only marginally relevant (if it is relevant at all) to whether feasible alternatives exist to the applicant's proposal.8

The Court's discussion is significant in that it represents further elucidation of agencies' responsibilities under Section 102(E) of NEPA. Further, it provides a different perspective on the question of how the underlying purpose and need for a proposed action should be framed for the purpose of evaluating alternatives. In Roosevelt Campobello International Park Comm. 

v. EPA, for example, the First Circuit Court of Appeals approved of an agency's evaluation of alternatives focused by the primary objectives of the permit applicant. Taken together, the NEPA case law on this issue requires consideration of both public and private purpose and need. The agency may properly focus its evaluation of alternatives based upon the applicant's goals, but it must also exercise independent judgment regarding the appropriate articulation of the objective purpose and need and the reasonableness of various alternatives.

Merrell v. Thomas, 807 F. 2d 776 (9th Cir. 1986) 

In Merrell v. Thomas, the Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals decided that the 

Environmental Protection Agency was not required to follow the National 

Environmental Policy Act when registering new pesticides under the

Federal Insecticide, Fungicide, and Rodenticide Act (FIFRA). Registra tions of certain pesticides in use were challenged as being invalid because the Environmental Protection Agency had neither prepared an EIS under 236

1 the NEPA requirements nor given an explanation of why one would not be necessary.

The court stated that the purpose of the NEPA EIS process is an assurance that agencies give proper consideration to the environmental consequences of their actions. The issue it sought to resolve was whether Congress, in passing NEPA in 1970, sought to superimpose the NEPA requirements onto the pre-existing FIFRA registration procedure. 

The court found that the FIFRA requirements should stand separately from those of NEPA on the basis of its interpretation of Congressional intent as expressed both by FIFRA amendments passed after 1970, and by the differing environmental standards between FIFRA and NEPA, which were left intact through the series of amendments. 

After NEPA's passage in 1970, Congress comprehensively amended FIFRA in 1972. It added a registration procedure to ensure consideration of environmental impacts, as well as an opportunity for public notice and participation. The court noted that Congress did not duplicate the NEPA requirements for either procedure, but rather it developed criteria specific to the FIFRA intent of compromise between environmentalists, farmers, and manufacturers.10

The court found that several marked differences between NEPA and FIFRA reveal Congressional intent that they remain distinctly separate. For 

FIFRA's public notice, the EPA Administrator must publish a notice only in the Federal Register for a pesticide with a new active ingredient or a changed use pattern. This process does not require the type of comprehensive information which NEPA requires by the preparation of an EIS. The 

FIFRA amendments also require the Administrator to act expeditiously to reach a decision on a pesticide within three months of receiving the application. The court noted that such time expeditions were incompatible with the lengthy EIS process required under NEPA. Trade secrets and test data for which subsequent users would have to compensate the applicant were prohibited from release to the public. NEPA contains no such restrictions on information.

In the subsequent amendments of 1975, 1978, and 1984, EPA interpreted FIFRA so as not to require compliance with NEPA. The court found that the Congressional failure to revise or repeal this interpretation on three different occasions was "persuasive evidence" that agency interpretation followed Congressional intent. The 1978 amendments lightened the regulatory burden on industry, pesticide users, and non-federal regulatory agencies, and revised the trade secret disclosure provisions. The court found in this amendment a balance between the public's right to know the basis for agency decision and the right of businesses to prevent manufacturing processes and trade secrets from being divulged. It noted that the Supreme Court specifically upheld the disclosure provisions in Ruckleshaus v. Monsanto 

Co.11 Applying NEPA to FIFRA, the court said, would "sabotage the deli- 237

cate balance that Congress designed", and increase the regulatory burden which Congress intentionally lightened. 12 The court referred to two bills then before Congress, which, it suggested, Congress would not consider if it had intended EPA to prepare an EIS for pesticide registration. The first addressed the limited disclosure to the public of health, safety, and environmental data. The second included a declaration that analyses in support of FIFRA's registration are equivalent to an EIS. The court inferred that Congress believes the analyses for registration are an adequate substitute for an EIS, and that NEPA does not apply. 

The court also emphasized the differing standards for environmental review between the two statutes. The FIFRA standard requires that a pesticide perform "without unreasonable adverse effects on the environment". 

NEPA's standard of review demands preparation of an EIS if it would "significantly affect the quality of the human environment". The court found the 

FIFRA standard to balance environmental harm against economic, social, and environmental benefit. It held that such a compromise developed by 

Congress between environmental and agricultural impacts should not be overturned by judges.

Further, the court noted the differing standards on consideration of alternatives. NEPA requires the discussion of alternatives in the EIS, creating the implication, the court said, of preferring the course of action least en vironmentally harmful. The 1972 amendments to FIFRA specifically make the existence of an alternative to a new pesticide an invalid criterion for deciding against its registration.

Lastly, the court supports its opinion that NEPA should not apply by noting that a similar position has been taken in other FIFRA cases by the D.C. Circuit Court of Appeals, the Tenth Circuit Court of Appeals, and the 

District Court of D.C." 14 13 

Court of Claims Alaskan Arctic Gas Pipeline Co. v. U.S., 9 Cl. Ct. 723, (1986) 

The issue of charging an applicant for an EIS was discussed in this case by the Court of Claims. The gas pipeline company filed an application with the 

Department of Interior for a right of way across federal lands for its proposed pipeline in Alaska, which had triggered preparation of an EIS. 

The plaintiffs' application was incompatible with the proposal finally accepted and its application became "inactive"; however, the costs of processing the application and of a comprehensive system wide environmental impact statement were still assessed against the plaintiffs. The court held that the Department of Interior was authorized to assess fees for its full costs, and that the fees charged were consistent with the National Environmental Policy Act and not excessive. 238

The court began its analysis by finding that NEPA would govern Interior's EIS process, and that to the extent that Interior acted within the guidelines of NEPA, any fees assessed against plaintiffs would be valid and reasonable. The question of whether Interior had acted within NEPA centered on three main points. First, the distance which plaintiffs' rights of way would cross federal lands were minimal compared to the entire distance of the pipeline. The plaintiffs claimed the fees assessed were excessive and not within the guidelines of NEPA in view of this fact. Second, the plaintiffs claimed Interior had gone beyond NEPA by assessing fees when plaintiffs' applications had not been approved. Third, plaintiffs contended that the scope of the EIS should reflect only that portion of the pipeline which would cross federal lands rather than of the entire pipeline system with its alternatives to it. 16 On the first issue of the minimal distance of the right of way, the court found that the accurate analysis of the reasonableness of the fees would be a comparison of the EIS costs to the value to the plaintiffs of the rights of way, not to the distances they cross federal lands.1 The pipeline segments rights of way for which plaintiffs had applied represented long term benefits worth considerably more than the cost of the EIS fees assessed. In view of this analysis, the court found the fees to be reasonable and within the scope of NEPA. 17 On the second issue of whether NEPA permitted EIS fees to be assessed when the application had been denied, the court stated that the value of the EIS to the plaintiffs should be determined at the time the EIS was prepared. In this case, it was prepared while the plaintiffs' applications were still active; therefore, the EIS had value to the plaintiffs to the extent that their applications were still under consideration. The costs of the EIS were incurred only because of the application. The court found the plaintiffs' argument that the value of the EIS should be measured at a later time to be inconsistent with the purposes of the application and EIS review process. The court further noted that at the time the costs were incurred the fees assessed were proportionate to the economic value the plaintiffs expected to receive from the rights of ways. 

The third issue of NEPA's proper scope for an EIS focused on Interior's analysis of the entire pipeline system and its alternatives. Plaintiffs claimed that an EIS prepared for their applications and assessed against them should only analyze the distances their pipelines would cross federal lands. 

The court determined that the central question for assessing the proper scope of the EIS completed by Interior was whether the pipeline segments crossing federal lands, which plaintiffs asserted should determine the scope, had a definable independent utility on their own. Congressional authorization of the pipeline project demonstrated that Congress intended one pipeline system to span the entire distance of Alaska, Canada, and the

United States. The segments were an indivisible fraction of the larger entity. NEPA requires agencies to include in the EIS an analysis of alternatives 239

in all proposals significantly affecting the quality of the human environment. 

This analysis was not only within the scope of NEPA, but was a prerequisite to the issuance of the right of way license sought by the plaintiffs. The court found that the EIS prepared by Interior was within the scope of and required by NEPA, and that the fees assessed against the plaintiffs for the EIS were valid and reasonable.

Trends in NEPA Litigation 

The lowest number of NEPA lawsuits ever filed was recorded during 1985: a total of 77 cases. Concomitantly, the lowest number of injunctions against the government was issued, with a total of eight injunctions. This compares to a high number of 189 cases, filed during 1974, and a high number of twenty-one injunctions during 1983 (statistics for injunctions from the years 1974 to 1978 are not available). See Table B-1 for cumulative NEPA Litigation

Survey. The decline in litigation may reflect the agencies' greater experience and expertise in carrying out their NEPA responsibilities. 

Environmental groups, individuals and citizens groups were the most frequent plaintiffs during 1985, and the Department of Transportation, Department of the Interior, U.S. Army Corps of Engineers, and the Federal 

Energy Regulatory Commission were most frequently sued as defendants. 

As in the majority of years, the most common cause of action was the allegation that no EIS had been prepared for a proposal which required an EIS; the second most common complaint was that an EIS was inadequate. Environmental assessments are beginning to receive increased judicial scrutiny: seven lawsuits contained allegations of inadequate environmental assessments and three lawsuits alleged that an agency failed to prepare an environmental assessment when one was required.
