Special Report: Environmental 

Referrals and the Council on

Environmental Quality 

The NEPA implementing regulations establish a process by which federal agencies may refer to CEQ proposed major federal actions that might cause unsatisfactory environmental effects. 40 C.F.R. Section 1504 (1987). The referral process was initiated in 1973 and has been used by federal agencies on 22 occasions. The 15th Annual Report of the Council on Environmental 

Quality (1984) contains a detailed description of the process and a summary of the first 21 referrals.

In 1985, the Environmental Law Institute was asked to study the past use of the referral process, evaluate its strengths and weaknesses, and, if appropriate, make recommendations for improving the process. That report has been completed and is reprinted in part here. [Sally D. Rand and Mark 

Steven Tawater, "Environmental Referrals and the Council on Environmental Quality" Report by the Environmental Law Institute to the Council on Environmental Quality, February 1986.] The only portion of the study not produced here is the appendix, which contains the individual case studies. 

Factual summaries of the first twenty-one referrals can be found in Environmental Quality, 1984, the Fifteenth Annual Report of the Council on Environmental Quality, 1985, Washington, D.C., pp. 527-558.

A Final Report by the Environmental Law Institute, 

Washington, D.C.

I. 

Introduction The Environmental Law Institute (ELI) was commissioned by the Council on Environmental Quality (CEQ) and the Environmental Protection Agen cy (EPA) to evaluate the effectiveness of the process by which CEQ attempts to resolve inter-agency conflicts concerning the environmental impacts of proposed federal actions. Commonly known as "environmental referrals," these attempts at dispute resolution constitute an important component of CEQ's mission to oversee and coordinate major federal activities that significantly affect the environment.

The purpose of this study was to conduct a thorough analysis of the referral process over the past 12 years, with an eye toward determining its strengths and weaknesses. This report presents findings and evaluations of 248

the effectiveness of the referral process, as well as some recommendations on how the efficacy of the process may be enhanced.

II. 

Methodology 

This evaluation of the CEQ referrals process was based on a case study approach. Case studies, summaries of which appear in the appendix to this report, were completed on 20 of the 22 referrals CEQ has received since the referral process was formally established in 1973. The case studies consisted of reviews of CEQ referral files and telephone interviews with referral participants from disputing agencies and their private sector supporters. 

During the interviews, we sought to determine the interviewee's perceptions of who the key participants in the controversy were; how the proposed federal action came to be referred to CEQ; whether or not broader policy objectives lay behind the referral; what actions CEQ staff and Council members took in response to the referral; and what responses came from the lead and referring agencies.

Additionally, more than a dozen other individuals who have been influential in the evolution and operation of the referral process over the last 13 years were interviewed. These persons include CEQ Chairpersons and 

General Counsels, both past and present; current and former CEQ staff members; and the heads of environmental policy compliance offices in various federal agencies. These individuals were queried more generally about the operation and objectives of the referral process, in addition to being asked about specific referrals in which they were involved. 

While we have made every effort to make an unbiased analysis of the referral process, readers should be aware of the inherent limitations of this study design. First, it is extremely difficult to isolate the impact of CEQ and the referral process from the myriad of other forces affecting federal project decisions. Second, while quantitative evidence is provided to support our conclusions whenever possible, the nature of this project requires certain value judgments to be made regarding the definition and measurement of such intangibles as "environmental quality," and "effectiveness.” Finally, our interviews were of an informal nature and because of the time lapse of up to 15 years since a referral had occurred, some targeted interviewees could not be located or had only a vague recollection of the referral details. 

The case studies and interviews have provided a good deal of information about the referral process and how it has operated over the years. In consideration of this data, we have reached certain tentative conclusions about the efficacy of the referral process. We will first present an overview of the referral process and its history, followed by our evaluation of the actual and potential effectiveness of the process. 249

ATSI FI957 GRI TH ​= ***⠀⠀⠀⠀ # 18 11 WIHR 

III. The Referral Process and Its History A. How the Referral Process Works 

The Council on Environmental Quality is perceived by many as a mediating institution, serving to reconcile competing environmental and developmental interests. While CEQ had substantive duties under the National Environmental Policy Act NEPA, it is first and foremost a branch of the White House staff and is frequently most effective working behind the scenes in quiet diplomacy. Many of CEQ's activities in the mediation field are informal and go unrecorded. A formal mechanism, however, does exist whereby environmental conflicts between federal agencies may receive an impartial hearing and the dispute resolution efforts of the Council. This mechanism is known as the referral process.

NEPA does not explicitly provide for referrals, but CEQ's dispute resolution responsibilities, including its referral activities, may be reasonably inferred from the statute's mandate that CEQ oversee and coordinate major federal actions significantly affecting the environment. The referral process allows federal agencies to bring to CEQ interagency disagreements concerning proposed major federal actions which may cause unsatisfactory environmental effects. CEQ often attempts to resolve the disputes through mediation, sometimes assisted by its own independent fact-finding efforts. 

CEQ normally issues findings and recommendations on referrals, although its recommendations are not legally binding on the parties to a dispute. 

There are two routes by which referrals come to CEQ. First, under Section 1504 of CEQ's NEPA implementation regulations, any federal agency or department may refer a proposed major federal action to CEQ within 25 days after a final environmental impact statement (EIS) on the action has been made available to EPA, commenting agencies, and the public. A second referral route is provided by 309 of the Clean Air Act. Under 309, the EPA Administrator may refer to CEQ any proposed major federal action which he/she deems to be environmentally unsatisfactory. Whichever referral route is applicable, the following criteria will be pertinent to an agency's decision on whether or not to refer a federal action to CEQ: (1) possible violation of national environmental standards or policies; (2) severity of impact; (3) geographical scope of impact; (4) duration of impact; (5) importance as precedent; and/or (6) availability of environmentally preferable alternatives.

A referral is initiated by the delivery of a letter of referral from the head of the referring agency to CEQ. The referring agency must inform the agen- 250

cy proposing the action (lead agency) about its intentions at the earliest possible time. Any other interested parties, whether federal agencies or members of the public, may submit written comments in support of the referral to CEQ up until the time the referral letter is received by the Council. The lead agency then has 25 days from the Council's receipt of the referral to make its response in writing. During that 25 day period, other agencies or members of the public may submit written comments in favor of the lead agency's position.

After CEQ has received the lead agency's written response to the referral, the Council may take any one of the several actions. (1) conclude that the process of referral and response has successfully resolved the problem; (2) initiate discussions with the agencies with the objective of mediation with referring and lead agencies; (3) hold public meetings or hearings to obtain additional views and information; (4) determine that the issue is not one of national importance and request the referring and lead agencies to more fully pursue their decisionmaking processes; (5) determine that the issue should be further negotiated by the referring and lead agencies and is not appropriate for Council consideration until one or more heads of agencies report to the Council that the agencies' disagreements are irreconcilable; (6) publish its findings and recommendations (including where appropriate a finding that the submitted evidence does not support the position of an agency); (7) when appropriate, submit the referral and the response together with the Council's recommendation to the President for action.

The case studies indicate that over the 12 years in which the referral process has been in operation, CEQ has chosen most often to mediate between disputing agencies and to issue findings and recommendations. 

B. A Brief History of the Referral Process 

The formal referral process has been in existence only since 1973, although our interviews indicate that the CEQ staff probably engaged in informal dispute resolution between federal agencies prior to that date. Because CEQ was not granted official rulemaking authority in NEPA, the first provision for formal referrals was made in 1500.9(b) of CEQ's 1973 “Guidelines."

This guidance, however, applied only to referrals made by EPA pursuant to 309 of the Clean Air Act and contained none of the criteria or procedural rules which have come to govern the current referral process. 251

#! ! !IFIKATUIT

RAVIAALI 

""m 

!!!!!

In an effort to establish some formal procedures for referrals, CEQ in 1977 issued further guidelines establishing criteria for referrals, filing and comment deadlines, and potential CEQ depositions. Finally, CEQ exercised its new rulemaking authority granted by President Carter's Executive Order 

11,190 in 1978 and transformed its "Interim Guidelines," with some changes and additions, into official agency regulations which, as previously noted, appear at 42 C.F.R. 1504.

C. A Look at the Referrals to Date 

Since the inception of the formal referral process in 1973, CEQ has received a total of 22 referrals of proposed federal actions. [Ed. note: As of 1987, 23 referrals had been received by CEQ.] We conducted case studies on 20 of those referrals. We were, however, unable to analyze the first referral received by the Council because of missing documents from CEQ's referral files. We also chose not to study the most recent referral because CEQ has yet to issue its findings and recommendations in the case.

The scope of issues embodied in CEQ referrals to date span the spectrum of environmental concerns: from the protection of national parks and forests to nondegradation of air and water quality. The issue of wetlands, however, appears most frequently in referrals. Wetlands protection has been at issue in nine or just over one-third of the referrals CEQ has received.

EPA has been the agency making the most referrals to CEQ. During the period 1973 to 1978, EPA was the only referring agency, making a total of eight referrals. EPA's dominance in these years is explained by the fact that 

CEQ's 1973 Guidelines provided only for referrals under 309 of the Clean

Air Act, and not for referrals by other federal agencies. 

After CEQ adopted its 1977 "Interim Guidelines" providing for referrals by all federal agencies, other agencies began to refer projects. Although most of CEQ's referrals have come from EPA and the Department of Interior (DOI), two federal proposals have recently been referred by the Depart ment of Defense (DOD) and the Advisory Council on Historic Preservation 

(ACHP). Lead agencies involved in referrals have included the Atomic

Energy Commission (AEC), the Bureau of Land Management (BLM), the 

Army Corps of Engineers (COE), the Federal Highway Administration (FHWA), the Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD), the Department of Transportation (DOT), and the Federal Aviation Ad ministration (FAA). 

The fact that CEQ has received only 22 referrals in 13 years is rather intriguing when one considers the vast number of federal and federally-assisted projects which are subject to the environmental impact statement requirements of NEPA. There are two important reasons for this dearth of referral activity. First, since the inception of the referral process, CEQ has discouraged the referral of inter-agency environmental disputes lacking elements of "national importance." This national importance standard is estab- 252

E 1 lished in CEQ regulations as one of the basic criteria for determining the propriety of Council intervention. The rationale for this important limitation on CEQ jurisdiction appears to be a combination of limited Council resources and the fact that NEPA's apparent charge for CEQ is to address environmental concerns which are national in scope rather than to pass judgement on highly politicized or purely local impacts of federal actions. 

Another, and perhaps more important, reason for the small number of CEQ referrals is that the mere existence of the referral process and the threat of its use prompts agencies to seek early and informal resolutions of environmental disputes with would-be referring agencies. Information obtained from our interviews strongly supports the proposition that the existence of the referral process forces lead agencies to more fully consider the objections of their sister agencies, to make accommodations when possible, and even to adopt less environmentally intrusive alternatives. 

For a descriptive chronology of the referrals CEQ has entertained to date, see Table 1.

IV. 

Measures of Effectiveness 

After examining the referral process, along with its history and objectives, we identified three basic criteria upon which the process and CEQ's role therein may be evaluated in terms of its effectiveness.

The first effectiveness criterion is the extent to which CEQ and the referral process are successful in providing a forum for mediation of inter-agency environmental disputes and for public comment and participation. We will refer to this as the "mediation/forum” measure of effectiveness. Indeed, two of the primary purposes of the referral process are to offer the mediation services of CEQ for inter-agency dispute resolution and to open up the environmental decisionmaking process to the views of a wider audience, including those of other federal agencies and private individuals and organizations. Relevant inquiries would thus include: how successful has 

CEQ been in bringing representatives from disputing agencies to the bargaining table for negotiating resolutions to their disputes? To what extent have agency participants in these negotiations been willing to take the process seriously and to arrive at appropriate compromise? Have they, instead, held to intransigent positions, preferring to await CEQ's issuance of its findings and recommendations? Have the comments of private individuals and organizations had a significant effect on the outcomes of the referral process?

The second criterion for effectiveness, which we will call the "NEPA compliance" measure, is the extent to which the referral process furthers the policy mandates of the National Environmental Policy Act. More specifical ly, is the referral process successful in prompting federal agencies to more fully consider the environmental consequences of their actions, as well as the availability of less harmful alternatives and the need for mitigation? relevant inquiry here is whether referrals prompt lead agencies to give more thought to the objections of referring agencies and the public, to commission further studies on the environmental impacts of their proposals and alternatives, and to change their plans in response to new information on potential environmental damage.

Finally, our third measure of effectiveness is the extent to which CEQ findings and recommendations on referrals are followed and implemented by the lead and referring agencies. This criterion we will call "CEQ clout." 

Both NEPA and the referral process establish CEQ as an expert advisory body on procedural and substantive elements of United States environmental policy, but CEQ's referral recommendations are not legally binding on any of the parties to an inter-agency dispute. Therefore, why do federal agencies have enough respect for CEQ's judgement and for its political authority to fully implement its recommendations? Do they view the referral process in effect as binding arbitration? Whatever the perceptions of CEQ may be, a referral provides an orderly way for major environmental disputes between federal agencies to be brought to the attention of the Executive Office of the President. V.

Discussion and Analysis A. How Effective is the Referral Process? 

(1) Mediation/Forum 

ELI's case studies and interviews reveal that the referral process, as administered by CEQ, had been largely successful in bringing disputing agencies together for negotiations aimed at resolving environmental disagreements. Of the 20 referrals studied by ELI, the Council chose to convene meetings between agency representatives in nearly three-quarters of the cases. It is through these meeting that CEQ provides one of its most useful services. "No one expects CEQ to negotiate a compromise" stated one interviewee but by providing a forum and a process for resolution, CEQ appears to be accepted as a neutral party and can provide the service of mediator. According to another interviewee, until a referral, the heads of the respective agencies are frequently not aware of the conflicts. Further, it is often difficult for the agencies involved to meet in an atmosphere where substantive negotiations can occur. CEQ's efforts during the referral process are frequently directed towards defining a mutually acceptable definition of the conflict, and moving towards the identification of potential forms of resolution that each agency would be willing to accept. Thus, the meetings may have been more successful at narrowing the scope of the issues in dispute rather than actually resolving agency disagreements entirely. 258

The referral process has also been successful in opening up the environmental decisionmaking process to a wider public audience. Our case studies reveal that numerous private individuals and organizations interested in particular federal proposals have exercised their right to comment. Participants from the private sector were identified as being active in at least half of the 22 referrals since 1973. The Wando River, Lake Alma and Marco 

Island case studies provide good examples of citizen participation. The effects of such public participation on outcomes is difficult to gauge, yet we can conclude that public input at least brings to the attention of federal decisionmakers the local issues and concerns that are often obscured at the federal level. These purposes are also served by CEQ's public meetings and hearings on particular referrals.

The success of CEQ in its role as a mediator, however, is more uncertain. 

As noted, CEQ is quite successful in bringing the parties together for discussions and assisting in narrowing the scope to disputed issues, but it has not been successful in resolving disputes entirely. Indeed, none of the 20 referrals studied by ELI was resolved purely through the mediation efforts of CEQ. Most of the referrals have followed a scenario of meetings in which disagreements were aired and sometimes narrowed, followed by CEQ factfinding and issuance of conclusions and recommendations. 

There appear to be two important reasons why CEQ's mediation efforts have not resulted in total resolutions of inter-agency disputes. First, by the time a letter of referral reaches CEQ, the positions of the agencies, especially those of the lead agency, are usually firmly entrenched. The lead agency is likely to have already expended a great deal of resources in the preparation of an environmental impact statement and, although it is willing to consider further alternatives and mitigation measures, the agency may be reluctant to abandon or substantially change its course of action. This attitude is strongly expressed in the Corps of Engineers letter of response to CEQ's Dickey- 

Lincoln referral recommendations.

A procedure which allows narrowly focused single-interest agencies such as the Fish and Wildlife Service to continue to raise, near the end of a long decision process, the same issues which have been extensively discussed and reviewed over a period of years, produces unnecessary and inefficient delays and use of agency resources. The referral process is particularly redundant when it is applied to actions which must have executive branch and congressional approval and where the referring agencies' views are routinely included in the decision documents. The key objective is to be sure that the concerns of other agencies are fully considered and recorded during the process, not to allow an agency to continue to raise the same issues again and again to produce added delay and expense. 259

Under such circumstances, CEQ's mediation efforts are probably more productive in a setting of informal negotiations prior to an actual referral. In fact, our case studies and interviews reveal that before 1981, the CEQ staff was conducting informal mediation between agencies prior to formal referrals with some measure of success. Since that time, however, CEQ appears to have limited such activity.

Another important reason why CEQ's mediation efforts during referrals have not resulted in complete resolutions of agency disagreements is the fact that the Council views itself as having a substantive mission apart from its role as a mediator. The Council, for example, would not encourage or approve of an agreement between the parties which it considers to be environmentally unsatisfactory. In addition to questioning the data and arguments of both the referring and lead agencies for some referrals, CEQ had conducted independent research and issued recommendations it found to be most protective of environmental values. (2) NEPA Compliance The referral process has been successful in prompting lead agencies to more fully consider the potential environmental impacts of their proposals in at least three stages. First, as noted before, the existence of the referral process and the threat of its use may have led agencies to be more thorough in the preparation of their environmental impact statements, to give more thoughtful consideration to less intrusive alternatives suggested by commenting agencies, and to examine more closely the need for mitigation measures in their proposals. Almost everyone interviewed for this study confirmed that the threat of referral is one of the most important, if not the most important, factors in forcing agencies toward greater NEPA compliance. 

The referral process and CEQ involvement have also prompted lead agencies to undertake more comprehensive analyses after formal referrals have been filed. In the time between the filing of a referral letter and final recommendations by CEQ, during which talks aimed at resolving the dispute are being held, the lead agency has the opportunity and the incentive to more fully explore reasonable alternatives and mitigation measures. The incentive to do so derives from the fact that CEQ may recommend such further investigation anyway and the recommendation may enjoy the support of the White House. Referral negotiations may not cause lead agencies to fundamentally change their proposals, but the mechanics of referral do force agencies to take a closer look at them, albeit with an eye toward bolstering their positions.

Finally, as part of its official findings and recommendations, the Council may itself suggest that the lead agency more fully consider impacts, alternatives, and mitigation measures. Our case studies reveal that in those cases in which CEQ had recommended further study, the lead agencies have generally complied, and their efforts have resulted in more environmentally 260

satisfactory projects. The Fire Island, I-475 and Wando River referrals are good examples of this facet of the process. (3) CEQ Clout Do lead agencies normally accept and implement the findings and recommendations of the Council? The answer to this question is "yes." In fact, our case studies reveal that lead agencies have almost always followed CEQ's recommendations issued at the conclusion of the referral process. This is especially intriguing when one considers that the Council's recommendations are not legally binding on the parties to a referral. 

There are probably a number of reasons why lead agencies defer to CEQ's findings and recommendations, but our interviews suggest that two reasons are paramount. The first is that CEQ, since its inception, has been viewed as an objective and non-political organization, employing a highly competent professional and technical staff. CEQ investigative reports and studies, both within and without the context of referrals, are respected for their quality and objectivity. Agencies have generally approached the referral process with the notion that their proposals would receive a fair and impartial hearing, thus prompting them to be more receptive to Council determinations. 

A second, and without doubt more important, reason for lead agency deference to CEQ findings and recommendations is the fact that the Council is organizationally situated within the Executive Office of the President and may, at least theoretically, invoke the power and persuasion of that office to further its objectives. Any lead agency within the executive branch is likely to be quite hesitant to offend the political sensibilities of the President and his staff by not following the recommendation of CEQ.

A note of qualification, however, should be injected here. CEQ's clout is dependent not only on its position within the Executive Office but on the moral and political support it receives from the President as well. 

B. Factors Contributing to the Effectiveness of the 

Referral Process

Based on the measures of effectiveness described above, and after careful consideration of our case study findings and the responses of interviewees, we have been able to identify several factors which significantly contribute to the effectiveness of the referral process. 

(1) CEQ's Position in the Executive Office of the 

President

Responses given during our interviews indicate that the single most important factor contributing to the overall effectiveness of the referral process is 261

the location of CEQ within the Executive Office of the President. According to one interviewee, a former CEQ General Counsel, CEQ's opportune organizational position gives the appearance to lead agencies that their proposals, if referred, may come under the scrutiny of White House staff. 

This perception encourages moderation of positions and facilitates compromise. In the Fire Island referral, the Council threatened White House involvement should the lead agency not take satisfactory action. 

The political authority CEQ derives from its position in the Executive Office contributes to the effectiveness of the referral process across each measure described in this study. It makes disputing agencies more willing to come to the bargaining table and to seek compromises, and it forces lead agencies to take more seriously their obligations under NEPA. And, as already alluded to, CEQ's position in the Executive Office gives its findings and recommendations more force than they would otherwise have, leading to greater lead agency deference and compliance.

Agency perceptions of CEQ authority also appear to be affected by the amount of support the Council can expect to receive from the President. 

Several interviewees suggested, for example, that CEQ and the referral process were perceived by federal agencies as being more authoritative during the Nixon and Carter administrations than in the Ford and Reagan years. The most dramatic decline in Executive support for CEQ and the referral process has come during the Reagan administration. This may be an important reason why CEQ has received so few referrals since 1981. [As of 

1987, CEQ had received 6 referrals during the Reagan administration, out of a total of 23 referrals received since the referral process began in 1973.] 

Would-be referring agencies may now see less to be gained from the referral process than before. 

(2) CEQ Competence and Objectivity 

The perception of CEQ as a competent and objective mediator of environmental disputes is another important factor contributing to the effectiveness of the referral process. Historically, federal agencies have been able to rely on the objectivity and independence of CEQ's professional staff and technical experts, hired and retained on a non-political basis. This has made agencies more willing to participate in referral mediation under the guidance of CEQ; it has forced lead agencies to do a more thorough job of considering the environmental impacts of proposals and available alternatives; and it has given CEQ findings and recommendations more weight than they might otherwise have had.

The presence of a highly qualified and competent staff has also allowed CEQ to undertake independent fact-finding in those referral situations in which both the referring and lead agencies' submissions have been inadequate from NEPA's substantive perspectives. For example, in the Lake 

Alma referral, CEQ biologists introduced new scientific evidence which 262

supported the referring agency's arguments. Further, CEO's independent fact-finding capability has pressured lead agencies to be more prepared. 

They have had to be more thorough in their analysis of impacts, alternatives, and mitigation measures. Furthermore, independent fact-finding has allowed CEQ to make findings and recommendations designed to ensure environmentally satisfactory results.

CEQ's image of competence and professionalism, however, may now be in jeopardy due to recent cutbacks in agency personnel. Current and former staff members have informed us that CEQ no longer possesses significant independent fact-finding capability, nor the resources to acquire it on a contractual basis. Instead, the Council must now rely on its powers of persuasion in efforts to force the agencies themselves to do the job of necessary fact-finding. 

(3) Early CEQ Involvement and Familiarity 

Our case studies and interviews reveal that the referral process, and especially CEQ's role as a mediator, is more effective when CEQ staff members are involved as early as possible in inter-agency environmental disputes. The potential for successful mediation and an acceptable resolution is much greater in the early stages of a dispute when agency positions are not so firmly entrenched. For example, interviewees for the Foothills Water 

Project referral all argued that this was one of the least successful referrals because the Council was brought in at the very last moment and had to make a rushed decision. In contrast, the Council was involved early on in the I-476 referral, and was viewed as being much more effective in their conflict resolution efforts. This early intervention by CEQ staff has sometimes taken the form of information mediation; but more often, it has consisted of staff efforts to assist the parties in shaping the issues that will be dealt with in a formal referral.

Whatever the objectives of these early contacts between disputing agencies and CEQ staff, the formal referral process, if and when it occurs, is likely to be beneficial. One former CEQ staff member suggested, for example, that the familiarity with the issues and participants gained through these early informal contacts makes for much smoother negotiations and heightens the potential for successful mediation at the referral stage. Early advice from CEQ staff may also prompt a lead agency to undertake a more comprehensive study of impacts, alternatives, or mitigation measures, thereby sharpening the referral issues or even obviating the need for a referral altogether. If the dispute reaches the point of CEQ final action, the Council, because of its historical involvement, will have a broader data base on which to rest its findings and recommendations. 263

(4) Presence of Independent Authorities 

The applicability of an independent statutory or regulatory authority, apart from NEPA, will enhance the effectiveness of the referral process in particular instances. If, for example, a proposed federal action which is on referral would violate another statute or an executive order, the chances for successful mediation and compromise are heightened. 

The presence of such independent authority strengthens the hand of CEQ in its roles as mediator and as interpreter of national environmental policy. Furthermore, it provides CEQ greater leverage with which to pressure lead agencies to examine more closely available alternatives and mitigation measures. If no compromise can be reached in a particular dispute, independent authority may serve as the definitive factor behind the lead agency's compliance with CEQ's findings and recommendations.

The predominance of wetlands issues in referrals sheds some light on this theory. Interviewees have suggested that, in addition to a substantive mission interest, the likelihood of success at the referral level is an important factor influencing an agency's decision on whether to refer. In wetlands cases, the chances of the referring agency's views prevailing are heightened by the existence of President Carter's Executive Order on Wetlands (EO 

11190) which established a highly protective standard. This standard, when combined with NEPA's substantive mandates, creates a compelling case for a protective pronouncement by CEQ. 

Recommendations for Enhancing the 

Effectiveness of the Referral Process

After careful examination and consideration of the information gathered in our case studies and interviews, we offer the following recommendations aimed at enhancing the effectiveness of the referral process. While we are cognizant of the fact that some of these suggested actions will require a greater expenditure of scarce CEQ staff resources, we think they are essential to a vital and efficacious referral process.

VI. 

A. Greater Use of Information Mediation and Early Involvement by CEQ 

Timing appears to be a major factor in CEQ's ability to play an effective role in resolving a conflict between two agencies. Informal communications between Council staff members and Agency liaisons prior to a formal referral has been identified as having a significant impact on the outcome of a referral. In fact, one interviewee, a former Council staff member, argued that some of the Council's best efforts have resulted in conflict resolution prior to a formal referral. 264

4 Early awareness of a conflict is advantageous to the Council for several reasons. First, at the least, it gives the Council staff an opportunity to become familiar with the project and to identify the key players or individuals involved. Second, it gives CEQ an opportunity to engage in "quiet diplomacy." As a neutral communicator, CEQ can channel concerns and verify the flow of information in a way that is frequently not possible in its absence. This behind-the-scenes activity is also a vital supplement to CEQ efforts to promote the implementation of NEPA. Third, if the Council is notified prior to the referral, CEQ has an opportunity to provide informal counsel which can make the formal process more productive. Finally, prereferral mediation gives CEQ an opportunity to act as a public ombudsman, voicing the concerns of citizens to Washington-level decisionmakers, without compromising the Council's role as Presidential advisor. 

B. Monitoring Implementation of CEQ 

Recommendations

Partly due to the system, and partly due to the large number of responsibilities CEQ has, there has been very little post-referral monitoring. In most instances the CEQ files end with the Council Chairman's letter to the lead and referring agencies. A more effective referral process can be achieved by: 

1. A report by CEQ by the lead and referring agencies tracing their responses to CEQ's recommendations. 

2. Better coverage in CEQ's own files, with continuing staff monitoring of lead agency compliance, through project completion or cancellation. 

3. Better communication to the public through the CEQ annual report, which should report on referrals in enough detail to identify the problems and also report on the lead agency's response to the referral.

C. Broadening Access to Referral Process 

Several interviewees, noting the success of the referral process in the interagency setting, proposed that the power to seek referral should be extended to the governor of the state in which a project is to be developed in order to use the Council as a mediating agency between the federal government and the state government in matters of intergovernmental conflict on environmental matters.

Based on our review of the process, we feel this proposal is of potential value for furthering the mandate of NEPA and improving the effectiveness of the referral process. We recommend that CEQ give this suggestion consideration. 265

VII. Conclusion 

Our analysis of the NEPA Referral Process has led us to conclude that it is a legitimate and viable tool for interagency conflict resolution. The existence of referrals broadens the environmental impact decision process and provides a forum for communication that would otherwise be unattainable. 

The referral process serves the purpose of the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA), which requires environmental values be considered in the balance of the agency decisionmaking process. The process, however, appears to be only as effective as the Council members and staff behind it. 

Perceptions within the federal agencies of the support of the White House, the unbiased professionalism of the Council staff and the credibility of Council Members all play a decisive role in the final effectiveness of the referral process.

Appendix C Environmental Trends 

The statistical tables included in this report as Appendix C present in tabular form all of the data that will appear as graphs and charts in Environmental Trends, 1986, a report currently in preparation by the Council on Environmental Quality. Accordingly, the following statistical tables are numbered to coincide with the format of that report, which will consist of nine chapters. However, since some of the graphic material to be presented in that report consist of maps and other illustrations, not all figures are accompanied by statistical tables; in these cases, the figure number and title appear in the table of contents for this appendix without a corresponding table. The user should be aware that the omission of some tables from the expected numerical order is not an error; rather, all tables in Appendix C are numbered to match the corresponding figures in Environmental Trends, 1986. Researchers will thus be able to confirm environmental trends information depicted in that report by consulting this appendix. 

The statistical series presented here have been taken from data available through government agencies, private studies, or the literature of each discipline. The data and citations have been reviewed by experts at various federal agencies and departments participating in the preparation of Environmental Trends, 1986. National data are used whenever possible, with breakdowns shown only when especially meaningful. 267
